# Geographical Survey of the Puranas

## Preface

The Puranas which belong to the class of religious literature are in fact a supplement to the Vedas. Almost all of them are sectarian in character. They are valuable from the historical point of view not only because they give the lists of dynasties but also because they record the tradition prevalent among the masses. They afford us far greater insight into all aspects of Hinduism, its mythology, its god-worship, its theism and pantheism, love of gods philosophy, superstitions, festivals, ceremonies and its ethics. They occupy a unique position in the sacred and secular literature of the Hindus being next in importance to Vedas only. Not only Vedic metaphysics, mythology and cosmogony, not only the great legends of Brahmanas, not only the accumulated religious tradition of India, not only the multiple cults but also history, culture, chronology, poetry sociology, geography, topography, art, architecture, painting, governmental administration, agriculture, cattle- breeding, economic and social life of the people and a thousand other similar topics mapping out the entire canvas of the nation's life have gone into the making of Pauranika literature.

The Puranas have always fascinated me not only because they are religious works but also due because they are encyclopaedic in character. So after completing my postgraduate studies, I decided to choose a topic from the Puranas for my research.

I finally opted to work on A critical study of the geographical Data available in the Puranas. Fortunately I got a Government of India research scholarship in Humanities. The then Chairman of the Selection Committee (Government of India) advised me to restrict the scope of my subject to the four Puranas namely the Nilamata, the Matsya, the Visnu and the Vayu Puranas only because otherwise the subject would become too vast to be completed within the stipulated time.

The present work is divided into eight chapters. The first one is introductory. Others deal with the general geography of the universe and that of India proper including the study of the geographical information regarding her Janapadas, mountains, rivers, flora and fauna and sacred places. The last chapter deals with certain geographical features regarding which the information available is very meagre and scanty.

Though, strictly speaking, I was required to study only these four Puranas, yet I could not limit my studies to them only. I had to survey other Maha Puranas, Upa-Puranas and the epics which abound in geographical information. I have tried to supplement the Pauranika information by those found in other Sanskrit and Pali works and, the accounts of foreign travellers. A large number of sacred places have been dealt with in the chapter, The sacred places of India. My efforts have been to identify as many of them as possible on the basis of the travelling accounts of the individual pilgrims. Besides, I have consulted the publications of the Archeological Survey of India and other relevant journals. I have tried to be as exact as possible so far as the references are concerned and have rechecked them with the original quite frequently. Unfortunately many of the old editions which I had consulted during the course of my research are not now available, so it is quite possible that I have erred at some places. But this was inevitable in the work of such magnitude. If the humble attempt of mine is able to give even a little satisfaction to the scholars, I would consider my labour amply rewarded.

I am grateful to those writers and authors, especially to Dr. B.C. Law for his references to Pali works, which works I have consulted and utilised for the preparation of this volume. They were the pathfinders (Purve Pathikrtah) whose pioneering attempts opened up a new field of research for the younger generation.

The present work was completed about more than three decades ago. Those days were very strenuous and I was working under heavy odds and rather hostile circumstances. I am grateful to my late parents, my sisters Santosh and Subhadra and my friends who not only provided me with reference books but supported me morally also. I cannot forget Dr. J.C. Mehta and Dr. D.R. Bhasin (both of them ex. - M.S., Central Hospital, N. Delhi) for their co-operation, concern and encouragement extended to me. I will ever remain grateful to them.

My subsequent stay at Aligarh Muslim University as a U.G.C. Research Scholar my job my responsibilities as a family member and my constant not so good health did not permit me to modify and improve my work for publication earlier. Even now its publication has become possible with the financial assistance extended to me by Rashtriya Sanskrit Sansthan to whom I am very grateful. It is only due to the persistent persuation of Shri Surendra Pratap (Nag Publishers) that the work has seen light of day. I owe my thanks to him.

April 9, 1995 Savitri Saxena

## Introduction

The geographical knowledge is very necessary for a proper understanding of the ancient history and culture of a nation. The physical features of a country immensely influence the character of its people and their political life. The Himalayas, the Indian ocean and the passes on the north-west frontier of pre-partition India are well known for the influence they have exercised over the political history of India. The climate and the products of a country greatly affect the physique and the nature of its people. Moreover, the geographical knowledge is all the more essential in the case of a country like India which has a long history and a brilliant past. The geographical knowledge may not only be helpful to enable us to understand the Indian history but also to comprehend fully the import of the geographical terms used in the various religious, mythological and the literary works.

A knowledge of space, no less than that of time, of geography no less than that of chronology is an indispensable prerequisite of a serious historical study. says Dr. H.C. Raychaudhuri.

The independent treatises dealing with geography are not common in ancient Indian Sanskrit literature. But many a time the geographical information is definitely found in the religious, legendary and astrological treatises of ancient India. In literary works, ancient and modern, we find a number of useful geographical references. These help us in knowing the proper sites of various mountains, rivers and cities etc., as well as the geography of the particular areas.

The geographical knowledge of ancient India is uptill now far from satisfactory and a large number of geographical names have not so far been identified. Consequently, the history of ancient India also remains incomplete. The Puranas which abound in geographical information can be of very great value in the correct identification of so many geographical names mentioned in Sanskrit literature. Some help from the Puranas has of course been derived to achieve this purpose. For example, N.L. Dey, B.C. Law, and Cunningham have taken the help from the Puranas in their attempts to identify various ancient sites, of the mountains and cities etc.. As. their works mainly deal with the geography of Buddhist India so there is a necessity to study the geography of Brahmanic India.

The different Puranas give us very valuable information about the geography of ancient India. Almost all the Puranas are replete with geographical data. The description of the creation (Sarga) is said to be one of the essential characteristics of the Puranas. The Puranas not only give the order of the four fold creation but also afford us an opportunity to have a glimpse of ancient India as a geographical entity. The geographical information found in the Puranas can be classified under the following heads :-

(1) Bhuvanakosa or Bhuvanavinyasa - The Puranas have two-fold account in these sections (i) the description of seven continents, their varsas, mountains, rivers and the people inhabiting them. The earth is conceived of as a lotus having four petals in the form of four continents (i.e. Bhadrasva in the east, Jambu in the south, Ketumala in the east and Uttarakuru in the north and the Meru mountain as forming the pericarp of this lotus. Later on, it seems, the earth came to be known as having seven continents surrounded by the seven oceans. The Jambudvipa, in the midst of which stands Meru, is the middle one, the other six being the continents named Saka, Plaksa, Salmali, Kusa, Kraunca and Puskara.

(ii) Description of Bharata - The country of Bharata, one of the nine divisions of Jambu-dvipa is lauded as 'Punyabhumi and Karmabhumi'. The Purarias in the first instance tell us'about the name, shape and extent of Bharata. Most of the Puranas have a list of Janapadas, mountains, rivers, sacred places, flora and fauna of India. Generally the Janapadas are classifie'd according to the direction (Disa) which they occupy. Thus we have the list of countries situated in the midland (Madhyadesa), in the east (Pracya), in the north (Udicya), in the south (Daksinapatha), in the north-west (Aparanta) and the Janapadas situated on the banks of NarmadaVindhya mountain and the mountain Himalaya.

Besides, the Kurma-Vibhaga or the Kurma Samsthana of the Markandeya Purana (ch.55), the Narada Purana (I ch. 56) and the Kurma Purana (I ch. 46) depict Visnu as seated in his Kurma form over the entire of Bharata as covered by his body with his face turned towards the eastern direction. The Janapadas are listed according to their position in the different limbs of the Kurma i.e. Madhyabhaga (centre), Mukha (east), Purva-daksinapada (south - east), Daksina-kuksi (south), Daksina pada (south - west), Vamakuksi (north), Purvottara-pada (north-east), Vamapada (north-west) and Puccha (west).

??? lists of Janapadas in the Vayu, the Brahmanda, the Matsya, the Markandeya, the Vamana and the Brahma Puranas are comprehensive and those found in the Visnu, the Kurma, the Garuda, the Visnudharmottara and the Siva Puranas are abridged and condensed. The Bhavisya Purana has no definite list but it refers to a good number of Janapadas. The information in the Padma Purana (account of the Asvamedha of Rama) and the Brahma Purana have a similarity with the Ramayana's account. The list of the Padma Purana has a close resemblance with the list of the BhTsmaparva of the Mahabharata as there is no definite order in both the lists. The description in the Vayu Purana has a close affinity with those of the Varaha Purana and the Brahmanda Purana. The Kurmavibhaga of the Markandeya, the Kurma and the Narada Puranas is based on the Kurmavibhaga section of the Brhatsamhita of Varahamihira (ch.14).

Among the mountains of India, the Himalayas and its various peaks are copiously described. Then there are seven Kulaparvatas i.e. Mahendra, Malaya, Sahya, Suktiman, Rksa, Vindhya and Pariyatra. Besides, a few smaller mountains are also described.

The division of the rivers listed in the Puranas is based on the basis of the place of their source. Thus we have the list of the rivers originating in the Himalaya and the seven Kulacalas.

(3) There are incidental references to many Janapadas, cities, mountains, rivers, forests, ponds, lakes, islands and sacred places in the (i) geneologies of the kings and sages, (ii) in the Digvijaya accounts of the kings, (iii) in the description of Abhiseka ceremonies, (iv) in the description of pilgrimages undertaken by the celebrities as well as by the individual pilgrims and (v) the description of the sraddhas.

(4) Description of the sacred places - Every Purana has got a list of tirthas. The Skanda Purana and the Padma Purana, the two most voluminous of all the Puranas have the largest number of sacred places. To say that the Skanda Purana is virtually an encyclopaedia of the tirthas would not be an exaggeration. Its various Khandas are devoted to the description of the tirthas situated in a particular area. Next to these two Puranas are the Visnudharmottara Purana the Agni Purana the Vamana Purana, the Linga Purana the Brahmanda Purana and the Narada Purana which have got a considerable number of tirthas.

The sixty-eight Sivaksetras sacred to Rudra find mention in the Linga Purana. The Siva Burana gives a number of saiva-tirthas situated in the Himvatpradesa i.e. Himalaya, Kailasa and Mandara mountains. The Vamana Purana records the Sivaksetras situated in the sacred forests. The Matsya Purana gives a list of Saktipithas.

The Puranas glorify some very important tirthas and give very minute details of the various sub-tlrthas situated in those tirthas. Thus we have the Mahatmyas of Prayaga (Siva Purana, Kurma Purana, Narada Purana Padma Purana and Matsya Purana), Gaya (Markandeya Purana Agni Purana, Narada Purana and Vayu Purana), Varanasi (Kurma Purana, Agni Purana, Narada Purana, Matsya Purana and Skanda Purana), KalindT Mathura and related tirthas (Padma Purana, Visnu Purana, Brahmavaivarta Purana, Bhagavata Purana and Linga Purana), Gangadvara (Narada Purana and Padma Purana), Setu-Mahatmya (Narada Purana), Narmada Mahatmya (Kurma Purana, Skanda Purana, Matsya Purana, Agni Purana Narada Purana and Padma Purana) GautamT Mahatmya (Brahma Purana) Ganga Mahatmya (Vayu Purana, Narada Purana, Padma Purana, Visnu-dharmottara Purana and Matsya Purana), Avanti Mahatmya (Narada Purana and Skanda Purana), Purusottamaksetra Mahatmya (Padma Purana and Skanda Purana), Puskara Mahatmya (Narada Purana and Padma Purana), Arunacala Mahatmya Venkatacala - Mahatmya, MahTsagara Samgama and Narmadasagara Samgama (Skanda Purana) and so on.

The collection and the classification of all the geographical material available in the Puranas and the identification of each and every place name is a stupendous task. It is very very difficult if not impossible to undertake such a study. The Puranas themselves declare -

(Matsya P. 1124-6, Brahmanda Purana Purva. Anu. 155.)

I have not ventured into the study of the geography of the seven continents because it, in itself, is a subject important enough to be a subject of independent study. To undertake such a study one must have a comprehensive knowledge of the topography of the areas adjoining Bharata such as Iran, Russia and China. Besides, one must have a thorough knowledge of not only the language of these regions, but also of their history, religion, cultural heritage and local traditions. It is not possible for a single researcher even to do a thorough and critical study of the geography of Bharata as depicted in all the Maha-Puranas and Upa-Puranas.

I have, therefore, chosen the four Puranas, namely the NTlamata the Matsya, the Visnu and the Vayu Purana for a critical survey of the geographical material contained in them. These four Puranas areby the consensus of opinion regarded as earlier than 1000 A.D. and some of them like the Vayu Puraria are dated as early as the second century A.D. Therefore, the information which these Purarias supply is highly valuable for reconstructing the geography of ancient India. Moreover, a large part of India is covered by the information contained in them. The Nilamata, for instance, gives authentic information about the topography of Kasmira, the northernmost part of India and it is quoted as an authority by Kalhana the author of the Rajatarangini. The Nilamata Purana also gives very interesting details about the products and seasonal festivals of Kasmira. For example, it describes the festival celebrated in ancient Kasmira at the first snow-fall of the year. The Matsya Purana, which is regarded as generally reliable about the history of the Andhra dynasty, yields a good deal of information about the geography of southern India. It also speaks of the important sacred places situated in the north. The Matsya Purana is definitely one of the earlier Puranas. The Visnu Purana and the Vayu Purana also belong to the group of the earlier Puranas and some of the facts recorded by them are very valuable. For example, the Visnu Purana gives reliable information about the Maurya dynasty and the Vayu Purana describes the rule of the Guptas. So the geographical data contained in them relates to the period of the Mauryas and the Guptas. Thus these four Puranas taken together would, it is hoped, present a clear picture of the geography of ancient India.

The whole work has been divided into eight chapters. The first chapter is introductory which deals with the importance, the scope and the plan of the subject. The various sources from which we learn about the ancient geography of India have also been discussed.

The second chapter entitled The Universe is devoted to the study of the seven continents, their encircling oceans, their mountains, rivers and races.

The third chapter India: Her Jana-padas deals with the extent, the shape, the divisions, and the Janapadas of ancient India. The Puranas clearly divide them in different groups according to the direction in which they are situated. Thus they speak of the people of the east, the west, the north, the south and the centre. Besides, they also make note of the people living on the banks of the Narmada, the Vindhya-plateau and those occupying the hilly regions.

The fourth chapter The mountains of India deals with the mountains described in the Puranas. The Puranas divide them into various groups just as Varsaparvatas, Maryadaparvatas and Kulacalas. They describe the Himalaya in detail and mention its various peaks. The seven Kulacalas namely the Mahendra, Malaya, Sahya, Suktimat, Rksa, Vindhya and Pariyatra along with the minor hills attached to them are also described.

The fifth chapter deals with the Rivers of India. The division of the rivers in various groups is based on a very simple principle i.e. according to the mountain in which they have their source. Thus the total number of the rivers is divided into eight groups i.e. those issuing from the Himalayas and those having their sources in the seven Kulacalas.

The sixth chapter India: Her Flora and Fauna deals with Indian animals and the vegetation. The animals and the vegetations are broadly speaking, divided into two main groups, forest animals and uncultivated vegetation and domestic animals and cultivated vegetation. The animals are further divided into those which are milk yielding and those whose flesh is eaten.

The seventh chapter The sacred places of India deals with the sacred sites of India. Since the Puranas are essentially religious in their character, a long list of the sacred places appears in them. The sacred character is attached not only to the mountains, rivers and cities but also to the bathing ghats, idols, stone slabs, temples, trees, Sivalingas and even to the fountains and springs.

The eighth chapter deals with a few geographical facts which though available in the above mentioned Puranas are not systematically treated. They include the climate, forests, means of conveyance and occupations.

A comparative study of these Puranas reveals the fact that they have got a general similarity in their geographical descriptions e.g. all of them speak of the seven continents and seven oceans encircling them. The order of their location and their extent is also the same. The major mountains such as the Himalaya, Meru, Nila, Nisadha and the seven Kulacalas are described practically in the same way in all the Puranas. The rivers and the Janapadas, too, have similar divisions. It is only in case of the sacred sites that the respective Purarias give preference to the local sacred places, e.g. the Nilamata describes the Kashmirian tirthas and the Matsya gives the detailed information regarding the tirthas that lay on the banks of the Narmada.

Again, sometimes the names of the places differ very little the difference depending merely on the corruption of the text. The variants in the names, too, may not unoften come across in the different Puranas. Such is the case only with the less known places. As regards the important places there is no such confusion.

One of the most striking features of the geographical description of the Puranas is that it is mingled with religious and legendary accounts to an extent that it becomes very difficult to hit at reality. The famous legends of the demon Gaya, Nikumbha, the Pisacas of Kasmira and the legend of the Safisara can be cited as examples.

The accounts given in the Puranas are generally fabulous, exaggerated fanciful and sometimes even self contradictory. The description of the mount Meru, seven continents, the residents of the Himalayas and such other descriptions may be mentioned in this context.

The greatest difficulty that comes in the way of proper identification of the various places, rivers etc. is that though the Puranas give long lists of the Janapadas, rivers, mountains and sacred places, yet they are silent regarding their details. We see that a long list of Janapadas is given, but nowhere a specific location is assigned to them. Similarly many rivers are mentioned as issuing from such and such a mountain but nowhere a precise location of their source or their fall into the ocean or in the other river is given. Except in the case of Ganga or its tributaries, nowhere a river is said to have passed through such and such a country. Similar is the case with the mountains etc.. Though a large number of the sacred places including even the minor ones are mentioned, yet nowhere, except only in a few cases, their location or nature is given.

So in the absence of any such internal evidence in the Puranas themselves, we have to depend on other sources. We see that from the Vedas upto the classical Kavyas, Sanskrit writers have thrown light on the geography of their own period in one way or the other. The horizon of the Vedic geography extended beyond the limits of India and included the plateau of Iran and the tableland of Central Asia. In the early Vedic literature we find references to the land of the seven rivers only but by the close of the Rgvedic period, we see that the Aryans had a knowledge of a vast territory extending from Eastern Afghanistan to the basin of the upper Ganges. They had heard even of the distant non-Aryan people like the Kkatas of Magadha in the east and the mount Urjayanta in the south (Rgveda II 13.8). Later on they conquered the fertile plains of Sirhind and Thanesvara and reached upto Ganga and Yamuna. Some of them pushed as far as the banks of Sarayu and occupied at least a part of the Madhyadesa. Though they knew of the Daksinapatha yet they had not the knowledge of the details about the topography of southern India. By the time of the Yajurveda, Atharvaveda and the earlier Brahmanas they had a firm hold over the Gangetic Doab and had flourishing settlements in central India on the banks of the Varanavati and even further to the east. They had occupied even the Malwa region, the banks of Yamuna and had crossed Sarasvati as well as the river SadanTra. On the northern side, they had reached upto the countries of Vahllkas and Gandharas. During the time of the later Brahmanas, Aranyakas and the Upanisads, the mountain Vindhya, the great southern mountain, makes its appearance for the first time. On the eastern side the Aryans had come to know of the habitats of the Dasyu people beyond the SadanTra. In the south, they knew of the Non-Aryan settlements just as those of the Andhras, Sabaras, Pulindas and Mutibas besides the Aryan settlements of Nisadhas, Satvatas, Vaidarbhas and the Bhojas.

By the time of the Brahmanical and the Buddhist Sutras they had founded new kingdoms in the west and in the south. References to Yavanas or Greeks are found now for the first time. The western realms of the mixed origin like AvantI, Surastra, Sindhu and SauvTra are also mentioned. In the south they had thoroughly explored the shore of the Indian ocean. They had come to know the countries like Kalinga, Vanga and Pundra etc. which they branded as impure. The Janapada of Pragjyotisa was wholly unknown to them and we see that even in the epic period that region was regarded as inhabited by the barbarious borders.

The evidence of the Ramayana shows that the Aryans had started infilterating the country south of the river Godavari. The territory upto the Pampa river was haunted by the Aryan sages. The Aryan princes had reached as far as Sri Lanka (Ceylon). The Kiskindhakanda of the Ramayana records a number of countries, mountains and rivers situated in the four directions. The Bhismaparva of the Mahabharata also gives very important information about the territorial divisions rivers, mountains, lakes, forests and the sacred places of India.

The Bhuvanakosa and the Kurmavibhaga sections of the different Puranas are equally important. The Jambudvipavarnana of the Mahabharata gives the Pauranic lists of the countries and people in a more detailed form but without any arrangement. Though the particulars of Bharata as given in the BhTsmaparva agree in general with those given in the Puranas, yet in some cases it gives some additional information also.

The majority of the names of the countries and peoples found in the Puranas agree with those given in the Nadyadivarnana of the Mahabharata. The details about the Indian topography given in the Kumankhanda (Mahesvarakhanda) of the Skanda Purana are far advanced than those found in the Bhuvanakosa section of the other Puranas. The extensive Mahatmya literature which forms part of the Puranas deals with the topography of the various sacred places and as such their importance from the geographical point of view cannot be over emphasized.

The Yogavasistha of Sri Valmiki also has got a section on geography which describes the seven continents and Indian Janapadas along with its rivers and mountains. Besides, a large number of the literary works on law like that of Manu, polity like that of Kautilya, poetics like that of Kavyamimamsa of Rajasekhara, lexicons like that of Amara, works on sectarian rituals and mythology, as well as the classical Kavyas, literary works like those of Kalidasa and epigraphs like those of Harisena contain passages which prove of immense help. Besides, astrological works like the Brhatsamhita and grammatical works like the Astadhyayi and the Mahabhasya and the Tantrika works like the Yogimtantra and the Saktisamgamatantra also prove very useful for the study of the geography of ancient India.

In addition to Sanskrit literature, the Pali literature, too, is important for the geographical knowledge of India in Buddha's time and thereafter. The main source of the geographical and historical information about India is supplemented by Buddhist and Jain sources here and there. The Pali Pitakas, especially the Vinaya and the Sutta give much information about the Madhyadesa and the localities bordering on the Madhyadesa. Milindapanha, an important non-canonical Pali text, and the Mahavastu, a Buddhist Sanskrit work, contain very important geographical notices in them. Besides, there are many other works which give valuable information regarding the geography of India. The DTpavamsa, the Mahavamsa and the commentaries on the Pali works furnish us with bits of the geographical knowledge of the Buddhists regarding ancient India. The JambOdivapannati, the sixth Upanga of the Jains describes Jambudvipa, Bharata and the seven Varsas constituting the seven main divisions of the Jambudvipa. The VividhatTrthakalpa of Jinaprabha SQri has geography mixed up with the legends.

Besides, there exist a large number of works written by foreign authors and travellers. The historians of Alexander who accompanied him on his Indian expedition were the first to give to the western world a fairly accurate acount of India and her people. Three of his companions wrote about India. All their works are now lost. Only a brief substance of their records is found in the works of Strabo, Pliny and Arrian. Arrian was the famous author of the account of the Asiatic expedition of Alexander the Great. His Indika is-divided into three parts. In the first part, he gives the general description of India which is based on the description of India as given by Megasthenes and Eratosthenes. An account of the voyage made by Nearchos the Cretan from the Indus to the Pastigris appears in the second part. In the last part he gives sufficient proofs that the southern parts of the universe are not inhabitable due to the excessive isolation. He refers to the regions beyond the Indus on the west and mentions the countries to the east of the Indus giving them the name 'India.'

Though Pliny's treatment of the subject in his Natural History is not critical yet his work contains many incidental facts which are important. The Periplus of the Erythraen sea is a sort of guide book having an account of the trade and commerce carried on from the Red sea and the coast of Africa to the East Indies. It has got a detailed information regarding the Indian ocean including its bordering seas, and some important cities of India.

The work of Ptolemy is of greatest importance. It contains eight books in which he deals with the Gangetic Valley, gives an account of the countries, cities, towns, rivers, mountains, hills and the parts of India lying beyond the Ganges, inland towns and villages of the trans-Gangeric India, seven mountain ranges of the south, rivers of the Indus system, and territories and peoples of India. He gives also the situation of a particular tribe or the situation of a particular mountain in longitude and latitude.

Among the Chinese accounts regarding India the most important ones are those of Fa-hien and Hiuen Tsang. That of Hiuen Tsang is fuller and more exhaustive. It is valuable for an accurate and exhaustive geography of northern India and the historic sites of the interior of India for the period of approximately two hundred years i.e. from 500 A.D. - 700 A.D.

Some Muslim writers have also written about India. Alberuni in his Tahkik-i-Hind deals with the Indian geography. India known to him was Brahmanic and not Buddhistic India. He describes Madhyadesa, Prayaga, Sthanesvara, Kanyakubja, Nepal, Kasmira, other countries and towns, rivers, animals, western and southern frontiers of India, western frontier mountains of India, islands and rainfalls etc.. It helps us in having a glimpse into the geographical texts of the Purarias which were available to him.

Besides, many inscriptions and coins prove to be of importance for both history and geography. They not only present to us a historical map of India in a particular period, but supplement the geographical information derived from the literary sources. The Imperial and Provincial Gazetteers are really a great source of information. The Report of Archeological Survey of India, geographical references in the Epigraphica Indica, Carpus Inscription Indicarum, South Indian Inscriptions and Epigraphica Carnatica are also valuable for their geographical material.

Thus to supplement the geographical information gathered from the Puranas I have studied a good number of works, including one on the general principles of geography. I have made a comparative study of the eighteen Puranas, the epics and other classical works of Sanskrit literature. Requisite references have. also been given from the Vedic and grammatical works.

Moreover the accounts of foreign geographers and the works of modern scholars like those of Cunningham, Dey, Law, Dave and others have also been consulted. Some help from the histories that deal with ancient India, such as those of Rapson, Raychaudhuri, Smith, Ghosh and others has also been taken in identification of the places. The various narratives of the travelling accounts of Chaudhuri, Gorde, WadeU, Murray, Tillman, Shri Gopal Nevada and Rahul Samkrityayana have also proved helpful.

## The Universe

## The Origin of the Universe

As the Puranas are essentially religious in their character we do not find any satisfactory answer to the question as to how this universe came into existence. As all the problems are solved on a mythological basis, the origin of the universe has also been given a mythological touch. The Visnu Purana (Part I ch. 1st) gives an answer to the query of Maitreya about the origin of the universe, the elements of which it is constituted, the origin of the oceans and mountains etc., the support of the earth, the dimension of the sun and the moon.

The answer is philosophical in its nature as it says that the universe has come out of Visnu, is stationed in Him, and it enters into Him after destruction. In the beginning the earth lay submerged under the water. The creator Brahma, under the guise of Varaha, brought it out in the beginning of the Varaha Kalpa (the one in which we now live) as he has done previously in the form of Matsya and Kurma etc.. He established the earth over water, made it even, and established the mountains upon it. The Vayu Purana gives almost the same account of the origin of the universe. Later on the god Hari divided the earth into seven continents and seven oceans, made forty varsas in these continents and the same number of mountains were also formed. The Matsya Purana and the Nilamata Purana give the famous deluge theory regarding the origin of the universe. It relates how the sage Manu gave up his kingdom and performed severe penances on the Malaya mountain. Brahma, pleased with him granted him a boon that whenever this universe were to be deluged, Manu would be able to save it. After some time, one day when Manu was offering oblation to Pitrs, a little fish came out from his water pot and fell down on the earth. Manu, having pity on it, again put it in his water pot. During the short period of a day, it grew up and the waterpot became insufficient for it. The sage Manu put it in a large pitcher. Soon it, too, became insufficient for the fish as it grew up more. Manu put it in the well and afterwards in a pond. But each time the place of accommodation became insufficient for it. From pond, Manu had to transfer it to the river Ganga and then to the ocean. Then only Manu came to realise that the fish was no other than Lord Visnu himself. He worshipped the god who in the end told him about the coming deluge, and asked him to protect a few species of all creatures from that deluge. Giving him a boat, he asked Manu to tie it to his horn (under the form of the fish) at the time of storm. Accordingly when deluge occured, god Visnu appeared before Manu, who put all sorts of seeds and the species of all the creatures into the boat and tied it to the horn of the fish on the Naubandhan peak in the western part of Kashmir. It was Sati who was pretransformed in the shape of a boat. Here sage Manu asks the fish about the origin maintenance and the destruction of the universe and the creation of the human beings etc.. The reply given is purely mythological in character.

After some time Manu started the creation and Sati became the earth. The 5th chapter of the first part of the Visnu Purana gives the order of the creation as plants, birds, quadrupeds, gods, men, mountains and immovable things etc. stage by stage.

The earth is supposed to be like a lotus with four continents of Bharata, Ketumala, Bhadrasva and Uttarakuru as its four petals and the mount Meru as ils pericarp.

The extent of the earth along with its mountains and oceans is said to be fifty crore yojanas (five hundred million of yojanas - Pancasatkotivistara).There are thousands of DvTpas in this universe and it is very difficult to describe them authoritatively in their entirety even in hundred years. All of them can be incorporated within the seven great continents.

## The seven continents

Though the universe is said to be variously divided into eighteen continents nine continentsand four continents as well, yet the general tendency among the Puranic writers is to regard it as having the seven continents. The earth is called as Saptadvipa prthvi or Saptadvipavasumati. There are se»en oceans surrounding them. Each continent and its surrounding ocean are equal in extent and are twice the preceding one. The names of the continents are Jambu, Plaksa, Salmali, Kusa, Kraunca, Saka and Puskara and the oceans surrounding them are called the oceans of Lavana, Iksu, Sura, Sarpis, Dadhi, Dugdha and Jala.

The king of the universe named Priyavrata had ten sons named Agmdhra, Agnibahu, Vapusman, Dyutiman, Medha, Medhatithi, Bhavya (Havya) Savana, Putra and Jyotisman. Out of them three i.e. Agnibahu, Putra and Medha were not addicted to worldly things and resorted to penance. The remaining seven sons were installed in the seven continents. Thus Agmdhra was given the continent named Jambu. Vapusman had Salmalidvipa. Dyutiman, Medhatithi, Havya, Savana and Jyotisman were given the continents named Kraunca, Plaksa, Saka, Puskara and Kusa respectively.

## Jambudvipa

The Jambudvipa is situated in the midst of all the continents and in its midst is situated the mountain Meru. The mountain ranges south of Meru are Himavan, Hemakuta and Nisadha while the northern ones are named Nila, Sveta and Srngi.

This circular continent which is named after the Jambu tree is said to be 100000 yojanas in extent.

The whole diameter of the Jambudvipa has been said to be 100000 yojanas. This is thus divided from north to south: Ilavrta, in the centre, extends each way 9000 making 18000: Meru itself, at the base, is 16000: the six Varsas at 9000 each are equal to 54000: and six ranges, at 2000 each, are 12000 and the total comes to 100000 yojanas from east to west. The Varsas are of the extent necessary to occupy the space of the circle (Wilson).

It is surrounded'by the ocean of the salty water, the form of which is like that of a bracelet and which is of the same extent. Colonel Wilford has identified it with India. About Jambudvipa V. Venkatacalam Aiyyer writes. It now signifies the whole known world. It was the land in all probability to the north of the Black Sea and the sea of Azov, the land through which the Borysthenes (Dnieper) and the Tanais (Don) made their way to the Black Sea. The importance which the name Jambudvipa has obtained in Puranic conceptions and its survival to the present day from ancient times, points to the fact that the ancient Sanskrit Aryans believed that their ancestors had their abode in a quarter which by tradition was known as Jambudvipa. Whichever country they entered was to them Jambudvipa irrespective of its physical geography. As the peregrinations of the. Sanskrit Aryans took them over a vast extent of Asia, the territory traversed was necessarily Jambudvipa. When they passed into India, this was also included in the vast orbit of the ever expanding Jambudvipa. After some time,  
however, India by itself could appear to have been regarded as Jambudvipa.

In the epics and Puranas, the Jambudvipa is one which in its narrower sense is identical with Bharatavarsa or the Indian Peninsula. Most probably the Vayu Purana and the Varaha Purana, too, suggest the identification of Jambudvipa with Bharata as they record the extent of Jambudvipa as one thousand yojanas, which is the extent of Bharata as well.

The continent is known to the Chinese as Shin-tup or Sindhu.

## The river Jambu

It issues from the water of the large fruits of the Jambu tree that fall on the earth. The Vayu Purana gives its source in the lake Candraprabha, situated to the west of the Meru. The Jambu tree is said to have been situated to the south of the Meru and to the north of the Nisadha.

## The Meru

The mountain Meru, a Varsaparvata. has been described in the Puranas as four coloured i.e. white on the eastern side yellow on the southern side black on the western side and red on the northern side. Its shape has been likened to a pericarp of a lotus or as a saucer.

The shape of the Meru, according to this descripiton is that of an inverted cone; and by the comparison to the seed cup, its form should be circular: but there seems to be some uncertainty upon this subject amongst the Pauranikas. The Padma Purana compares its form to the bell-shaped flower of the Dhatura. The Vayu represents it as having four sides of different colours.... but notices various opinions of the outline of the mountain, which according to Atri had a hundred angles, and according to Bhrgu a thousand. Savarni calls it octangular, Bhaguri quadrangular and Varsayani says that it has a thousand angles. Galava makes it saucershaped and Garga considers it to be twisted like braided hair and others maintain that it is circular. The Linga Purana describes its eastern face as of the colour of the ruby, its southern face as that of the lotus, its western face as golden, and its northern like that of a coral. The Matsya Purana gives a description similar to that of the Vayu Purana.

Its extent and the length varies in different Puranas. It is said to be 100000 yojanas, 24000 yojanas, 16000 yopnas, 64000 yojanas and 36000 yojanas in extent. The extent of its trip is 32000 yojanas, 84000 yojanas and 48000 yojanas. Its circumference is said to be twice its breadth and thrice its extent. It is 16000 yojanas deep under ground and is 28000 yojanas broad. Its height is 84000 yojanas.

This beautiful, uneven, quadrangular, charming and celestial mountain has got unfold minerals, ample water and many herbs. It is situated in the midst of the Jambudvipa, beyond Harivarsa, to the south of Nila, to the north of Nisadha and in between Gandhamadana and Malyavan mountains.

The continents of Bhadrasva, Ketumala, Jambudvipa and Uttarakuru are situated to the east, west, south and north of the mountain Meru. There are four Viskambha mountains on the four sides of Meru. To the east of Meru is the mountain Mandara and to the west is the mountain Vipula. The mountain Suparsva is situated to the north of Meru while to the south of Meru is the mountain called Gandhamadana. The Kadamba, Asvattha, Nyagrodha and Jambu trees are placed on the mountains Mandara, Vipula, Suparsva and Gandhamadana respectively.

The boundary mountains of Meru are Hemakuta, Himavan and Nisadha to the south and Nila, Sveta and Srngi to the north. The mountain ranges on the east of Meru are Jathara Devakuta, Sitambha, Kumuda, Kuran, Malyavan and Vaikanka which have got extremely charming valleys and picturesque cities situated in their bosom. The mountain ranges in the west of Meru are Sikhivasa, Vaidurya, Kapila, Gandhamadana, Jarudhi, Nisadha and Pariyatra. To the north of Meru are situated Sankhakuta, Rsabha, Naga, Hamsa, Kalanjara, Trisrnga and Jarudhi. The mountains Trikuta, Sisira Patanga, Rucaka, Nisadha, Kapila, Siva, Kailasa and Himavan are situated to the south of Meru. The Markandeya Purana gives a more elaborate list of these mountain ranges.

The mountains Jathara and Devakuta, extending northward and southward, connect the mountains Nila and Nisadha. The mountains Gandhamadana and Kaliasa extend in the east and the west respectively.

This mountain is regarded to be the Rudra Himalaya in Garhwal, where the Ganga has got its source. It is called Pancaparvata, from its five peaks i.e. Rudra Himalaya, Visnupuri, Brahmapuri, Udgarikantha, and SvargarohinT. The Ganga is said to have issued from the Sumeru Parvata and to fall into the ocean flowing through the country of Bharatavarsa on the south. The Kedaranatha mountain in Garhwal is still traditionally known as the original Sumeru. Mr. Sherring says that all local traditions fix mountain Meru as lying direct to the north of the Almora district. It has been identified with the Hindukush mountain, the Pamir plateau and the Karakoram range as well. There are two mountain peaks called Kinnara Kailasa and Mani Mahesa Kailasa in the Kinnaur and Chamba districts of Himachal Pradesh. The Kinnara-Kailasa is considered to be associated with the god Siva as the place of his devotion and penances. There is a iva-linga on this mountain peak at the height of 21000 ft. above sea-level which changes its colour during the day. It is white before the sun-rise. It turns yellow after sun-rise and becomes red at noon. At the time of sun-set it becomes black. The mountain Meru is also sa'd to be four - coloured i.e. white to the east, yellow to the south, red to the north and black to the west. It is quite possible that the phenomenon of the changing colours of the Siva-linga (situated at the Kinnara-Kailasa) got transferred to the mountain itself. In that case, the mountain Sumeru should be identified with the Kinnara Kailasa mountain in the Kinnaur district of Himachal Pradesh.

## The six boundary mountains of the Jambudvipa:

They are Himavan, Hemakuta, Nisadha, Nila, Sveta and Srngi. Out of them the first three are situated to the south of Meru and the latter three to the north of Meru. Nila and Nisadha, the larger ones extend upto 100000 yojanas, the height and breadth being 2000 yojanas. They extend upto 2000 yojanas in north and the south. Hemakuta and Sveta, the smaller ones extend to 88000 yojanas and Himavan and the Srngavan, the smallest ones extend upto 80000 yojanas.

## Himavan

The mountain Himavan, which is situated to the south of Meru, Kailasa and Hemakuta, separates eastern and western oceans on both the sides. It is 20000 yojanas less then the Rsabha i..e Meru, thus being 80000 yojanas in extent. It is always covered with snow. Consequently the countries beyond the Himalayas have only .nominal rainfall. Its inhabitants are the Raksasas, Pisacas, Yaksas, Vidyadharas, Kinnaras, Gandharvas and Apsaras.

## Hemakuta

The mountain Hemakuta, also known as Hemaparvata, is situated on the banks of the river Nanda. It is another name for the Kailasa mountain. It is 12000 yojanas less than the Rsabha mountain, is 2000 yojanas in height and breadth. It is situated to the south of Meru and Nisadhaand to the north of the Himvan. Since the rainfall here is always due to the frost (Tusara) only, the Himalayan breeze are always snowy. Beyond Hemakuta is the Varsa named Ibhasya. The rivers named Manasvini and JyotismatT rise from the Sayana lake which is situated behind the Hemakuta. Its inhabitants are Apsaras, Sesa, Vasuki, and Taksakas.

It is the Bunderpunchchha range of the Himalayas in which the Alakananda, the Ganga and the Yamuna have their source.

## Nisadha

This is situated to the south of Meru beyond Hemakuta. It has been located in the west of Meru also. Its extent is given as 100000 yojanas and the height and breadth as 2000 yojanas. Its varsa is called Ilavrta. The lake Visnupada is situated on the mountain Nisadha from which rise the GandharvT and Nanvall rivers. The mountain which lies to the west of Gandhamadana and to the north of the Kabul river is called by Greeks Paropamisos and is now called Hindu-kush. Paropamisos is perhaps a contraction of Parvata-Upa-Nisadha, or might have been derived from the word Paripatra, the name of the western most peak of the Nisadha range. Pamir is perhaps a corruption of Paripatra. The Paropamisos, the Hindu-kush and the Koh-i-Baba appear to be the names of the different parts of the westerly continuation of the great Himalayan chain.

## Nila

The Varsaparvata called Nila has been placed to the north of Meru beyond Ilavrta. It is a boundary mountain of Jambudvipa particularly that of Ramyakavarsa stretching upto eastern and western oceans. It is a hill in Utkala (modern Orissa) near Ganga-Sagara Samgama. The temple of Jagannatha is situated on a gentle slope, by the sea, which is known as Nila Parvata. Another hill about three miles from Guvahati in Assam on which the temple of Kamakhya is situated L' also known as Nilacala.

## Sveta.

It is situated to the north of Meru. There are three lakes on it (1) Uttaramanasa from which issue the rivers Jyotsana and Mrgakanta, (2) Rudrakanta lake and (3) the lake called Jaya from which rise the river Santi and Madhvl. These two rivers flowing over the mountains Rsabha and Dundubhi etc. flow into the Salty ocean. It is a boundary mountain of Hiranyavarsa and belongs to the Himalayan chain of mountains. This may be identified with that portion of the Himalaya which is in the east of Tibet.

## Jarudhi

It is a northern varsaparvata and is situated to the north of Meru. The lake Anandajala which is 30 yojanas in length and breadth has pure water, blooming lotuses and birds.

## Jathara and Devakuta

They have been variously located in the eastern and southern direction, northern and southern direction and only in the eastern direction, as extending upto Nila and Nisadha.

## Srngavan

This three peaked multicoloured Varsaparvata is placed to the north of the Meru and Sveta mountains, stretching up to the eastern and western ocean. It extends over 80000 yojanas, the height and breadth being 2000 yojanas. A lake named Sailoda is situated on it from which rises the river Sailoda and Caksusi. It is placed near Visuva lineand is mentioned as an Indian mountain along with the mountain Suktiman.

## Malyavan

It extends for 32000 yojanas in the western direction of Meru, to the east of Gandhamadana, to the south of Nila and to the north of Nisadha. It is an Indian mountain situated between Nila and Nisadha. Here flows the river Jambu. It has been placed in the Kiskindha region also.

The Amaragandika is situated to the west of Gandhamadana and extends for 32000 yojanas. Its varsa is called Ketumala. The Purvagandika is situated to the eastern side of Malyavan and extends for 32000 yojanas. Its varsa is called Bhadrasa in which a forest named Kalamra is situated.

The mountains Mandara, Vipula, Suparsva and Gandhamadana are situated to the east, west, north and the south respectively of the mountain Meru.On the Mandara mountain and on the bank of the Acchhoda is situated the Caitraratha forest. The forest named Nandana is situated on the bank of MandakinT on the mountain named Gandhamadana. The Vaibhraja forest is situated on the bank of Sarayu on the mountain Vipula. The forest on the mountain Suparsva has been called Savitr in some Puranas and Nandana in others.

They may be understood to be the exterior barriers of the Meru separating it from the Ilaavrta. The names of these mountains, according to the Bhagavat, are the Jathara and the Devakuta on the east, the Pavana and the Paripatra on the west, the Trisrnga and the Mandara on the north, the Kailasa and the Karavira on the south...... The scheme was suggested by the imperfect acquaintance with the actual character of the country, by the four great ranges, the Altai, Muztag or Thian-shan, Ku-en-lun and the Himalaya, which traverse central Asia in a direction from east to west, with a greater or lesser inclination from north to the south, which are connected or divided by many lofty transverse ridges, which enclose several large lakes and which give rise to the great rivers that water Siberia, China, Tartary and Hindustan.

## Varsas of the Jambudvipa

AgnTdhra, the king of the Jambudvipa had nine sons among whom he divided the Jambudvipa.

Ketumala was given the Gandhamadana varsa.Nabhi got the Hemavarsa situated to the south of the Himavan. Kimpurusa had the Hemakuta varsa,and Hari got the Naisadhavarsa. Ilavrta's share was the Ilavrtavarsa in the midst of which is the Meru mountain. Ramya's territory was situated between the Ilavrtavarsa and the Nila mountain. Hiranman got the Svetavarsa to the north of which is situated the Nila mountain. Kuru's territory lay to the south of the Srngavan. Bhadrasva got the varsa called Malyavata situated to the east of the Meru.

The number of the Varsas in the Matsya Purana and the Visnu Purana is only seven. The Ilavrtavarsa is situated in the centre of all the varsas. The Bhadrasvavarsa and the Ketumalavarsa are situated to the west and east of it respectively. To the south of Ilavrta is situated the country of Bharata beyond which is the Kimpurusavarsa. To the north of the Ilavrta is Hiranyakavarsa beyond which is the Uttarakuru or Kuru varsa. The Harivarsa is located in the south-west and Ramyakavarsa has been placed in north-west direction.

The Vamana Purana is more precise in locating these varsas. The Ilavrtavarsa is in the centre, of Jambudvipa. To the south of it is Bharata. Hirarimanvarsa is in the south-east and Harivarsa is in the south-west. The Kuruvarsa is in the north of Ilavrtavarsa. The Campaka (Ramyaka) is in the north-west while Kimpurusavarsa is in the north-east. Ketumala is in the west and in the east is Bhadrasvavarsa.

## Ilavrtavarsa

This varsa, the middle one extending on all sides of the Meru for 36000 yojanas, is situated in between the Ketumala and the Bhadrasvavarsa and between the mountains Gandhamadana and Malyavan. It is situated in the midst of the Jambudvipa beyond Harivarsa. It is named so after ?? i.e. Budha in guise. At another place this varsa is said to have extended for 9000 yojanas in each direction from the four sides of the Meru. There is no sun, no moon and no stars. The people here are fair coloured, red eyed and long lived, and they put on golden clothes. The Bhavisya Purana says that people from Bharata came and settled here.

## Himavatvarsa

The Himavatvarsa also known as Bharata, is situated to the south of Meru and the Himalayas and to the north of the ocean. Bharata, the son of Rsabha was given this southern varsa which came to be known as Bharata after him. It extends for 9000 yojanas.

## Kimpurusavarsa

The Kimpurusavarsa, also called Hema-kutavarsa extends over 9000 yojanas, from the boundary of the Himavatvarsa upto the second mountain Hemakuta in the south of Meru beyond Bharata. Another Purana places it in the north-east. The people are very beautiful and longlived. There is a great Plaksa tree here.

## Harivarsa

The Harivarsa, known as Naisadhavarsa as well, extends for 9000 yojanas in between the Hemakuta and the Nisadha, and to the south of Meru beyond Kimpurusavarsa. People are beautiful, longlived and do not suffer from old age.It is the western portion of Tibet.

## Ramyakavarsa

It is called NUavarsa also. The Puranas do not agree about its location. The Matsya and Varaha place it beyond Ilavrta and say that it extends upto the Nila mountain. The Visnu and the Kurma locate it to the south of the Nda and to the north of the Nisadha and the Meru. The Varaha, at another place places it to the south of Meru and to the north of the Sveta, while the Vayu places it to the south of Sveta and to the north of the Nila.The people of Ramyakavarsa are amorous, faircoloured, healthy and prosperous. There is a charming Banyan tree named Rohina here.

## Kuruvarsa or Srngasaka

The Srngasaka, also famous as the Kuruvarsais situated beyond the Himavatvarsa and stretches upto Srngavan. The Vayu Purana places it to the south of northern ocean and the Skanda Purana gives its location between Srngavan and the ocean.

The Brahmanda also places it to the south of ocean and to the north of Srngavan. The Matsya Purana agrees with the Brahmanda. Its shape is likened to that of a bow. The trees of the sweet fruits also yield ornaments and clothes, perhaps implying that they put on bark garments and flowery ornaments.

## Hiranyakavarsa

Hiranyakavarsa whose extent is 9000 yojanasis situated to the south east of the Ilavrta, in the north of the Meru, beyond the Ramyaka upto the Sveta. The Sveta and the Srngavan mountains are situated to its north and south respectively. A river named Hirarivati flows here. The people are valiant, strong, gentle, of good descent and pleasing nature, fair coloured, charming in appearance and longlived.

## Bhadrasavarsa

The Bhadrasvavarsa also called Malyavan-varsa is situated on a mountain named Purvagaridika in the east of the Ilavrta. The Mandara mountain and the Caitraratha forest are located in this region. There are five Kulaparvatas, Saivala, Varna malagra, Karanja, Sveta and Nua. It has been divided into many regions having many rivers. The river Sita is said to be flowing through the country of Bhadrasva. The countries are woody, prosperous and rich. The people are fair coloured, strong vigorous, truthful, non-violent and of large physique. Dr. Raychaudhuri has identified it with Eastern Turkistan and North China.

**Ketumalavarsa**

The Ketumalavarsa was named as such after Ketumala, the son of AgnTdhra. The Vayu Purana gives an account of its getting the name Ketumala. It says that Indra defeated the Daityas during the churning of the KsTrasagara. The garland which Indra was wearing got crushed during the fight. He took it off and placed it on the Pipala tree situated on the western peak of Meru as a banner of victory. The garland is still fresh there. Since the continent is distinguished with the garland (Mala as a ketu i.e. flag) it is Called Ketumala. It is called Gandha-madanavarsa as well.

It is situated on the Amaragandika to the west of Gandhamadana, Meru and Ilavrta and Nisadha mountain. The Skanda Purana places it between Gandhamadana and ocean while the Visnudhar-mottara Purana locates it between Nisadha and Nila mountains.

The river Caksu is mentioned as traversing through the region of Ketumalavarsa and ultimately entering the north-western sea. The Vayu Purana and the Varaha Purana give a long list of rivers, mountains and Janapadas of the Ketumalavarsa. The country is charming and prosperous. The inhabitants are devoted to the performance of pious deeds. The men are dark in complexion. They are very strong, healthy and high-spirited. The women are fair-coloured, attractive and very lovely.

According to Dr. Raychaudhuri the western varsa Ketumala drained by the river Vamksu (Caksu = Oxus) which flows past Cina, Maru, the countries of Tusaras, Pahlavas, Daradas and Sakas etc. is to be connected with western Turkestan.

## Uttarakuru.

The country of the Uttarakuru, whose extent is 9000 yojanas, is situated beyond Hiranmaya to the north of the Meru and the Jatudhi, extending from the mountain Srrigavan upto the southern ocean.There are two famous mountains Candrakanta and Suryakanta in the midst of which flows the mountain Suparsva and the Savitr forest in the country of Uttarakurus. According to the Mahabharata and the Padma Purana it is situated to the north of Meru and to the south of the Nila mountain in the foothills of the Gandhamadana mountain. The Buddhists give its extent as 10000 yojanas and regard it as a Pratyantadvipa. The rivers are of sweet water, the trees yield clothes, and the wind is agreeable. There is abundance of eatables and it looks like heaven on the earth on account of its virtues. This is regarded to be a semi-mythical country.

Dr. V.S. Aggarwal has identified it with Chinese Turkestan in the Central Asia. It is the northern portion of Garhwal and the Hunadesa where the river Mandakini and the Caitraratha forest are situated. It originally included the countries beyond the Himalayas. It is the Ottorokorra of Ptolemy. Lassen places it to the east of Kashgar. Tibet and Eastern Turkestan were included in the Uttarakuru. It was situated in the Himalayas. According to Dr. Bunsen the slopes of the Belur Tagh, a mountain range in Central Asia in the high land of the Pamir in which the great rivers of that region have got their source, are the Uttarakuru of the Aryan Hindus. The Belur Tagh is also called the Kinnlun. It forms the northern boundary of the western Tibet and is covered with perpetual snow. It is also called Mustagh, Karakorum, Hindukush and Tsunlung.It was also called Harivarsa. The Brahmanda Purana places it far to the north of India, and mentions that it was bounded on the north by the ocean.

The mention of the Uttarakurus as living beyond the Himalayas can easily be accounted for as it is just possible that a branch of their tribe had settled in Kashmir and another branch seems to have settled on the Indus and the Chenab.

The Candravlpa situated to the south of Uttarakuru has a mountain named Kumudaprabha. The river Candravati flows here. It has got variegated gardens, springs and large peaks. The Bhadrakara dvipa is situated to the west of the continent.

## The lakes

## Arunoda

The lake Arunoda is situated to the east of the Meru on the mount Mandara. To the east of this lake are situated the mountains Silanta, Kumunja, Vaikanka, Manisaila, Kraunca, Mahariua, Savindu, Mandara, Renuman, Sumedha, Nisadha and the Devacala. A Droni, 300 yojanas long and 100 yojanas broad, is situated between the Kumunja and the Sitanta. There is a lake named Srisara and a forest named Vilvavana also known as Srivana. A forest named Campakavana is situated in between Vaikanka and Manisaila. It is 100 yojanas long and two yojanas broad. The Tilavana, 50 yojanas long and 30 yojanas broad, is situated between Mahanlla and Kumunja. There is a plain, 1000 yojanas long and 100 yojanas broad, in between Venuman and Sumedha. No tree, no shrub, or living being is found there. Only the green turf can be seen there.A rocky plateau, 1000 yojanas long and-100 yojanas broad is situated to the north of the Devacala (Nisadha). Here, too, no trees etc. are found.

## Manasa

It is situated to the south of the Meru on the mount Gandhamadana. The Matsya Purana places it on the Vaidyuta mountain near the Kailasa. The .Kurma and Agni give the name of this lake as Mahabhadra. The mountains to the south of this lake are Srisikhara, Nagottama, Sisira, Patanga, Tamrabha, Visakha, Samula Visadhara, Vasudhara, Ratnadhara, Kalinga, Rucaka, Sanuman, Svetodara, Ekasrnga, Mahamula, Gajasaila, Pisacaka, Pancasaila and the Kailasa. The forest named Udumbaravana is situated in between the mountains Sisira and Patanga. Its circumference is 100 yojanas. Its soil is soft and slippery. Here the sage Kardama had his hermitage. A lake named Mahapunya which is 100 yojana long and two yojanas broad is situated between the mountains Patanga and the Tamrabha. The forest called Amravana is situated between the Visakha and the Patanga. The Vilvavana is situated between the Samula and the Visadhara. It is 30 yojanas in length and 50 yojanas in breadth. Another forest named Kimsuka is situated between the Vasudhara and the Ratnadhara.It is 30 yojanas broad and 100 yojanas long. Another forest land, 100 yojanas long and 63 yojanas broad, horrible and inapproachable, is situated between the Pancasaila and the Kailasa.

## Sitoda or Asitoda

It is situated on the mountain Vipula to the west of the Meru. The mountains opposite (west of) Sitoda are Suvaksa, Sikhisaila, Kala, Vaidurya, Kapila, Pingala, Rudra, Surasa, Kumuda, Madhuman, Anjana, Mukuta, Krsna, Pandura, Sahasrasikhara, Pariyatra and Trisrnga. Between the Kumuda and the Anjana mountain is situated a Kesaradrom. The country situated between the Krsna and Pandura is 90 yojanas long and 30 yojanas broad. No trees or creepers are found there. There is a mountain peak 100 yojanas long and 30 yojanas broad in between the Sahasrasikhara and the Kumuda mountain. It may be identified with the Sarik-kul lake in the Pamir.

## Mahabhadra

It is situated on the mount Suparsva to the north of the Meru. The mountains to the north of the Mahabhadra are Mahasaila, Sankhakuta, Puspaka, Vrsabha, Meghasaila, Jarudhi, Hamsa, Naga, Kapila, Sanuman, Indrasaila, Nlla, Kanakasrnga, Satasrnga, Karanja, Vasudhara, Ratnadhatu, Hemasrnga, Gajasaila, Sumedha Hemakaksa, Anala, Pancakuta, Satasrnga, Tamrabha, Visakha, Svetodara, Pisaca, Harikuta and other peaks.

The ParusakasthalT is situated between Sankukuta and Vrsabha. The Parusa fruits of this SthalT are sweet and delicious. KananasthalT is situated in the midst of the Puspaka and the Meghasaila. It is 100 yojanas broad and 60 yojanas long. The soil is hard, yellow and solid. There is entire absence of the trees, creepers, shrubs and grass. The ManoharasthaE, situated between Naga and Kapila mountains is 200 yojanas long and 100 yojanas broad. It is situated in the Malli Desoli.

## Other Lakes

The lake Sailoda is situated on Snmana mountain. The lake Candraprabha from which issues the river Jambu is situated behind the Meru.The Payoda lake is on the Nila mountain. From this lake issue the Pundarika and the Payoda rivers.

## Bharata

The continent of Bharata, which is said to have been situated to the south of the Hemadri and the Ilavrta was ruled over by Rsabha. It was named Bharata after Manus who were called 'Bharata'. It was named Bharata after the king Bharata, the son of Rsabha and grandson of Nabhi. At another place it is said to have been named after Bharata, the son of Sakuntala and Dusyanta.

It is said to have been situated to the south of the Himalaya and to the north of the ocean. The Markandeya Purana is more precise in its location when it records that it has the Himalayas in the north and is surrounded by the ocean on three sides.

The continent of Bharata is said to have been divided into nine parts namely Indradvipa, Kaseru, Tamraparna, Gabhastiman, Nagadvipa, Saumya, Gandharva, Varuna and Bharata also called Kuman. The Vayu, at one place records that the continent was previously divided in seven parts only.

Its extent from north to south is said to be 1000 yojanas in some Puranas and 9000 yojanas in others. It is said to be long and oblique in its appearance. The four castes namely the Brahmanas, Ksatriyas, Vaisyas and Sudras live in the middle of Bharata. The Kiratas, Yavanas, Turuskas, and Andhras live in the east, west, north and south respectively.

Almost all of the Puranas refer to Bharata as Ayam because the authors of the Puranas composed the Puranas here in Bharata. They did not give its name but referred to it by a pronoun Ayam. In the 9th cent. A.D. the royal poet of the Gurjara Pratiharas named Rajasekhara in his Kavya-Mimamsa (ch.17) quoted the same verse and used the name Kumaridvipa for this continent of Bharata. The Vamana Purana and the Skanda Purana, too, call it as Kumaridvipa.

From the first century A.D. onwards the Indians extended their sway over the eastern and the western oceans and brought a Greater Bharata into existence. Slowly and gradually Indian culture, language, religion and art enveloped the islands such as Java, Sumatra (Sri Vijaya) Bourneo (Varuna Dvlpa) Malaya, Kambuja, Campa, Syam (Dvaravati) Keda (Katahadvipa) etc. so much that these regions were taken to be part and parcel of Bharata. Thus the territorial sovereignty of India extended to cover not only the mainland but also the islands beyond the ocean.

Puranic authors divided it into 9 parts. Indra dvipa has been identified with Burma. In Kautilya's Arth'asastra, Tampraparna has been called as Parasamudra meaning on 'the other side of the ocean'.

To these Vayu Purana adds six minor Dvipas, i.e. Angadvipa, Yamadvipa, Matsyadvipa, Kumu'da-dvipa, Varahadvipa and Sankhadvipa. They are said to be inhabited for the most part by the Miecchas who worship Hindu divinities. The Bhagavata names ̮eight such islands Svarnaprastha, Candra-sukia, Avartana, Ramanaka, Mandaraharina, Panca-janya, Simhala and Lanka.

Colonel Wilford identifies Varaha with Europe, Kusa with Asia minor etc., Sankha with Africa, Malaya with Malacca. Yama is undetermined and Anga is understood as China.

## Salmali

The continent Salmali which is named after the silk cotton tree. is twice in extent to that of Kraunca. Vapusman the king of the continent, had seven sons named Sveta, Harita, Jimuta Rohita, Vaidyuta, Manasa and Suprabha. Their respective Varsas were named after them. There were seven mountains named Kumuda, Unnata, Balahaka, Dronacala, Kamika, Mahisa and Kakudman, and seven rivers named Yom, Toya, Vitrsna, Candra, Mukta, Vimocana and Nivrtti. The continent encircles the ocean of the juice of sugar cane and is encircled by the ocean of wine. The castes that live here are Kapila, Aruna, Pita and Krsna.

It has been identified by Sircar with Mesopatimia Assyria and Choldia. Choldia appears to be a corruption of Salmali-dvipa. Perhaps the rivers Nivrtti and Vitrsna are the Euphrates and Tigris respectively. Colonel Wilford regards it to be the Eastern Europe while V. Venkatachalam Aiyyer thinks it to be Sarmatia.

## Kusa

The Kusadvipa, which is twice of Sakadvipa in its dimension and is named after a huge clump of Kusa grass is encircled by the ocean of ghee and itself surrounds the ocean of wine. The continent had seven varsas named after their respective kings. They are called Udvida or Udbhida, Venuman, Svai-ratha or Vairatha, Lavana, Dhrti, Prabhakara and Kapila. Though there are many mountains, and rivers only seven mountains and seven rivers are important. The mountains are Vidruma, Hemasaila, Dyutiman, Puspavan, Kusesaya, Hari and Mandara-cala and the rivers are Dhutapapa, Siva, Pavitra, Sammati, Vidyudambha and Mahi. The four castes are named DambhT, Susmi, Sneha and Mandeha.

The continent is circular in form, the extent being twice that of Salmalidvipa and Ksirasagara. The mountains are lofty, the cities are beautiful and there is an abundance of crops, fruits flowers, beasts and domestic animals.

It seems that the ancient Persian people had the knowledge of a continent named Kusa for the old Persian Inscriptions mention both the country called Kush and its people. The Hamdan Inscription of Darius (C.522-486 B.C.) gives the boundaries of the empire of that great monarch as from the Scythians that are beyond Sogdiana (i.e. the Bukhara region between the Oxus and the Jaxartes) from there as far as Kush, from Hindu (i.e. Sindhu or India meaning the land of the river Indus) there from as far as Sparda (in Sardis in Asia Minor). Some scholars identify Kush with Ethiopia while others place it in central Egypt.

George Stanley Faber in his 'Origin of Pagen Idolatry (Vol. II) writes What the Greeks called Ethiopia the sacred writers denominated the land of Cush. The Hindu geographers speak of Cushadvipa with and Cush dvipa without, meaning by the former the vast tract of country which comprehends Persia, Babylonia, Bactria, Cashgar, Boutan and Thibet, and by the latter the African districts separated from them by the sea, which were also colonized by the descendents of Cush and to which we at present exclusively apply the name of Ethiopia.

As Mudraya (i.e. Egypt) and Kush are both mentioned in the list of̮Satrapies of the ancient Persian emperors, the identification of Kush with Ethiopia seems to be more reasonable. There is no doubt that the country called Kush was situated somewhere in North-East Africa beyond Mudraya or Egypt. Wilford identifies it with the countries between Mesopotamia and India.

The dvipa ends on the east with the Amu Darya and confines to the west of upper India. It is the country between the Tigris to the west and the Amu or Oxus and Indus to the east. The name survives in modern Khuzistan which is the ancient country of Elam and Susiana.

## Krauncadvipa

The continent of Kraunca which is named .after the mountain Kraunca, is encircled by the ocean of curd and itself encircles the ocean of ghee.

The Krauncadvipa, the extent of which is twice to that of the Kusadvipa was divided into seven varsas by its king who had seven sons and who gave one Varsa to each of them. The Varsas named Kusala, Mandaga, Usna, Pivara, Andhakaraka.

Muni and Dundubhi had seven mountains named Krauftca, Vamanaka, Andhakaraka, Divavrta, Divinda, Pundarika and Dundubhisvana and seven rivers named Gauri, Kumudvati, Sandhya, Ratri, Manojava, Ksanti and Pundarika. The four castes named Puskara, Puskala, Dhanya and Tisya live here. Tamil grammar has a section on poetics giving regulations about the proprieties to be observed by poets in discription of countries, peoples and their habits. Climatic conditions are differentiated in only five divisions. One of these divisions is named Kurinji or Kurinchi Nilam stated to be highly mountaineous, and the god worshipped there is Skanda. This Kurinchi Nilam was Lycia and Skanda was Lycian Apallo. Sanskrit Kraunca is an adaptation of that word. Lycia and Cilicia are adjoining provinces, the western portion of the latter is of the same geographical formation and topographically is but a continuation of the former. Both are deeply intersected by the ramification of the Taurus mountains by which they are bounded on the north. The Krauncadvipa is the Asia minor.

## Plaksa

The Plaksa continent, named after the Plaksa tree is encircled by the ocean of sugarcane and itself surrounds the salty ocean. Its extent is twice to that of Jambudvipa. The king of the continent had seven sons and their respective varsas were named after them. The Santabhayavarsa is near Gomeda mountain; Sikhara is near mountain named Candra. Sukhodaya exists near Narada while Ananda is in the vicinity of Dundubhi. The Varsa of Siva is near Soma, Ksemaka is near Rsabha and Dhruva is situated near Vaibhraja.

There are seven mountains named Gomeda, Candra, Narada, Dundubhi, Vaibhraja, Somaka and Sumana. The seven rivers are called Anutapta, Sikhi, Vipasa, Tridiva, Akiama, Amrta and Sukrta.The four castes living here are Aryaka. Kurara, Vidisya and Bhavi. People are meritorious, longlived, always healthy and prosperous. Divine herbs, fruits, corn, paddy, plants vegetables and thosuands of domestic and wild animals are found there. The Padma Purana says that the king Divodasa ruled over the Plaksa Dvlpa. It has been identified with Asia Minor.

The name was obtained by Metathesis from Pelasgia (Plagsia-Plaksha). It was the land of the Pelasgi, inclusive of Thessaly, Mecodoinia, Epirus, the Peloponnesus, the Isles of the Aegean etc. The Pelasgians were an ancient race lost to history under their proper name but rendered illustrious, according to some writers, as claiming the ancestory of the Greeks, the Romans and some other nations of Europe. According to Herodotus the Athenians were certainly Pelasgi. Plaksadvipa was the Pelasgia of ancient history. Dr. H.C. Raychaudhuri places the Plaksadvipa in the region immediately to the west of Indus.

## Saka Dvipa

The Sakadvipa named after the Saka (teak) treeencircles the ocean of curd and in tarn is encircled by the ocean of milk (or by the ocean of Lavana).Its extent is twice to that of Kraunca (or Jambu).The king had seven sons after whom were known the seven divisions of the continent. The names of the Varsas are given as Jalada, Kumara, Sukumara, Maricaka, Kusumoda, Maudaki and Mahadruma. The charming and excellent mountains are Udaya, Jaladhara or Jatadhara, Raivataka, Syama, Astagiri, Ambikeya and Kesari. The seven rivers are Sukumari, Kuman, NalinT, Dhenuka, Iksu,-Venuka and Gavasti or Gabhasti. The countries on their banks are called Santa, Pramoda, Siva, Ananda, Sukha, Ksemaka and Nava. The four castes living here are Nrga or Vanga Magadha, Manasa and Mandaga. The Bhavisya Purana (Brahma 13977) says that they worship the god Sun.

Wilford has identified it with British Isles. The country inhabited by the Saka people was called Scythia by the Greeks. They gave the name Scythians to its people. The different settlements of the Sakas are mentioned in the old Persian Inscriptions. One of these settlements was in the plains of the river Jaxartes or Syr Darya and Oxus or Amu Darya in the central Asia. Some scholars think that later on these Sakas occupied the valley of the Helmund river in the eastern part of Iran.Both the old and the new settlements of the Saka people were called Scythia while east Iran is known to have been called Sakastan (modern Seistan, medieval Sijistan) by Persians and Sakadvipa or SakadvYpa by Indians. The Sakadvipa or Sakadvipa in the original Puranic conception of the Saptadvipa VasumatT may have indicated the Saka settlement in the Oxus and Jaxartes valleys, the region to the east, north and west of the Caspian sea in central Asia.

Dr. Raychaudhury proposes its identification with Seistan in Iran, the land of Magi and of the Mihira cult, on the basis of its habitants being the worshippers of Sun.

According to Dey the Tartary including Turkestan in the central Asia is the country of the Sakas. Scythia and Sogdiana are the corruptions of the Sakadvipa. The Greek geographers placed them to the east of Sogdiana (modern Pamir), the country between Bokhara and Samarkhand. Strabo located them in the country situated to the east of the Caspian sea. In 160 B.C. the Yushtis or Yucchis, a tribe of the Tartars, expelled the Sakas from Sogdiana. The Sakas made their way through the Greek kingdoms which were given to Candragupta by Selukos and which became independent after Ashoka, and invaded India through Sindh. They established themselves in the Gangetic Doab  
(Mathura), Ujjayini and Girinagara as Ksatrapas under the king at Seistan (the land of Sse or Sakas). The Yushtis or Yucchis conquered Bactria in 126 B.C.. After about a century one of their branches called Kusanas became powerful. They, after defeating the Sakas in Seistan moved forward to India, conquered Punjab and ousted the Sakas from Mathura. Their kingdom extended from Bactria to the Gangetic Doab. Their capital lay at Taksasila and their subordinate capital was at Mathura. Kanishka, the king of the Kusana tribe, established his empire in India in 1st or 2nd century A.D..

We find a striking similarity of the names of Sakadvipa given in the Mahabharata and those given by Ptolemy.

The Kumuda of Mahabharata corresponds to the country inhabited by the Komedai (a mountain district called Komedorum Montes by the Greeks) between the source of the Oxus and the Jaxartes. Komedorum Montes is the Tsung-king mountain of Hiuen Tsang. Sukumara is Komaroi and Jalada is Golakto Phagoi. The Jaladhara corresponds to Sala Teroi or the Zaratoi.

The Janapada of Mrgas is Margine or Margiana, present Meru. Masaka is called Massagetai and Mandaga appears as Makhaitegoi.

The river Sita is the Syr-Darya or the Jaxartes. The Caksuvarddhana is the Oxoz or the Oxus. The Kuman appears to be the Rha or the Volga.

The mountain Meru is called Imaus, Malaya is Alana and Syamagiri is the Kaukasos mountain (including the Bailor Tag and the Mustag mountain which means the Black mountain). It is identical with mountain Syamaka of the Avesta.

The Kusumoda of Visnu corresponds to the country inhabited by the Khorasmai. The Maudaki is the country inhabited by Mardyenoi. The Iksu is no other than Oxus. The Astagiri is the Aska Tangka (Tangka means mountain) Durga-saila is the Elbuzz mountain (both the words Durga and Burg mean a fort). The Markanda is the town Samarkand. The capital of Sogdo or Sargindana was called Maracanda.

In Sakadvipa, the river Hiranvati mentioned in the Mahabharata as forming the boundary of the country of the Surparnas or Garudas is evidently Zarafshan (the distributor of gold). It is the river HatakT Nad! (Rasatola) of the Bhagavata Purana. It rises in the Fan-Tau mountain and falls into the Karakul lake.

## Gomedaka

The continent Gomedaka encircling the ocean of Sura and encircled by the ocean of Iksurasa is twice in extent to that of Salmalidvipa and the ocean of Sura. There are only two extensive divisions, the upper one is called Kumuda and the lower one or the southern one is called Dhatakikhanda. The Skanda Purana records seven Varsas in Gomeda out of which at least two are common with those of Plaksadvipa. The mountains, too, are only two in number, one is Gomeda after which the continent is known as Gomedaka, and the varsa of which is called Gomedaka as well as Sarvasukha. The other-one is called Sumana which is black like collyrium and the varsa of which is called Dhatakikhanda as well as Saumana. The golden and beautiful mountain Kumuda is full of all kinds of herbs and is situated in the half portion in the western direction. Its Varsa is called Kumuda.

## Puskara

The continent of Puskara, named after the golden lotus which the king of this continent obtained from Brahma, is twice in extent to Gomeda.
