It encircles the ocean of Iksurasa and itself is encircled by the ocean of the sweet water. There is a great Nyagrodha tree here. The two sonsof the king of Puskara. had their territories named after them as MahavTtavarsa and the Dhatakikhanda. There are only two mountains, the first on the eastern half of the continent is called Citrasanu which is 27000 yojanas in its circle and 24000 yojanas in its height. The other one called Manasa is situated in the western half of Puskara on the sea-shore and is 5500 yojanas in height. Another subsidiary mountain range of Manasa is MahavTta. The Manasottara or the Uttaramanasadivides the island in the middle thus forming the two divisions of a circular form. The region outside the Manasa is called MahavTtavarsa and the one inside is called the Datakikhanda. The mountain of Puskara is called Puskara in the Padma Purana. It has been identified with a portion of central Asia, commencing from the north of the Oxus including the western Tartary. Perhaps it has derived its name from Bhushkara or Bokhara. It was included in the Scythia of the Greeks. The continent has been identified with the land of Turkestan east and west. What lies to the east of mountain is Scythia Extra Imaum i.e. eastern Turkestan. The main feature of the southern and eastern portion of this vast region is a series of complexes of mountain systems. The great mountain range forming the divisions of the two Turkestans is the Belur Tagh (or Balor) with its continuation, the Thian Shan mountains. — Scythia within Imaus contained the countries since known by the names of Dash-i-kipchak, western Turkistan and the north part of Ferganh. Scythia Extra Imaus extended eastward according to Ptolemy, to the neighbourhood of the Ochardae, south ward to mount Emodus, the great ridge of Tibet, northward to the parellel of 55°. These limits include generally the country known as Eastern Turkistan.

On the north east and east of the sea of Aral, there expands between the rivers Irghiz and Sir Darya, an extensive desert of black shifting sand known as Karakum. Between rivers Sir Darya and Amu Darya is the desert of Kizil-Kum. On the south this steppe borders on the still more barren clayey steppe of Bokhara. The other deserts in the neighbourhood of the Amu Darya are for the most part sterile deserts of sand. The Kirghiz steppe consists of vast plains, not a tree, nor a shrub can be seen. The Amu Daryan basin has the Turkomanian or Kharazmian desert on the left and on the right the desert of Kizil-Kum. — Eastern Turkestan is walled on all sides except the east by the loftiest mountain ranges. On the south is Kuen Lun mountains of Trans Himalayan system. On the west are Pamir mountains of the Belut Tangle. To the north are Thian - Shan and its Dzungarian extensions. To the east the basin opens towards the Gobi desert. A river named Tarim flows from west to east in these regions and loses itself in the salt swamps which are the outparts of the Gobi desert. Except along the banks of Tarim and its tributary streams, there is hardly any agriculture or vegetation. All the rest to the north and south of this stream is mere desert. Southern is the desert of Takhla-Makan and northern is the Tarim desert.— It seems that the area of the Oasis is less than 1-12%. No wonder Puranas give a horrible description of the continent of Puskara. Probably the compiler of the Vamana and Garuda Puranas had some reliable information from travellers about the conditions prevailing in this dvipa. The Puranas state that there is no rainfall, no rivers, no streams, fountains or springs. Except near the mountains and in the valleys of the few streams fed by glaciers there is hardly anything like a supply of water available in these lands.

The Puranas at the same time call it a heaven also. The inhabited portions of Turkestan are blessed with a fertility and luxuriant vegetation. Variety of production is great and the soil is extraordinarily productive. The most large and fertile oasises are those of Kashgar, Yarkand and Khotan' Wilford takes it to be Iceland.

## India : Her Janapadas

## The Name

A major portion of the geographical material available in the Puranas is devoted to the description of the states of Bharata designated as Janapadas or Desas. India, primarily called Himavatvarsa or Himavarsa was later on called Bharata after Bharata. This Bharata is said to be the son of Rsabhadeva. At other places he is the son of Sakuntala and Dusyanta. Manus were also called 'Bharatas' since they supported the subject.

## Her situation

The southern country of Bharata is said to have been situated to the south of Himalayas or Ilavrta and as lying prostrate from east to west in the north of the ocean. The Padma Purana perhaps records an old tradition that the country of Bharata extended only upto Vindhya mountain in the south. The Markandeya Purana and the Brahma Purana are more clear and accurate in placing her to the south of the Himalayas and as surrounded by ocean on three sides i.e. east, west and south.

## Her extent

According to the Puranas Bharatavarsa from Himalayas to Kumari or from north to south, extended upto 1000 Yojanas in a Tiryak form. At other places its extent is given as 9000 Yojanas. The distance given by Megasthenes and Deimaches is over 2000 stadia from the southern sea to the Caucasus. Megasthenes gives the breadth of India at the shortest as 16000 stadia and its length at the narrowest as 22300 stadia. From east to west she measures as 28000 stadia and from north to south as 32000 stadia and thus embraces the whole of the northern tropical zone of the earth. According to the Chinese, its circuit was about 90000 li. About the dimensions of India Strabo writes, As regards the length of India from west to east, we can state it with great confidence as far as Palibothra since it has been measured in Schoinoi as a royal road of 10000 stadia. The extent of the parts beyond can only be conjectured from the ascent of vassals from the sea by the Ganges to Palibothra.

As far as Sanskrit Buddhist texts are concerned, they are silent regarding the size and shape of India. The Dharmasutras and Dharmasastras give the limit of the Aryavarta as extending from the region where the Sarasvati disappears in the west to the Black forest in the east and from the Himalayas in the north to the Pariyatra in the south and thus exclude the region lying beyond Pariyatra mountain. The Padma Purana (Kriya 224) evidently records this tradition.

## Her Shape

India, according to the Puranas, is oblique and long. Her shape is said to resemble that of a bow.Although Nllakantha, the commentator of the Mahabharata, confirms this bowlike description yet he believes Bharata as being triangular in shape.At other places the Mahabharata, besides giving her bowlike shape, describes India as an equilateral triangle divided into four smaller equal triangles.This shape corresponds very well to the general form of the country if we extend the limits of India to Gazni on the north-west and fix the other two points of the triangle at Cape Comorin and Sadiya in Assam.' The most accurate description is given in Nadyadivarnana section of the Markandeya Purana (ch.54) where Bharata is said to have been constituted with a four conformation. She is encircled by ocean on her south, west and east and the Himalayas stretch along her northern side like a string of a bow. The Kurmanivesa section of the Markandeya Purana (Ch.55), the Kurma Vibhaga of the Narada Purana (Part I ch.56) and early astronomical authors give the shape of India as that of a tortoise outspread and facing eastward. Thus their description omits the extreme southern part of India.

Alexander's historians give the shape of India as an unequal quadrilateral in shape with Indus in the west, mountains on the north and sea on the east and south. In the geography of Ptolemy the acute angle formed by the meeting of the two coasts of the Peninsula at the Cape Comorin is changed to a single coast line running almost straight from the mouth of the Indus to the mouth of the Ganges.

Hiuen Tsang gives the shape of the country as a half moon with the broad side to the north and the narrow end to the south. His travels were mainly confined to northern India which may be said to resemble a half moon with the Vindhyas as its base and the Himalayas spreading its two arms on two sides as the diameter.

According to Megasthenes the shape of India is quadrilateral, the eastern and western sides being bounded by the great sea and the north side being divided by the mountain Hemodos from that part of Scythia which is inhabitated by Sakai on the western side of the Indus.

The early Buddhist writers knew it to be broad on the north and in the south having the form of the front portion of a cart and being divided into seven equal parts. This shape of India exactly corresponds to the actual shape of the country which is broad on the north where the Himalayas extend from east to west, and triangular towards the south. The Chinese author Fah-Kai-Lih-To, too, gives a similar description when he says that the country is broad towards the north and narrow towards the south.

## Her Various Divisions

The whole universe, according to the Puranas, was divided into seven continents. The middle one named Jambudvipa was divided originally into seven Varsas namely:- (1) Ilavrtavarsa (2) Ramyakavarsa (3) Hiranmayavarsa (4) Uttarakuru (5) Himavatvarsa (6) Kimpurusavarsa and (7) Harivarsa. Bhadrasvavarsa and Ketumalavarsawere later added to the original seven varsas mentioned above thus bringing the number of varsas to nine. The varsa called Bharata is again divided into nine parts called Indradvipa, Kaseruman, Tamraparna, Gabhastiman, Nagadvipa, Saumya, Gandharva, Varuna and the ninth one called Bharata. These nine continents, being surrounded by ocean are inaccessible to each other. The ninth dvipa enircled by sea, extending over a thousand yojanas from north to south had̮Kiratas in the extreme east, Yavanas in the west, Andhras in the south and Turuskas in the north. Bharata is inhabited by Brahmanas, Ksatriyas, Vaisyas and Sudras in the mainland. The Vayu Purana at one place records that the continent was divided previously only in seven parts. The Puranas describe the nine Dvlpas as seperated by sea and being mutually inaccesible but our present Bharata is not separated by sea within itself, nor its component parts are mutually inaccesible. Evidently the Puranic Bharata did not constitute our India of present geographical area. It denoted rather a much larger area and only one of its islands the ninth one named Kumari, stood for India proper. The famous astronomers Parasara and Varaha Mihira also have described India as having nine divisions. They give the shape of India like that of a tortoise lying outspread. Each of the divisions is called a Varga by Varahamihira, thus corresponding to the nine of the ten points of compass. The Markandeya Purana (Ch. 55) has adopted its nine-fold division from the works of Varahamihira and Parasara. It distributes the various Janapadas accordingly over the different parts of the body of the tortoise.

To these the Vayu Purana adds six minor islands situated beyond the sea. They are Angadvipa, Yamadvipa, Malayadvipa, Kumuda or Kusadvipa, Varahadvipa and Sahkha DvTpa which are inhabited mainly by Miecchas who worship Hindu divinities. The Bhagavata Purana and the Padma Purana name eight such islands called Svarnaprastha, Candrasukia, Avartana, Ramanaka, Mandaharina Panchajanya, Simhala and Lanka.Colonel Wilford has tried to identify five islands out of the first list. According to him Varaha is Europe and Kusa is Asia Minor. The Sarikha, Malaya and Ariga are identified with Africa, Malacca and China respectively.

From the first century of Vikrama era, the Indians started to hold their sway over both the eastern and western oceans. Gradually they colonized Java, Sumatra, Malaya Kamboja, Campa, Syam, Kedah, Borniyo and other islands. These islands were so much influenced by Indian art, language, religion and culture that they came to be regarded as a part and parcel of the main land. Thus in course of time the geographical territory of India expanded. She included not only the India proper but also the islands of Indonesia and thus a greater India came into being. The geographical account of the Puranas seems to describe the geographical limits of India during 5th cent. A.D.

Ptolemy locates Kiratas in the eastern region and Ashoka Inscriptions place Yonas or Yavanas along with Kambojas and Gandharas in the west. Evidently the limits of Puranic India exceeded far beyond the limits of our present India and even undivided India before independence.

It is highly disappointing that the compiler of the Puranas ended his geographical information only by giving the names of the Janapadas, mountains, rivers and sacred places. Nowhere has he given their location or mentioned their boundaries. Such being the state of things we have to depend, as far as possible, on the material supplied by later works for the identification of the places mentioned in the Puranas.

Indradvipa - The Indraprathai of Ptolemy, has been identified With Madhyadesa by Alberuni and with Burma by S.N. Shastri. Abulfazi places it between Lanka and Mahendra hills which agrees with the location of this Dvlpa as described in the Skanda Purana (Kaum. 39113).

Kaserumat - It has been placed to the south-east of the Madhyadesa (Alberuni) between Mahendra and Sukti (Abul Fazi) and Tamraparna between Suktimat and Malaya mountains.

Tamraparna **-** It may be identified with the region watered by the river Tamraparm in the extreme south. As Kautilya's Arthasastra refers to it as Parasamudra, it has been identified with Ceylon called Taprobane by Greeks and Tambapanni in the inscriptions of Ashoka.

Gabhastimat **-** Abul Fazi places it between Rksa and Malaya and Alberuni to the south of Madhyadesa.

Nagadvipa **-** Abul Fazi placed it between Rksa and Pariyatra while Alberuni placed it to the south-west of Madhyadesa. It has been identified with the Jaffna Peninsula of Ceylon, which Tamil tradition represents as the domain of Naga king Tamraparna. Nagadvipa and Sirhhala may refer to distinct parts of Ceylon.

Saumya - Though Saumya has not been identified yet its substitute Kataha has been identified with Kedah in Malaya 'Peninsula. Abul Fazi identified it with the tract between Pariyatra and Sahya. Alberuni placed it to the west of Madhyadesa.

Gandharva **-** Alberuni -places it to the northwest of Madhyadesa. It can easily be identified with the well known ancient region of Gandhara as Ramayana seems to suggest. Its substitute in Garuda Purana i.e. Sirhhala is modern Ceylon.

Varuna - It has been placed between Sahya and Vindhya mountains by Abul Fazl.

The later division of India into five or seven parts in the Puranas and Mahabharata is more accurate. The division of Bharata into five parts is as old as Atharvaveda and Aitareya Brahmana.The same has been followed by Brahmanical and Buddhist authorities as well. The Smrti writers like Baudhayana, Buddhist writers like Hiuen-Tsang, Rajasekhara and the works like the Mahabharata, the Visnu Purana, the Matsya Purana and the Vayu Purana agree with it.

The five traditional divisions in early Indian texts are Uttarapatha, Daksinapatha, Pracya, Aparanta and Madhyadesa. According to KavyamTmamsa the countries to the north of Prthudaka (mod. Pehoa about 14 miles west of Thaneshwar) were included in Uttarapatha. To the south of Mahismat lay Daksinapatha. The Pracya countries lay to the east of Benaras and the Aparanta countries to the west of Devasabha. The Madhyadesa or AntarvedT was the tract lying between the confluence of the Yamuna and the Ganga. The five divisions of the Bhuvanakosa section of the Puranas are identical with those of Kavyamimamsa.

Panini mentions Pracya Bharatadesa and Patanjali refers to Aryavarta. According to Brahmanical Aryans, Buddhist writers and Dharmasutras Aryavarta extended from the region where the river Sarasvati disappears in the west to the Black forest in the east (the tract somewhere near Prayaga) and from the Himalayas in the north to the Pariyatra in the south. The Dharmasutras of Manu call the Aryavarta as Madhyadesa which extended from the Himalayas in the north to the Vindhyas in the south and from Vinasana in the west to Prayaga in the east. The Majjhimadesa of the Buddhists extended in the east to the town of Kajarigala, in the south-east to the river Satakarnika, in the west to the Thuna Distt. (Sthanesvara) and in the north to the Usiradhaja (mountain Usira) to the north of Kankhala and Haridvara. The Divyavadana extends the eastern boundary of the Majjhimadesa to the east and includes Pundrawardhana also, where as in the copperplate of Bhojvarman and Barrackpore copperplate of Vijayasena it is said to have been situated between the Himalaya and Pariyatra mountain, the southern boundary line of Madhyadesa.

According to the Brahmanical tradition recorded in Kavyamimamsa the Uttarapatha lay to the western side of Prthudaka and included the whole of Indus valley. It did not include the greater portion of the land of Rgvedic Aryans. Sanskrit Buddhist texts are silent about its boundaries but probably the Uttarapatha in Pali literature signified the whole of northern India from Ariga in the east to Gandhara in the north-west and from Himalaya in the north to the Vindhyas in the south. Banabhatta's Uttarapatha included the western part of Uttarapradesh, Punjab, and North West Frontier Province of pre-partitiop India. The Uttarapatha in the Hathigumpha Ins. of Kharavela signifies the region including Mathura in its south-eastern extension upto Magadha.

According to Dharmasutras, Daksinapatha lay to the south of MahismatT (Kavyamimamsa) aJid Pariyatra. The Sanskrit Buddhist texts make it extend southward beyond the Sarasvati river and the Pariyatra mountain. Thus the entire tract lying to the south of the Ganges and to the north of the Godavari was included in Daksinapatha.

Mahavagga of Vinayapitaka and Divyavadana regard the region to the south of the town of Satakarnika as Daksinapatha.

The Pracya countries lay to the east of Madhyadesa. The western boundary of the Madhyadesa changed from time to time in accordance with the constantly changing boundaries of the Madhyadesa. The Dharmasutras placed them to the east of Prayaga and Kavya - Mimamsa to the east of Benaras. They are mentioned to be to the east of Anga in Katyayana's commentary. The western boundary of the Purvadesa shrank still more and extended to Kajarigala (Vinaya Mahavagga) or to Pundravardhana (Divyavadana).

The Aparanta countries referred to in the Ashoka Rock Edict No. 5, Luder's list No: 965, Nasik record of Gautami Balasri and Junagarh Rock Inscription lay to the west of Devasabha (Kavyamimamsa). In the Pali literature they are placed to the west of upper Irawady. The Mahabharata mentions it and the Markandeya Purana places it to the north of Sindhu-SauvTra. Dr Bhandarkar has identified it with the northern Konkana with its capital at Surparaka (modern Sopara). Bhagavan Lal Indraji regarded it to be the western sea board of India and according to Dr. Bhandarkar it was Ariake.

Sanskrit Buddhist texts refer to only three divisions of India namely Uttarapatha, Daksinapatha and Madhyadesa.

Panini and Patanjali refer to Uttarapatha.Dandin refers to the people as Daksinatya and Adaksinatya. The five fold traditional division is not definitely stated anywhere in Sanskrit or Pali Buddhist texts. Though a detailed description of Majjhimadesa is found in Vinayapitaka yet an accurate description of the four other divisions is not found except in Hiuen-Tsang's itineraries. They are only suggested by the description of the boundaries of Madhyadesa. The Uttarapatha is referred to in name only but Madhyadesa is elaborately mentioned. We find a detailed description of its towns, cities, parks, gardens, lakes and rivers etc.. The towns in south or north or north-west seem to have been known only by names handed down to them by traditions. The main reason behind this was that Madhyadesa had always been an important centre of Aryans as well as Buddhists. But gradually with the spread of Buddhism their geographical knowledge went ahead. By the time of Ashoka, it embraced not only Gandhara and Kamboja on one side and Pundra and Kaliriga to the other but also the countries later on occupied by the Cheras, Cholas and Pandyas.

The Chinese concept of five-fold division of India was directly taken from the Puranas with slight modification. The official records of the Thang dynasty of Chinese rulers (7th cen. A.D.) described these five divisions. According to Chinese the entire land was divided into 70 countries or so.

## (A) Central States

According to the lists given in the Matsya, Visnu and Vayu Puranas, the central countries were Kuru, Pancala, Salva, Jangala, Surasena, Bhadrakara Vahya Sahapataccara, Matsya, Kirata, Kulya Kuntala, Kasi, Kosala, Avanta, Muka, Andhaka, Bodha, Satpathesvara, Vatsa, Sisna, Tailanga, Magadha, Vrka, Kanyakubja and Bhargava. Before we proceed to examine them one by one, we must note one thing that the Puranas, except enlisting the names of the countries, give no other definite clues either about their location or about their boundaries. No doubt they sometime give very vague information as Gangafire Suprasiddha Kasi, Gangayamuna sambhedam Prayagam, and Uttare YamunatTre Pratistanametc. but such information does not help much. In identifying them and defining their boundaries we have to depend much on works other than Puranas such as epics, historical inscriptions, later literary works, ancient histories etc. etc.. We have to supplement the meagre information with other evidences such as Jain and Buddhist literature and foreign geographers' information as well.

## Kurus

The Kuru people were the most ancient, prominent and powerful of the'In do-Aryan Ksatriya tribes. The Bharata princes are said to have performed sacrifices on the banks of Sarasvati, Drsadvati and Apaya.

A king named Kurusravana and his ancestors are mentioned in the Rgveda. This Kurusravaria is called a descendent of Trasadasyu who is well known in the Rgveda as a king of Purus who lived on the Saras vati. Probably the Tritsu-Bharatas, the enemies of Purus later merged with them to form the Kuru people as the Bharatas, like the Purus, are seen to have occupied the territory which Kurus later on occupied. It was the Paurava dynasty which afterwards came to be known as Kuru clan. That the name Kuru was already given in early Vedic age to a prominent Indo-Aryan Ksatriya tribe is also proved by the mention of the name of a king Pakasthaman Kaurayana and the term Kauravya. The Atharvaveda refers to Panksita as a Kuru king.

During the Brahmana period they are seen as connected with Pancalas along with whom they were placed in Madhyadesa. At the time of the composition of the Brahmanas and earlier Upanisads the kingdoms of Kuru-Pancalas were the main political units in the Delhi region. In the religious field also Kuru-Pancala Brahmanas excelled.

Both the epics mention them. The Ramayana simply mentions them but the Mahabharata grew up with the Kuru people and their country as its back-ground.

Kuruksetra, according to Mahabharata was the region lying between Saras vati and Drsadvati. The river Oghavati in this region is the Sarasvati itself.Iksumati is another river of this region. The Mahabharata giving its boundaries locates the Kuruksetra between Tarantuka, Arantuka, Ramahrada and Macakruka. Tarantuka, Arantuka and Macakruka were the Yaksas guarding the Kuru country. It was here that the great battle of Mahabharata was fought and it was here that Krsna gave his famous discourse of Gita to Arjuna. The Puranas also state that the rivers Drsadvati and Sarasvati flew through Kuruksetra. It was here in Kurujangala that Visnu took his Vamana incarnation. The Narada Purana enumerates six more rivers in Kuru region. They are Vaitaram, Ganga, Mandakim, Madhusrava, Kausiki and Hairanvati besides Sarasvati and Drsadvati. But, it adds that all except Sarasvati appear in rainy season only (varsakalavaha). The Vamana Purana adds Apaga and Amiu to this list. The Puranas make mention of seven forests in Kuruksetra. They are Kamyakavana, Aditivana, Karuvana, PhalakTvana, Suryavana Madhuvana and Sitavana.

They are located in cenral India. Here, too, they are associated with the Pancalas and thus their old contiguity is affirmed by the Mahabharata and the Puranas as well. They are related to Srnjayas and Matsyas also. The Puranas and the Mahabharata describe two Kurus, one Uttarakurus and the other simply Kurus. Even in the Brahmana period Uttarakurus were distinguished from Kurus of Punjab. The Aitareya Brahmana regards the country of Uttarakuru as a Devaksetra. It was the country of northern Kuru people. The Puranas refer to it frequently as a tirtha. It was here that the king Pururavas enjoyed the company of Urvasi. In the famous Samjna-Chaya-katha, Samjna, unable to bear the brilliance of the husband Surya, resorted to the Uttarakuru country to perform penances. The Ramayana places the Uttarakuru country (located by some scholars in Central Asia) beyond the river Sailoda which has been identified with river Khotan. The Mahabharata associates them with Kurus and they are said to have been defeated by Arjuna. It seems that they were then a historical people. In Buddhist literature, Uttarakuru is more a mythical and fabulous region while Kurus were one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas. But even in Buddhist literature there are some passages which have a faint memory of a country that once had a historical existence e.g. reference to Kurudvipa in DTpavamsa (p. 16) and the statement in the Sasanavamsa (p. 12) that the place of the inhabitants of Uttaradvipa is called the kingdom of Kurus. The existence of the Uttarkurus in central Asia beyond the Himalayas is noted by Greek writers of the 5th century A.D.. Ptolemy mentions a town Uttorokoprha together with a mountain and a river of the same name in Serigine (Chinese Turkistan) near the mount Emodos (-Himalaya). Uttarakuru thus signified Chinese Turkistan. Zimmar placed northern Kurus in Kashmir especially as Kuruksetra is the region where tribes advancing from Kashmir might naturally be found.

Southern Kurus, who were favoured by Buddha's discourses are referred to very frequently in Buddhist literature.

Kurus and other allied people formed the sacred land of Brahmarsis ranking immediately after Brahmavarta. The land of Kurus was regarded very holy and was called Dharmaksetra which extended from Hastinapura region with Pancalas lying in the east.

The ancient capital of Kurus was Hastinapura which was situated on the river Ganga. It was founded by the king Hasti of Paurava dynasty.According to Puranas the city of Hastinapura was destroyed by Balarama who threw it on the bank of the Ganga where it can still be seen on the southern side, thus suggesting its location on the river Ganga. Hastinapura remained a prosperous city till the time of Nicaksu who had to shift his capital to Kausambi after Hastinapura was washed away by Ganga. Kausambi, perhaps the Kosamba of Ptolemy and Kian Shangmi of Hiuen Tsang, which is refered to in the Ramayana, Mahavamsa and Meghaduta and which was situated in the north-west of Yamuna became the capital of Pandavas after Hastinapura was swept by Ganga. The excavations carried out at the site have supported this evidence of Puranas. The site was abandoned due to the flood after 800 B.C. The ruined site is located on the bank of Ganga in the Mawana Tehsil of Meerut district in Uttara Pradesh. Indraprastha, the Inderbara of Ptolemy (modern Indrapat near Delhi) was the second capital of Kurus. Actually speaking, HastTnapura was the capital of Kauravas while Indrasprastha was the capital of Pandavas. It was situated on the bank of Yamuna in Khandava forest. From east to west it was one yojana in extent and to the south (right) side of KalindT (Yamuna) its boundary extended for four yojanas. Krsna had to cross Anarta, SauvTra, Maru, Drsadvati, Pancala and Matsya territories to reach Sakraprastha.

The ancient Kuru country may have comprised the Kuruksetra or Thaneshvara region. It was situated between the Sarasvati on the north and Drsadvati on the south and included Sonipat, Amin, Karnal and Panipat. Pargiter holds that the Kuru people occupied the country from the Sibis and sub-Himalayan tribes on the north to Matsya, Surasena and south Pancala on the south and between north Pancala on the east and Rajputana Desert on the west.

Their territory seems to have been divided into three parts, Kurujangala, Kuruksetra and Kuru country. Kurujangala or simply Jangala was the eastern part of their territory. It was the waste land of Kurus that stretched from the Kamakhya fores on the bank of the Sarasvah to Khandava forest near Yamuna and seems to have comprised the tract between the Ganges and north Pancala. It was greatly sanctified. Dey takes Kurujangala to be a forest country situated in Sirhind distt. that is on the north-west of Hastinapura. Dr. V.S. Aggarwal included Rohtak, Hansi and Hissar in Kurujangala.

The other part of Kuruksetra - the cultivated land of the Kurus - which was formerly called Syamanta Pancaka was later on called Kuruksetra after the king Kuru. It comprised the whole tract on the west of the Yamuna and included the sacred region between the Sarasvati and the Drsadvati.

The middle region between the Ganga and Yamuna was simply called Kuru country. The Brahma Purana mentions Kuruksetra in the sense of Kuru Janapada and the Mahabharata differentiates between Kuruksetra and Kurujangala. According to Manu, the land between Sarasvati and Drsadvati is called Brahmavarta and the countries of Kuruksetra, Matsya, Pancala and Surasena constitute Brahmarsidesa that lies after Brahma varta. The territories occupied by Kurus extended to the east far beyond the limits of Kuruksetra. The Kurus must have occupied the northern portion of the Doab or the region between the Yamuna and the Ganga having as their neighbours on the east North Panchalas and South Panchalas who held the rest of the Doab as far as Vatsabhumi, the corner where the two rivers meet at Prayaga.

Dr. Raychaudhuri places Kurus in Bahllka (Bactria) and not within any territory in India proper. A king named Vahlika Pratipeya is called Kauravya in Satapatha Brahmana. The Mahabharata also refers to this Vahlika Prafipeya who was the son of Kuru king Prafipa and Sunanda, a Sibi princess. According to Ramayana also the princes of Aila dynasty (descendents of Ila- son of Prajapati Kardama) as well as the dynasty of Kurus have been called Karddameya. Dr. Raychaudhuri opines that the Karddamas derived this name from the river Kardama in Persia. He also refers to the Kamasutra of Katyayana wherein a peculiar custom prevalent among the Vahlikas (i.e. several young men marrying a single woman) is recorded. In Mahabharata also the five Pandavas married a single woman DraupadT. He concludes that it is just possible that they originally belonged to Baikh

(Bactria) with which the Uttarakuru country was identical.

It seems that sometimes before the 4th century B.C. the monarchical constitution of Kurus was changed into a republican form of government as Kautilya calls them Raja-Sabda-UpaJivinah. They played some part in Indian politics as late as 9th century A.D. for when Dharmapala placed Cakrayudha on the throne of Kanauja, he took the consent of neighbouring kings among whom Kurus are especially mentioned.

## Pancalas

Pancalas, the Krivis of Rgveda, lived in the region near Sindhu and AsiknT in Punjab during Vedic age. In later Samhitas and Brahmana literature they are frequently referred to as associated with Kurus. A branch of the Pancalas probably lived in the east as we find references to Pracya Pancalas in Brahmana literature. They can be identified with northern Pancala of the later literature which was situated to the east of Ganga. They are associated with Purus and are said to have occupied the midland. The five Vedic tribes Krivi, Turvasu, Kesin, Srnjaya and Somaka later on came to be called as Pancalas.

The Pancalas, who are referred to by Panini Patanjali, Kautilya, Valmiki, and the authors of the Puranas and Brhatsarhhita were named after the five sons of king Dharmasva, who were called Pancala as they were capable of protecting their five countries. Many stories are tpid about them in Mahabharata also. They along with Matsyas and Surasenas formed the land of Brahmarsis ranking immediately after Brahmavarta. During the epic period they are seen as divided into two clans namely Uttara Pancalas and Daksina Pancalas. Evidently they had spread and added to their territory by conquests since the Vedic period. The Jatakas also refer to the foundation of Uttara Pancalas. The Adiparva of the Mahabharata gives a different story of the division of Pancala. Prince Drupada of Pancala and Drona, the preceptor of Kuru princes, were friends in their childhood but a rivalry between them grew up in their young age. Drona offered him the revival of their old friendship and half of his kingdom. Drupada accepted the offer. Thus the country extending from the river BhagirathT (Ganga) to the river Carmanvati (modern Chambal) with its capital at Kampilya was given to Drupada whose territory came to be known as Southern Pancala. The northern half with its capital at Ahicchatra, known as Uttara-Pancala was retained by Drona. The Mahabharata associates them with the Somakas Kekayas, Cedis, Vrsnis, Matsyas,Kurus, Karusas and Surasenas.

According to Sadpancasaddesavibhaga the country of Pancalas lay at about thirty yojanas from Indraprastha with Kuruksetra both in the west and the north.

The chief cities of Pancalas were Kampila, Ahicchatra and Kanyakubja. Kampila, which is the same as Kampilya of the later literature and which is as ancient as Yajurveda sarhhita was the capital of Pancalas in Madhya-Desa. It finds mention in the Ramayana also. Kampilya identified with Kampil was situated on the old banks of the Ganges at a distance of 28 miles north-east of Fatehgarh in Farokhabad Distt. (Uttar Pradesh).

The country of Pancalas has been frequently mentioned in the Buddhist literature. According to Divyavadana their capital was Hastinapura while according to Jatakas it was Kampillanagar. The Jain works, too, refer to Pancalas.

Pancalas seem to have been a composite tribe as the name which is clearly derived from Panca shows. The Padma Purana refers to them in plural.According to Satapatha Brahmana the old name of Pancalas was Krivi and we may at least believe that the Krivis, who with Kurus appear to have constituted the two Vaikarana tribes of the Rgveda were a part of the people and the disappearance from history at this period of Anus and Druhyus may indicate that they also were mixed in the new consideration.

In the post-Ashokan period Pancala was invaded by the Greeks. It passed through troublous time after the death of Harshavardhana but from about 840-910 A.D. under Bhoja and his son, it became the principal power in northern India extending from Bihar to Sindh. In the 12th century A.D. it again became important under the Goharwar dynasty.

Ahicchatra, the Adisadra of Ptolmy is known as Ahiksetra and Chatravati (MBH) also. The fort is also called Adikot but the more common name is Ahicchatra. The Jain Vividhatirthakalpa gives its earlier name as Samkhyavati. Its history goes back to 1430 B.C. The name of the city apeared to have extended to the whole of the country of Uttara Pancala in Hiuen Tsang's time who gives the extent of the country of 0-hi-chi-ta-lo as 3000 li with its capital of 17 or 18 li. It is the modern Ram Nagar in the Bareilly Distt. of Uttar Pradesh but St. Martin locates it to the south of the Vindhya mountain. Jwala Sahay Mishra identifies it with the modern village Arura, a little north of village of Bhadaur in Patiala.

Kanyakubja is our modern Kanauj on the river Ganga. Hiuen Tsang gives the extent of the country of Kie-Po-Kio-Sha as 4000 li with its capital of 20 li in length and 4 or 5 li in breadth. The climate was soft and agreeable.

The Janapada of Pancalas, the Passala of Ptolemy continued to be one of the great and powerful countries of northern India down to the time of Buddha as we see that it is called one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas.

It comprised Bareilly, Budaun, Farrukhabad and the adjoining distt. of Rohilkhand and the central Doab in U.P. It seems to have been bounded on the east by the river Gomati and on the south by Chambal and extended from Himalayas upto Chambal. The Northern Pancala comprised those districts of Uttar Pradesh, which lay east of the Ganges and north west of Oudh and had its capital at Ahichatra, a ruined site still bearing the same name near Ramnagar in Bareilly Distt.. Even now the place is known to Jains as Ahichatra. The Southern Pancala included the country between the Yamuna and the Ganga on the east and south-east of the Kurus and Surasenas, and had its capital at Kampilya the ruins of which are found at the village of Kampil in the Farrukhabad Distt.Salvas

Earlier references to them are found in the Rgveda where they seem to have occupied the territory near Yamuna. In the Gopatha Br. where they are connected with the Matsyas, King Salva's kingdom was also known as Martikavata or Mrttikavati. Their capital seems to have been Salvapura also called Saubhaganagara.

They are somewhere classed among the western nations and somewhere among the central nations. Both the epics and Brhatsamhita refer to them. There are several references to them in the

Mahabharata as Danavas and Daityas probably because of their enmity to Visnu who is called *Salvari. The Salvas, who sided with Kurus lived near Kuruksetra not far from Kurus and Trigarttas.It seems that there were two branches of Salva because they are said to have been on the side of Pandavas also. The story of Krsna's conquest of them suggests their nearness to Yadavas also. The father of Satyavan, the husband of SavitrT, famous in the Mahabharata legends ruled over the Salva country.*

Panini mentions their territory as including Audumvara, Tilakhala, Machakara, Yugandhara, Bhulinga, and Sara-Danda. Reference to a town named Vaidhumagni in the country of Salvas are also found in his work. It is known to Patanjali also. In the Puranas they are said to have been located near Yamuna. The Visnudharmottara says that Salvas along with Matsyas lived near Yamuna in the northern part of Kuruksetra. The Bhagavata Puraria, too, places them on Yamuna and associates them with the city of Mathura. The Mahabharata suggests their migration to the south.

Pargiter places them to the western side of the Aravali range. It has been identified with the native state of Alwar. It included portions of territories of Jodhpur, Jaipur and Alwar and its capital lay at Sodrapur.

According to Dr. V.S. Aggarwal they were ancient people who migrated from the west through Baluchistan and Sindh ... and advancing towards north of Sauvira and along the Sarasvati finally settled in north Rajputana.

## Surasena

The Surasenas, though not mentioned in Vedic literature are spoken of in high terms by Manu who praised their martial qualities. They belonged to Brahmarsidesa and enjoyed a high position in society, which was second only to Brahma varta.They were descendents of Yadu who is referred to in the Rgveda many times. Possibly they were included in Yadu race then, but attained significance only in later period. Mathura, the capital of Surasenas is especially called the capital of Yadavas and the kings of Mathura also were Yadavas.

They are mentioned in the Astadhyayi, the Mahabhasya, the Ramayana and the Brhat-samhita. The great epic Mahabharata places Surasena, an Indian Janapada, to the south of Pancalan the border of Kuru kingdom. The Surasena people fled to the southern direction out of fear of Jarasamdha. It was first to be conquered by Arjuna on his victory march to the south. They were great warriors and it appears that some of them sided with Kauravas and some with Pandavas. Pandavas, on their journey from Dvaitavana to the capital of Matsya (Viratanagara) passed through the territory of Surasenas. It appears that they occupied the region between the Kuru country and the Matsya Janapada. Their capital city was called Surasenapura which is identical with Mathura.

The semi-legendary accounts of the Puranas give details regarding Mathura, which was founded by Satrughna who killed the demon Lavana. It was the home of Vrsnis and Andhakas, who out of fear left Mathura and established their capital at Dvaravati. It was attacked by demons and beseiged by Jarasandha - the ruler of Magadha - also. The Surasenas who occupied a place in central India,lived immediately to the south of Kuru country and east of Matsyas on Yamuna with their capital at Mathura. They had an increased fame in the epics and the Puranas on account of their association with Krsna and Yadava tribe. The story of Krsna and Kamsa is known even to Patanjali and the authors of Jataka stories. The whole of the first chapter in the Bhagavata-mahatmya section of the Vaisnava Khanda of the Skanda Purana describes the glory of Mathura. They had matrimonial alliances with the people of Avanti. The Andhakas and the Vrsnis of Mathura are referred to in Astadhyayi. But Brhatsamhita and the Markandeya Purana regard Surasenas and Mathura as two different people.

It was one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas of Buddhist India. The capital of Surasenas is called Madhura in Lalitavistara and Milindapanha. Mathura appears to be one of the most famous cities at that time. The town was surrounded by a number of high mounds - the ruins of the two Buddhist monastaries. Buddhism was the religion prevalent in Mathura for several centuries. It was several times visited by Buddha and stood at a distance of four Yojanas from Sankassa. Jain cult was also practised at Mathura and the Jains were firmly established in Mathura from the middle of the 2nd century B.C.. Many inscriptions prove that they were a flourishing community at the time of Kanishka, Huvishka and Vasudeva. It was the birthplace of Bhagavata religion also. During the time of Megasthenes it was known as the centre of Krsna-worship, but in Saka-Kushana period, it ceased to be a stronghold of Bhagvatism.

Greek writers refer to Sourasenoi, Methora and Kleisohora (Krsnapura i.e. Gokula). Arrian and Ptolemy, too, mention the city of Mathura. Fa-Hien called it Ma-Ta-Oula or the Peacock city.Hiuen-Tsang named it Mo-(Mei)-tu-lo which was 5000 li in circuit with a capital of 20 li and records that the soil was rich and climate warm. According to Pliny, the river Jomanes flew between the town of Methora and Carisobara.

Near Mathura, the capital, stood Madhu-paghna to which Kalidasa refers in his work.

According to the Sadapancasaddesavibhaga the Surasena country lay from Magadha in the south -east to the west of the Vindhya. The Surasena country was named after Surasena, the father of Vasudeva and Kunti.

Mathura, also known as Madhupuri is our present Maholi, 5 miles to the south-west of the present town of Mathura. Another important city of the Surasenas was Kanyakubja. In the second century B.C. Mathura was governed by the native princes whose names are known from the coins. From them it passed into the hands of Saka -Satrapas in and about first century A.D. as is known from epigraphic and coins' evidence. In second century A.D. it came under the sway of Huvishka, the Kushana king. Rapson has placed them in the Distt. of Mathura and some territory still further south.

## Sahapataccara

The Sahapataccaras were also central people. The Vayu Purana reads Sahyasya Cottarardhe Tu in place of Sahyasya Chottare yas tu. The former reading is better for the people who are said to have been located just to the north of the Sahya cannot be said to be in the Madhyadesa. The Mahabharata locates them on the border of Kuru land and they had sided with Pandavas during the war. They fled away to the south due to the fear of Jarasandha.Nothing more can be said about them except that they perhaps lived near Allahabad and Banda Distt.. Pargiter places them to the south-west of Gwalior. It has been identified with modern Pataudi in south-east Punjab.

## Bhargavas

The Bhargavas, the same as Bhargas are located along the nothern half of the Sahya mountains and the region in which Godavari flows. This region, the country west of it on the other side of these mountains and the tract north-wards to the Narmada are connected in many stories with Bhrgu, his son Cyavana, and his descendents. There might have been several branches of Bhargavas because in the Markandeya Purana, they are said to be one of the eastern people. The Buddhist works locate Bhaggas on Simsumaragiri in the Majjhimadesa. We may locate them in the western Assam the country of the Bhars or Bhors.

## Matsya.

The Matsya people belonged to the Vedic time.The Gopatha Br. connects them with the Salvas, Kurus, and Pancalas etc. and the KausitakI Up.with the UsTnaras, Kurus and Pancalas etc.. The Satapatha Br. refers to a Matsya king who acquired fame by performing the horse sacrifice. In later times they are associated with Cedis and Surasenas. The two people are constantly associated and might have been united under one ruler. During the Mahabharata age we find them associated with the Cedis, Karusas, Pancalas, Kekayas, Srnjayas,and Salvas.

The Ramayana places them both in the southand in the north. In the Virata Parva of Mahabharata they are located in the neighbourhood of Kurus. The evidence of the Visnudharmottara Purana also suggests that they occupied the region between Yamuna and Sarasvati in the north of Kuruksetra. During the Mahabharata age, they appear to have extended themselves both in the east and in the south. In the Sabha Parva of the Mahabharata, they are said to have fled to the south. Though they lost their importance during the age of the Ramayana they attained a very important position in the Mahabharata war. It was here in the kingdom of Virata that Pandavas lived in disguise for a year. One branch of Matsyas helped Kauravas during the war while other sided with Pandavas. There was an extensive forest named Dvaitavana outside the Matsya country.

Manu Samhita ranks the Matsya country in the Brahmarsidesa which lay immediately after Brahmavarta. It was one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas. In later times it was known as Virata or Vairata for we see Hiuen-Tsang calling it Vairata which was 3000 li in circuit with its capital of 14 li or 15 li in circuit. It was famous for sheep and goat and it had few fruits or flowers.

Though its precise boundaries cannot be determined, yet it can be said to have included the modern territory of Jaipur, the whole of Alwar and a portion of Bharatpur. It extended from Jhunjhun to Ajmer, 120 miles; on the south from Ajmer to the junction of the Banas and Chambal, 150 miles, on the east from the junction to Kot Kasim, 150 miles or altogether 490 miles. It formed a part of Brahmarsidesa which included the eastern half of the former Patiala state, Delhi division, Alwar state and adjoining territory in Rajputana, the region lying between the Ganga and the Yamuna, and the Mathura division.

The capital of the country is generally called Vairatanagara in the Virataparva and elsewhere in the Mahabharata but sometimes called Matsya-nagara or Matsyasyanagara. It was this Virata-nagara which afterwards became known as Vairata. The earliest known reference to Viratanagara is found in the records of Hiuen Tsang according to whom it was 14 li in circuit and it is the correct size which corresponds to the size of the ancient mound on which the present town is situated. After that we hear about it during the rule of Mahmud of Gazani only who invaded the country in 1009 A.D.. Though its king submitted to Mahmud, yet his kingdom was again attacked in 1014 A.D. and Vairata was taken and plundered by Amir Ali.

The modern town is situated on a mound of ruins about one mile in length and half a mile in breadth in the midst of a circular valley surrounded by low bare redhills. It is at a distance of 105 miles to the south-west of Delhi and 41 miles to the north of Jaipur. The old and deserted city of Vairata was reinhabited probably during the reign of Akbar. It is certain that it was a populated town in Akbar's time for Abul Fazi mentions it.

The kingdom perhaps came under the neighbouring kingdom of Cedis and finally annexed by Magadhan empire.

## Vatsa.

Vatsas or Vasas were one of the Vedic people for we find mention of a certain Vasa Asya in the Rgveda and the Sankhayana Srautasutra. The Aitareya Brahmana places them in the Dhruvamadhyamadik or Madhyadesa of Manu along with Kuru, Pancala and Usinaras. The Gopatha Br. places Vasas along with Usinaras in north. They were also associated with Matsyas and Surasenas who were their close neighbours.

The Ganga formed the boundary between Kosala and Vatsa. Its king Vatsa was the grandson of the king Divodasa of Benaras. The Mahabharata places them to the east of Indraprastha. The Puranas, the Brhatsamhita and the Yogavasistha locates them in central India. When Hastinapura was washed away by the Ganges, king Nicaksu, the fifth in descent from Kuru prince Abhimanyu, transferred his capital to Kausambl. According to Brhatsamhita, too, Vatsas were situated in the middle region. It probably lay to the north-east of Avanti along the bank of the Yamuna, southwards from Kosala and to the west of Allahabad.

The Janavasabha Suttanta associates Vamsas with Cedis than with Usinaras. King Udena of Vamsas was contemporary of Buddha. Many stories are found about him both in Pali Buddhist and Brahmanical Sanskrit literature. As his capital was at Kosambi or Kausambl, we may safely conclude that Vamsas are no other than Vatsas. Vatsa dynasty was rich, thriving, kind and generous one. Their country which is one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas has been referred to many times in the Buddhist literature. Pali tradition says that fourteen pre-Iksvaku kings of the solar dynasty ruled the Vatsa kingdom with their capital at Kausambi. The Jains call them as Vacchas, who had their capital at Kosambi. The Arthasastra of Kautilya, too, refers to them.

Hiuen Tsang mentions it under the name of Kiau-Shang-Mi which extended up to 6000 li with its capital of 300 li. The land was productive and the climate was very hot.

Immediately before the rise of Buddhism, there were four powerful kingdoms in northern India, each of which expanded by the annexation of the adjacent countries. Thus Magadha took Anga Kosala took Kasi, Vatsa overpowered Bhargas and Avanti conquered Surasenas. The Vatsa country served as a buffer state between Magadha and Avanti on one hand and Kosala and Avanti on the other. Avanti, Kosala and Vatsa remained independent for one century even after the death of Buddha, but were conquered by Nandas, and upto the time of accession of Ashok on the Magadhan throne, the three were included in his kingdom. As before, so also during the rule of Ashok Kausambi stood on the high road connecting Vidisa and Ujjayini with Benaras and Pataliputra. Later on Vatsa country was included in the Sunga empire. This history of Vatsa as a political unit ended with the rule of Yashpal of Kanauj.

The capital of Vatsa country was Kausambi which is said to have been founded by Kusamba, son of Kusa. From the use of the term Kausambeya in the Brahmana literature we may conclude that the name Kausambi was known in the Brahmana age. According to the Brahmanas, Kausambi stood either on the Ganges or close to it, and the discovery of the name Kosambimandala in an Inscription over the gateway of the Khara fort seems to confirm this, but its being in the south-west direction of Prayaga as recorded by Hiuen-Tsang points to the line of Yamuna. Fa-hien arrived at Kausambi from the Deer Park to the north of Benaras and had to walk thirteen yojanas north-west from the Deer Park in order to reach Kausambi.

Kausambi, the Kosamba of Ptolemy and Kian-Shang-Mi of Hiuen Tsang was named after Kusamba, the third son of the Cedi king Uparicaravasu. In the Ramayana this Kusamba is said to be the son of Kusa. According to another version it was called Kausambi as it was the dwelling place of the sage Kusamba. Others say that the town abounded in shady Kosamba trees and when it was founded, many Kosamba trees were uprooted from the site. It was called as Vatsapattana also.

Matsya Purana says that when Hastinapura was swept by the Ganges, Kuru king Nicaksu abondoned Hastinapura and shifted his capital to 1 Kausambi. Ptolemy records the same incident and places Kaushambi on the Yamuna but Pargiter regards this flood theory as inadequate and thinks that it meant only that he was forced to leave the previous capital. The Kathasaritsagara places it at the centre of the Vatsa country of which it was the capital. According to the Vividhatlrthakalpa the forests of Kausambi were reached along the course of Kalindi. The Buddhist legend of Bakkula unmistakably proves its situation on the bank of the Yamuna.

The Pali Buddhist literature abounds in the references to Kausambi as a well known city in northern India and an important trade centre. Buddha stayed here several times. Though Jains knew Kausambi as a sacred place yet the influence of Jainism was not extensive there.

Kausambi seems to have been on the southern bank of the Yamuna, at a point about 400 miles by road from UjjayinT and about 230 miles upstream from Benaras. According to Smith, the site of Kausambi is to be looked for near the Satna Railway Station in the valley of the Tons river. Cunningham has identified it with Kasam, about 30 miles south west of Allahabad on the Yamuna.

The point in favour of Cunningham's identification are:-

(1) Epigraphic evidence - In a stone pillar inscription at Kosam dated Chaitrabadi Pancami in 1621 Samvat, the locality is distinctly referred to as Kausambipuri. This proves that Kosam was known as Kausambi even in sixth century A.D. .

(2) The Jain Dharmasala in the village of Pabhosa, which lies only at a distance of 2.5 miles north-west of the remains at Kosam, contains a dedicatory inscription (dated Samvat 1881) in which the hill of Pabhosa is placed just outside or in the suburb of city of Kausambi.

(3) The name Kosam itself is a shortened form of the Pali or the Prakrit name Kosambi.

The question of the site of Kosambi has been much disputed mainly because it is difficult to reconcile the Cunningham's identification with the records of Chinese pilgrims. We may add here that such records may either have been incorrect in themselves or may have been misinterpreted later on. Hiuen Tsang might have gone to Kausambi by a round about way from Prayaga. The description and the direction of Kausambi from Saranatha given by Fa-hien is fairly correct. His location of Kausambi on the north-west of Benaras would be due to his having walked along a road following a north-west direction for some distance.

## Kosala

Kosala as the name of a people is not- found in the earliest Vedic literature, but in later works just as Satapatha Br. and Kalpasutra, it is referred to as a country. Kosalas were connected with Kasi and Videhas. It appears that the Kosala-Videha came later than the Kuru-Pancalas under the influence of Brahmanism. They are known to Panini and the author of Brhatsamhita.

The country of Kosala emerges into importance during the epic age. The scene of action in the Ramayana is Kosala, whose princes carried Aryan civilizaition to the extreme south, even upto Lanka. The whole Ramayana story centres round the country of Kosala, its king Dasaratha and his sons. During the Ramayana period, Kosala had friendly relations with the eastern kingdoms of Videha, Anga, Magadha, the Punjab kingdoms of Kekaya, Sindhu and SauvTra, western kingdom of Surastra and the southern kings. No mention is made of any of the kings of middle region or northern India except Kasi. It was under Dilipa II and his immediate successors that the country acquired the name Kosala. Its extent can be known from the story of Rama's exile. Its capital Ayodhya was situated on the river Sarayu. It was twelve Yojanas long and three yojanas broad. It was a prosperous city with plenty of rice and paddy.

The Mahabharata refers to it as a Janapada.During the time of the Puranas also, the Kosala retained its prominence among the north Indian Ksatriya tribes. The famous kings of Kosala such as Mandhata, Hariscandra, Sagara, Bhaglratha, Rtuparna,. Raghu, Dasaratha and Rama are well known in Indian history. After Rama, the extensive Kosala kingdom was divided amongst the sons of himself and his brothers. The sons of Satrughna ruled at Mathura. The sons of Laksamana established their two kingdoms in far north in the proximity of the Himalayas, while sons of Bharata established the cities of Taksasila and Puskaravan in the Gandhara country. The Kosala country proper was divided into two '(i) Southern Kosala was given to Kusa who shifted his capital from Ayodhya to Kusasthali, founded by him on the Vindhya range. Lava ruled at northern Kosala and had his capital at Sravasti. Later on, northern Kosala came to be known as Sravasti so that it may easily be distinguished from southern Kosala. The two Kosalas, with Sarayu as the boundary line in between the two, are also mentioned in Avadana Sataka. Northern Kosala was called Sravasti, by Hiuen-Tsang also, according to whom Shi-Lo-Fu-Shi-Ti (Sravasti) was 600 li in circuit and had good crops and equable climate.

The Puranas refer to it frequently and place it in the central region on the back of the Vindhya.The Vayu Purana says that Kusa transferred his capital at Kusavati on the Vindhya precipices. The same is alluded to in the Patalakhanda of the Padma Purana and in the Raghuvamsa for the purpose of explaining the return of Kusa to Ayodhya.

In later times, the country of Kosala lay south of Vatsa for Ratnavali gives the information that the Commander of the Vatsa army has surrounded the Kosala king in the Vindhya mountains. The Puranas sometimes speak of Saptakosala which suggests the expansion of Kosala kingdom. The Visnudharmottara Purana places them in the east. An inscription dated 915 A.D. found at Ratnapura in Chhattisagarh records that Shrideva, having obtained the favour of Prithvi Deva king of Kosala, was able to build temples and dig tanks etc. thus indicating the expansion of Kosala's power across the Ganges in that side. Kosala of the Puranas, dramas and poetry was more to the west along a part of Vindhya range.

During the time of Buddha Kosala kingdom was one of the sixteen great Janapadas and the Kosala people were one of the great Ksatriya tribes. Buddhist literature is full of information about them. King of Kosala such as Kalasena, Dighiti Dighayu, Mallika and Vatika are famous in Pali legends. One of them had his capital at Ayodhya, some at Saketa and the rest at Sravasti. Ayodhya was an important town in Buddha's time while Saketa and Sravasti were very prominent among the six great cities of India. Hiuen Tsang gives the extent of the kingdom of '0-Yu-T'o (Ayodhya) as 5000 li with its capital being 20 li in extent. It is said to have abound in cereals, fruits, flowers, and' its climate was temperate and agreeable.

From the Buddhistic evidence, we may conclude that there had been a great rivalry between the kings of Kasi and Kosala. There are four clear stages seen in this rivalry. In the first stage Brahmadatta, the powerful king of Kasi, attacked Kosala, executed its king and queen living harmlessly in disguise. Then the Kosalan prince took the revenge and was recrowned in his father's kingdom. During the second stage, both the kings of Kasi and Kosala appear equally powerful. In the third stage The Kosala king invaded the neighbouring nations and the king of Kasi remained passive. In the fourth stage Kasi was absorbed by Kosala. Mahakosala, the king of both Kasi and Kosala married his daughter to king Bimbisara of Magadha. When Bimbisara was murdered by his son Ajatashatru, Mahakosala imprisoned Ajatashatru but later on set him free and married one of his daughters to him. Before the end of Ajatashatru's rule, some portions of Kosala were annexed to the Maghadhan empire.Kosala finally disappeared from history as an independent kingdom evidently on account of being absorbed by Magadha.

Many Buddhistic texts such as Anguttara, Majjhima and Jatakas etc. give details about Kosala, its capital Sravasfi and its kings According to Buddhist literature Sravasfi, which was situated on the banks of Aciravati, was a great emporium of Indian trade and a great centre of culture and religion.

According to Jains the Licchavis and Nallikis were the chief of Kasi and Kosala. They seem to have succeeded the Aiksavakus of the Ramayana fame. Jains called Sravasfi as Candrapuri or Candrikapuri, which was the birth place of two famous Tirthankaras.

Hiuen Tsang who calls it the country of '0-Yu-T'o gives the extent of Kosala as 5000 li. Its frontiers consisted of encircling mountain crags, forests, and jungles together in succession. Its capital was 40 li in circumference and the soil was rich and fertile. Ptolemy has a Kontakossula in the south, probably one of the Kosalas.

According to Kalidasa both Ayodhya and Saketa were identical. Hemacandra says that Saketa, Ayodhya and Kosala were the name of one and the same city. Saketa, mentioned in the Mahabhasya and known to Ptolemy as Sogeda and Shachi to Fa-Hien, was the capital of Kosala country in the period preceeding the Buddha's time. During Buddha's time it was a flourishing city. Saketa especially belonged to the Guptas. The VayuPurana also mentions Saketa and not Ayodhya as being one of the four important Janapadas of the Guptas. It was a town on the border-land of Kosala towards the south-west. Saketa has been identified with Sujan Kot on the river Sai in the Unao distt. of Uttar Pradesh.

Ayodhya which was located on Sarayu was the name of the city as well as of the kingdom. In the Visnudharmottara Purana, the extent of the city is given as ten yojanas and its circumference as three yojanas. The Ayodhya of Ramayana extended for twelve yojanas. The Jatakas mention Ayodhya as one of the three big cities of Kosala. It appears that after Iksavaku dynasty this city never had the privilege of being a capital but it is suggested that the successor of Skandagupta had a capital at Ayodhya till the rise of Maukharis. It was very sacred for Jains as their first Tirthankara Rsabha Deva belonged to Iksavaku race. In the Puranas the city of Ayodhya is first of the seven sacred cities. During Buddha's time it became comparatively insignificant but Saketa was a flourishing city. Perhaps one was the suburb of the other. Sravasvi was founded by Sravasta after whom it was called as such. In the 4th cent. A.D. Sravasfi was a dependency of the powerful Gupta dynasty of Magadha as the neighbouring city of Saketa is especially said to have belonged to the Guptas. From this time Sravasfi gradually declined. Hindu lexicographers give Saketa and Kosala as synonyms of Ayodhya. It may be identified with the place now called Avadha which is on the right bank of the Sarayu or the Ghagghara near Fyzabad, built from its ruins. At some distance north from Avadha is the site of Sravasvi.

The exact boundaries of Kosala are not known, but this much can be said that it must have been bordered on the Ganges in its sweep downwards in a south-westerly direction from the Himalayas to the plains at modern Allahabad. The territory was practically the modern province of Avadha and the valley of the Mahanadi. The chief-city was Ayodhya with which the Saketa of Buddhist writers was probably either identical or closely associated. The Kosala country lay to the east of Kurus and to the west of Videhas from whom it was separated by the river Sadanira probably the great Gandak. The northern frontier of Kosala must have been the hills in what is now Nepal. Its southern boundary was the Ganges and the eastern was the eastern limit of the Sakya territory, for the Sakyas, as one of our oldest documents leads us to infer, claimed to be Kosalans. On the west it was bounded probably by the Gomati which flowed past the Naimisa forest and formed the boundary between the Kosalas and the Pancalas. They were almost certainly of the Aryan race, belonged to the solar family and took their lineage from Manu through Iksavaku. They were the ruling clan in the kingdom whose capital was Savatthi (modern Meheth of the village group Saheth-Maheth on the southern bank of the river Rapti on the borders of the Gonda and Bahraich distt. of the U.P.) in what is now Nepal, 70 miles north west of modern Gorakhpur. It included Benaras, Saketa, and probably had the Ganges for the southern boundary Gandaka for its eastern and mountains for its northern boundary. Smith places Savatthi on the upper course of the Rapti near the foot of the hills. Daksina Kosala has been identified with modern Raipur, Bilaspur, Sambalpur and the great forest region of Madhya Pradesh and Orissa.Uttarakosala was synonymous with Ayodhya.

## Bodha

The Bodhas are associated with Kalingas, Bhadrakaras and Salvas in the Mahabharata. Ramayana places it to the east of Satadru. The Bodhas though a central tribe are placed by B.C. Law in the eastern district of Punjab.

It was the country round Indraprastha, which had the famous tirtha called Nigamabodha, perhaps briefly called only Bodha. It may have been in the proximity of the Bahya river which is mentioned in the Niti-Mayukha. Pargiter also places them on the eastern confines of Punjab.

## Bhadrakara

The Bhadrakaras, a section of whom were Uttama Bhadras, might have lived near about the Kurus, Matsyas and Surasenas. They were a branch of the Salva tribe and lived in the north eastern part of the Punjab and adjoining regions. According to Mahabharata they were a republican people.Perhaps they later migrated to the south. Pargiter places them on the western bank of Yamuna, somewhere between Delhi and Mathura.

## Andhaka

The Andhakas, who were considered as impure, were a branch of the Yadavas and perhaps lived originally not far from Mathura. Later on they appear to have migrated towards Kathiawar. The Puranas locate them both in the east and the centre.May be they occupied the eastern portion of central India.

## Atavya

The Atavyas are placed both in the centre and the south. They were probably connected with the Atavlsikhara of Mahabharata. The Atavyas of Allahabad Pillar Ins. of Samudragupta might have denoted the Afavyas of the Puranas who were the aboriginal tribes of central India. They occupied probably the region of Indravati in Bastar.

## Kalinga

The Kalinga country was named after the prince Kalinga, the son of Bali. It is placed both in the centre and the south. The Matsya and the Vayu Puranas place them to the south of Amarakantaka hills and the Garuda Purana to the south-east along the Vindhya range. The Bhavisya Purana places them to the south-east of the Pancalas. The Ramayana locates the Kalinga-nagara between the river Gomati and the city of Ayodhya. Perhaps they occupied the central India on the eastern side and were comparatively nearer the southern people. They must have lived either between the Amarakantaka and Vindhya mountains or near them. Their position in the south as contended by some of the Puranas and the Mahabharata must have relative value with regard to other northern and central people.

Panini, Patanjali, Kautilya and the author of the Brhatsamhita also knew them. The Mahabharata which refers to them again and again informs us that they were Ksatriya people who fled away out of fear of Parasurama. The Puranas regard them as impure and taboo for Sraddha.

A passage in the TTrthayatra section of the Vanaparva of Mahabharata shows that the river VaitaranI, forming the eastern border of the Cuttock Distt. of Orissa was regarded in ancient times as the boundary of the Kalinga country. The kings of Kalinga were called the lord of Mahendra which shows that the country included Orissa state. The Matsya Purana refers to Jalesvara as a holy place on the Amarakantaka mountain. The Padma Purana places Sripurapattana in Kalinga country.

The city of Dantakura the Dantapura of Buddhists was the capital of the Kalinga country. Dandagula or Dandaguda the capital of Kalinga, mentioned by Pliny shows that the original form was Dantakura and not Dantapura. It has been confirmed by the tradition also which relates that after Krsna was killed by Jara, his bones were collected and kept in a box till king Indradyumna was directed by Visnu to form the image of Jagannatha and put those bones of Krsna into his belly. Many other Kalinga capitals existed in Ganjam district.

Kalidasa knew Kalinga to have extended upto the Godavari. He mentions Utkala and Kalinga as two separate kingdoms, the country of Kalinga stretching along the coast of the Bay of Bengal from Utkala in the north to the mouth of Godavari in the south. They first appear in the Paippalada recension of Atharvaveda. Brahmanas speak of them on the river Varanavati.

The Saktisamgamatantra places it to the east of Jagannath, the celebrated deity in the shrine of Puri in the Puri Distt. of Orissa. Puri has been called Utkala also (V.42) extending upto as far as the banks of the river Krsna. The commentary on the Kamasutra which places it to the south of Gauda-Visaya appears to indicate the extension of ancient Kaliriga as far as the Ganges. The expansion of Kalinga from the river Mahanadi to the river Krsna in the south has been referred to in an inscription of the 5th cent A.D..

The Buddhists refer to the Kalinga country and its kings. According to them, the capital of Kalinga country was Dantapuranagara. Other cities were Rajapura and Simha ???? probably identical with Singupuram near Chicacole. The Kancanapura of the Jains and Kalinganagara have been identified with the modern Mukholingam on the river Vamsadhara.

The ancient literature refers to Trikalinga people. Pliny also refers to three Kalingas in his time (1) Ganga-Ridae - Calingae (2) Macco-Kalingae (either Mekala Kalingas or Muka - Kalingas), (3) and Calingae proper. The Arabic word Tilang and the word Talaing, used to designate the ancient people of lower Burma is evidently a corruption of the word Tri-Kalinga. The term Kling applied to the people of Malay Peninsula must have originally been derived from Kalinga which seems to be the original home of the Kling people.

Hiuen Tsang knew it to be 5000 li in circuit with its capital being 20 li or so. The land was regularly cultivated and there was an abundance of fruits and flowers. It contained extensive forests and the climate was hot. From the accounts of Diodoros, Curtius and Plutarch we know that at the time of Alexander's invasion there were two very powerful people in the lower Gangetic Valley. Pliny adds a third important people of eastern India, namely Kalingas who were nearest the sea and higher up the Mandaei and Malli. The capital of Kalingas was called Parthalis identified with Purvasthah, a large village about 20 miles from the present Burdwan town. According to all foreign authorities, including the Greek writers, the Kalingas occupied the region of or about the modern Orissa bordering also the sea-coast.

The Kalinga country has been referred to in a number of Inscriptions. Most of the early Gangas of Kalinga like Hastivarman, Indravarman and Devendravarman, who described themselves as the lord of Kalinga, issued grants from the victorious camp at Kalinganagara. The Hathigumpha Ins. refers to Kalinga and its capital Kalinganagara. King Kharavela has been called Kalingadhipati and Kalinga Chakkavatti. This is the modern Mukhalingam on the Vamsadhara river and adjacent rivers in the Ganjam district. Khibira was really the capital of Kalinga in Khar vela's time. The capital appears to have stood on the bank of the rivfei! Praci flowing on the northern part of the Puri district.

A Nepalese Ins. mentions Sriharsa or Harsadeva to be the king of Kalinga and neighbouring countries. A copper plate grant of eastern Chalukya king Bhima I mentions a village in Elamanca -Kalingadesa. Another copperplate Ins. discovered at Sumandala near Khallikot in Ganjam distt. shows beyond doubt that the south-western part of the coastal Orissa was called Kalinga as late as the second half of the 6th cent. A.D. Other Inscriptions referring to Kalinga country are Modhainagar grant of Lakshamanasena, the Komarti grant and the Ins. of the Kalingadhipati Vasisthiputra Saktivarman of the Mathara family.

Though it was often distinguished from Odra, yet in its widest sense, it denoted almost the whole of modern province of Orissa. It included Pithudaka, Pithudaga or Pithunda on the sea coast, which existed near the river Languliya. On the south-west of Orissa, it comprised of Ganjam and Vizagapatana. It also included the Amarakantaka range, which is said to be in its western part, and the river Vaitarani.

According to R.D. Banerjee, roughly speaking the country lay in two parts viz, tract lying between the Damodara and Mahanadi in the north, and that between the Mahanadi and Godavari in the south. The then Kalinga was politically divided into 3 parts (1) Utkala - country from Midnapur to Brahmani -Vaitaram (2) Tosala - the central tract consisting of the modern distt. of Cuttack, Puri, northern parts of the Ganjam distt. and some of the former small states on both the banks of Mahanadi such as Ranpur, Nayagodh, Khandapara, Daspalla, Tigira and Athgadh (3) Kongoda - country between the Chilka lake and Mahendragiri.

Basak includes the south-western portion of Bengal i.e. parts of Midnapur and Howrah distt., the whole of modern Orissa and some northern portion of former Madras Presidency in ancient Kalinga country. It has been located between the Godavari and Mahanadi on the eastern coast of India to the south of Orissa and north of Dravida.

Cunningham places it between the Godavari on the south west and Gaoliya branch of Indravati on the north east. It comprised the entire tract lying along the coast of Vaitarani in Orissa to the borders of the Andhra country. Wilson places it along the sea-coast west of the mouths of the Ganges with upper part of the Coromandel coast. According to Pargiter, the Kalinga occupied all the eastern coast between the Utkalas on the north and the Telingas or Telugus on the south. The river Vaitaram (mod. Byturni) flowed through it and the Mahendra mountains were within its southern limit. Kalinga, therefore comprised the modern Orissa, distt. of Ganjam and probably also Vizagapatanam.

At the time of Ashoka and Kharavela, it certainly comprised the Puri - Cuttack region in the east. Ashoka (369-332 ?.?) conquered and annexed the Kalinga country to his empire. The province of Kalinga in Maurya empire was divided into administrative units. The north-eastern part of the country had its headquarters at Tosali (modern Dhauli near Bhuvaneshvara in Puri distt. of Orissa). The south-western Kalinga had its headquarters at the city of Samapa near modern Jangadh in the Ganjam Distt. The kings of Arya - Mahamegh-avadhana family of the Cedi clan, which is represented by Kharavela, also had their capital at Tosali. The Kharavela Ins. records the conquest of Kalinga by Nanda king but later on Kalinga was broken off from Nanda rule and Ashoka had to conquer it again. It became independent of Magadha after the death of Ashoka and retained its independence upto the time of Kanishka.

At the time of the eastern Ganga king Anantavarman Codaganga, the Kalinga kingdom extended from the Ganga to the Godavari. King Ramapala of Pala dynasty also conquered Kalinga, Utkala and Kamarupa. King Vijayasena of Sena dynasty, too, conquered Kalinga. Towards the first half of the 8th century Kalinga seems to have. come under the Kamarupa for sometime. In the age of Vijayabahu (1055-1100 A.D.) the name Kalinga was exclusively applied to the kingdom of the eastern Gangas of Kalinganagara near Srikakulam, who styled themselves as Kalirigadhipati. Simhapura (modern Singupuram) was the capital of Kalinga in the 4th Cent. A.D.

Radha and Kalinga do not appear to have contiguous boundaries in any period of history. Simhapura in Radha (modern Singur in the Hoogly distt. of west Bengal) cannot be regarded the same as Simhapura, the Kalinga capital.

Indian literature intimately associates Mahendragiri, standing on the borders of the Ganjam distt. of Orissa, with Kalinga. Godavari or Krsna was often regarded as the south-western boundary of Kalinga. About the 5th Cent. A.D. some rulers of Kalinga not only had their capital at Pistapura, modern Pithapuram in the east Godavan distt., but sometimes even claimed lordship over the whole coast land between the Krsna and the Mahanadl. Actually, however, the north-eastern boundary of the ancient Kalinga country lay even beyond Mahanadi, although Kalinga did not include the whole of Orissa region.

## Kasi

Kasi, also known as Varanasi has been variously called. It is called Kasi because supreme brilliance shines there and lights the way to liberation. It is Avimukta, as it is free from sin and is never left by the Lord Siva. It is Anandakanana as it gives the greatest delight to the Lord. To the Buddhists also it is known by different names such as Surundhana Sudassana, Brahmavaddhana, Pupphavati, Ramma, Moline, Kasinagara and Kasipura.

The kingdom of Kasi is of Vedic fame as references to it are found not only in the Brahmanas but also in the Upanisads. It is known to both the grammarians and the author of the Yoginitantra.

Both the epics mention it, and we are told that though Kasi was kingdom, yet Prayaga and the adjoining areas were still a forest. The foundation of Kasi has been attributed to Sulapani Mahadeva.

The Anusasanaparva (ch.30) of the Mahabharata attributes the foundation of Varanasi to Divodasa, the king of Kasi. The VTtihavyas were constantly waging a war with the kings of Kasi. They had killed Haryasva, the grand father of Divodasa and defeated Divodasa forcing him to flee. It was Pratardana, the son of Divodasa, who ultimately subdued the Vitihavyas.

In the Puranas, Divodasa is said to be the grandson of Sunhotra. A demon called Ksemaka attacked Varanasi and desolated it. Divodasa had to leave the city and had to found another city on the river Gomati on the border of Kasivisaya. It was Alarka, the grandson of Pratardana who killed Ksemaka and regained Kasi.

Another king with whom Divodasa had to fight was Bhadrasrenya. Divodasa, having killed all the sons of Bhadrasrenya except one Durdama who was only a child, captured Varanasi. Durdama later on fought with Divodasa, and snatched Varanasi from him. It was ultimately conquered by Pratardana, the son of Divodasa.

The city was burnt by Krsna also. The Matsya Purana locates Varanasi between rivers Varana, Asi, Suklanadi (Ganga), BhTmcandika and Parvatesvara. From east to west it extended for two yojanas and from north to south its extent was half a yojana.

It was situated in central India in the midst of rivers Varana and Asi which bound the city to the north and the south. It derives its name Varanasi from these two rivers.
