River Varana, probably identical with the Varanavati of Atharvaveda, is a considerable rivulet rising to the north of Allahabad and has a course of about 100 miles while Asi is a mere brook. It is regarded very sacred by the Puranas which refer to it frequently. Kasi is mentioned by the author of the Brhatsamhita also.

It is Kassida of Ptolemy and Po-lo-ni-'see of Hiuen Tsang, according to whom its capital bordered on the eastern side on the Ganges. It was 4000 li in circuit and its capital was 18 or 19 li in lengh and 5 or 6 li in breadth. Its climate was soft and crops were abundant.

According to Buddhist traditions it extended for 300 leagues on the left bank of Ganga and the river Varana. It was bordered by Kosala on the north, Magadha on the east and Vatsa on the west.It was one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas and its capital Varanasi was one of the four places of pilgrimage for the Buddhists. But Ashoka had his capital at Potali and king Udayabhada at Surundha.

Though by the time of Buddha, Kasi had lost its freedom, references to it are found in many Jatakas as an independent country. It appears to have been a great, populous and prosperous city an important centre of trade and industry and was famous for clothes, perfumes, ivory work, stone culture, and its carpenters, hunters and snake charmers. A good number of Buddhist works refer to it and its kings. In Jain litarature, Kasi with its capital Varanasi is said to be one of the Aryan countries.

There seems to have been frequent wars between Kasi and Kosala, now one side coming out victorious now the other. At different periods Kasi came under the sway of three successive suzerain powers of northern India i.e. Purus of Vatsa, Iksavakus of Kosala and kings of Magadha, but it must have enjoyed independence between the decline- of Vatsa and the rise of Kosala. During the time of Buddha it was absorbed into Kosala, before the accession of Prasenajit of Kosala. It was finally conquered and incorporated into the Magadhan empire when Ajatashatru became the most powerful ruler of northern India. The chieftains of Benaras and kings of Magadha had friendly relations.

Kasi also figures in the stories of Mahavira and his desciples. It was an important town within the kingdom ofJujasattu. It was divided into four parts namely Devavaranasi, Rajadham, Madana Varanasi and Vijayavaranasi.

It is mentioned in some inscriptions also, e.g. a Buddhist Inscription from Bodhagaya of the reign of Chandradeva, Inscription from Saranath and Kamauli plate of Govindachandra (V.S. 1194). From some of the Gahadavala records, e.g. the Rewain Grant, we come to know that Adikesavaghatta near the confluence of the Varana and the Ganga to the north of Benaras was then included in Benaras. Its southern boundary extended at least upto the confluence of Asi and the Ganga. The Chandravati grant of Chandradeva shows that Gahadavala dominions extended from Beneras and Kanauj to the confluence of the Sarayu and the Gharghara in Ayodhya.

Though Kasi is a later word, it is quite possible that the town is older, as the river Varanavati may be connected with the later Varanasi. The city proper included the land between Varana and Asi. Its extent including the suburb is often said to be 12 leagues or about 35 miles. Now Benaras extends four miles along the bank of the Ganga.

## Kulya

The Kulyas have been placed both in the south and the north. Law takes them to be the Kulutas of Punjab dwelling along with tribes such as Malavas, Yaudheyas, Arjunayanas and Udumbaras etc.. Sircar locates them in the present Kulait region on the upper Ravi. Dr. S.B. Chaudhuri places them between Aligarh and Bareilly distt. of Uttar Pradesh.

## Jangala

They are mentioned along with Kurus and other contiguous tribes. In the absence of any other tribe of this name in the ancient texts or literature, we are inclined to identify them with the Kurujangalas. The entire Kuru country was called as Kurujangala in the Mahabharata. Its capital Hastinapura, too, was included in it. The Skanda Purana places Kurujangala on Gariga. It was called Srikanthadesa during the Buddhist period. It capital Vilasapura was in Kuruksetra. In 6th cent. A.D. its capital was Thanesar which was later shifted to Kanauj by Harsha Shiladitya. Some scholars have identified Jangala with the former Bikaner state and northern part of Jodhpur, the region being called Jangaladesa. The kings of Bikaner were called the kings of Jarigaladhara and the region of earstwhile Bikaner state was called Jarigalu.

## (B) Northern States Vahilka

Their history may be traced back to the Vedic period. In one hymn of the Atharvaveda fever is asked to go to the Mujavant mountain (a hill in or about Kashmir) and the country of Bahlikas further off. A king of this tribe Vahllka Pratipeya is referred to in the Satapatha Brahmana. This Vahllka Pratipeya or Pratipeya is mentioned in the Mahabharata and the Puranas also. They are placed in the north, west as well as in the centre. The Bengal recension of the Ramayana associates them with the northern and western people. The northern recension places them outside Madhyadesa, which must have extended as far as Sarasvati in the west. At another place they are seen living between Ayodhya and Kekaya.

According to the epic tradition they, being the descendants of the demons Bahi and Hika of the Vipasa river, were regarded as impure and the Aryans of mid-India were forbidden to go there.Similarly in the Vartikas of Panini, Katyayana derives the word Vahika from Vahi or Bahi meaning outside, thereby suggesting their being outside the pale *of* Aryandom. They were known as Jarttikasand Arattas and had their capital at Sakala (modern Sialkot in Pakistan). They lived generally between Sutlej and Indus but specially on the west of the rivers Apaga and RavT i.e. in the distt. of Jhang. It was a Ksatriya colony which was situated to the east of Mahamlecchas and bordered on Kamboja.

Ptolemy knew it as Bakrioi which was near Archosia in his time. The Bactriana of the Greeks was situated in Turkestan, and was the Capital of Bactria comprising modern Kabul, Khurasan and Buhenna.

Vahikas were a different people living between the Sutlaj and the Indus. The word 'Vahlika' was already in use in India for some of the semi-foreign peoples in north-western India. They were grouped together as Vahlikas (Bactrians), a term which in its narrowest sense meant the Mallas, west of the Jhelum. The reference to them in Sanskrit literature shows that the two tribes of Vahlikas dwelt in Punjab, one of them was closely connected with the Madras for their King Salya is called the lord of Vahlikas also and his sister Madri is called Valhiki. The other Madra people were closely connected with the Daradas. Thus one Vahlika was situated in the plains of Punjab alongside the Madradesa i.e. between Chinab and Sutlaj and the other among the lower slopes of Himalayas i.e. between Chinab and Bias. One of them seems to have migrated southwards near the mouth of the Indus. In the Puranas also we find them located in the north, as well as in the western part of the central India.They have been located beyond the Himalayas also.Later on the term Vahlika was used in a more general way like the term 'Pahlavas' 'Yavanas' etc. and it may not have any reference to the people of Balkh. In Sanskrit literature also we do not find any indication which may enable us to identify it with Baikh, though Wilson and others have identified it with the modern Baikh region in the north part of Afghanistan.

Bhandarkar has placed them on the Vipasa and the hill Visnupada is said to be in its vicinity. As this Visnupada hill is situated in Kashmir, they might have been a neighbouring people of Kashmir. The fact of its being inaccesible and famous for horses places them in the neighbourhood of Gandhara and Kamboja, and their habitat beyond the Indus is definitely proved by the Mehrauli Iron Pillar Ins. of about 4th century A.D.

## Mathara

The Matharas, who are associated with Ruddhakatakas were a north-western people. They seem to have migrated to the south later on. Levi has placed them between Gazni and Wakhan. Ptolemy locates them on the Indus. This race appears to have many branches and one of them was located among the Vindhyas. Yule associates them with Ramagiri, modern Ramtek near Nagpur.

## Vatadhana

The Vatadhanas were a Ksatriya people on the northern border. They were connected with the Trigarttas, Kekayas, Vahtikas, Madras, Suviras, Sibis, Daserakas and Puskalas of the northern India. The Brahma Purana locates them in the central India, and the Mahabharata in the west.From the references found in the Mahabharata they appear to have occupied the territories on the eastern side of the Sutlaj. Vatadhana was the off spring of an outcaste Brahmin and a Brahmin woman. But, says Pargiter that is no doubt an expression of the same arrogance which in later times, stigmatised all the Punjab races as an outcaste. He has identified them with the people living on the east of Sutlaj, south-ward from Ferozpur. They are said to have occupied the Punjab Rajasthan region and had a settlement at Madhyamika near Chitor. They are named between VahEka and Abhira and are placed in the west of Indraprastha or Delhi. The Vamana Purana associates Safhyadhanakas with Abhlras and Vatadhanas with Vahlikas and Abhlras both and places them in Aparantadesa. It appears to be a country in Punjab and may be identified with Bhatnair.

## Barbara

The Barbaras, a Vaisya Sudra clan, occupied the north-western region of India with Sakas, Yavanas etc.. It appears that they had more than one settlement. In the north they are associated with Yavanas, Kambojas, Gandharas and Kiratas. In the Matsya Purana, the river Caksu (modern Oxus) is said to have watered their land passing through the Sindhu-Maru country. The Sabha-Parva of the Mahabharata places them in the west. Again they have been placed in the east. The river Sita, the eastern current of the Ganga (modern Yarkand) is said to have passed through their country. The Padma Purana associates them with Videhas and Tamraliptakas indicating their eastern habitat. Their presence near the sea is indicated not only by the Mahabharata but also by the commentary on Arthasastra. The Barbarian of Periplus, the Barbarika of Dhantantariya Nighantu and the Barbarei of Ptolemy, along with Fatal formed the two towns of the islands formed by the Sindhu.Their country seems to have extended to the Arabian sea and their port Barbarika was known to Greek geographers as Barbaricum.

## Madraka

They are named among the Aparanta people.The Garuda Purana places them in the north beyond the Himalayas, and the Brahma in the western part of central India. The epic tradition, which related them to the Sauviras and Vahikas, regarded them as impure.

The Madraka Janapada was named after Madraka, one of the four sons of Sibi. The other three sons of Sibi were Prthudarbha (Vrsadarbha) Suvira and Kekaya. Their Janapadas were known as Kekaya and Madraka. Thery were closely related to Salvas as both Salvas and Madras were progeny of the same parents. The Visnudharmottara Purana associates Prabhadrakas with Salvas and Sibis. The Vayu and the Matsya Puranas associate them with Sindhu and Sauviras. We have seen that both Suvira and Madraka were related. The evidence of the Puranas shows that Madrakas and Bhadrakas were one and the same people. As Visnudharmottara Purana associates Prabhadrakas with Sibis we may conclude that Prabhadrakas and Bhadrakas also were synonyms. The Prabhadrakas are mentioned as Ksatriyas of Pancaladesa and in that case they might have been two different branches of the same tribe. The Mahabharata mentions Paribhadras and Prabhadras as well. The Srsti Khanda of the Brahma Vaivarta Purana mentions Puspabhadras, Paribhadras and Bhadrakas as the names of the places. The Uttamabhadras of the Nasik cave Inscription of Saka Usavadata were a sub-clan of Madras. , Panini refers to two Madras, Purva Madras and Apara Madras. The Purva Madras occupied the region between Ravi and Chenab while Apara Madras had their territory from Chenab to Jhelum. The Visnudharmottara Purana places them between Devika (modern Deeg, a tributary of Ravi) and Candrabhaga (modern Chenab). River Iravati formed its boundary on the north-east. Their capital was Sakala. i.e. modern Sialkot in Pakistan and they occupied the region near Iravati which flowed through their country.Cunningham has identified it with Sangalawala Tiba or Sangala Hills situated to the west of Ravi in the Jhang district of Pakistan.

## Paradas

The Paradas, who were considered to be the Miecchhas and who were associated with the Adhiras etc. were a barbarious hill tribe of northern India dwelling on the slopes of the Himalayas. The river Caksu flowed through their territory. Brahma Purana is apparently wrong in placing them in central India. Originally they were Ksatriyas who lost their status through avoidence of sacred rites and were excluded from the pale of Ksatriyism by Sagara. They had to suffer defeat at the hands of Kekayas also. The term Parada is used, in the Puranic lists to represent people living beyond Indus. They probably lived in Khorasan region. Dr. Oppert places them in northern Baluchistan.Ptolemy mentions them under the name of Parthia (ancient Persia). They are known as Parthava in Behistun Ins. of Darius. A tribe Palidas occurs in the edict of Ashoka, with Andhras and Bhojas, A variant of Palidas Pulida and Pirinida appears in another version of the same edict at Girnar and Kalsi. So a controversy has arisen whether the tribe was known as Paradas or Pulindas in Sanskrit literature. Some locate them in south but Pargiter has placed them in the north-west, on the western part of Himalayas.

## Kuserakas

The Kuserakas, who are known as Kaserakas and Daserakas as well, were a Vaisya-Sudra dan of northern India. The Daserakas are placed to the east of the Rajasthan desert and it is said that one has to cross Vipasa to reach there.

## Dasamanikas

They were a north-western Ksatriya people.They have been placed in the Dasht Valley in Kalat (Pakistan) by Sircar. Nothing much is known about them from the evidence of the Puranas. The Mahabharata's Dasamalika may perhaps be identified with the Malli.

## Bharadvajas

Evidently they were the descendants of the sage Bharadvaja. The sixth mandala of the Rgveda has been attributed to the family of Bharadvaja. They, along with Atreyas, Puskalas, Lampakas etc. are placed in the north. The Mahabharata connects them with the upper Gangetic region near the hills. Pargiter places them in the Garhwal or Kumaon region.

## Atreyas

The Atreyas descended from the sage Prabhakara of the Atri race. They are said to have lived in the Dvaitavana. The epic evidence locates them along with Bharadvajas not far from the upper regions of the Ganges near the hills.

## Prasthalas

The Prasthalas, who were a Vaisya-Sudra clan, and were closely connected with Trigarttas, were greatly despised in the epic age. They are known to Ptolemy as Passala. They seem to have occupied the districts between Ferozpur, Patiala and Sirsa in Punjab. Sircar has placed them in the Marwar region in Rajputana.

## Bahubhadra and Ruddhakataka

The Bahubhadras were considered to be Barbaras living in western India. The Ruddhakatakas might have occupied the gold producing Rudok area of Tibet. Nothing else is known about them.

## Lampaka

The Markandeya Purana enlists them with the Kuserakas, Sulakaras, and Culikas etc. as a northern people who were a subdivision of Kiratas. They were a very rude Vaisya-Sudra clan living in the north beyond the Himalayas. They are also knownas Murundas. Murunda is a later form of Saka word meaning master of lord. Lampaka was thus the capital of Sakas (Dr.Sten Konow). Hiuen-Tsang gives the circuit of Lan-Po country as 1000 li. It is said to be backed by the snowy mountains in the north and surrounded by Black-Ridge mountains. The capital was 10 li in circuit and the hoar frost was in plenty, but there was not much snow. Lassen identifies them with Lambagae and places them south of Hindukush in modern Kafiristan. Cunningham identifies them with the people of Lamghan situated to the north-west of Kabul near Peshawar. It is a small territory lying along the bank of the Kabul river which is bounded on the west by Alingar and Kumar rivers and on the north by snowy mountains. Dey places them twenty miles north-west of Jalalabad. They had their capital at Lampaka or Lamghan. They must have lived round about the Lampak range of Himalayas lying 18 miles north of Nanda-Devi, bounded towards the north and south by the Girthi-Ganga and Dunagiri Gad. There are ten peaks, five of them being more than 20000 ft.;

Lampak range is 20380 ft. and it appears seven miles east, north-east at the head of the Kalla Gad.

## Haramusika

It was a Ksatriya colony on the northern border. Motichandra locates Harahuras in the Herat region. The Harahunas or Harahuras were probably a branch of the Hunas who subjugated the whole land from central Asia to central India about the end of the 5th cent A.D.. Harahaura is the tract lying between the Indus and Jhelum, Gandgarh mountain and the Salt range in Pakistan. As far as the Haramusikas are concerned they may be the same Musikas who are referred to as Musikenoi by Greek historians and who have been placed in Punjab.

## Hamsamarga, Tomara and Ahuka

They belonged to the Vaisya-Sudra clans of northern mountains. In the absence of any specified information about them, it is difficult to locate them. River Paosni is said to have flown through the countries of Tomara, Hamsamargas and Samuhakas. The description of the Matsya and the Vayu Purarias may place the two tribes in the region east of Tibet. Hamsamargas are placed in the Humza distt. in the north-west Kashmir.

## Pahlava

Their origin has been attributed to the discussion between the sages Vasistha and Visvamitra regarding the possession of Kamadhenu. The Mahabharata mentions them as a non-Aryan people born of the tail of the Nandini cow. They were close allies of Haihayas and Talajanghas. The Vayu Purana regards them to be a branch of Kiratas. According to the epic-Puranic tradition, supported by the evidence of Harivamsa also, they were annihilated and degraded by Sagara. The Mahabharata mentions two tribes of this name but it is difficult to make a distinction between the two since the information given there is very vague.

The Puranas place them in the north, west, centre and south-west as well.

The word Pallava or Pahlava is a coruption of the word Parthava i.e. the Indian name for Parthians. Their history is still abscure and their origin is one of the most vexed problems of ancient Indian history. Only a few facts may be collected from the history of coins and inscriptions. The earliest known prince of this dynasty was Vonones who attained power in Archesia or Seistan. Rapson calls Vonones a ruler of the kingdom of eastern Iran.He was succeeded by Spalirises after whom came Gondo Phernes. The epigraphic evidence shows that the Peshawar Distt. then was under him. The types of his coin show that he became the ruler of the Saka-Pahlava dominions both in eastern Iran and north-west India. When this Pahlava king died his empire broke up and was appropriated by several princes, one of whom named Pakores perhaps ruled over the western Punjab and some portions of southern Afghanistan The family was then overthrown by the advance of the Kushanas.

They are generally regarded as foreign intruders. The southern Pahlavas might have been a branch of the Pahlavas of north-western India, but except the similarity in names there is no other evidence that may prove their migration to the south. Wilson, too, does not take them to be Hindu people and regards them to be some of the tribes living between India and Persia. It is said that Media, when it formed a part of the ancient Parthian kingdom (modern Persia), was the Pahlava country. The Avesta is written in the Pahlavi or Pehlvi character of the Parthian times.

They were known in the Sangama literature as Tondaiyar and are said to have descended from the Naga chieftains. Sir K.P. Jayaswal regards them neither foreigners nor Dravidians but good Brahmin aristrocrats from the north, soldiers by profession. They are said to be an offshoot of Vakatakas. The suggestion regarding the northern affinities of the Pallavas has probably some substance for it is significant that their earliest characters are in Prakrit and they were also patrons of Sanskrit learning and culture. But their Brahmanical pedigree despite certain traditions of their connection with Dronacarya and Asvatthama does not appear to be correct. The expression Pallava Ksatriyah in the Talogunda Inscription clearly indicates the Ksatriya stock of the Pallavas.

## Abhiras

It seems that they had more than one settlement in India. The earliest known reference to them is found in the Mahabhasya where they have been located in the Sindhu-desa. The Matsya Purana and the Vayu Purana locate them in the north, and the Brahma Purana in the west and south as well. The Vayu, the Markandeya, the Brahmanda and the Vamana Puranas, too, record their settlement in the south. The Visnu, the Markandeya and Varahamihira note their habitat in western India.The Visnu Purana, associating them with Surastras, Sudras, Arbudas, Karusas and Malavas, places them along the Pariyatra mountain. The Varaha Purana locates them on the banks of Sindhu. The Bhagavata Purana groups them with Sauviras beyond Marudhanva. Formerly they belonged to the Ksatriya stock.

Out of fear of Parasurama they had to resort to the mountains and due to their nonadherence to their duties ordained by scriptures, they were degraded.

The epic tradition despised them as a miecchha tribe dwelling along the Sindhu and Sarasvati rivers. The Mahabharata classifies them as (1) dwelling along Sarasvati, (2) living by fishing thereby indicating their habitat near the sea-coast and (3) dwelling on the mountains along with Daradas, Kasmiras and Paradas. It tells us more of their movements from the Punjab region and Mathura to the Konkana region. Here, too, they are frequently associated with Sudras. The Ramayana regards Abhiras as Dasyus living near the sea. The Sadapancasaddesavibhaga of the Saktis-amgamatantra and the Matsya Purana place them on the Vindhya between the Konkana in the south and the Tapl in the north-west. The Kamasutra commentary applies the name Abhira to the tracts including Srikantha (Thanesvara) and Kuruksetra.

They are referred to in the Nasik Cave Inscription and the Gurida Inscription of Rudrasimha (A.D.181). They were a western and south-western tribal state and a semi-independent people living outside the borders of the Samudragupta's empire. Pargiter finds some relations between them and the incidents following the great Mahabharata war. The Yadavas were attacked and defeated by them. On the evidence of the Mahabharata Pargiter places the first group in the northern portion of the Rajputana desert as far east as Vinasana and as far west as Pancanada. He locates the second group along the Rann of Kurdi and the delta of Indus. The third group, according to him, had its habitat in the Aravali range and hills of Malwa. Another section of this group appears to have occupied the hilly tracts in the north and west of Punjab for they are grouped with Daradas, Kasmiras. Paradas, Hunas and Pulindas etc.. They are again connected with the Pandu migration to the south. Bhandarkar places them in the south. They lay on the Indus above where it bifurcates from the delta. They were pastoral tribes living in the north-west districts as far as Sindh.They originally lived in the western Rajputana near desert but gradually moved southerwards. In the first or second century A.D. they were not far from Saurastra i.e. southern Kathiawar. In the third cent. A.D. they occupied parts of the northern Konkan and the Nasik region, but it cannot be said with precision that this area was called Abhira in the late medieval period'. Sircar places them in the northern Rajputana distt. and regards Abiravan between Herat and Kandhar to be their original home. Elliotplaces them on the western coast of India from Tapi' to Devagarh, and Schoff locates them in the southern Gujarat including Surat. Acsording to Ward they lived in the region extending from Konkan southwards to the western bank of the river Tapi. The traditional and legendary history of Nepal as contained in the Vamsavalls record their settlement in Nepal in very early times. Some locate them in the province of Ahirwara- between Parvati and Betawa in central India in the country between Jhansi and Bhilsa. They occupied the territories in Punjab (Crooke: Caste and tribes of north-west province and Oudh), Kathiawad (Euthoven: Castes and tribes of Bombay Presidency) and parts of Nasik and Khandesh distt. Even today the Ahirs in tribal groups abound largely in Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, Nepal and some protions of Rajputana. They are simple, sturdy people mostly cowherds and agriculturists.

The Abhira country was ruled over by the western Ksatrapas who held'their sway over the entire Indo-Scythia of Greeks. It was known as Ophir to Bible, Abiria to Periplus and Aberia to Ptolemy. Ptolemy locates Abiria in the region above Patalene, the insular portion formed by the bifurcation of the Indus towards its mouth. According to Periplus the interior part of Ariake which bordered on Scythia was called Aberia and its sea board Syrastrene. Aberia of Periplus appears to be the land lying to the east and near the mouth of Indus. The Greek sources locate them on the Indus, north of Sauvlra. They are referred to in the Nasik Cave Inscription and the Gunia Inscription of Rudrasimha. Rudrasimha had an Abhira general in -his realm. Soon after an Abhira king named Isvarasena became the Mahaksatrapa of west Indusand captured portions of Maharastra in 3rd cent. A.D. from the Satavahana rulers. The dynasty of Isvarasena has been suggested to be identical with Traikutaka line of Aparanta and the establishment of Traikutaka era commencing from 249 A.D. marks the time when the Abhlras succeeded Satavahanas in northern Maharastra and the adjoining regions.

## Gandhara

The Gandharas were a people of the Vedic fame. It is interesting to note that during the Vedic age they were not excluded from Vedic Aryandom and they are mentioned along with other Aryan peoples of the east and the west. They are known to Panini also. Valmiki knew Gandhara territory as Gandharvadesa. The Mahabharata, too, mentions it as a very ancient country. It was conquered by Arjuna and later on by Janamejaya who performed his snake-sacrifice at Taksasila.

According to Strabo, the country of Gandaritis lay between Khoaspes, Indus and along the river Kophes. The Gandarioi were included in the seventh satrapy of Darius, along with the Sattagydai, Dadikai and Aparytai. According to the Behistun Inscription of Darius I (522-486 B.C.) and big Susaplace Inscription of Darus, the people of Gadar appear to be one of the subject peoples of the Persian empire. They were known to Ptolemy as Gandorae and as Gandare to Hekatores. Indus is said to have been their eastern boundary.

The Gandhara people were named after Gandhara, the son of Angarasetu and are descendants of Druhyu, son of Yayati. King Druhyu and his people are mentioned in the Rgveda several times and it is possible that they were a north-western people. Thus the Puranic tradition, which places them in northern India, in the neighbbourhood of Kashmir is in accordance with the Vedic evidence.

The Buddhist writers included them among the sixteen Mahajanapadas of India. They occupied the Peshawar and Rawalpindi distt. of Pakistan. The Jatakas testify to the existence of the trade relations between Kasmira, Gandhara kingdom and the north-eastern kingdom of Videha. The Rock Edict of Ashoka mentions the spread of Buddhism in Gandhara.

The Kiantolo of Hiuen-Tsang lived on the river Sindhu. Their country was 1000 li from east to west and above 800 li from north to south. The capital Po-Lu-Ska-Pu was 40 li in circuit. The climate was warm and moist in general without lee or snow. It had a luxuriant crop of cereals and there was a profusion of fruits and flowers. The Gandaraei of Periplus dwelt on both sides of the river Kabul above its junction with Indus. Poclais (Skt. Puskalavati) was its western capital.

The Nagarjunikonda Ins. of Virapurushadatta mentions them. Wilson has placed them in north-west India, to the west of Indus river in Punjab. They lived south of Hindukush in the region about the modern Kandahar to the Indus and extending into the Punjab and Kashmir. Rhys Davids placed them in modern Kandhara which was the district of eastern Afghanistan, probably including the north-west Punjab. V. A. Smith, too, places them in the north-west Punjab and the adjoining regions. Dr. S.K. Aiyanger takes it to be equivalent to the eastern Afghanistan extending from the Afghan mountains to the. districts somewhat to the east of Indus.. In the opinion of Dr. D.R. Bhandarkar the Gandhara territory included western Punjab and eastern Afghanistan. Its capital was Taksasila whose ruins are spread near Sara-i-kala in the Rawalpindi Distt. of Pakistan.Renell places them to the west of Bactria in the province called Margiana. Cunningham placed them in Laghman and Jalalabad on the west, the hills of Swat and Bunir on the north, Indus on the east and the hills of Kalabagh -on the south. Dey locates them in the modern distt. of Peshawar and Hoti Murdan (Pakistan) or what is called the Eusofzai country i.e. the country between the Indus and the Panjokora, where at Ranighat, Sanghas and Nuttu, discoveries were made of excellent Buddhist architecture and sculptures of the time of Kanishka.Ancient sculptures have also been discovered at Jamalgiri in Eusafzai Pargana of the Peshawar Distt., Jamalgiri being 30 miles distant from Peshawar. The Eusafzai country is bounded on the north by Chitral (Pakistan) and Yasin (Jammu and Kashmir), on the west by Bejawar and the Swat river, on the east by the Indus and on the south by the Kabul river. It seems that they first emigrated to Kandahar in the 5th cent A.D.. The country of Gandhara comprised the whole of lower Kabul valley lying along the Kabul river between Khosapes and Indus.Raychaudhuri opines that it included Kashmir and Taksasila region and was equivalent to the north-west Punjab and adjoining regions and lay on ' the both sides of the Indus. According to Dr. V.S. Aggarwal it extended from Taksasila to the river Kunar, its western boundary and from the river Kabul in the south to Swat in the north and included the whole of the lower basin of the Kabul river.

The Ain-i-Akbari, too, locates them in the district of Pukely lying between Kashmir and Attock. In the opinion of Rapson it was on the north-west frontier of India in the neighbourhood of Kamboja, Madras and similar other tribes. It denotes the region comprising the modern district of Peshawar in the north-west frontier province and Rawalpindi in Pakistan but in the old Persian Inscriptions it seems to have included also the district of Kabul in Afghanistan. From the reference to Gandharas and their cities we come to know that they had two capitals, one at Taxila (Skt. Taksasila) and other at Peukelaotis (Skt. Puskalavati) and the former situated to the east and latter to the west of Indus. So it seems that their territory lay on both sides of that river though in later days it was confined to the western side only. It appears that the boundaries of the Gandhara country differed at different periods in its history. Once they seem to have occupied the Afghan district round Kandhar but later on it shrank to the mountains on the Indian frontier.

Most probably it was conquered by Cyrus (559-530 B.C.) and remained a Persian province for about two centuries. After the downfall of empire in 331 B.C. it, together with the Persian Province of India which had been added to empire by Darius not long after 516 B.C. came under the sway of Alexander the great. The Gandhara territory was included in the kingdom of Chandragupta and Ashoka. In Ashoka's time it was feudatory to him and included also the Iran-Indus region, the capital of which was Puskalavati (modern Bala Hisar at the junction of Swat and Kabul rivers). It seems that Agathocles conquered the country and expelled the Mauryas.

Shortly after the death of Ashoka, it became independent and later on came under the sway of Greek kings. After Greeks it came in the hands of Sakas who were finally overpowered by Kushanas. In the end of the 5th cent. A.D. it was taken by Hunas. Dharmapala of Pala dynasty dethroned Indrayudha, king of Pancala and installed in his place Chakrayudha, with the assent of the neighbouring northern powers such as Bhoja, Matsya and Gandhara etc. Near the 5th cent A.D. Gandhara was conquered by the Ye-Ta i.e. Yets or Jats. R.D. Banerjee presumes that Diyadafa II conquered Gandhara since some gold coins of Diyadata II have been found by Sir John Marshall in the ruins of the city of Taksasila. After this, information is scanty till the 9th cent A.D. when Lalliya founded the Hindu Shahiya dynasty. Trilocanapala was the last king of Shahi dynasty. During his rule, the Hindu rule was lost in Gandhara. In the llth cent A.D. Trilocanapala was defeated by Sultan Mahamud of Gazni on the banks of the river Tosi. His son Bhimapala remained independent for five years. After him no information is available of the Hindu rule in Gandhara.

## Capital Cities of Gandhara.

Kapisa was a Gandharan capital during the days of Greek rule. The early capital cities of Gandhara were Puskaravati or Puskalavati and Taksasila, the former being situated to the north and latter to the south of Indus. They were founded by Taksa and Puskara, sons of Bharata.

## Puskalavati

It is mentioned in Brhatsamhita. It was the capital of an Indian prince named Hasti at the time of Alexander's expedition (326 B.C.). It is the Proklais of Ptolemy, and Peukelaotis of Arrian. The Greek name seems to have been directly derived from the word Pukkalaoti, the Pali or spoken form of Sanskrit Puskalavati. According to Pliny it was a frontier town of India and was connected by a road from central Asia crossing Bactria, Kapisa, Puskalavati and so across the Indus and Ohind to Taxila. According to Hiuen Tsang it was 14 or 15 li in circuit and most populous. The Sanskrit word Puskala is still represented by the modern Pekhely or Pakholi in the neighbourhood of Peshawar. It has been identified with modern Charsadda or Charsada which is one of a group of small towns beside the multiple courses of Swat river a little above the junction of the Swat with the Kabul river.Other identify it with modern Prang and Charsadda, 17 miles north-east of Peshawar on Swat river. It was situated to the west of the Indus and together with Taksasila came under the Sakas during the rule of Manes. Kanishka's son used to live here.

## Taksasila

It was also known as Bhadrasila and later on came to be known at Taksasila because it was here that the head of the king Chandraprabha was cut by a begger Brahmin. The city was rich, prosperous, populous and 12 Yojanas in length and breadth. It was situated to the north of the Himalayas. Bharata gave it to his son Taksa. Earliest known reference to it is found in the Ganapatha of Panini's Astadhyayi. It figures prominently both in the Buddhist and Jain stories. From the Divyavadana it seems that the city was included in the empire of Bindusara of Magadha. It is known as Tezila to Ptolemy. According to Arrian it was a great, wealthy and populous city. Strabo and Hiuen Tsang praise its fertile soil. Pliny gives its location on a level where the hills sank down to the plains.About eighty years after its submission to Alexander it was conquered by Ashoka. It was not the capital of Gandhara country in his time and his Rock Edict V informs us that Gandhara was not in his dominion proper, but Kalinga Edict I clearly states that it was under his direct control. Ptolemy, too, states that the Gandarai country was situated to the west of Indus with its capital at Proklais i.e. Puskaravati.

The city of Taksasila has been identified with Taxila in the Rawalpindi distt. of Pakistan. The site of Taxila is found near Shah-Dheri, just a mile to the north-east of Kala-Ka-Sarai, in the extensive ruins of a fortified city. The distance from Shah-Dheri to Ohind is 35 miles and from Ohind to Hasht-nagar another 38 miles, making 74 miles in all which is 10 miles in excess -of the distance between Taksasila and Puskalavati as recorded by Pliny. Cunningham suggests that to remove the discrepancy the 60 miles of Pliny should be read 80 miles equivalent to 73.5 English miles or within half a mile of actual distance between the two places.St. Martin placed Taksasila at Hassan-Abdal which is 8 miles north-west of Shah-Dheri.

References to Taksasila are found at several places in Buddhist works. It was associated with Mahavira, the founder of Jainism also. It was a great centre of learning.

In the early part of the 2nd cent. B.C. it became a province of the Graceo-Bactrian empire and then it came in the hands of the Indo-Scythians. In Ashoka's time it was feudatory to him. The evidence of Kalinga Edict I shows that it was under his direct control.

## Yavana

The Yavanas were a north-western people living beyond the western border of Bharata, but Brahma Purana places them in the centre on the Vindhyas..» They can be traced in Indian literature and Inscriptions from the 3rd Century B.C. to 2nd cent. A.D. . They are the most esteemed of foreigners but all Yavanas are regarded as sprung from Sudra female and Ksatriya males. Panini gives a form Yavanam, a feminine of Yavaria and Katyayana makes a distinction between Yavani and Yavanam.The Ramayana makes mention of the mixed hordes of Sakas and Yavanas. Sugriva places them in between Kurus, Madras and the Himalayas. The Brhatsamhita regards them as Miecchas. According to the Mahabhasya, Saketa or Ayodhya as well as Madhyamika (near Chitor) were beseiged by a certain Yavana. According to the Mahabharatathey were the descendants of Turvasu. Primarily they belonged to the Ksatriya stock but were later on degraded and despised as Miecchas due to their bad actions. They had been placed in the west by Mahabharata and are seen as associated with other northern people such as Kamboja, Gandhara Kirata, Barbaras Haihayas and Talajanghas. The Purarias associate them with Sakas, Kambojas, Pahlavas, Paradas, Gandharas, Tusaras, Barbaras, Cinas, Sulikas, Daradas, Khasas, Lampakas, Ketas and Kiratas.

The Buddhists associate them with the Kambojas. According to them they were a prospering tribe in the time of Buddha and Assalayana and their territory was regarded as a fit place for Nirvana. They have been mentioned as loma in the Nagh-i-Rustum Ins. of Darius. The evidence of the coins, similar to those of the earliest type of Athens which are collected from the north-west frontiers of India, goes to prove the existence of a pre-Alexanderian Greek colony.

They are referred to in Ashoka's Rock Edicts V and XIII, Nagarjunikonda Inscription of Virapuru-shadatta and Nasik Cave Inscription of Vasisthaputra PulumayT. They occupied a territory in the vicinity of Gandhara on the western border of his dominion but were outside India in Ashoka's time. During his time a Yavanaraja named Tushaspa ruled as governor of Surastra with his capital at Girlnagara. V. Smith argues that the form of the name shows that Yavanaraja must be a Persian, but Raychaudhuri holds that if Greeks and other foreigners took Hindu names, why not some of them assume Iranic appelation and then there is no reason to believe that Tushaspa was not a Greek but a Persian. After the death of Ashoka, a Yavana army crossed the Hindukush, the northern frontier of Ashoka's kingdom, on the ruins of which an Indo-Greek kingdom arose. The Yugapurana section of the Gargisamhita points to the decline of the Maurya power in the Madhyadesa, and so does the Mahabhasya of Patanjali.

The Malavikagnimitram of Kalidasa refers to a conflict between the Sunga prince and the Yavanas on the southern bank of the Sindhu. The Yavanas' progress to the interior of India was thwarted by the Sungas. In the west, they were defeated by the rising powers of Andhras and Satavahanas of Deccan. The Nasik Cave Ins. of GautamTputra Satakarm informs us of the destruction of Sakas, Yavanas and Pahlavas at his hands. In the north-western India they were finally swept away by the onrush of the Parthians as we learn from the Chinese sources.

Usually the word 'Yavana' in mediaeval Indian literature was used as a synonym of Mieccha and indicated any foreigner but by the early centuries of Christian era it denoted the Greeks only. The 'Yona' subject of Ashoka seems to have lived in the district around Alexandria near Kabul. They graudally became Indianised by adopting Indian names, religious beliefs and customs and were ultimately absorbed in the Indian population.

## Kamboja

Though there is no direct reference to them in the Vedic literature, yet the Rgveda mentions a sage Kamboja Upamanyu who may be the father of the teacher of Kamboja Aupamanyavah of the Vamsa Brahmana of the Samaveda. Panini, Patanjali and Manu mention them.

Previously they were ranked high among the Ksatriya tribes but later on when their country was overrun by barbarious hordes, they lost touch of Brahmanism. As a result they were looked down upon as one of the barbarious people and were ranked with Yavanas and were degraded to the rank of the Sudra people by Sagara.

In the Ramayana Kambojas are associated with Yavanas, Sakas and Himvat region. The Mahabharata associates them with the northern people like Yavanas, Sakas, Daradas Cinas, Khasas, Vatadhanas Madras, Kekayas, Gandharas,Pulindas and Sindhus, with eastern people like Kalingas, Salvas and Malavas, with central people like Matsyas, Kurus and SQrasenas, with Trigarttas living on the Vindhya plateau, and with southern people like Colas, Pandyas, and Andhras which shows that though originally a north-westernpeople they had spread far and wide. The Puranas associate them with the Talajanghas and Haihayas,and place them in the north, north-west, south and south-west. The country was known for its best species of horses.

Raghu is said to have met them after defeating the Hunas on the banks of Vanksu. During the reign of Ashoka they lived on the border of his empire. In the Saktisamgamatantra the Kamboja country is described as extending from Pancala and lying to the south-east of the Mieccha country.

References to them are found in the Buddhist and Jain works also. They are known as Kambu in Hiuen Tsang's work and Kambujiya in old Persian inscriptions.

They were a north-western people and occupied the territory north-west of Indus. Pargiter had placed them in the extreme north of Punjab beyond Indus. Wilson places them in Arachosia.At another place they are associated with the mountains of Gazni in Afganistan, especially its eastern part. Rhys Davids places their capital at Dvaraka. But this Dvaraka was different from the one in Gujarat. Dr. S.K. Aiyangar and Banerjee place them in modern Sindh and Gujarat and place their capital at Dvaraka (in Gujarat). Raychaudhuri on the basis of a passage in the Drona Parva of Mahabharata thinks that Rajapura was their homeland. Their association with the Gandharas in ancient literature and edicts of Ashoka helps us in identifying this Rajapura with Rajapur of Hiuen Tsang which lay to the south or the south-east of Punch. Probably they are Tibetans as the Nepalese traditions apply the name Kambojadesa to Tibet.Smith locates them among the mountains of Tibet or of the Hindukush. Law agrees with Raychaudhuri in placing them beyond the Indus in the western Himalayas. Thus they are said to have occupied the province round about the Rajauri valley (ancient Rajapura) in Kashmir including the Hazara district of the former North-West Frontier Province. They are located near Badakhshan beyond the Hindukush. On the evidence of Rajatarangini, Dr. Moti Chandra places them in Kafiristan, Baikh, Badakhshan and the Pamir region. As the Mahabharata distinguishes between Kambojas and Apara-Kambojas it is possible that there were two Kamboja settlements, one in the Pamir region and the other one in Rajauri in Kashmir.

The Kambojas were defeated by Devapala of Pala dynasty of Bengal in the 9th cent. A.D. but in the latter part of the 10th cent. the rule of the Pala kings of Bengal was interrupted by the Kambojas. It seems that the Kambojas first tri°d to conquer Gauda during the reign of Devapala but were pushed back at that time. The Kambojas again attacked north Bengal in the 10th cent A.D. The present inhabitants of Koch, Mech and Palia have descended from them. Mahipala I expelled the Kambojas and regained his ancestoral kingdom about 978-980 A.D.

## Saka

The Sakas were previously ksatriyas but were later on degraded by Sagara. The Vayu Purana, too, regards them as Ksatriyas and places them in the north. Perhaps they lived in the neighbourhood of Kashmir. Later on they appear to have moved southwards for we see that they are placed in the central region, in the south as well as in the south-west.

The Mahabharata associates them with many tribes such as Videhas, Magadhas, Pundras, Bhargas, Kiratas, Nisadas, Nisadhas, Anarttas, Daradas, Malavas, Barbaras, Cinas, Odras, Pahlavas, Gandharas, Yavanas, Kambojas, Khasas, Salvas, Matsyas, Kurus, Pulindas, Andhras, Surasenas, Madras and Kekayas. In the Puranas they are mentioned along with Yavanas, Paradas, Kambojas, Pahlavas, Haihayas, and Talajanghas.They are said to have been defeated by Kekayas.

They were bounded on the west by the Sogdianoi along their eastern side, on the north by Scythia along the line parallel to the river Jaxartes, on the east in like manner by Scythia along the meridian lines prolonged from thence and through the adjacent range of mountains called Askotangkas as far as the station at Mt. Imaos whence traders start on their journey to Sere.... and through Imaos it ascends to the north as far as the limit of the country... and on the south by Imaos itself along the line adjoining the limits that have been stated. They were nomads and had no towns but dwelt in woods and caves. They were mountaineers of Kafiristan, Badakhshan, Shighun, Roshan Baltistan or little Tibet. The name exists even to this day being preserved in Soghd, the country lying along the river Kohik from Bokhara eastward to Samarakand.The Periplus mentions the district of Scythia from which flows down the river Sinthus and its capital Minnagar.

The Sakas were a well known people who had left their traces in Indian history about 165-160 B.C.. At that time there were momentous movements of nomadic tribes in central Asia. The Scythians, originally a tribe of central Asian nomads, appear to have founded various settlements in different regions in very ancient times. In the days of Darius, the Archamenid king of Persia (B.C. 522-486), the Sakas lived beyond Sogdiana in the vast plains of Syr Darya of which modern capital is the town of Turkestan. In the records of the early emperors of the Archamenid dynasty of Persia, reference is made to no less then three settlements of the Sakas who were the subjects of Archamenid, (1) Saka Tigrakhanda (with pointed helmets) who were supposed to have lived in the plains of Jaxartes or Syr Darya of which the capital is the city of Turkestan, (2) Saka Haumavonga who have been identified by Thomas with the Scythian settlements of Drangiana in the Helmund valley which afterwards came to be known as Sakastan (modern Seistan), (3) Saka Taradarya (over or beyond the sea) who are believed to have lived in the Russian steppes to the north of the Black sea.

The Yuchches, dislodged from' their position in north-west China were forced to migrate northwards. During the course of their movements they came across the Sakas who were living to the north of the Jaxartes. The Sakas, who were pushed southwards, swooped down to Bactria and the Parthian kingdom between 140-120 B.C. The Bactrian monarchy which had already become weak by foreign wars and internal divisions easily submitted to Sakas. Then they pressed southwest. In the following struggle, Parthians were at first defeated but later on in 123-88 B.C. they regained their power and the Sakas had to divert eastward and finally entered the Sindhu valley through northern passes. Probably some of the Saka chieftains entered the Parthian army and came to the eastern Iran in the train of Parthian governors of those regions.

The Saka occupation of the western part of the northern India was principally the work of the Sakas of eastern Iran who established themselves in the valley of the lower Sindhu in first cent B.C.. They probably also penetrated as far east as Kathiawad and adjoining regions and continued to dominate over considerable part of the western India as late as the time of early emperors of the Gupta family till Chandragupta finally annexed their dominions to the Gupta empire about 390 A.D.. The inscriptions at Mathura and Nasik prove that they extended their sway as far as the Yamuna in the east and the Godavari in the south and destroyed the power of the Mitras of Mathura and Satavahanas of Pithan. Some of them seems to have penetrated to the far south of India.

A Nagarjunikonda Ins. refers to a Saka and his sister. They are mentioned in the Prasastis of Gautami-putra Satakarni and Samudragupta. The princes bearing Saka names are mentioned in several inscriptions discovered in Taxila, Mathura and western India. The nomenclature of early Sakas in India shows an admixture of Scythian, Parthian and Iranian elements.

According to Rahula Samkrityayana the Sakas originally belonged to Khasas. The word Khasa becomes Sakha or Saka by interchanging the words.

They were nomads and had not'only chiefs and kings but their army also in the form of Urdu clan. In due course they extended in the plains as Rajputs, Gujaras, Jats and Ahiras. Some of them were forced to take refuge in the Himalayas when they were attacked and destroyed by Guptas and Huns. The Kushana kings who were an offshoot of Sakas were greatly infuenced by Hinduism. The Kushana king Kanishka had been the second Ashoka of Buddhism. His successors went even further. We find not only their names such as Vasudeva but the signs on their coins are a definite proof of their being speedily Hinduized.

Their relation with Kumaun and Garhwal is proved by the images of Sun. They were a great devotee of Sun. The usage of Saka Samvat in hilly regions and the relations between the royal families of Garhwal and Kumaun with Shalivahana is a strong evidence of their relation with Kumaun and Garhwal.

## Sindhu - Sauvira

The earliest known reference to SauvTras is found in the Baudhayana Dharmasutras where they are branded as impure and as living outside the limits of Aryandom proper. Sauvira was the country of Suvira, son of Sibi, who is called both Suvira and Sauvira. Arthasastra refers to Sauvira. Sometimes they are mentioned as separate tribes and sometimes as one, which shows that they were two separate tribes who merged to form the Sindhu-Sauvira people. They have been placed in the west (sometimes extreme west), north, and in the centre to the west of the Pancalas. During the epic age they were associated with the Surastras, Cinas, Gandharas and Kasmiras. The Ramayana associates them with Surastras. The- Puranas associate them with the Gandharas» Madras, Hunas, Matsyas, Salvas and the people of Sakala etc.,

The name 'Sindhu-Sauvira' suggests that they occupied the region between the Indus and Jhelum.The river Devika is said to have flowed through the SauvTra country. The name Saindhava itself suggests that the country was situated near the river Sindhu. The evidence of Bhagavata Puranasuggests that they had occupied the territory along the Indus reaching the desert in the east beyond which lived the Abhiras and Matsyas.

References to them are found in the Buddhist literature also. They had their capital at Roruka which has been identified with modern Rohri originally called Rur. According to the Saktisamgamatantra the country of SauvTra lay to the west of Surasena and east of Kanthaka i.e. it was situated to the east of lower Indus and included Multan in the north, and the Sindhudesa lay to the west of lower Indus.

Alberuni identified SauvTra with Multan and Jahrawar and Rapson places them in the modern Sindh (Pakistan). Cunningham identifies them with Eder, a district in Gujerat at the head of the Gulf of Cambay. Rhys Davids locates them in the north of Kathiawar along the Gulf of Cutch. According to Bhagavanlal Indraji Sindhu was equavalent with modern Sindh and Sauvira lay in the upper part of Sindh, the capital of which was Dattamitri. The country of Sindh lay to the west and SauvTra to the east of lower Indus.

## Pulinda

Perhaps the Pulindas had more than one settlement. The Aitareya Brahmana associates them with the southern people like Andhra, Sahara and the eastern people like Pundras. Bengal recension of the Ramayana places them both in the south and the north, while the northern recension places them only in the north. The Puranas associate them with the Vaidarbhas, Dandakas, Pundrakas, Keralas, Kalingas, Abhiras, Andhras and Kuntalas etc. and place them in the south. They are associated with other tribes also like the Kiratas, Hunas, Abhiras Sindhus, Karusas, Bhojas, Dasarnas, and Mekalas.During the Mahabharata age they seemed to have spread far and wide for we see that they are mentioned along with several tribes such as Kurus, Pancalas, Salvas, Madreyas, Jangalas, Surasenas, Bodhas, Matsyas, Cedis, Kosalas, Karusas Bhojas, Sindhus, Vindhya-Culikas, Malavas, Kiratas, Angas, Vangas, Pundrakas, Pandyas, Odras, Kambqjas, Sakas and Khasas and are placed in the south.

Kalidasa mentions them but gives no clue to their identification. The Saktisamgamatantra places them to the east of Silahatta and possibly to the south of Kamarupa.

Ptolemy locates them along the banks of Narmada to the frontiers of Larice (Lata or Lar), Khandesh and a part of Gujarat. Greeks called them Agriophagoi indicating that they subsisted on raw flesh, roots and wild fruits.

They were an aboriginal hill tribe usually connected with the Vindhyan range. They indicated the people of the deserts along the Indus and are met with many other places, especially in the mountains, and forests across central India, the haunts of Bhils and Gondas. The name was later applied to mountaineers in general and still later applied to any aboriginal people. Yule placed them to the north-east of the Gulf of Cutch lying between Khatriaioi in the north and Larike. Pargiter noticed several branches of the Pulinda tribes i.e. (1) western branch (2) Himalayan branch related to the Kiratas and Tanganas and (3) a southern branch. The one referred to here seems to be that living in the hills of Assam, more probably in the land of Paliyas or Rajbamsi of northern Bengal.

The capital of southern Pulindas was Pulindanagara which lay to the south-east of Dasarna in Vidisa or Bhilsa region and may have been identical with Rupanath. In Ashoka's time they formed a group of vassal tribes within his dominion which extended upto the Pennar river in the Nellore district in the south.

## Kulinda

They are regarded to be the same as Pulindas.In the Mahabharata they are seen as associated with the Kundalas, Musakas, Paradas, Khasas, Tanganas and Paratanganas. The Puranas place them in the north-west and to the north-east of Pancala. The Visnu Dharmottara Purana records further extension of Kunindas towards west between Vipasa and Ravi. The Mahabharata refers to Pulindas and Kulindas as seperate people. The Vanaparva of the Mahabharata speaks of all the countries of Kulindas suggesting that they were distributed over different countries or that they were a composite people consisting of different tribes. The passages in the Sabhaparva of the Mahabharata indicate that they occupied the territory along the southern slopes of the Himalayas from Punjab to Nepal and were associated with the Kiratas.

They were aboriginal tribe and were considered as impure. The Ramayana associates them with the Sabaras and locate them somewhere in central India.

It was visited by Hiuen Tsang who calls it Kin-lu-to. The Kulindrine of Ptolemy included, the region of the lofty mountains, where in Vipasa, Yamuna and Ganga have their source. The name still exists under the slightly modified form of Koluta. They were probably identical with the Kunindas, a tribe known from coins, and located in the west Punjab along with the Malavas, Yaudheyas,' Arjunayanas, Udumbaras, Kulutas and Uttamabhadras. They occupied the narrow strip pf land at the foot of the Siwalik hills between Jamuna and Sutlej and the territory between the upper course of Beas and Sutlej. The conquest of Arjuna in the Mahabharata shows that the Kulindas extended further east along the southern slopes of the Himalayas from Kulu to eastward of Nepal.Cunningham has identified Kulindrine with the kingdom ofJalandar (Jullundhar). Dey locates them in the Bunder Punchha range of the Himalayas where Yamuna has got its source, that is why Yamuna is named KalindT. The entire tract of the land lying between the upper portion of the Ganges and Sutlej was called Kulindas, and included Garhwal together with the district of Saharanpur. They are modern Kunets occupying Kullu and Simla hills and slopes below along both sides of Sutlej.

## Sudras

They are almost invariably associated with the Abhiras, Daradas and Kasmlras and considered to be outside the pale of Aryandom. They were Aparanta people who are placed in the north-west, in the centre and in the south. The Mahabharata locates them in the region where Sarasvati vanishes into the desert.

The country named Sudraka is Ouxydrakai of Greek historians. Alexander's historians place them in the western region of Assam. The Sogdi of Ptolemy and Sudraki of Pliny lay between the Indus and Sutlej above the junction of five rivers near Mithankot and included the Montgomary District.Their capital was Uch also called Kachchee.

## Culika: Sulika.

The Culikas are associated with the Vidarbhas and are placed, in the north. The river Caksu flowed through the country of Sulikas. The Brhatsamhita locates them in the north west.Pargiter places them about the north-western region and associates them with the Pisacas. He quoted passages from the Matsya, the Markandeya and the Vayu Puranas in his support. They were the Sogdiana living to the north of the Oxus in Turkestan.

The Haraha Ins. of Maukharis make a reference to the Sulikas who are identified by some with the Calukyas but that does not agree with the Puranic description. They are placed on Snsaila in Andhra and Sule region in central Asia.

## Carmakhandika

They were a north-western Ksatriya people and were probably connected with the Puranic river Charmanvati. The Brhatsmhita mentions them as a north-western people and refers to an island named Carma-dvipa in the south-western direction.Pargiter has suggested their identification with Samarkand.

Pliny mentions a king of a people so called 'Charmaarum rox'. M.V.De.St. Martin finds a relation between them and the Chamars of the present day. Sircar locates them in Samarkand.

## Kekaya

According to Puranas they descended from the non-Aryan Anu tribe or the Anavas who in the Vedic times dwelt on the banks of Parusnl and occupied the Punjab region. The earlier known reference to them is found in the Satapatha Br. and Chandogya Upanisad. The Mahabharata associates them with the Pancalas, Matsyas, Srnjayas, Cedis, Kasi, Kosalas, Vangas, Karusas, Trigarttas, Kasmiras, Yaudheyas, Salvas, Ambasthas, Avanti, Valhlkas, and Magadhas. The Ramaya'na located them beyond the river Vipasa and their territory is said to have extended upto the borders of ancient GandharaThe Puranas locate them in the north, north-west, more precisely to the north-west of Pancala. The Kavyamimamsa lo'cates them in the north along with Sakas, Hunas Kambojas and Vahlikas. The Bhagavata Purana associates them with Kuru, Vidarbha, Yadu and Kosala. Kaikeyi, the wife of king Virata was so called after her Janapada's name. One of the eight wives of Krsna was a Kekaya princess. They were closely associated with Madrakas as both Kekaya and JMaaraKas were son of Sibi. The capital of the Kekayas has been placed beyond river Sudama which flowed at some distance west-ward from Vitasta. Their capital was Rajagrha, Girivraja and Seyaviya.Cunningham has identified Girivraja with Girjak or Jalalpur on the Jhelum and locates them in the district of Shahpur in Punjab and the country between the Bias and Sutlej. Pargiter, too, places them in Punjab. The Sadpancasaddesavibhagalocates them between Brahmaputra and Kamarupa and may indicate the land of Kukis in Assam and Manipura. A branch of the Kekaya might have migrated to the south also, for we see that they had a settlement there in 5th cent A.D.

## Tusara

The Vayu associates them with'the Gandharas, Paradas,. Pahlavas, Yavanas, Sakas, Barbaras, Pulindas, Daradas and Andhakas and places them in the north while the Visnudharmottara Purana locates them in the north-west. The Mahabharata associates them with Sakas, Daradas, Pahlava etc.

and regards them as impure. The river Caksu is said to have flowed through their territory. During the epic age they lived in the north and their country was crossed by the Pandavas while coming from Gandhamadana mountain to Dvaitavana.

The country is known as Baikh and Bactria to the Greeks and as Tukharistan to the Arab geographers. Both Ptolemy and Periplus locate them in the province round Bactria. The Tu-Kho-Lo of Hieun Tsang was above 1000 li north to south and 3000 li east to west. It reached to the Ta'ung Ling on the west to Persia, on the south to great snow mountains and on the north to the Iron Pass. The river Oxus flowed through the middle of it from east to west.

They lived to the north of Hindukush. Eitel identifies it with Yue-she, the country of the Indo-Scythians of the Greeks, and Tartar of the Chinese writers, who destroyed the Bactrian kingdom in 126 B.C. and finally conquered Punjab and other parts of India.

## Tangana

The Tanganas were a Ksatriya-Vaisya clan living in the Himalayas in the north in the neighbourhood of Kasmira. In the Mahabharata they are associated with the Paratanganas, Vahlikas, Colas, Pandyas, Khasas, Paradas and Pulindas, and are located near Stri-rajya which has been identified with Garhwal Kumaun region. They were intermixed with Kiratas and Pulindas for they all lived in the kingdom of one king Sabhahu in the middle portion of Himalaya.

Pliny locates them between Tons and Sharda (Kali) i.e. Garhwal and Kumaun. According to Ptolemy, they along with Kiratas occupied the region from Yamuna to Sharda, and the country stretching from the Ramaganga river to the upper Sarayu.

It seems that there were two branches of Tanganas because the Padma Purana refers to both Tangana and Apara-Tangana. They have been regarded as the Paratangana of the northern and central Asia and are said to have occupied the northern Garhwal and Tibet. The ancient Tanganas and Paratanganas lived in the upper valley of the Alakananda and Mandakim which was previously occupied by the Kiratas. They seemed to have their headquarters at Tanganapura near Joshimatha in Garhwal Distt., Uttar Pradesh. There is a Tanganachatti 18 miles upward from Chamoli and 15 miles below Joshimatha. These Tanganas were Khasas and not Kiratas. Even today a sub-divison of Rajput in Garhwal and Almora is known As Tanganiya.

Regarding them St. Martin writes They are represented in the Mahabharata as placed between Kiratas and Kulindas in highlands protecting the plains of Kosala on the north. They were one of the barbarous tribes which the Brahmanic Aryans drove back into the Himalayas or towards the Vindhyas. It is mainly in Vindhya region that the descendants of Tanganas are now to be found. One of the Rajput tribe now known as Tank or Tonk is settled in Rohilkhand, the very district where Mahabharata locates them. The Rajputs extended westwards to a part of Doab and even as far as Gujarat, but it is in the race of Dangayas which has spread over the entire length of the Vindhya mountain and adjacent territory from the southern borders of the ancient Magadha to the heart of Malava and to the north of lower Narmada and which is subdivided into clans and known according to the district inhabited as Dhangis Dogas etc. that we must search for the point of departure of the family and its primordial type. This type which the mixture of Aryan blood has modified and ennobled in the tribes called Rajputa preserve its aboriginal type in the mass of mountain tribes and this type is purely Mongolian, a living commentary on the appellation of Mieccha or barbarian, which the ancient Brahmanic book apply to Tangana.

## Darada

The Daradas were a Vaisya-Sudra clan in northern India as well as the north-eastern India.They were formerly Ksatriyas but were later on degraded. They were associated with Sudras, Abhiras, Kasmiras, Sakas, Pahlavas and Malavas,with Abhisaras and Tanganas and with Kambojas as well. In the Matsya Purana the Daradas have been placed on the river Sindhu. It may be identified with Dardistan on the upper bank of the Indus, their capital being Daratpuri identified by Stein with Gurej. Their seat which does not seem to have changed ever since the time of Herodotus, extend from Chitral and Yasin across the Indus region of Gilgit, Chilas, and Bunj to the Kishanganga valley in the immediate north of Kashmir. They were related to Ladakh region from very early times. Many names used in Ladakh are Dard words. Dard of this area were immigrants from Gilgit.

## Ambastha

During the Mahabharata age they were associated with the Kosalas, Sivis, Trigarttas, Malavas, Pahlavas, Salvas, Kekayas, Kalingas, Magadhas, Paundras, Madrakas and Gandharas.They have been located in the south-west also.

During the Brahmana period they lived in Punjab. Ptolemy knew them as Ambastai who lived in the east of the country of the Paropanisadai.Greek historians of Alexander mention Abastanoi as living on the northern part of Sindh at the time of Alexander and also on the lower Akesines.According to the Smrti writers they were an offshoot of mixed Brahmana and Vaisya parentage. They occupied the lower Chinab region on the eastern border of Punjab. Later on they migrated to some place near the Mekala mountain.

## Cina

The Cinas were originally Ksatriyas who became degraded due to their omission of sacred rites. They were called Miecchas. The Mahabharata associates them with the Yavanas, Kambojas, Kiratas, Sakas, Odras, Suviras and Saurastras. They have been placed in the north, in the extreme east and in the west as well. The Markandeya Purana places them outside the boundaries of Bharata. The Sadapancasaddesavibhaga places them to the southeast of Manasesa. The country to the south-east of Manasa is Tibet which once formed a part of the dominions of the Chinese kings. Cina, according to Pargiter, comprised the country of Tibet along the whole range of the Himalayas.

Evidently they were the people of Cina but the name here does not refer to their original country. Perhaps these Cinas were those who settled down along the Indian side of the Himalaya mountains from the north-west to the extreme east.

## Madra

The people of Madras were ancient Ksatriya tribe of Vedic fame. Though the early Samhitas do not mention them yet the Vamsa Brahmana of Samaveda refers to a teacher named Madragara Saungayami who seems to be a Madra. The Satapatha Brahmana states that the sages of northern India went to the country of Madras to have education. The Aitareya Brahmana places northern Madra beyond the Himalayas in northern regions close to Uttarakuru.

Though Madra people were held in esteem during the Buddhist period, yet they are not mentioned in the list of sixteen Mahajanapadas.

During the epic period they occupied the district of Sialkot between Chinab and Ravi. They are mentioned along with the Trigarttas, Kekayas, Sauviras, Pulindas, Kiratas, Angas, Vangas, Pundras, Pandyas Odras, Andhrakas, Kasmiras, Sakas, Yavanas, Kambojas, Surasenas, Daradas, Kekayas, Kalingas, Ambasthas and Gandharas. The Puranas place them in the east, in the north, on the Himalayas and to the north of Pancala. The Nilamata Purana places them in the neighbourhood of Kashmir. In the Padma Purana Laksamana is said to have installed his son in Madra-desa.

The capital of the Madra country was Sakala(modern Sialkot in Pakistan). The Vamana Purana says that the region between Sakala and Saurastra was a desert. The Sadpancasaddesavibhaga places them in the south-east between Virata (Alwar, Bharatapur and Jaipur region) and Pandyas, to the south of Kamboja and to the west of Indraprastha.

Cunningham identifies Sakala with Sangala Wala Tiba to the west of Ravi, with Jhang district of Pakistan which is said by some to have extended from Bias to Jhelum, and by others only up to the Chinab. Dey places them in modern Sialkot and surrounding region between Ravi and Chinab, by the side of the territory of the Yaudheyas. Smith places them in the central part -of Punjab. At the time of Alexander's expedition it came under his sway and was placed under the Satrapa of the adjoining territory. Menander ruled it in about 78 A.D.. In the 4th cent. A.D. it paid taxes to Samudragupta, and in the 6th cent A.D. it was conquered by Mihirakula.

## (C) Southern States

## Kuntala

The Kuntala country was so called because it resembled the hair „of the goddess of earth. They have been placed variously in central India, 'southern India and in Aparanta. The Dasakumara-carita mentions it as a dependant kingdom of Vidarbha, but in the 10th century A.D. the town of Vidarbha is said to be situated in Kuntaladesa.

It was known to Ptolemy as Kandaloi and to Yule as Gondaloi. The Mysore Inscriptions show that it extended in the north of Mysore. The Kundalas occupied a region almost having the same boundary as the Kanarese district. The country of ' the Kuntalas of the Madhyadesa should be identified with the region near Chunar' The Kuntala of central India and of west had no historical importance but those of south appear to have risen to a considerable importance.

Literary and epigraphic references have now proved beyond doubt that there were several families of the Satakarnis of the Deccan and one or more of these families ruled over Kuntala of the Kanarese distt. before the Kadambas. From some of the Mysore inscriptions we come to know that it included the southern part of the former Bombay Presidency and the northern portion of Mysore and more particularly as the country between the rivers Bhima and VedavatI, bounded by the Ghats on the west and including Shimoga and Chitraldurg districts of the earstwhile Mysore state, Bellary, Dharwar, Bijapur and the adjacent tracts of Hyderabad. Once it was under the Nandas. Later on the Vakataka king Harishena conquered it along with Lafa, Avanti, Andhra and Kalinga. The Talagunda Pillar Ins. and the Rewah stone Ins. knew the Kuntala as the country of later Chalukyas. In later inscriptions it is called Karnatakadesa and Karnata. At the time of the Chalukyas, it was bounded on the north by Narmada, on the south by Tungabhadra, on the west by Arabian sea and on the east by Godavari and eastern Ghats. Its capitals were Nasik and Kalyana at different periods. In later times i.e. llth and 12 centuries the southern Maratha country, the adjoining Kanarese distt. and north of Mysore country was called Kuntala. Sircar has identified them with the Kadambas of Vanavasi, whose territories comprised the north Kanara distt., parts of Mysore, Belgaun and Dharwar (Karnataka). In the later Kadamba Inscriptions, the Kadamba family is represented as the ruler of the Kuntala country the land round the capital city of Banavasi in north Kanara distt.. V.V.Mirashi placed them between Bhima and Vedavati and says that their territories comprised the Kanarese distt. of Bombay, Madras and Mysore states and also perhaps a part of Maharastra with Vidarbha having its capital at Pratisthana on Godavari. Smith locates them in western Deccan and in the north of Mysore. Pargiter says that there were three people of the same name, one in Dekhan, second near Chunar and third in the west.

## Mulika

The Mulikas were a small tribe which was closely related with Asmakas. The Puranic tradition holds that both were scions of Iksavaku family and Mulaka, the originator of Mulaka race, was the son of the king Asmaka. Thus though they were closely related yet they were two different people and lived in seperate regions. In early Pali literature Assaka is distinguished on the one hand from Mulaka in the north and on the other from Kalinga in the east. Godavari formed the border line between the two. In later times Mulakas were included in the Assakas. In the Sonadanda Jataka the Assaka country is associated with Avanti. This contiguity according to Bhandarkar can only be explained if it is assumed that the Mulaka was included in Assaka and the latter was contiguous with Avanti. They were distinguished as late as second quarter of the second contury A.D. Dey has regarded them to be one and the same.

They were situated to the south of Avanti and Bhattaswamin, the commentator of the Arthasastra regarded the Mulaka country to be identical with Maharastra. The land of Mulakas lay beyond the hills running from Daulatabad to Jalna, occupying parts of the low land that terminate in the valley of Godavari. Their capital was Patitthana i.e. Paithan on the northern bank of the Godavari in the Aurangabad Distt. and Hyderabad.

## Bhogavardhana

The Bhogavardhanas appear as Bhogavadham in Barhut Inscription. The name seems to do some thing with the Bhokardan Taluk of the Aurangabad District of Maharastra.

## Maharastra

The name Maharastra has been derived from the name of their kings i.e. Ratthis or Rastrikas. During Buddha's time it was called Asmaka or Assaka. Its ancient capital Pratisthana i.e. Paithan on Godavari was the capital of the junior princes of Andhrabhritya family of the Puranas, also called Satakarnis or Salivahanas. The Kamasutra commentary locates Maharastra between the Narmada and Karnata and the Padma Purana includes it in the Gurjaradesa. The Saktisamga-matantra gives its extent from Tryamvaka (Traya-mvekasvara silvaliriga near Nasik) to Karnata comprising UjjayinT, MarjaratTrtha and Kolapur NivasinT (goddess Mahalaksmi of the great shrine at Kolhapur).

According to the Saupara and Maski inscriptions it formed a part of Ashoka's kingdom and was called Maharat, where Dharmarakshita was sent by Ashoka to spread Buddhism.

Hiuen Tsang gives the extent of Mo-Ho-La-Cha as 5000 li in circuit with its capital of 30 li round. It was situated to the west of a great river. The soil was rich, fertile and regularly cultivated and the climate was hot. Ptolemy and Periplus give the extent of Ariake (Maharastra) as 6000 li which had its capital on a great river.

The Aihole Inscription records its three divisions each one being called Maharastra in the 7th cent. A.D.\\ According to Raychaudhuri the three divisions of Maharastra may refer to the three traditional 'Thadis' into which the Maratha country was divided namely Gangathadi (on the banks of Godavari) Bhima thadi (banks of the Bhima) and Nira-thadi (banks of Nira, a tributary of Bhima).The Maharastra is the Deccan in the narrowest sense, watered by the upper Godavari and lying between this river and Krishna. Its ancient capitals were (1) Pratisthana i.e. Paithan on the Godavari, (2) Kalyana on the eastern shore, of the Bombay port, (3) Vatapi of the early Chalukya kings (4) and Badami- the real capital in Hiuen Tsang's time.

## Pulinda

The Pulindas were a non-Aryan people connected with the Sabaras. Formerly they belonged to the Ksatriya stock but were later on degraded. The Puranas locate them both in the northern and western region. The Padma Purana mentions them as living on the Kailasa mountain, while the Vamana Purana locates them on the Himavan and Kal'anjara mountains. The Pulindas of the south seem to be an offshoot of the northern ones. The Matsya Purana suggests that they lived on the banks of river Haimavati (Airavati) in the Himalayan region. They appear to have scattered in various parts of India. Ptolemy gives the name of the town of Phullitoe as Agara (Sagara). Ashoka edict No. XIII mentions them under the name Pirinida and Pulida.

Some scholars read it as Panda and regard them to be the same as Pulinda of Aitareya Brahmana. Dr. Raychaudhuri places them in the Vindhya region and their capital not far from Bhilsa. It is identified with Rupanath, the spot where one recension of Minor rock Edicts was found.Bhandarkar accepts the reading of the Girnar copy i.e. Pirinidas and places them in the country occupied by the Barendras i.e. northern and eastern parts of modern Bengal. It included the western portion of Bundelkhand and the Distt. of Sagar. The Kathasaritsagara confounds the Sabaras with Pulindas. The Sahara is the same as Sagar. A branch of this tribe called Podas lived in Bengal. According to the Tara-Tantra Pulindas lay to the east of Silhatta (Sylhat) and to the north of Kamarupa. The Mahabharata records that the Pulindas lived in the region north-west of Haridvara. They have been placed on the Vindhyas near the Narmada but the term 'Pulinda' was used vaguely and sometimes meant Himalayan tribes.

## Sahara

The Sahara people were associated with other non-Aryan tribes such as Andhras, Pundras and Pulindas etc. They were the progeny of the elder son of Visvamitra who was cursed to become the progenitor of such servile races as Andhra, Pundra and Sahara. This implies that they were non-Aryans of the south. The story of the Sahara woman of the Ramayana also proves their habitation in the south. The Mahabharata relates that they were born of the cow named Nandini. Formerly they, too, were Ksatriyas and were later on degraded due to the anger of the Brahmins. During the epic age they lived in the forest region of central India and Deccan.

Ptolemy mentions them as Sabarai and Pliny as Surai. Yule places them further north in Dosarene, towards the territory of Sambhalpur. Cunningham identifies them with aboriginal Savaras or Suras, a wild race living in forests without any fixed habitation. Their country extended upto Pennar river. They were only a single branch of a widely spread race found in large number to the south-west of Gwalior, Marwar and south Rajputana where they are known as Surrins. Sircar takes them to be the Soras of Ganjam and Vishakhapatnam living on the banks of Sabari river in Bastar. They are represented by the Savaralu or Sauras of Vizagapatam hills and the Savaris of the Gwalior region. They were also found in the basin of the Vamsadhara - Parlokimedu in the Kodaka Taluk north of Barhampur in the Ganjam District, in the hills near the Chilka lake.

## Musika
