Perhaps the Muchipa of Sankhayana Srautasutra and the Mutibas of the Aitareya Brahmana are the same as Musikas of the Puranas.Patanjali refers to a people called *Mausikara .who* may be said to have some relation with our Musikas. Ptolemy mentions them under the name Musikanus. Arrian records the influence of Brahmanas over them. They were the main agents in bringing about an uprising of the people against Macedonian invader, but Alexander took them by surprise and they had to yield to him. Strabo gives an interesting account of them.

They lived on the bank of the Must river in Hyderabad. They were probably so called because their territory lay in that portion of the north west trade-route which was known as Musikapatha. Dr. Fleet takes Musika country to be the Malabar coast between Quilon and Cape Comorin. Wilson, too, places them in the southern most part of Malabar coast. Cochin, Travancore and the Pirate coast of Konkan in Maharashtra. Cunningham identifies their territory with Upper Sindh (Pakistan) of which the capital was Alor in Sikhur district. As Strabo also places the Musikanos near Patalena in Sindh (McCrindle: Ancient India as described in Classical literature) there must have been two countries of that name, one in upper Sindh and the other in Malabar coast i.e. Travancore. Arrian refers to Mousikanos as living in the north of Patalians, region of the Indus-delta. In the Mackenzie Mss. Musika is said to be one of the four districts of Malayalam, namely Tuluva, Mushica, Keralam and Cuva. A Musikanagara is referred to in the Hathigumpha inscription of king Kharvela of Kalinga also.

## Vaidarbha

The Vaidarbhas lived in southern India along with Pulindas, Dandakas, Vindhyas, Pusikas and others.

According to the Puranic tradition the eponymous leader of the Vaidarbhas was a Yadava.The Vidarbhas of Vedic fame are mentioned in Mahabhasya, Yoginitantra and Brhatsamhita as well. The Mahabharata which mentions them very frequently connects them with the episode of Nala and Damayanti, a Vidarbha princess. Their first capital was Kundinapura and the second one was Bhojakatanagara founded by Rukmi and which was called Rukminanagara on this very account. The Bhagavata Purana repeatedly mentions Rukmani not by name but as Vaidarbhi. Kalidasa tells us that the Suriga dynasty was founded alongwith the foundation of a new kingdom of Vidarbha. It was ruled at by the Bhoja family and the river Varada was fixed as the boundary between the two kingdoms formed out of Vidarbha. The distance between Anarta and Vidarbha could be covered during a single night using horse as a mode of travelling. The Kavyadarsa of Dandin and the Nasik Cave Inscription also mention them. The Saktisamgamatantra places them to the east of BhadrakaE (Kalika of Ujjain) and west of Ramadurga (former state of this name in the south Maratha country lying between Belgaon and Bijapura). The Siva Purana mentions Vidarbha both as a 'Nagara' and as a visaya'. It was the earliest Aryan kingdom in the southern India. Cunningham has identified it with Berar, Khandesh, part of former Hyderabad state and part of Madhya Pradesh. In ancient times it included the kingdom of former Bhopal state and Bhilsa to the north of Narmada, Rapson, too, identifies it with Berar. Dey takes it to be identical with Bidar or Kundinapura (modern Kundpur) in Amaraoti Distt. on the banks of Wardha river. The city of Bhojakatapura is the modern Bhojapur, ? miles south-east of Bhilsa in Bhopal. According to Sircar the territory of Vidarbha lay on both the banks of the river Varada (modern Wardha-tributary of Godavari) and roughly corresponded to the major part of Berar and the western portion of the Madhya Pradesh. Its capital Kundinapura was situated on the western bank of Wardha in the Chandur Taluk of the Amaraoti Distt. of Berar. The Bhojakatapura is the modern Bhatkuli in the Amaraoti Distt. of Berar. The city of Dasapura has been correctly identified with modern Padampur near Amagaon Railway station in the Bhandara Distt. of Madhya Pradesh. We may, therefore, say that the Vidarbha included at least Amaraoti region in the west and Bhandara region in the east.

## Mahisika

The Mahisikas were formerly of Ksatriya stock, but were later on degraded and expelled from the pale of Ksatriyism by Sagara. They lived in the ancient region of Mahismafi which seems to have been situated on the river Narmada between Rksa and Vindhya mountains and can be identified with modern Mandhata, where flows a river named Mahisiki. The founder of Mahismafi is said to be Mucukunda at one place and Mahismat at another.The Puranas say that its founder belonged to Yadu lineage. It has been identified with either Mahisa Visaya in Mysore or with the country on Narmada of which Mahismafi was the capital. Some identifiy it with Mahesvara on the northern bank of Narmada in the Nimar Distt. of the earstwhile Indore State.V.V. Mirashi has quoted passages from the Ramayana, Mahabharata and Puranas to show that Mahisa was situated in the southern part of Deccan. According to him the royal name Mahisa was probably derived from the country of Mahisika of Mahisamandala where this family was ruling. He further writes, Previously I followed Rapson in supporting that Mahisika was the country round Mahismafi i.e. modern Mandhata in the Nimad distt. of M.P. Since then some coins have been found in the excavations at Kondapur and Maski in Hyderabad. The country under the rule of the Mahisa dynasty seems therefore, to have been the southern portion of the Hyderabad state.... . There is definite epigraphic evidence regarding the existence of a tract of the land associated with Mahisas in the Mysore state which is not very far from the southern portion of Hyderabad. From an Inscription of the Kadamba king Vishnuvarman I (about the end of the 5th Cent. A.D.) found in the Tumkur Taluk of the Tumkur District in the northern part of Mysore, it is clear that the present Tumkur area in the Mysore was known in the 5th cent. A.D. as the Mahisa territory. Mysore must have been derived from the word Mahisika or Mahisa as the original name of Mysore was derived from the words 'Mahisa' and 'Ura' i.e. the city of Mahisas. It is possible that there were more than one settlements of Mahisa people. Mahismati on Narmada seems to have derived its name from Mahisas but there is as yet no genuine evidence to show that the southern part of the former Hyderabad state was known as Mahisa country.

## Pandyas

The southern territory of the Pandyas was named after Pandya, the son of AndTra, and its capital was Madura. The earliest known reference to them is found in the Astadhyayi. Katyayana derives Pandya from Pandu. In the Mahabharata and the Jatakas Pandus are told to be the rulers of the Indraprastha. The Jain legends, too, find a relation between the sons of Pandu and the Pandya country of the south with Mathura or Madhura as its capital. Likewise, the Tamil chronicles connect the two families and say that Madhura at the time of Bharata war was ruled by Babhruvahana, son of Arjuna by the Pandyan princess of Madura. The Pali chronicles of Ceylon invariably represent the Pandyas as Pandu or Pandus. Megasthenes has associated the Pandiyas of the south with Surasena of Madura and Pandus of northern India on the basis of popular etymology. Dr. Bhandarkar says that there was a tribe called Pandu round about Mathura and when a section of them went southwards and were settled there they were called Pandyas. It seems reasonable to assume that the Pandyas, an offshoot of Pandu tribe which lived near Mathura also named their capital as Madhura (Mathura). It is quite natural for the migatory tribes to give their new habitat and places those very place-names which they had in their homeland and which were so dear to them. The Mahabharata associates them with Colas and Keralas and Valmiki speaks of their wealth.

Both Ptolemy and Periplus knew them as Pandion and Madura as Modoura. Strabo, too knew them as Pandion. In the Periplus it is said that Nelkynda lying on the Malabar coast as well as the pearl fishery at Kolkoi belonged to the Pandion. Nelkynda has been identified with Nakkita situated at the junction of Pumpa, Manimala and Achchhankovil. Kolkoi is the same as Korkai of the Tamil tradition which was situated near the mouth of the river TamraparnT within five miles of the sea.At that time it (Pandion) extended beyond the Ghats and included Travancore. The original capital at Kolkai had been removed to Madura later on.

Raghuvamsa refers to them and their capital Uragapura. They are known to Kautilya's Arthasatra also. They are said to be the descendants of the mythical three brothers of Korkoi who respectively founded the Pandya, Cola and Cera kingdoms. In fact they were friendly nations. The Hathigumpha Ins. of Kharavela of Kalinga refers to them. In Ashoka's time they lived outside his empire, and were frontier kings of his dominion.

The Pandya country comprised the Madura and Tinnevelly Distt.. It included the greater portion of the Tinnevelly and extended as far north as to the highlands of the Coimbatore Gap. Their western boundary was the southern range of the Ghats. It had a sea board on the east, extending for some distance along the Sinus Orgolicus (modern Palk's passage). It comprised Travancore also in the first century A.D.. Its capitals at different periods were Uragapura or Uraiyur (modern Trichinopoly), Madhura (modern Madura) and Kolkoi at the mouth of the river Tamraparni now five miles inland.Ancient Madhura was situated at a distance of six miles to the south-east of modern Madura on the northern bank of the river Vaigai. Kolkai is mentioned by Ptolemy and Marcopolo as Kolkhoi and Kael respectively. It was its chief commercial port also. The Pandyas probably had two settlements (1) one including Tinnevelly on the south and extending upto Coimbatore gap and (2) the other including the Mysore state. They ruled the southern extremity of the Indian peninsula along the eastern coast. Their kingdom expanded and shrank. It included Madura, Tinnevelly, Travancore, parts of Coimbatore, Cochin and Ramnad, and was watered by the rivers TamraparnT and Krtamala or Vaigai.

Very little information is available regarding their early history. A very few references in the works of classical writers (like Pliny) and the material available there in help us in gathering their history. From these references we come to know that Nedun - Jeliyan II was their first ruler who made them the leading power of the south. But the supremacy of the south ultimately passed on to the Pallavas.

## Kerala

They, like the Pandyas, were named after Kerala, son of Janaplda or Andira in the lineage of Yayati. They were born of the cow Nandini and were regarded as Miechhas i.e. non-Aryan people. They are said to have occupied the southern territories and the Vindhyan region. They were associated with Pandyas, Colas, Gonardas, Karnatas and Trigarttas.

They are the Sares of Pliny and Cerabothra of Periplus and Ptolemy. During the age of Periplus they were included in the Damirica and denoted the western Tamil kingdom extending from (ape Comorin to Karwar point. Its northern part had then been separated while the southern end came under the Pandya kingdom, leaving Kerala nearly identical with modern Malabar and Cochin Distt.. Its capital was at Karur or Parur opposite Muziris or Cranganore. Ptolemy mentions Cerabothra as ruling in the kingdom of Karoura.

Ceraputra was one of the three lengndary brothers who founded Dravidian kingdoms in the south. Kerala is the Kanarese form of the Tamil Cerala, previously called Ceralam or Cerala-nadu (Mountain range). To the north it had the country of Karnata, which is joined among the Ghats, nearly upon the present Mysore frontiers, stretching from thence eastward it penetrated into the districts of Salem as far as Trichengode; from thence it proceeded southward... to the coast of Travancorc and it included the western coast as high up c Calicut in Malabar. It was the rugged region of the western ghats south of Candragiri river, and comprised the present south Malabar, Cochin, Travancore, Coimbatore and Salem. According to Pargiter they occupied the whole *of* the west coast from Calicut to Cape Comorin They lay to the south ofKupaka (Sahya) extending down to Kannati in central Travancore-Karuna Gapiilli Taluk. To the south of it were the Musikas. The river Periyar flew through it, on which stood ib \>.)pital Vanji near Cochin. The Kongudesa i.e. Coimbatore distt. and the southern part of the Salenv distt. was annexed to it. Originally its capital was Vanji (also called Karoura or Karur), which was situated near Cranganese on the left bank of Amaroti, a tributary of Kaveri-Vanji and which is our present Tir Karur on the Periyar near Cochin. Its later capital was Tiru-Vanjikkalam near the mouth of Periyar.According to the ancient Tamil literature the Chera or Kerala kingdom comprised five districts namely (1) Pooly (the sandy) extending from Agalappula to the north of Ponani river, (2) Kudam (the western) extending from Ponani to the southern most mouth of the Periyar near Ernakulam, (3) Kuddam (the land of lakes) about Kottayam and Quilon, (4) Ven from below Quilon nearly to Cape Comorin and (5) Karka (the rocky) the hill country to the east of Kudam. The second and the thirteenth rock edicts of Ashoka mention them as living on the border outside his empire.

## Cola

The Colas, also called Dravidas are said to have derived their name from the king Cola of the Kanchipura, who was the son of Andira or Janapida, in the lineage of Turvasu - the son of Yayati. They are located alongwith Pandyas and Keralas in the south. If we rely upon the Puranic tradition of their being the descendants of Janapida then they must have been Ksatriyas once in the north having connection with Turvasus. The Vayu Purana and the Brahma Purana relate that they were Ksatriya people who were vanquished by Sagara alongwith Khasas etc.. Pargiter regards their connection with the Sagara story as erroneous.Since in the reference of the Vayu Purana they are connected neither with the Pandya-Kerala nor with other southern tribes, Pargiter suggests that these Colas, who are said to have been vanquished by Sagara were not the southern Colas but rather frontier people called Culikas.

Cola, the Telgu Chola, the Tamil Sora, indicated both the people as well as the country. The Cola rulers are said to have belonged to the Tiraiyar tribe. Their connection with the sea is probably indicated by a reference of Kelian to the realm of the Soras and its chief city. The Chinese writers give the extent of the Chulli-Ye country as 2400 li in circuit. It was then deserted, wild and hot, Ptolemy refers to the kingdom of Sora whom he calls Soringae whose capital was Orthoura. Periplus also notes that the kingdom of Argaru (Uragapura) was included in Damirica.

Ashoka Rock edicts II and XIII mention Pandyas, Ceras and ? olas as friendly people living outside his empire. As Colas are spoken of in the plural in all the versions of Ashoka's edicts, it seems that there were more than «one Cola people.

The Saktisamgamatantra places them between the Dravida and Tailanga. Wilson takes them to be the people of the lower part of the Coromandal coast. Sircar places them in the Tanjaur and Tiruchirapalli distt. Tamil Nadu. According to Raychaudhuri their territory stretched along the eastern coast from the river Pennar to Vellar and on the west reaching to about the borders of Coorg,The tradition holds that the Chola country comprised the land between the two streams having the same name Vallaru in the north and the south, the sea on the east and Kottaikarai in the west. This area includes the districts of Trichinopoly, Tanjore and part of former Pudukkotah state. In the 7th cent A.D. the Cola kingdom flourished in the Telugu districts i.e. portions of Cuddapah, Kurnool and Anantapur.

In ancient times the Tamil kingdom, as Dr. Barnett thinks, extended over the greater part of the modern Tamil Nadu state, its boundary being on the north a line running approximately from Pulicat on the coast to Venkatagiri (Tirupati), on the east the Bay of Bengal, on the south Cape Comorin and on the west the Arabian sea as far north as the white Rock near Badargara to the south of the Mahi. Malabar was included in it. The Cola country was thus watered by Kaveri which is often alluded to and associated with the name of the Colas in the south Indian Mss.. In the 2nd cent A.D. its capital was Uraiyur (Skt. Uragapura, and Orthoura of Ptolemy) on the Kaveri, near Trichinopoly, and KanchTpura Combaconum and Tanjore in the llth cent A.D. The Bhagavata Purana mentions Kanci, along with the river Kaveri. The Padma Purana records two Kanchis, Siva Kanchi and Visnu Kanchi the eastern and western portion respectively of the city. Kanchi is the modern city of Kanjivaram. Their principal port Kaveripattanam or Pugar was situated on the northern bank of Kaveri.

The early history of the Colas is obscure. The limits of their territory differed as their power increased or declined. From the Colas, the supremacy of the south passed to Ceras and later still to the Pandyas. The Cola kingdom merged into the Pandya kingdom and continued so for 570 years. The Pandyas were ousted by the Pallavas, who later on became the suzerain power of the southern India.

## Andhras

They were a rude uncivilized non-Aryan people. The Aitareya Br. mentions them to be the exiled sons of Visvamitra thus proving them to be non-Aryan people who were associated with the other non-Aryan peoples such as Sabaras and Pulindas etc.. They were considered impure and sinful people. The Ramayana connects them with the Godavari. The Anargha Raghava speaks of Saptagodavan to flow near the country of Andhras. The An-ta-lo (Skt. Andhra) was 3000 li in circuit. The land was rich, fertile and regularly cultivated and the climate was hot. According to Pliny they had a large number of villages and about 30 towns. Megasthenes knew them as a separate race altogether.

They are mentioned in Buddhist literature as well. P.T.S. Aiyanger holds that they were originally Vindhyan people who extended their political power from the west gradually to the east down the Godavari and the Krsna valley. They were a Dravidian people and the progenitor of the present Telugu-speaking people. Their territory included the distt. of Kistna and Vengi. Five miles to the north of Ellar was its capital which is the same as Telingana, south of Hyderabad. The Indian Museum Ins. of the 9th year of Narayanapaladeva of the Pala dynasty, the XIII Rock Edict of Ashoka and the Haraha Ins. of Maukhari king Kumargupta III (550 A.D.) refer to them.

The Satavahanas are said to have been Andhras or Andhrabhrtyas in the Puranas. They ruled over the whole of Andhradesa and the adjoining regions.

They were succeeded by the Iksavakus. Then they came under the sway of Pallava dynasty whose headquarters at that time were at Dhamnakada (Dhanya-kataka) or Bejwada. The Vakataka king Harishena also claimed to have conquered the Andhra and Kalinga regions as also Kuntala, Avanti, Kosala, Trikuta and Lata.

Their capital seems to have been Dhankataka at the mouth of the Krsna. Their earlier capital Andhapur was situated on the Telavaha river which was probably identical with Tel or Telingiri both flowing near the confines of the Madras state present Andhra Pradesh and Madhya Pradesh.

## Asmaka

We find no mention of them in Vedic literature. Panim associates them with Maunika, Paunika and Bhogavardhana. BhattasvamT, the commentator of the Arthasastra placed it on the Godavari and identified it with Maharastra. The Mahabharata associates them with the most prominent Ksatriya people of the ancient India such as Kurus and Surasenas and places them between Godavari and Mahismati.

Buddhists mention them as Assaka, with their capital at Potana. They were associated with Mulakas who had their capital at Palithana, and are mentioned as living on the banks of river Godavari immediately to the south of Palithana.

Ptolemy mentioned Assakenoi in the eastern Afghanistan and Kunar valley with their capital at Massage or Masakavati. The Asmakas, greek Aspasian, was a minor state in Alexander's time. The Iranian Aspa is equal to Sanskrit Asva or Asmaka. The Aspasians perhaps were some western branch of trie Asvaka or Asmaka people. One of their cities is said to have situated on or near the river Enaspal (Kunar), a tributary of the Kabul river. Auxoamis,' identified by St. Martin with Sumi, lying a little to the east of Sravasfi, and situated at a distance of about 25 miles from sea was considered to be the ancient Asmaka country.

The Brhannaradlya Purana (Ch.9) connects the Iksvakus and Asmakas. The Asmaka and Mulaka of Iksvakus founded the kingdom of Asmaka and Mulaka region. Though Vayu mentions both Asmaka and his successor Mulaka yet it is difficult to conclude that this line can be brought as far as south, for we find that Raghu and Rama, the descendants of this very Asmaka, ruled in the Ayodhya region. Pargiter is wrong in his assumption that there is no evidence in the Puranas which may place them in the south also. According to him it seems that the mere similarity of names has confused the authors of Visnu Purana and Brhannardiya to relate the king Asmaka with the region Asmaka. We have dearly the evidence of the Brahma Purana which places Asmakas in the south.

Asmaka was contiguous to Mulaka and had its capital at Paundanya, identified by Raychaudhuri with Bodhan in the Nizamabad Distt. of Andhra Pradesh. He places Asmakas on the Godavari in the part of the Maharastra state. In the opinion of Rhys Davids they lived to the north-west of AvantI and had a settlement on the banks of the Godavari in Buddha's time and their capital was Potana. Their settlement on Jthe Godavari was a later one. Some place it at Ahmadnagar on the south of Godavari and the adjoining regions, the capital of which was Pratisthana on the Godavari.

Asanga in the Sutralankara mentions an Asmaka country in the basin of Indus and we see Greeks' knowledge of an Assokenoi in Eastern Afganistan. Law and Raychandhuri relying on the evidence of the Kurma Purana and Brhatsamhita place them in eastern Afganistan and Suvastu (mod. Swat Valley). On the other hand Dr. Bhandarkar places them on the Godavari. In the opinion of Sircar Asmakas are no other than the Vakafakas of Vatsagulma mentioned separately from Vidarbha in the KamasQtra. The capital of this branch of Vakafakas has been identified with modern Basin in the Akola Distt. about the southern fringe of Berar, but their dominions certainly included the Ajanta region in the Aurangabad Distt. and very probably also Nandikota (Nander distt.), both in the northern part of the former Hyderabad state. The Vakafakas of Vatsagulma, therefore, ruled over the southern most region of Berar and the northern part of the former Hyderabad state and the ancient Asmaka country has been located by scholars precisely in this region.

They are mentioned in the Hathigumpha Ins. of Kharvela. They were a part of Maharastra in Ashoka's time and in the 6th cent. A.D. they were a dependant kingdom of Vidarbha.

## Anarta

The country of Anarta has been placed in the south. The Bhavisya Purana places it to the west of the country of Pancalas. The cities of Anartas were Kusasthali which was founded by Kusa, son of Rama and which was later on associated with Krsna and Yadavas. This city of Kusasthali is said to have been destroyed by the demons called Punyajana. Krsna later on founded Dvaraka at the site of Kusasthali. The Visnu Purana explicitly says that Kusasthati and Dvaraka are one and the̮same.Revati, the daughter of Raivata, the king of Anartas was married to Balarama. The distance between Anarta and Vidarbha could be covered on horse-back during one single night which shows the proximity of the two Janapadas. If we rely on the evidence of the Puranas that Kusasthali and Dvaraka are one and the same then the country of Anarta must have been situated round Raivataka mountain and should be placed in north Kathiawad.

## Smaller Tribes

The Vayu and Brahmanda Puranas place the Andhravakas in the east which shows that they were different from Andhras of the south. The Naisikashave been identified with the Nasikyas of the Puranas. The Tapasas, who are the Tabasoi of Ptolemy are mentioned as a western nation. They may have dwelt on the banks of the river Tapti (an older form of Tapi). The Musikas are the Mousopalle of Ptolemy. The Kulyas were named after Kulya, the son of Janapida in the mixed lineage of Turvasu and Puru. Though mentioned in the Mahabharata they cannot be satisfactorily identified. Perhaps they lived at Kulam on the sea coast. The Atavyas are mentioned as Atavikas in the Allahabad Pillar Inscription of Samudragupta. A southern city named Atavi is mentioned in the Mahabharata also. Perhaps they were aboriginal tribes dwelling in the forest tracts of India. The Abhiras of Deccan must have been a branch of the northern tribe of the same name. The Paunikas may be connected with the king of Purika country in the Mahismafi region. No specific location has been attributed to the Vindhyamauleyas who seem to have lived along the Vindhya mountain, its south-east forests and at its base. The Dandakasinhabited the famous Dandaka forest of the Ramayana, according to which it was situated between the Vindhya and Saibala mountains, a part of it being called Janasthana. It comprised all the forests from Bundelkhand to the river Krsna. Northwards it must have stretched upto the Yamuna for Guha, the king of Srngvera-pura is said to have well known the Dandaka forest (Ayo. 923). Later on the limit of this extensive forest contracted and according to the Tirthayatra section (ch.85) the Dandaka forest appears to be situated somewhere between the Bhopal and the source of Godavari. Bhavabhuti places it to the east of Janasthana. The country of Samasta Karaskara was regarded a sinful country. Perhaps they included five people, Lohajangha, Vajeya, Raja-Bhadra, Tusara and Tumbara who are named as Samasta Karaskara in the Markandeya Purana. The Vanavasikas seem to have occupied the BanavasI in North Kannara d.stt.. The Harivamsa places them on the river Vena. They are same as Banavasi of Ptolemy, a town, the remains of which are still extent in the district of Sunda. The Sutikas were the people of the Setubandha Ramesvara. The other countries of the south that are referred to are Kumana,Udbhida, Sirala Rupasa and Taittarika.

**(D) Eastern States**

## Anga

The country of Anga was named after Anga, the son of Bali. The Anava kingdom, the nucleus of which was Anga was divided into five kingdoms which were named after the five sons of Bali. The Ramayana gives a very interesting explanation of the name Anga. The country was called Anga because the god Cupid fled to this country to save himself from the anger of Rudra and became Ananga by giving up his Anga here.

It is the country of the well known author named Aurava of Rgveda. During the Atharva age, though they lived as a distinct people yet no specified territory has been attributed to them and they were despised as Vratyas. The Gopatha Br. associates them with the Magadhas and Astadhyayi with Vanga, Kalinga etc.. According to the Mahabharata which refers to them frequently, the races of Anga, Vanga, and Kalinga etc. were the descendants of the sage DTrghatamas through Sudesna, the wife of Bali.

The Yoginitantra refers to it. The Buddhist literature mentions Anga as one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas. Many towns 6f the kingdom of Anga are described in Buddhist works.

It was annexed to Magadha by Bimbisara in the 6th cent. B.C.. The Campeya Jataka records a fight between Anga and Magadha. Vinaya Mahavagga proves that Anga came under the sway of Bimbisara who had his son Ashoka from a daughter of a brahmin of Campa.

The Saktisamgamatantra says that it extended from Vaidyanatha (Baidyanathdhama in the Santhal pargana distt. very near to the southern frontiers of Monghyr and Bhagalpur distt. in Bihar) to Bhuvanesvara (Puri distt. of Orissa). Sriharsa, too, mentions it.

The ancient Anga country is said to have included the hermitage of sage Rsyasrnga (at Riski kund, 4 miles to the south west of Bariarpur), Karnagarh, Jahnvasrama (at Sultanganj), Modagiri (Monghyr) and Mandara hills (at Bansi 32 miles to the south of Bhagalpur). It included the modern district of Bhagalpur probably including Monghyr and a portion of the district of Santhal Pargana. According to Sir George Wood it included also the districts of Birbhum, Murshidabad and Manbhum.Many inscriptions such as Belava copper plate of Bhojavarman, Rewala stone Ins. of Kama, Saranath Ins. of Kumaradevi, Nilagunda stone Ins. of Amoghavarsha (9th Cent. A.D.), Deoli grant of Krishna III and Hathigumpha Ins. mention them.

The capital of Anga was previously called MalinT, which was later on called Campa or Campavati on the banks of Bhaglrathi in honour of a king named Campa. It is variously called as Campapuri, Campanagara, Campamalim or Campavati. A place of piligrimage, surrounded by the groves of Campaka trees, it stood on the banks of the Bhagirthi.

The city was visited both by Buddha and Mahavira. Buddhists attribute its foundation to Mahagovinda and in Buddha's time it was one of the six great cities of India. It was also a great centre of Jainism. According to the Jain Campakasresthikatha it was in, a very flourishing condition. Kunika, son of king Srenika left Rajagrha at the death of his father and made Campa his capital. It was a seat of Magadhan viceroyalty from the time of Bhattiya, father of Bimbisara. River Campa marked the boundary between Anga and Magadha and the river Ganga flew through Anga country. According to Hiuen Tsang, the kingdom of Campa, called by him Campo, was situated on the southern bank of the Ganges. It was 4000 li in circuit having a capital of 40 li round. The soil was fertile, regularly cultivated and productive, and the temperature was mild and warm. According to Fa-hien also the kingdom of Campa was situated on Ganga. It was situated at the confluence of the rivers Ganga and Campa (modern Chandana). The two villages of Campapura and Campanagara, near Bhagalpur represent the ancient Campa. The epigraphic evidence suggests its location near Lakhi-sarai station on the eastern Railway in the western fringe of Monghyr Distt..

## Pundra

The country was named Pundra after Pundra, son of Bali. It is also known as Paundra, Pundra vardhanaka, Pundraka and Paundraka. In the Mahabharata, these names are sometimes used as equivalents, but sometimes a distinction is made between them. According to Pargiter there were two tribes of this name. The Pundras dwelt in between Anga, Vanga and the hilly countries of Himalayas, and comprised the distt. of Malda, portion of Purnea to the east of river Kosi and parts of Dinajpur (West Bengal) and Rajshahi (Bangladesh). The Paundras were associated with Udras, Utkalas, Mekalas, Kalingas etc. and occupied the distt. of Santhal Pargana (Bihar), Birbhum (West Bengal) and northern portion of Hazaribagh (Bihar). They are mentioned alongwith Andhras, Sabaras and Mucipas. But no such distinction can be seen in later literary and epigraphic records.

The Ramayana locates them near Videhas and Vangas and the Mahabharata associates them with Vangas and Kiratas at one place and with Udras, Utkalas and Mekalas at other and with Kalinga, Magadha, and Tamralipta at yet another place.They have been placed in the east. The Brahma Purana places them in the south. The Garuda Purana and Visnudharmottara Purana are more precise in locating them in the south-east along the Vindhya range. KavyamTmamsa of Rajashekhara associates them with Pragjyotisa and Tamralipta in the east. The Bhavisya Purana refers to the divisions of Paundradesa i.e. Gauda, Varendra, Nivrti, Suhma, forest region called Narikhanda, Varahabhumi, Vardhamana, and Vindhya-Parsva.

According to the Buddhist tradition the eastern boundary of the Madhyadesa extended upto Pundravardhana. Hiuen Tsang is of the same opinion but Mahavagga of Vinayapitaka extends its boundary upto Kajangala. They are referred to in the Divyavadana and Bodhisatvavadana Kalpalata. The Sumagadhavadana of Avadana-Kalpalata places Pundravardhana at a distance of 160 yojanas to the east of SravastT. The Pun-Na-Fa-Tan-Na of Hiuen Tsang was more than 4000 li in circuit with its capital being more than 30 li in circuit. The soil is said to have been flat, loamy and rich in all kinds of grain produce. Its boundaries were Kosi on the west, Brahmaputra on the east separating it from Pragjyotisa and Ganga on the south.

A number of inscriptions refer to it. According to the Damodar plate, it extended from the Himalayas in the north to Khadi in the Sunderban region of the south. The Madhyapada plate of Vishvarupasena extends its eastern boundary upto the sea, and in the Meher copperplate of 13th cent. A.D. it is said to have comprised a portion of Tippera Distt.. During the age of Mauryas, it formed a part of Magadha empire, a fact that is approved by the records of Hiuen-Tsang also. It formed a provincial division of Bengal from 443 A.D. to 543 A.D. and included Kotivarsavisaya which must have included the whole or a part of Dinajpur. By that time it included the greater part of the northern (undivided) Bengal including at least the present districts of Rajshahi, Bogra, Dinajpur and portions of Maldah and Rungpur (all in Bangladesh except Maldah and a part of Dinajpur). A number of Pala records mention them which shows that in the time of Palas it must have comprised a larger area and Senas must have ruled over a still larger area. They had their capital at Pundranagara even in 2nd contury B.C.. Not long after, they spread over a wider area and their country came to be known as Pundravardhana for the name Pundravardhana is found in at least two inscriptions of the Sanchi stupa. The Mahasthana fragmentary inscription tells us that the Distt. of Bogra (Bangladesh) was certainly included in the later Janapada of Pundravardhana. The Paharpur copperplate (428-429 A.D.), issued from the Pundravardhana city itself, included in it the Rajashahi district (Bangladesh) or at least poriton of it. The Kotivarsavisaya was a subdivision of it. It included Varendri (North Bengal) as well as Gauda (Maldah in West Bengal and Dinajpur). Vyaghratati identified by S.N. Majumdar with Begdi, mentioned in Khalimpur grant of Dharmapala, Nalanda Inscription of Devapala and Anulia copperplate of Lakshamanasena, was one of the divisions of Bengal. According to Prof: H.C. Raychaudhuri the Pundravardhanabhukti was divided into several Visayas and mandalas of which twenty-four find mention in known epigraphs. These were (1-2) VyaghratatI-Mandala to which was attached the Mahantaprakasa - Visaya. *(3-5)* Sthalikkata visaya to which was attached the Amrashandika-Mandala near the Udragrama-Mandala. (6) Kuddalakhata-Visaya. (7-9) Kotivarsa-Visaya in which were included the Gokalika and Halavarto - Mandalas, (10) Brahmanigrama - Mandala. (11) Namya-Mandala, (12-13) Khediravalli-Visaya which included the Valllmunda Mandala, (14-15) Ikkodasi - Visaya which included the Yola - Mandala, (16-17) Satatapadmavati - Visaya in which was included the Kumaratalaka Mandala, (18) Pancavasa - Mandala, (19) Adhahpattana - Mandala, (20) Khadi - Visaya or Mandala (21) Varendra or Varendri Mandala (22) Vanga which included the Vikramapura - Bhaga and Navya, (23-24) Samatata - mandala which included the Paranayi - Visaya.

The Pundranagara, also known as Sri-Pundravardhanapura and Pundranagara in Ramacaritam of Sandhyakaranandin (llth cent. A.D.) was their capital. It was situated in Varendri. Cunningham has identified the capital of Pundravardhana with Mahasthana on the authority of Hiuen-Tsang's description of Po-Shih-Po monastary in Pundravardhana. The present ruins of Mahasthana lie on the river Karatoya, 12 miles south of Bardhankuti and seven miles north of the town of Bograha. The river Karatoya which still washes the base of the mounds of Mahasthana separated Pundravardhanabhukti from Pragjyotisa or Kamarupa. The city lost its importance from the third quarter of the 12th cent. A.D. for the later Sena kings of Bengal transferred their capital first to Deopara (RaJashahi Distt.) and later to Gauda (in Maldah Distt.) . In the Keshavasena plate found at Edilpur in Faridpur distt. Vikramapura is said to have been a part of Pundrakas.

Rapson has placed the Pundras in Chhota Nagpur and Pargiter locates them in the districts of Birbhum (West Bengal), Santhal Pargana and the northern portion of Hazaribagh (Bihar). It appears that the Pundras had Kasi on their north, the Anga, Variga and Suhma on their north east and east and Odra on their south. Stein, on the evidence furnished by Hiuen Tsang locates it somewhere in the Rajshahi district (Bangladesh). Ferguesson assigned it a place near Rangpur (Bangladesh). Westmacott takes them to the adjacent districts of Bogra (Bangladesh) and Vardhana - Kuti close to Govinda Ganj on the Karatoya in Dinajpur about 35 miles north-west from Rungpur (Bangladesh). He also suggests an alternative Pondua or Panrowa afterwards called Firzupur or Firuzabad, ? miles north-east of Malda (West Bengal) and 18 miles north, north-east from Gauda. It was previously situated on the river Mahananda which has now receded four miles to the west. The Pundranagara, the head quarters of Pundras was the capital of the Samvamgiyas at the time of the Mahasthana Inscription. Thus the Pundra territory comprised the districts of Rajshahi, Dinajpur, Rungpur, (Bangladesh) Nadiya, Birbhum, Burdwan, part of Midnapore (West Bengal) Jungel Mahals, Ramgarh, Pachete Palamau and part of Chunar (Bihar).

## Videha

The country of Videha, also called Tirubhukti was the name of the capital as well as of the country itself. The word Tirubhukti, which is derived from Tira(bank) and Bhukti (limit) only refers to the lands lying along the banks of the river and does not denote the boundaries of the districts. Videha was so named after Mathava, the Videgha, its coloniser. According to the Buddhist tradition it took its name from the early immigrants from Pubbavidheha, the eastern subcontinent of Asia, placed to the east of the mountain Sineru. In the Mahabharata, this region is called Bhadrasvavarsa. The famous legend of Videgha Mathava, the ancestor of the Kosalas and Videhas, shows that by that time the Videha country had been Aryanised. During the Brahmana age they had attained a highly advanced stage of civilization and took the lead in spiritual and intellectual field. The king Janaka was a great patron of Vedic culture and its Upanisadic phase of development. We have many Jataka stories and Puranic account of sacrifices performed here.During the Vedic period, Kasi, Kosala and Videha were closely related.

King Videha had matrimonial relation and friendly relations with the neighbouring people. We have the famous stories of the marriage of Rama-Sita and Vasavadatta-Udayana. Both Udayana and Ajatashatru were 'Vaidehlputras'.

It was called the country of Vajjis in Buddha's time and played a great part in the history of Buddhism. The Videha's extent is given as 300 leagues. Its capital Mithila extended upto seven leagues and was situated at a distance of 60 yojanas from Campa, the Anga capital. Mithila was the capital of the king Janaka as well as Makhadeva in the modern distt. of Tirhut. It was a great centre of trade. Vardhamana Mahavira, the propagator of Jainism was closely related to Videha. The Saktisamgamatantra gives its extent from the banks of GandakI as far as Campakaranya (modern hamparan). Gandakitira appears to indicate the t\>outht»rn boundary of the country and the place where the modern Gandak falls into the Ganges.

Its capital Mithila was founded by Nimi. The Visnu Purana gives a fanciful account of the name 'Videha' who was also called Mithi. All the kings in his lineage were called Maithilas. It was a lovely, fair, and fertile city on the bank of the Ganges.Nearby situated was an old and deserted wood. It was also called Tirubhukti and was bounded by the KausikT (modern Kosi) in the east, the Ganges in the south and the SadanTra (Gandak or Rapti) in the east and Himalayas in the north. During the reign of Janaka, Videha king, the sage Visvamitra took four days to reach Mithila from Ayodhya resting at Visala on the way for one night only.

From the Upanisadic and Brahmanic period down to the age of Buddha, the scholars of Videha took the lead in spiritual learning and devotion to the practice and study of the true law. The civilization of Bengal, the new learning, especially that of the logic came from Mithila, when Magadha had ceased to give light to eastern India.

The Videhas were a branch of the Iksavaku raceand lived to the east of the Madhyadesa at the time of the redaction of the Brahmanas and constituted the extreme east of the land of Aryans. The land of Tirubhukti has been identified with the valleys of Burgandaka and Baghmati rivers.Northwards it extended close to the Himalayas and on the south, it was bounded by a kingdom, the capital of which was Vaisali or the modern Besarh which is about twenty seven miles north of Patna.It has been identified with the modern Janakpura in the district of Darbhanga a small town within the Nepal border. The districts of Muzzaffarpur and Darbhanga meet to the north of it. Rhys Davids places Mithila at about 35 miles north-west of Vaisali. Rapson takes Tirubhukti to be the modern Tirhut i.e. northern Bihar. Their capital was Mithila. They were one of the numerous clans possibly of Tibeto-Chinese origin. Smith includes north Bihar, Champaran and Darbhanga districts in Videha country.

## Magadha

The oldest name of this country is said to have been Kikata. Though Rgveda does not mention them, yet the Vedic literature generally contains many references to them. In the Atharvaveda they appear as associated with the Vratyas while in the Apastamba Srauta Sutra they are mentioned alongwith other eastern and western people such as Kalinga, Gandhara, Parakaras and Sauviras. In the later texts they are represented as a special caste having its origin in the intermarriages among the old established four varnas. Magadha is not a man of Magadha but a person of a mixed caste produced from the union of a Vaisya man and a Ksatriya woman. Panini and Patanjali mention them under the name of Magadha and Sumagadha. The Brhatsamhita, too, refers to them. The Puranic tradition holds that the country was named Magadha after Magadha, a courtier of king, on whom the territory was bestowed by the king who was pleased with his services.

The Epics and the Puranas mention them.They have been variously placed in the east, centre, south, north, and to the east of the Pancalas. Their capital was situated at Girivraja. A number of literary works refer to them. Raghuvamsa refers to the intermarriages of the early kings of Kosala with the ruling family of Magadha. The Saktisamga-matantra places them in the region extending from Kalesvara or Vyasesvara (Kalabhairava of Benaras-Kasi) as far as Taptakunda (hot spring near Monghyr). Magadha, therefore, comprised the region between Benaras and Monghyr district. The Magadha country was bounded by the river Campa, the Ganga and the Son on the east, north and west respectively. Hiuen Tsang gives its (Mo-kie-to) extent as 5000 li. The city was shut in by lofty and steep mountains. Its soil is said to be rich and fertile and its temperature as pleasently hot. Fa-hien's records also show that the city was surrounded by mountains.

Magadha and its ancient capital Rajagrha were closely associated with Buddha and the birth of Buddhism. The Jatakas and other Buddhist worksare full of interesting information about Magadha. The famous universities of ancient Magadha were at Nalanda, Odantpuri and Vikramasila. According to Parasara and Varahamihira it was situated in the eastern division of the nine portions into which the sub-continent of India was divided. Its northern boundary was the Ganges, and the western was the distt. of Benaras. On the east was situated the Hiranyaparvata or Monghyr and on the south was Kirana Supavana or Singhbhum. According to Cunningham it extended from the river Karmanasa on the west to the sources of Damodara river on the south. The people of the neighbouring distt. still call the district of Patna and Gaya as Maga (a corruption of Magadha). Identifying it with southern Bihar Rapson observes, This was a narrow strip of country of some considerable length from north to south. Magadha in Buddha's time corresponded to the modern district of Patna along with the northern half of the modern Gaya Distt.. Its boundaries were probably the Ganges to the north. Son to the west, a dense forest reaching to the plateau of Chotta Nagpur to the south and Anga to the east. The river Campa formed the boundary between Magadha and Anga but in Buddha's time Anga was subject to Magadha.

It was ruled over by Jarasamdha and his descendants, Pradyotas, Sisunagas, Sudras, Mauryas, Sungas, Kanvas and Andhras. A Jain work tells us that a Magadhan prince governed Anga as a separate kingdom with Campa as its capital. It was Bimbisara who annexed Anga to the kingdom of Magadha. During his time Ajatashatru governed Campa as its viceroy. The annexation of Anga was a turning point in the history of Magadha. Ajatashatru afterwards annexed Kasi permanently and conquered the land of Lichchhavis. Ashoka annexed Trikalingas to his empire. A merely tiny state in southern Bihar in the time of Bimbisara expanded during the interval from the time of Bimbisara to the Kalinga war of Ashoka. Its boundary extended from the foot of Hindu-Kush to the borders of the Tamil country. After the Kalinga war, Magadha's power was gradually reduced and the empire became smaller and smaller till it came to have its pre-Bimbisarian area and position.

In the beginning of the 4th cent. A.D., the Magadha empire again attained prominence in the political field. The Samudragupta of the Gupta dynasty came in conflict with many kings such as Mahendra of Kosala, Vyaghraraja of Mahakantara, Mantaraja of Kaurala, Swami Datta of Pistapura, Damana of Erandapalla, Vishnugopa of Kanci, NTlaraja of Avimukta, Hastivarman of VerigT, Ugrasena of Palakka, Kuvera of Devarastra and Dhananjaya of Kusthalapura. The western tribal states, Malwa, Samatata, Davaka and Kamarupa also submitted to him. The Damodara plates inform us that Pundravardhana (North Bengal) formed an integral part of Gupta empire. The Guptas had their dominions in almost all of the populous and fertile provinces of northern India. The Gupta empire extended from the Brahmaputra in the east to the Yamuna and Chambal on the west, from the foot of the Himalayas in the north to the Narmada in the south. Besides, the frontier kingdoms of Assam and Gangetic delta, those on the southern slopes of the Himalayas, and almost all of the southern kingdoms had to acknowledge political supremacy of the Guptas.

The original capital of Magadha under Chandragupta II was Pataliputra but after his western conquest, Ujjain was made a second capital. The Magadhan empire did not fall completely on the death of Skandagupta, because the Damodarpur plates, Sarnath Inscriptions, the Eran epigraph of Buddhagupta and the Betui plates of Parivaajak Maharaj Samksobha (518 A.D.) testify to this fact. Later on Adityasena and his successors ruled over Magadha and Madhyadesa. In the early part of the 8th century A.D. it came under the Gaudd king, as the Pala Inscriptions show.

Saktivarman, a king of Kaliriga belonged to Magadha family. The Mahakuta Inscription of Mangalesh records the conquest of Kirtivarman over Magadha. The inscription of Jayadeva of Kathmandu, the Sirpur stone inscription of the time of Mahasivagupta, the Aihole Inscription of Mahamandalesvara.Chamunda II, the Sirur Ins. of the time of Amoghavarsa I, the Nilgund Ins. of the time of Amoghavarsa I, the Govindapura stone Ins. of the poet Gangadhara, Albur Ins. and the

Hathigumpha Ins. refer to Magadha and its ancient capital Rajagrha.

## Its Capitals

The capital of Magadha had many names in its history. Its ancient capital was Girivraja (modern Rajgir about six miles west of Giryak) at the time of Jarasamdha. Ramayana speaks of the city of Girivraja and the five hills surrounding it. From the Ramayana's account it is quite evident that at that time Son flowed eastward, a day's journey to the east of Patna. Though Pataliputra had not then come in existence, the people inhabited both the banks of the Ganges and used to cross the river Son at the site of Pataliputra and went over to Vaisali and Mithila and vice versa. The city was called Girivraja since it was surrounded by the mountains. The names of these five mountains as given in the Mahabharata are Vipula, Varaha, Vrsabha or Rsabha Rsigiri or Matanga and Caityaka. The Ramayanaattributes the foundation of Girivraja to Vasu, the fourth son of Kusa. On the evidence of the Puranas, Pargiter has tried to show that the dynasties of Magadha and the adjoining regions descended from Kuru's son Sudhanvana. Vasu, the fourth in succession from Sudhanvana conquered Cedi from the Yadavas and also annexed the adjoining regions as far as Magadha. When he divided his territories among his five sons, the eldest one Brhadratha took Magadha with Girivraja as its capital and founded the famous Brhadratha dynasty there.

Rajagrha (modern Rajgir) - its another capital -is said to be founded by the king Mandhata. The name Rajagrha is derived in many ways. It was called Rajagrha because (1) it was founded by a king and every house in it resembled a palace, or (2) because it was used as a residence by kings like Mandhata, Mahagovinda and others or (3) because it was used as a prison for inimical kings by Jarasamdha.

Not only Buddhism, but also Jainsm, and Naga and Yakkha worship developed here. The Tapodas (hot springs of Rajgrha which survive even today) and the Tapoda (Sarasvati) carrying water from these hot springs were regarded very sacred. The sorrounding hills of Rajagrha were the Grdhrakuta and the Vipula Parvata. It was a rich, happy and thriving city. Hiuen-Tsang records that the name Rajagrha was strictly applicable only to the new city founded either by Bimbisara or by Ajatashatru, not far to the north east from Venuvana, the old city being known as Girivraja. Fa-hien, too, speaks of the old city and the new city. Both of them saw only the remains of Rajagrha.

The Rajagrha lost its importance with the shifting of the capital to Pataliputra or Kusumapura by Udayibhada, some 28 years after the death of Buddha, but it still had its military value.

Its later capital Pataliputra also called Kusumapura and Puspapura was originally a Magadhan village known as Pataligrama lying opposite to Kotigrama on the other side of the Ganges. It was previously called Kusumapura or Puspapura due to the abundance of flowers in the royal enclosures. Later on it came to be called Pataliputra.

Patanjali states it to be actually on the Son, at the confluence of the Ganga, Son and Gandaka. It extended about a mile along the northern bank of the Son which now has receded some distance away from it.

Jain tradition holds that it was built by Udaya, son of Darsaka, but the first beginnings were made by Ajatashatru, for the Buddha when on his way to Vaisali from Magadha saw Ajatashatru's ministers measuring out a town. A number of Pali Buddhist works refer to it as a delightful place and as an excellent city.

Strabo gives its location at a distance of 6000 stadia from the mouth of the Ganges. It was 80 stadia in length and 75 stadia in breadth and was situated at the confluence of the Ganges and another river. The Ganges is said to have flown by it. Arrian, too, gives the same account. Pliny reckons its distance from the junction of the Ganges and the Jumna as 425 Roman miles. Both Megasthenes and Arrian call it Palibothra. Ptolemy gives its name as Palimbothra. Fa-hien visited it during 400-415 A.D. and was greatly impressed by its glory and splendour. Hiuen-Tsang says nothing of the submerged ruins of this city which had become deserted by his time. He places the ruins of Kusumapura to the south of the Ganges.

The city is said to have been in existence even in Aja's time. Dandin described it as a foremost of all the cities.

It used to be the later capital of the Sungas, Nandas and also the great Mauryan emperors. During the reign of Candragupta Vikramaditya the city was still a magnificient and a populous one. It was not ruined untill the time of Huna invasion in the 6th cent. A.D.. About 600 A.D. Sasanka Narendra Gupta of Gauda and Karnasuvarria destroyed many Buddhist temples and monasteries. It ceased to be the ordinary residence of the Gupta kings after the conquest of Samudragupta. Dharmapala, the most powerful of the Pala kings of Bengal and Bihar took some steps to renew its glory.

From the account of the old course of the river Son it appears that the two rivers flowed by Pataliputra viz. the Ganga on the north and the Son on the south. Regarding this Cunningham writes, The course of the Ganges and the Son would thus have been nearly parallel for many miles and in this narrow tract lying between the two rivers, was situated the famous city of Pataliputra.

Probably the decline of Pataliputra was due to the fall of the Gupta empire and the consequent removal of the seat of the government to another place. From the fall of the Gupta dynasty in about 473 A.D. down to 1541 A.D. the old city of Pataliputra continued to exist as a small and unimportant town. It now lies buried within the modern city of Patna and Bankipur in Bihar.

## Suhma

The country was named after Suhma, one of the five sons of Bali. They have been placed both in the east and the northern mountainous region.The epic and Puranas distinguished Suhma from Vanga, Pundra and Tamralipta. The Suhma country was a portion of the more comprehensive region later on known as Radha. The Nilakantha commentary on the Mahabharata informs us that the Suhma and Radhas were one and the same people. The Jain work Ayorangasutta tells us that the Suhma country formed a part of Radha country. The Buddhist evidence placed it to the west of Vanga, between Vanga and Kalinga. Brhatsamhitatoo, places it between Vanga and Kalinga. The Dasakumarcarita mentions Damlipta as having been a city of Suhma. Wilson identified it with Tippera and Arracan, and Rapson with Bankura and Midnapur. It lay on both the banks of the Ajay, a river in the modern Burdwan region in south-west Bengal. Pargiter includes the districts of Midnapur, Bankura and perhaps also Purulia and Manbhum (West Bengal) in the Suhma country.

## Brahmottara

This country is also known as Suhmottara, which shows that they lived to the north of Suhma. Suhma, the son of Bali (after whom the Janapada was known as Suhma) is called Brahma also. So the country Suhmottara may also be called Brahmottara. Their location in the east by the Puranas also support this. They are mentioned along with Anga, Vanga and Magadha. As Anga and Vanga were the two other sons of the king Bali, so they must have been kindred people.

Brahmottara may be associated with the name Burma which is derived from the Mrama (Brama) one of the three tribes that migrated into Burma and ultimately gave their name as a national designation to all Burmese. It is said to be one of the -mahallas of Satgaon in West Bengal.

## Tamraliptaka

It was a north-west Janapada. Tamralipta is mentioned in the Mahabharata (BhTsma Ch.9). The Digvijaya section of the Sabhaparva distinguished it not only from the territories situated in northern, eastern and central Bengal, but also from Suhma. Later on it formed a part of Vanga in the time of Jain Prajnapana, and of Suhma in the days of Dandin. Hiuen Tsang gives its situation in the following words, South of Kamanpa is Samatata. From Samatata going west 900 li or so is the country called Tan-mo-li-ti 1400 li or 1500 li, with its capital being 10 li in circuit. It borders on the sea. The ground is low and rich and the climate is hot. Ptolemy mentions it as Tamalites.

It may be identified with modern Tamluk in Midnapur distt. of West Bengal which was situated formerly on the mouth of the Ganges and is now situated on the western bank of the Rupanarayana formed by the united stream of the Silai (Silavati) and Dalkisor in the Midnapur distt..

## Manavartika and Madguraka

Nothing much is said about them in the Puranas. The word Madguraka appears to be a corrupt reading of the word Mudgagiri or Modagiri of the Sanskrit literature and the Inscriptions. It may be identified with Monghyr in Bihar, for Monghyr in old days was also known as Modgalapuri or Mudgalasrama. It was called Mungiri in the llth cent. A.D.. Manavartika is perhaps Vardhamana (Burdwan). Pargiter suggests its identification with Man-Bhum (Mana-bhumi), a district in West Bengal.

## Munda and Mala

The Mundas, who are the Maroundi of Ptolemywere a well known pre-Aryan tribe. Mundas are a large Dravadian tribe in Chota Nagpur. The Malas are the same as Mala or Malas, an indigenous tribe which now spreads all over Bengal. The Malta country was situated to the east of Videha, north-west of Magadha and on the north of the Ganges including evidently the Chapra distt (Bihar). They are said to be Dravadian tribe which now inhabit the Raj Mahal and Ramgarh hills in West Bengal.

## Matanga

The Matangas lived in the south-east of Kamarupa in Assam and were famous for their diamond mines.

## Vangeyas, Bhargavas

They are also called Vanga, Rangeyas and Vangeyas so they must be the people of ancient Vanga or Bengal. The Mahabharata mentions both Bhargas and Bhargavas. They might be connected with the prince Bharga or Bhargava, the foundenof Bhrgubhumi or Bhargabhumi. They were probably an eastern branch of the Bhaggas or Bhargas of Sirhsumaragiri. They may be placed in the western Assam, the country of the Bhars of Bhors. The Rhargava Brahmins even now live near Ayodhya and Kanauj in Uttara Pradesh.

## Malada.

The Maladas, probably the Molindai of Megasthenes, are placed in the east on the Vindhya mountain. Pargiter places them in the Maldah Distt. of West Bengal in which are situated the old cities of Gaur and Pandua. It is said that the hermitage of Visvamitra was situated in Malada and Karusa country. This Visvamitrasrama has been identified with modern Buxar. The Malada country has been identified with a portion of the Distt. of Shahbad. Perhaps the Maladas lived to the west of Karusas and east of Prayaga.

## Antargiri, Bahirgiri

They may be located towards the north of Pragjyotisa. The Antargiri was a Janapada of the inner chain of the Himalayas. Pargiter located them in the hilly stretch of the Rajmahal ranges of the Santhal Pargana (Bihar). Since Bahirgiris were associated with the Antargiris they perhaps occupied the outskirts of the hills of Bhagalpur and Monghyr region of Bihar province.

## Govinda and others

The Govindas are the same as Gonardhas of the Matsya. Jneyamarthakas are also known as Jneyamallakas, Jneyas and Jneyamalavas. The Plavangas might have represented the people living in front of the Vangas and might be Angas. Pravijaya is same as Pragvijaya which is Jaintia in Assam.

## Vanga

The country of Vanga, also called Pravanga,was named after the king Vanga, the son of Bali.

They are closely associated with Anga, Kalinga, Pundra, Suhma and Tamraliptaka. They have been placed in the east, or south-east along the Vindhya range in the delta formed by the Ganga and the Brahmaputra.

The Vangas as a tribe are not mentioned in earlier Vedic literature. The Aitareya Brahmana associating them with the Magadhas etc, brands them as impure. The Baudhayana Dharma Sutra, too, regards them as impure. They are known to Panini. Patanjali, too, mentions them as outside Aryavarta. But it seems that they were Aryanised before Manu wrote his work, because Manu Samhita extends the eastern boundary of Aryavarta to the sea. The Ramayana, the Mahabharata, the Yoginitantra and Kavyamimarhsa also mention them. The Mahabharata locates the Vangas on sea-shore. Anga is said to be a dominion of Vanga. The Pundras had their habitat on the northern side of the Vanga. The Raghuvamsa informs us that the river Kapisa (modern Kasai) formed the south-western boundary of the Vanga territory.The Buddhist literature makes no mention of them. The Jain Prajnapana ranks Anga and Vanga in the first group of Aryan people.

In Baudhayana's time they were distinguished from Pundra. The Epics and Puranas differentiate them from Pundra and Suhma as well as from Anga and Kalinga. It seems that during the period of the epics and the Puranas. Vanga, Suhma and Pundra were the three important divisions of Bengal. But it is very difficult to define their exact boundaries. This much can only be said that Pundra and Suhma were two adjacent tracts, identical roughly with the modern Rajshahi (Bangladesh) and Burdwan divisions (West bengal) respectively. The ancient literature and epigraphic records distinguish it not only from Radha including Suhma, and Gauda including Karnasuvarna and a portion of Radhanow roughly equal to Western Bengal, but also from Pundra including Varendra or Varendri roughly identical with northern Bengal. The Vangas were distinguished from Gaudas even in the 8th century A.D. and this distinction seems to have been maintained as late as 12th cent. A.D.. In the Tirumalai Rock Ins. of king Rajendra Chola (1025 A.D.) the territory of Vangala is distinguished both from the Uttaraladam and the Takkanaladam. The Ceylon chronicles also place Lalha between Vanga and Kalinga, thereby distinguishing them from Vangas. Thus Vanga in ancient times denoted only a portion of the present day Bengal. It comprised the Dacca and Chittagong divisions (Bangladesh).' The important divisions of ancient Vangas were Samatata (modern Faridpur in Bangladesh) and for sometimes even Tamralipta (Tamluk in Midnapur district of West Bengal). The epigraphic references to the Vanga comprising the Vikramapur region of Dacca and Faridpur in Bangladesh and to the Navya region of Vanga very probably in the Faridpur and Backergunge Distt. of Bangladesh leave no doubt that Vanga certainly included at least parts of the present Dacca, Faridpur and Backergunge Distt. of Bangladesh. In the lower part of this region very high roads or earthern embankments were constructed in order to prevent the flood.

In the Tirumalai Rock Ins. of Rajendra Chola (llth cent A.D.) and Goharwa plate of Chedi Karnadeva, it is referred to as Vangaladesam which in 13th cent. came to be known as Bangala and in Mohammadan times as Bangla. About its name Abul Fazi writes The original name of Vangala was Vanga. Its former rulers raised mounds measuring ten yards in height and 20 yards in breadth, throughout the province, which were called Al. From this suffix the name (Bengal) took its rise and currency. A number of Inscriptions refer to this country. The Raghuvamsa placed them in the land watered by the lower stream of Ganga. The Sadapancasaddesavibhaga gives the extent of their territory from the sea (Bay of Bengal) as far as Brahmaputra (that part of the river that bifurcates from Jumna, a river in Bangladesh). It thus included eastern part of the Sunderbans in the south and half the Mymen Singh Distt. (Bangladesh) in the north. The verse excludes the region to the east of Brahmaputra and Mehna (v.37) and agrees with the medieval epigraphic evidence which places the heart of Vanga in Vikramapura-Bhaga comprising the Mymen Singh and Madaripur subdivision of Dacca and Faridpur Distt. of Bangladesh.

In Hindu geography, Banga is applied only to the eastern position of the Delta of the Ganges, Upavanga to the centre of this territory and Anga to its western limit. Dr. Bhau Daji places them in the country lying between Brahmaputra and Padma. Bengal was then divided into five provinces (1) Pundra (north Bengal), (2) Samatata (east Bengal), (3) Kama Suvarna (West Bengal), (4)

Tamralipta (Southern Bengal) and (5) Kamarupa or Assam. Pargiter included in it the districts of Murshidabad, Nadia (West Bengal) Jessore, parts of Rajshahi, Pabna and Faridpur (Bangladesh). Sir George Birdwood included in it the distt. of Burdwan and Nadia (West Bengal). It was called Bangala even in the third century. Rapson included in it the Distt. of Birbhum, Murshidabad, Burdwan and Nadia (West Bengal). At the time of Adisura, according to DevTvara- Ghataka, Bengal was divided into Radha, Banga, Barendra and Gauda. At the time of Kesavasena, Vanga was included in Pundra vardhana.

In the early centuries of the Christian era, the people of Vanga occupied the deltic region of southern Bengal watered by the mouths of the Ganges. Their capital city was called Ganga (modern Gangasagara) near the confluence of BhagTrathI and the Bay of Bengal. The country was called Ganga after the name of the capital. The Vangas are known to early European writers as Gangaridae i.e. Ganga or Gangeya people. The Greco-Roman writers assign them the region about the mouths of the Ganges and their capital at the confluence of the Ganges and the Bay of Bengal. The Raghuvamsa clearly supports the identification of the Vangas with the Gangaridae and the place of the location of their territory. The Raghuvamsa relates that after having defeated the Vangas, the Iksavaku hero crossed the Kapisa (modern Kasai river in Midnapur distt.) and through the country of the Utkalas' reached Kalinga in the Cuttack-Puri-Ganjam area. Probably the Kasai once demarcated the boundary between Vanga and Utkala with the Vangas occupying the delta of lower Bengal as far as the Kasai in the west.

After the Christian era it came to be divided into four separate districts (1) Varendra (lying between Mahananda and Karatoya and corresponding to Pundra), (2) Vanga (east Bengal), (3) Radha (lying to the west of Bhagirathi to Karnasuvarna and (4) Bagdi (Samatata of Hiuen Tsang and Bhati of Akbaranama) lying to the south of Bengal. It seems that from the fall of the later Guptas to the break up of the Sena dynasty the more important divisions of Bengal were Vanga, Pundra, Gauda and Suhma, other important divisions being Karnasuvarna, Varendra, Tamralipti, Bagdi, Samatata and Harikela. During the Sena rule, Vanga was included in Pundravardhana Bhukti. It lay between Karatoya on the west, the Ganges on the south, Meghna on the east and the Khasi Hills on the north and included both Decca and Sunargaon.

## Kamarupa

It was also called Pragjyotisa. There is a legend regarding the name Kamarupa which tells us that Siva burnt Kama (Cupid) for having disturbed him in his penances. Since Kama regained his original form (Rupa) in this country it is called Kamarupa. The Kalika Purana gives the derivation of the name Pragjyotisa as such:- It was at this place that Brahma created in the past (Prak) the various stars and planets (Jyotisah) and hence the name Pragjyotisa. In the later Puranas as well as in the Raghuvamsa of Kalidasa both the words Pragjyotisa and Kamarupa occur as the name of the old province of Assam. Kalidasa applied the name Kamarupa to the province and Pragjyotisa to its capital. The Ramayana, the Mahabharata, the Harivamsa and the Visnu Purana also mention Pragyotisa as a town and not as a country. Kamarupa is a later name and came into use in medieval times. Pragjyotisa, which stood on the bank of the river Lauhitya in the eastern direction, was its capital. When Naraka, who was brought up by king Janaka of Mithua, became the ruler of the country of Pragjyotisa, the name of the country was changed from Pragjyotisa to Kamarupa. It was a beautiful city under him.

Though Kamarupa was regarded, as a Mieccha kingdom during the epic age yet its early contacts with Aryan India are copiously referred to in the Mahabharata and the Ramayana.

According to the Saktisamgamatantra it extended from Kalesvara to Svetagiri and from Tripura to NHaparvata. Hiuen Tsang knew it as Kia-Mo-Leu-Po which was situated 90 li east of Pundravardhana and was about 10000 li in circuit. Its capital was 30 li in circuit. It was moist but crops were regular, and its climate was genial. The Yoginitantra describes its boundaries as such It extends from the Kancana mountain of Nepal to the junction of the river Brahmaputra and from Karatoya to Dikkharavasirii. In the north is Kanjagiri and on the west is the river Karatoya. River Dikshu is on the east and on the south is the confluence of Brahmaputra with Lekshad. It extended for 300 yojanas and was 100 yojanas in length.

The ancient Kamarupa generally occupied an area larger than that of the modern province and extended westwards to the Karatoya river. If included not only the whole of Assam but also the portions of the north and east Bengal, Bhutan, probably also of north Bihar. Its capital Pragjyotisc was not far from the site of modern Gauhati which was the heart of ancient Kamarupa. The river Karatoya formed its western boundary.

It comprised the whole of Brahmaputra valley together with Rangpur (Bangladesh) and Cooch Bihar (West Bengal), Manipur, Jaintia, Cachar (Assam), western Assam and parts of Mymensingh and Sylhet (Bangladesh). According to Beal it extended from Karatoya river in Rungpur to the eastward.

It is bounded on the north by Bhutan, on the east by the districts of Darang and Newgong on the south by Khasi hills and on the west by Goalpara. The greater part of the Kamarupa consists of a wide plain, through the lower portion of which Brahmaputra makes its way flowing a steady course from east to west. South of this river, this plain is much broken by hills. In the north-east of Assam is a small river Dikrang (Skt. Dikkaravasirii). Some of the most important Tirthas of Assam were Siddhesvari, Apunarbhava, Urvasi, Mani-Karnes-vara, Paundunath, Kamakhya, Asvakranta and Hayagriva etc..

The valley of Brahmaputra was divided in ancient times into three tracts, Sadiya (Eastern Distt.), Assam proper (middle Distt.) and Kamarupa. As Kamarupa was the powerful state, its name came to denote the whole valley. On the eastern side it touched the frontiers of south-western boundaries of the Chinese province of Shu. On the eastern side the forests were full of wild elephants. Pragjyotisa, according to Pargiter comprised the whole of north Bengal proper.

The country of Kamarupa with its capital Pragjyotisa was one of the tributary kingdoms on the eastern side of India. It was a frontier state outside the limits of the Gupta empire. Though it paid taxes to Gupta kings, yet it retained its autonomy in internal administration. The Deopara and Madhainagar copperplate records its conquest by Vijaysena and Lakshmanasena. The Belava Copperplate of Bhojavarman says that king Vajravarman crippled the power of the king of Kamarupa. Harsha concluded a treaty with Bhaskaravarman, king of Kamarupa. The Gauda kings also conquered it for several times. Kamarupa was included in the dominions of some of the Pala kings of Bengal. Early in the 13th cent. A.D. the Ahom chiefs ruled over it.

Both the Kamarupamandala and Pragjyotisa Bhukti are mentioned in the Kamauli grant of Vaidyadeva. The Tezpur plates also refer to it.

## Gonarda

It is placed between Ujjain and Vidisa. They appear as Gonaddha in the Pali Buddhist texts.

It is the nearest step to Vidisa, which was the capital of Malava and waS closely connected with Greek politics. The geographical horizon of the Mahabhasya sets itself harmoniously around the Gonarda-Vidisa region as the centre. Patanjali himself is called GonardTya.

Varahamihira places it among the countries of the south. In his geographical nomenclature, he seems to throw the nam'es at the hazard of the metre so that nothing about their relative position can be inferred from a stand on the order of classification.Sircar places it in the heart of Malwa.

## (E) The States lying on the banks of Nartnada.

## Bhrgukaccha

Bhrgukaccha, meaning high coast land, was actually situated on a high coast land in the weston the bank of Narmada. The Markandeya Purana locates it on the river Venva.

It was a very sacred place of high antiquity. The goddess Laksmi built a city named Srinagara for her residence on the hill on which the town of Bhroach was situated. After some time the city became deserted and a forest grew up in its place. It was here in this forest that the sage Bhrgu built his hermitage on a hill side near the river. His disciples and followers occupied the adjacent area. Seeing their large number the sage called Visvakarman and founded this city of Bhroach on the back of the Kurma. From that time onward it came to be known as Bhrgukaccha.

Jaina Suvrata visited it for instructing Jitasatru. The Buddhist writers describe it as a rich and prosperous city. From the time of the Aryans upto the time of Buddhists and early centuries of Christian era it remained an important centre of trade and commerce. It was known as Barygaza to Periplus and Ptolemy according to whom it was the greatest seat of trade and commerce in the western India. It was a large city about 30 miles from the sea on the northern side of the river Narmada. Hiuen-Tsang knew it as Polu-Ka-Che-Po which was 2400 or 2500 li in its circuit. The soil was impregnated with salt and was blackish. Its vegitation was sparse, the trees and shrubs being scare and scattered and the climate was hot. It was situated across the river Narmada at a distance of 1000 li from Maharastra.

Varahamihira mentioned it. A grant of the Gujara king Jayabhatta III refers to it. Broach (Skt. Bhrgu Kaccha), a part of the Lata country, was included in the empire of Ashoka. The Ksatrapa and Andhra rulers also ruled over here for some time. Afterwards it came under the rule of the Guptas.

It was situated on the bank of the river Narmada en its confluence with the sea and was identical with modern Broach.

## Kacchhiya

Panim refers to the Janapada of Kaccha and its people. The country named Kacchhiya denoted the land in the neighbourhood of water and marshy spots. The name still survives in the name of the province of Cutch.

## Samaheya

The Samaheya country lay in the west on the northern bank of the river Narmada. They must have been the people living along the banks of Mahl and the country lying between MahT and Narmada.

## Surastra

The Surastras of the epic and Puranic famelived in the west along the banks of Narmada. The Ramayana applies the name of Surasfra to the country from Sindh to Bhroach i.e. Gujarat, Kutch and Kathiawad. The Mahabharata assigns them to the south-west and records their famous tirthas namely the Camasodbheda, Prabhasa, Pindaraka and Ujjayanta. During the Mahabharata age it was under the Yadavas but by the time of Kautilya the people of the Surastra had a federal form of government. The Baudhayana Dharma Sutra associates them with Daksinapatha. Panini mentions them in his Astadhyayi. Rajasekharaplaces them in the west along with Bhrgukaccha, Anarta, Arbuda, Daseraka and others. The Visnu Purana locates them in the extreme west and associates them with Sura, Abhira etc. all of whom lived along the Pariyatra mountain. In the Padma Purana, Saurastra is included in Gurjaradesa. It is a common name for both the city and the Janapada.A forest named Saurastra was situated between Kusasthali, Avanti, Arbuda and the river Narmada.A desert seperated Surastra and Sakala.

The Buddhists called it Surattha. A stream called Satodika flew along its border. Both the Buddhists and Jains knew it as a great centre of trade.

It is the Syrastrene of Periplus, Syrastra of Ptolemy and Horatae of Pliny. Syrastrene, extending from the mouth of the Indus to the gulf of Cutch, formed one of the three divsions of Indo-Scythia in Ptolemy's time. Periplus also mentioned it as the sea-board of Aberia, the region to the east of the Indus above the insular portion formed by its bifurcation. According to Hiuen Tsang the Sa-la-cha i.e. Surastra had MahT on the west and its capital lay at the foot of the mountain Yoh-Shan-Ta i.e. Prakrit Ujjayanta, the Girnar hill near Junagarh. It was 5000 li away to the west of Valabhi. It was above 4000 li in circuit and its capital was above 30 li.

It was also called Girinagara, Ujjayantadri and Raivata. The epigraphic evidence indicates that Kaundinyapura (modern Kodinar in south Kathiawar), Hastavapra-Ahara (modern Hatab in earstwhile Bhavanagar state), Kalapaka Pathaka (modern Kalavad 60 miles north-west of Porabandar, VamanasthaE (modern Vanthali) in the erstwhile Junagarh state and Shri Somnatha were included in Surastra.

The name still survives in modern Surat, a fertile and still the richest country in India. It comprised modern Kathiawad and other portions of Gujarat.

The Indo-Scythian rule of Saka Ksatrapas was interrupted by a member of the Andhra dynasty Wiliyavakur II 'Gautamiputra Satakarni' (113-138 A.D.). After the Scythian occupation Surastra seems to have passed into the hands of the Guptas. The fall of Sakaksatrapas is alluded to by Bana and also proved by coins.

It capital was Valabhi. Under the Guptas, its capital city was VamanasthaE, modern Banthali, before Valabhi became its capital. When Hiuen Tsang visited Surastra, it was included in the kingdom of Valabhi. It was included in the Maurya empire during the reign of Candragupta for the Junagarh Rock Inscription of Rudradaman refers to Candragupta Rastriya Pushpagupta, the constructor of Sudarsana lake. Later on it was included in Ashoka's dominion for the same Inscription refers to Tusaspa, a Persian vassal of Ashoka, who carried out further repairs on the lake. Later on Tusaspa became an independent ruler of Surastra.

The rule of the Guptas in this region does not seem to have long survived after Skandagupta. Soon the Maitrakas of Valabhi gained their supermacy over western Malwa, Baroda, Gujarat, Kathiawar and the adjoining regions. After the extinction of the Maitraka's rule about the middle of the 8th cent. A.D. the country of Surastra suffered a reverse at the hand of the Tajikas (Arabs). In a Nansari copperplate grant of Gujarat, Chalukya Pulakesiraja (8th cent A.D.) is credited with having defeated the Tajikas who are therein reported to have destroyed the Saindhavas, Surastras etc. before they were themselves defeated by the Chalukya kings.

The Saktisamgamatantra of the 18th century A.D. observes, from Konkana upto Hingulaja, in the west lies the Surasfra country covering 100 yojanas on the coast. This country has also been called Gurjara.

## Sarasvata

The Sarasvatas are placed along the banks of the Narmada. They must have dwelt in the vicinity of Sarasvati, after whom they appear to have been so named. So they might have occupied the region about Prabhasa, near which flew Sarasvati on its way to ocean, and which may be identified with modern Somnath.

## (F) The States lying on the Vindhya Plateau

## Avanti

Though not mentioned in the Vedic literature yet they are known to both the grammarians. Panini and Patanjali. In the Mahabharata, which refers to it as a Janapada they appear as one of the most powerful of Ksatriya clans. The epic places it, along with other western Janapadas such as Surasfra and Kunti, on the banks of the river Narmada. The Brahma Purana places it in Malwa. The Bhavisya Purana locates it to the south of Pancala.
