The Janapada of Avanti, one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas of Jambudvipa lay in the valleys of Vindhya mountain. The king of Avanti in Buddha's time was Pajjota who is believed to have been a contemporary of the king Udena of Kausambi. The Puranas also speak of the intermarriages between the royal family of the Avanti and the ruling dynasty of the Yadus. The Matsya Purana traces the origin of the Avanti people to the Haihaya dynasty. They were one of the five Ganas of the Haihayas, who are said to be a great martial race. Kartaviryarjuna of the Haihaya dynasty was a very powerful king who ruled over Mahismati.

It was closely associated with the Jains also, as Mahavira performed some of his penances here, especially at its capital Ujjayini. A few inscriptions, just as the Nasik cave Inscription of VasisthTputra PulumayT, the first separate Rock Edict of Ashoka in which the Bhoja and the Ristikarastrika territories and their offshoots are placed outside the territorial limits of the then Maurya prince of Avanti, refer to it.

The capital of Avanti was Ujjayini (modern Ujjain in Gwalior) on the banks of the river Ksipra a tributary of the Carmanvati (modern Chambal). It was 6000 li in circuit and had the famous shrine of Mahakala. Hiuen Tsang gives the name Ujjayini to the whole country surrounding the capital which was 30 li round. Periplus places the city of Ozene to the eastward from Barygaza. The Buddhist work Dipavamsa attributes its foundation to Accutagami. In some of the canonical scriptures, Ujjain is located to the west of Kanauj which lies between Ujjain and Benaras.

The ancient city no longer exists, but its ruins can be seen at a distance of a mile from the present Ujjain. Owing to its position it became a great commercial centre and a great centre of science, literature and religion as well. It was one of the seven most sacred cities of the Hindus.

MahismatT, situated on the banks of Narmada was its other capital. The city was founded by Mah'isman in the fifth generation of Haihayas.According to Raghuvamsa the city was girdled by the river Reva (Narmada). Dr. Bhandarkar has suggested that Avanti was divided into two parts, the northern part had its capital at Ujjayini and the southern part at MahismatT. Fleet identified MahismatT with the village of Mandhata in the NTmar District of Madhya-Pradesh. It lay on both the banks of Narmada.

The stories of the famous king Canda Pradyota, a contemporary of Buddha and the marriage of Udayana and Vasavadatta, princess of Avanti are well known in Indian literature. In the 4th century B.C. Ujjayini became subject to Magadha. Before his accession to throne, Ashoka was the viceroy of Avanti. His grandson Samprati ruled over Ujjain. It was the residence of Viceroys of western provinces both under the Maurya and the Gupta empires.

After the fall of Maurya empire, Avanti came under the sway of Gautami Balasri. Vikramaditya, the famous king of Ujjain is said to have expelled the Scythians and established his power over the greater part of India. The Ujjain was also ruled by the Parmar dynasty of Malwa, Tomar clan and Chauhan kings. From Chauhan kings it came under the Mohammadan kings in 1401 A.D.. Akbar suppressed the local dynasty in 1569 A.D. and incorporated Malwa in the Mughal empire.

Avanti was the ancient name of Malwa. From 7th or 8th century onward Avanti came to be called Malwa. But as both the Matsya and the Vayumention Malwa and Avanti as two seperate Janapadas, it seems that they were neighbouring territories which were later on merged together. Rhys Davids places it to the north of Vindhya mountain and north-east of Bombay. Sircar identifies it with western Malwa Nimar and adjoining parts of Central Province (Madhya Pradesh) .

## Anupa

The Anupa country, the capital of which was Mahismati is placed by the Puranas on the Vindhya plateau. The word Anupa literally means any marshy tract or country lying not very far from the sea. Thus, the word Sagaranupa is used in the Mahabharata at several places, e.g. for the tracts known as Anupa in Bengal, in the far south, on the western coast in or near about Kathiawar.

They are mentioned in the Mahabharata.Harivamsa suggests its proximity to Surastra and Anarta. The Vayu Purana associates them with the Vitihotras and Tundikeras, the branches of the Haihaya dynasty. Thus they might have lived near the region ruled by KartavTryarjuna. At another place Vayu Purana mentions Narmadanupa and supports the evidence of Mahabharata. We have another reference to Anupadesa in connection with the story of Prthu which may connect them with the Magadha territory. It has been suggested to be a part of Bengal in the east of Magadha.

The Nasik cave inscription of 'Gautami Balasn and Junagarh Rock Inscription of Rudradaman refer to Anupa and Anupanivrta. The epigraphic evidences show that the Anipas occupied the region south of Surastra around Mahismati on the river Narmada. They may be identified with the country on Narmada about Nimar in southern Malwa.

## Utkala

The country of Utkala, which was so called after Utkala the name of the son of Ila, was situated on the Vindhya plateau on the shore of the southern ocean upto Viraja Mandala. The Brahmanda Purana regards it as a central country while Skandaplaces it on the southern ocean and says that it comprised the territory from the river Rsikulya to the river Suvarnarekha and MahanadT. The eastern boundary of Utkala seems to have extended upto the river Kapisa and to the realm of the Mekalas in the west. The Markandeya Purana locates them in the Vindhya region along with Karusa, Uttamarnas and Dasarnas etc.. The Dronaparva of the Mahabharata ̮ connects Utkalas with Udra, Mekala, Kalinga, Andhra, Nisadha, Trigartta and Vahlika etc. while the Ramayana associates them with Mekala and Dasarna countries. The word Utkala is a corruption of Utkalinga i.e. northern part of Kalinga. It seems that during the Mahabharata age it was a part of Kalinga, but by the time of Kalidasa it had become independent. We see that Utkala and Kalinga were separate kingdoms at the time of the Brahma Purana. It is evident that Utkala was distinguished from Odra or Udra and this distinction was maintained throughout the ancient literature and Inscriptions. The Ramacaritam of Sandhyakara Nandin distinguished Utkala from Kalinga even in the llth century A.D.

The Utkalas are mentioned in both the epics and the Puranas as well. The Saktisamgamatantra places the temple of Jagannath on its boundary.They are known to Buddhists also. The Apadana of the Khuddaka Nikaya of the Suttapitakamentions Okkala or Ukkola or Utkala along with the Mekalas. Raghu having crossed the river Kapisa (mod. Kasai in the Distt. of Midnapur), entered the territory of Utkalas who guided him to Kalinga.Thus the territory of Utkala seems to have the river Kapisa on the north-east and Kalinga on the south.

Various Inscriptions refer to Utkala among which the mention of the Badal Pillar Inscription of the time of Gudavamisra, the Bhagalpur grant of Narayanapala and a 12th cent. epigraph of Gahadvala Govindachandra may be made. A Sonapur grant of Mahashivagupta Yayati distinguished Utkaladesa from Kalinga and Kongoda. The Bhuvanesvara stone Ins. of Narasimha I refers to the building of a Visnu-temple at Ekamra, modern Bhuvanesvara and Purl, in the Utkala visaya. From the evidence of this inscription we may conclude that the Utkalavisaya comprised the Purl and Bhuvanesvara region. Utkala is still the native name of Orissa and may be identified with modern Orissa except Cuttack. Brhatsamhita also mentions Utkaladesa as denoting modern Orissa.According to Basak, it included the country from Midnapore to the rivers Brahman! and Vaitarani.Pargiter says that Utkalas must have comprised the southern portion or modern Chota Nagpur, the northern tributary states of Orissa and Balasore Distt.. The north eastern part of coastal Orissa was included in early times in the land of the Utkalas.

It lay between Kasai and Vaitarani rivers. Roughly speaking, therefore, Utkala comprised the present Balasore Distt. of Orissa together with the parts of the Cuttack Distt. of Orissa and the Midnapore District of Western Bengal. The Inscription of the Bhaumakaras of a later age make it clear that the ancient Utkala country in the Balasore region was roughly known as Uttaratosati while the Ganjam-Puri-Cuttack area was known as Daksinatosali. Gradually the name Tosali lost its popularity and the names Utkala, Odra, Udra or Audra came to be applied to the whole of coastal Orissa and ultimately to the entire Oriya speaking area.

Utkalas were indeed a Vindhyan people in as much as the Chota Nagpur hills are just an extension of the Vindhyan range.

## Dasarna

The Dasarnas are the people of the epic fame as both the epics refer to them. This city was given to Satrughna by Rama. It was prosperous, pompous and on abode of many religions. During the Mahabharata age Dasarnas had more than one settlements because Mahabharata refers to two Dasarnas one in the west conquered by Nakula and the other in the east or south-east subjugated by BhTma. The eastern Dasarna has been identified with a part of Chattisgarh Distt. including the former native state of Patna. A great forest named Kamyakavana lay between Tripurl, Dasarna and Mekala and a desert was there in the west of the country called Dasarnaka.

The Puranas and the Ramayana associate the people of the Dasarna country with Malava, Karusa, Mekala, Utkala and Nisadha etc.. The Vayu and Matsya mention them with Malava, Karusa and Utkala and thus place them some where in central India. The Markandeya mentions Vidisa, the capital of eastern Malwa, as one of the western neighbours of Avanti. According to Dr. Bhandarkar Vidisa, the capital of Dasarna was situated on the Vetravati. The city was also called Vaisyanagara an old name of Besnagar. The famous drama of Kalidasa's Malavikagnimitram refers to the love of Agnimitra, king of Vidisa and Malavika, the princess of Vidarbha. A hill named Nica was situated in their country.

The Buddhist literature, which mentions it as one of the sixteen Mahajanapadas, mention a city named Eraka'ccha in the Dasarna country.

Periplus places the region of Dosarerie towards the east of Masalia (country around Masulipatam in Andhra). Ptolemy locates Dosara in the land of the Kokkonagai who lived in the west of the country 'watered by the Ganges. He makes mention of both the river Dosaron and the people Dosarene.

Pargiter thinks that Dasarna was a Yadava kingdom during the period of Kuruksetra war.They occupied a site on the river Dasarna, modern Dhasan near Saugar that flows through Bundelkhand, rising in Bhopal and flowing into Betwa. It has'generally been identified with Vidisa or Bhilsa region in Gwalior (Madhya Pradesh) at a distance of 26 miles north-east of Bhopal. Eastern or south-eastern Dasarna formed part of the Chattisgarh district in the Madhya Pradesh. The western Dasarna, the capital of which was Vidisa (modern Bhilsa) corresponded to eastern Malwa including the earstwhile state of Bhopal. Its capital at the time of Ashoka was Caityagiri. The capital Vidisa remained as the western capital of Pusyamitra and Agnimitra of the Sunga dynasty. According to Sircar it formed parts of modern eastern Mahva and the adjoining regions. It was also known as Akara which had its capital at Vidisa.

## Karusa

Since the gods purged Indra from his Mala and Karusa here, which stuck to him due to the murder of a brahmin, it was named Karusa. The people of Karusa were the descendants of Karusa, one of the sons of Manu Vaivasvata. In the early and later Vedic literature they are seldom mentioned. It appears that they were a very insignificant tribe during these days. Their later appearance in epics and Puranas is very sudden. They are placed between the Matsyas and the Bhojas on the back of the Vindhya mountain and are generally named with the people of Malava, Kerala, Utkala, Dasarna and Arbuda. The Visnu Purana definitely locates them along the Paripatra hills. This is same as Adhiraja, the kingdom of the Dantavakra of the Cedi lineage. They were related to not only Cedis but to Yadavas also through matrimonial alliances.The Ramayana mentions Karusa and Mlada together. They were great warriors. Vedagarbha-puri (modern Buxar) is said to have been situated in the Karusadesa. In the Bhagavata Purana it is used as another name for Pundra, which shows that the Karusas had another settlement in the territory of Pundra vardhana. The Vayu Purana says that the territory of Karusas was located near the forest of Supratika.

Pliny refers to them i.e. Malada and Karusas under, the name of Molindas and Chrysei. According to Pargiter they lay to the south of Kasi and Vatsa, between the Cedis on the west, Magadha on the east and Dasarnas on the north enclosing west the Kaimur Rills, i.e. the country of Reva from the Ken river in the west to the confines of Bihar in the east. They, being determined fighters, protected northern India perhaps from southern inroads.

It may be identified with the southern district of Shahbad in Bihar between the rivers Sona and Karmanasa as a local Inscription discovered at Masar in this district designates the territory as Karusadesa.

## Tosala

They are the Tosalei of Ptolemy. They are associated with Daksinakosala, comprising the whole of the upper valley of MahanadT and its tributaries from the source of Narmada on the north to the source of MahanadT itself -on the south, and from the valley of the Wenganga on the west to the Hasda and Jonk river on the east.

Tosalas were the people of Tosali which denoted both the country as well as the city. Ashoka had the seat of his provincial government of Kalinga at Tosali which may be identified with the ruins of a city near Dhauli in the vicinity of Bhuvaneshvara in the Puri district of Orissa where the Ashoka edicts have been discovered.

Tosala in medieval times appears to be divided into two parts, Daksinatosala and Uttaratosala. As Ptolemy assigns Tosali to the east of the Ganges, Lassen and Burnoff concluded that there were two cities of this nameLassen places Ptolemy's Tosali somewhere in Decca province of Bangladesh.

Regarding the location of Tosala, R.D. Banerjee also writes, The Central tract consisting of the modern distt. of Cuttack, Puri and the northern part of the Ganjam distt. along with some of the small states on both the banks of the MahanadT such as Ranpur, Nayagadh, Khandpara and Daspalla, Tigiria and Athgadh was called Tosala. Most probably the country between the Chilka lake and Mahendragiri was called Kerigoda, because one of the epigraphic charters make it clear that Kerigoda was situated in the southern Tosala.

## Aundra

The earliest known reference to them is found in the Manavadharmasastra where they are classed outside the Brahmanical pale, alongwith the Sakas, Paradas, Pahlavas and Cmas etc.. They are the same as Udra of the Natyasastra of Bharatamuni and Brhatsamhita of Varahamihira, Odra of Yoginitantra, Aundra of Padma Purana , Telugu Odhrulu or Kanrese Oddaru. The Mahabharata has both the readings i.e. Undra and Odra. The Buddhist work called it Oddaka.

They are associated with the Paundras, Utkalas, Mekalas, and Kalingas. The Mahabharata regarded them to be southern people. According to the Brahma Purana Odra country extended northward to Biraja Mandala (Jajpur) and consisted of hree Ksetrae called Purusottama or Sriksetra, Savitu or Arkaksetra, and Birajaksetra through which flowed the river Vaitaram. The Skandapurana places Odra on the banks of the southern ocean.

Pliny in his Natural History mentions Oretes as Indian people in whose country stood mount Malaus. In another passage he locates them in between Monedes and Suari identified by Cunningham with Mundas and Suras. According to Cuhningham, the Oretes must be the people of Orissa. Hiuen Tsang travelled from Karnasuvarna south-west for about 722 li and then reached the Wo-Tu or U-Cha country i.e. Aundra country, which was about 7000 li in its circuit. The soil was rich and fertile and the climate was hot.

The Utkalas are mentioned in a few inscriptions also sueh as Tirumalai Rock the Ins.' of 13th yr. of king Rajendra Cola and Adilpur Copperplate of Narendra-Bhanjadeva. It cannot be said with precision as to what was their original home. Perhaps they lived originally in the land lying to the north of the ancient Utkala country. It is just possible that the conquest of Utkala by a king of Udra led to the use of Udra to denote the whole of Udra-Utkala region i.e. the whole of coastal Orissa.In the later half of the 6th century there was a struggle between the Vigrahas and the Manas for the sovereignty of coastal Orissa'. The Vigrahas, the ruler of the whole of TosaE were driven by Manas first from Uttara-TosalT and then from Daksina-Tosai. Thus in the beginning of the 7th cent. A.D. Manas were ruling over the whole of the coastal Orissa. If these Manas may be said to have belonged to the Udra clan, it would be very easy to explain the popularity of' the name Udra in the sense of the whole of Coastal Orissa from the sixth century or seventh century.

The fact that they conquered Utkala or UttaratosaE sometimes before the expansion of their power over Daksina TosaE may be the reason underlying the use of Udra as a synonym of Utkala first in the sense of the Balasore region and then to indicate the Cuttack-Puri-Ganjam area. If the Mana family belonged to the Udra clan, it may be supposed that the Udras generally lived in the Manabhum and Singhbhum region and the adjoining parts of Orissa. Thus Aundradesa may be identified with modern Orissa.

## Nisadha

The earliest known reference to them is found in Panini's Astadhyayi where they are named as Naisadha. The Mahabharata mentions a mountain named Giriprastha in the country of Nisadha.

The Puranas place it on the Vindhya plateau.The Visnu Purana refers to the nine kings of Naisadhas while the Vayu mentions the king of Naisadha country who belonged to the Manidhanya dynasty , They all were descendants of Nala and lived in the Naisadha country. The epic and the Puranic traditions attribute their origin- to the primeval king Prthu, son of Vena and they seem to have derived the name from Nisadha, father of Nala. Nisadha is the name of the city as well as that of the Janapada.

It is difficult to give their exact location. But it is sure that they did not live very far from Vidarbha, the country of Damayanfi, the queen of Nala. From the direction given by Nala to Damayanfi, Wilson thinks that it was near the Vindhyas and Payosni river and near the roads leading from it across the Rksamat to Avanti and the south as well as to Vidarbha and to Kosala.

Lassen places it along the Satpura hills to the north-west of Berar. Burgess also places it to the south of Malwa. They were associated with the Pariyatra and might have lived about Nalapur (modern Narwar in the Sivapuri Distt. of Madhya Pradesh), the capital of Nala, which is placed on the right bank of the Sindhu, 40 miles to the south west of Gwalior, somewhere near the Satpura range.

## Bhoja

The Bhojas are once mentioned in the Rgveda.Sayana takes them to be the Ksatriya descendants of Sudasa. The Aitareya Brahmana mentions them as a southern people who made the Satvatas their subject. These Satvatas have been located near the Ganga and the Yamuna. It seems, therefore, that the Bhojas spread over central and southern India in very early times. They were one of the five branches of the Haihayas, other four being Vitihotras, Saryatas, Avantis and Tundikeras. According to the Puranas the Bhojas and Saryatas. or Satvatas were allied tribes with both belonging to Yaduvamsa. The Vi-snu Puraria alludes to a branch of the Satvatas as Bhojas. According to the Bhagavata Purana Satvata had seven sons named Bhajmana, Bharji, Divya, Vrsni, Devavrdha, Andhaka and Mahabhoja. By the epic period at least Bhoja and Satvata became undistinguishable, as we see that the same person, Krtavarma is called both a Bhoja and a Satvata in the Mahabharata.They were related to Andhakas, Vrsnis and the Haihayas also., who were the branches of the Yadavas. At several places in the Puranas they are said to be the branches of Yadu family who dwelt at Mathura on the banks of Yamuna. The Mahabharata gives their descent from Druhyu, the third son of Yayati but they had friendly relations with Pauravas, sons of Puru, the favourite son of Yayati. Another tribe with whom they were closely associated is the Kukuras. The expression Dandakabhoja in the Brahmanas suggests that either they were included in or were within the reach of Dandakas. The Ramayana ranked them among the southern nations. In the Puranas they are seen as associated with the Utkalas, Uttamarnas, Dasarnas and Kiskindhakas etc. and are placed on the Vindhya plateau. During the epic age they seem to have spread far and wide as they are found in the west, and in the south as well for king Bhismaka, father of Rukmani is called Bhoja. They had matrimonial relations with Avantis and with Magas of Sakadvipa. They were called Bhojakas as they were born out of wedlock of a Maga father and a Bhoja mother. Like Magas and Sakas Bhojakas were also the worshipper of Sun.

Its capital was Bhojakata. A story is told about the foundation of Bhojakata in the Mahabharata. When Krsna carried away Rukmin's sister Rukmaru by force from his father's capital Kundinapura, Rukmin swore that he would not return back without defeating Krsna. As he was defeated he, true to his words, did not return to Rukminapura but built a new city on the site of the battle field and called it Bhojakata.

Regarding its name Smith writes The name implies that the province was named after a castle formerly held by the Bhojas' This city of Bhojakata is said to have been conquered by Sahadeva. If Bhojakata be the same as Bhoja and Bhojya then it must be a country of Vindhyan region, a fact approved by the Vayu and the Matsya Puranas. It is clear from Mahabharata list that Bhojakata was distinct from Bhojakatapura or Bhojapura the second capital of Vidarbha.

According to the Bhagavata Purana its capital was Mathura. In the Khila Harivamsa, Bhojakata is expressly identical with Vidarbha.

The Jain sacred books refer to them as Ksatriyas. They are known to Buddhists also.Ptolemy mentions them under the name of Stagabaza (i.e. Tataka Bhoja).

Ashoka R. Edict .V and XIII refer to Raetrikas, Bhojas and others who lived in his dominion and who held the present Thana and Kolaba Distt. of Maharashtra state. The Arulal Perumal Ins. and the Ranganatha Inscription of Raviverma refer to a Bhoja king belonging to the Yadu family of Kerala country in southern India. The Khalimpur grant of Bharmapaladeva of Gauda (800 A.D.) mentions king Bhoja along with the'kings of the Matsyas, Kurus, and the Yadus etc.. The Hathigumpha Inscription of Kharvela informs us about the victory of Kharvela over Bhojakas.

It is now ascertained from Inscriptions and other sources that there have been several Hindu celebrities, some of whom dwelt remote from central India, denominated Bhoja. Of Kanauj alone there were two Rajas so named, and so it was probably from one of them that Bhojapur near Farrukhabad, derived its designation. Thus they are placed (i) on the right bank of the Ganges, 30 or 35 miles from Kanyakubja, (2) near Dumraon in the Distt. of Shahbad in Bengal, (3) in Berar of ancient Vidarbha and Chammaka, four miles south east of Elichpur in Amaraoti Distt. and (4) in the present Rajputana.The Bhojas are also placed along the Vindhya chain but at different times they appear to have occupied different territories. A Bhoja Raja is amongst the warriors of the Mahabharata (Sabha Ch. 426, Drona 488). At a later period Bhoja, the king of Dhara, preserves an indication of the people and from whom Bhojapuris, a tribe still living in western Bihar professes to be descended. They are not improbable relics of the older tribes. According to Sircar they originally lived in Berar but later on founded a kingdom in the Goa region. They are also supposed to be Yadava tribe dwelling in the north-east Gujarat while the main branch inhabited the western slopes of the Vindhyas.

## Vitihotras

Vitihotras, a branch of the Haihaya race,descended from the king Vitihotra. The Haihayas were divided into five clans namely the Bhoja, the Vitihotra, the Avanti, the Tundikera and the Talajangha. The Haihaya king KartavTryarjuna ruled at Mahismati and Vitihotras .probably lived on Narmada to the south of Avanti. The Tundikeras and the Vitihotras are placed in the geographical li'sts behind the Vindhyan mountains and the termination Kaira is common in the valley of the Narmada as Barikaira etc.

## Tundikera

The Tundikeras Uke the Vitihotras belonged to the Haihaya race. They might have been related to the town of Tendukhera, a little to the north of the source of the Narmada. We may have Tundikera abbreviated as Tunddri, on the Taptee.

## Mekala

They have been placed both in the south and on the Vindhya mountain. They were previously Ksatriyas but were later on degraded. They are seen associated either with Ambasthas or with Utkalas.

They occupied the territories near the Amarakantaka hill. There, is a Mekala mountain, a peak in the Amarakantaka range, from where issues the river Narmada. The Mekala range is the connecting link between the Vindhyas and true Satpura and stretches from the Khairogarh area ? Madhya Pradesh to the Rewah region. So they may easily be located on the Mekala hills and the country around.

## Tripura

Tripuri or Tripura was both a city and a country and derived its name from Tripura demon. The Brhatsamhita places them in the south-east. The Revamahatmya places Tripuriksetra, where Siva threw down Tripura, to the north of Narmada. The same Mahatmya gives a story connected with Tripura. The demon Bana received from Siva, pleased as he was with his penances, a gift of a city. Brahma and Visnu added one each, thus he obtained the capital of Tripura. When killed by Siva, as he was traversing the heaven, a part of his carcase fell near the mountain Srisaila in Siddhaksetra, another not far from Amarakanfaka, and the remaining in the vicinity of Gangasagara.

The Tripuravisaya roughly corresponded to the Jabalpur region (ancient Cedi country) and the city of Tripuri is our present Teor on the river Narmada, seven miles to the west of Jabalpur. Yule identifies it with Tripura, a town in the district of Tippera 48 miles east, south-east of Dacca.

It is the same as Triglypton of Ptolemy, the three districts being Kamila, Chattala and Burmanaka or Rasanga.

## Vaidisa

The Vaidisa people lived in the neighbourhood of Avanti, on the mountain Vindhya in the western direction. The country of Vaidisa was given to Satrughatin, son of Satrughna. It was situated on the bank of the river Vidisa and was included in the kingdom of Avanff. Rama is said to have reached the city of Vaidisa after crossing the Rksa mountain.

According to Kalidasa the city of Vidisa, situated on the river Vetravati, was the capital of the Dasarna.

The Besnagar Inscription testifies to the existence of diplomatic relations between the Greek king of Taxila and the king of Vidisa. Kalidasa refers to the love of Agnimitra (king of Vidisa) and Malavika (princess of Vidarbha). There was a war between Vidisa and Vidarbha in 170 B.C. in which the former was victorious. Vidisa was one of the many small kingdoms into which the Yadavas appear to have been divided.

Since the time of Ashoka it became an important centre of Buddhism and later on of Vaisnavism. It was also the centre of communication between the seaports of the western coast and Pataliputra, and between Pratisthana and Sravasfi. It was a halting place on Daksinapatha.

The dominion of Pusyamitra extended upto the nver Narmada and included Vidisa, Pataliputra and Ayodhya. It remained as a western capital of Pusyamitra and Agnimitra of Sunga dynasty.Sungas were especially associated with the kingdom of Vidisa in literature and inscriptions. They ruled originally as feudatories of Mauryas at Vidisa.'After ruling Vidisa as viceroy Agnimitra succeeded his father and ruled for eight years.

When the Sunga rule came to an end, one SisunandT began to rule at Vidisa. Perhaps the residual power of the Sungas lingered side by side with the suzerainty of the Kanvas. It is generally assumed that first Vidisa and then Ujjayini became the official headquarter of Candragupta II.

Vidisa, also called Vaisyanagara may be identified with modern Besanagar, a ruined city situated in the fork of the Bes or the Vedisa river and Betwa. This ancient city has been identified with Bhilsa in Gwalior state and was situated at a distance of 26 miles N.E. of Bhopal. It lay at a distance of 50 yojanas from Pataliputra.

## Kiskindha

About a mile easterly from Nimbapur, a small hamlet in the suburb of Bijannagger, lies an oval-shaped heap of calcareous scoria, partially covered by the grass and other vegetation. The Brahmins aver it to be ashes of the bones of the giant Ball. It is still called by that name and also by the name of Anagandi. It is a small hamlet situated in Dharvad on the southern bank of the Tungabhadra near Anagandi, 3 miles from Bijaynagar and close to Bellary, with the Tungabhadra and Vedavati for its chief rivers.According to Dey it comprises the hills on the opposite side of the valley that seperates it from Humpi. In the opinion of Sircar they may be the people of Kekind in Jodhpur or of the Ramayanic Kiskindha, identified with the Raichur-Bellary area of Mysore and adjoining regions.

The Patus Uttamas, Tumura and TumburasMasas and Padgamas cannot be satisfactorily identified.

## Malava

Though not mentioned by Panini, the Malavas are referred to in the Mahabhasya, which refers to the republics of Ksudrakas and Malavas. The Vayu and the Matsya locate them on the Vindhyas while the other Puranas associate them with Surastras, Avantis, Abhiras, Suras and Arbudas and locate them on the Pariyatra mountain. Others simply call Malava a mountainous territory. The Mahabharata places it in the western direction and associates them with Sudrakas. The Greek historians also mention the two people together as Sudracae and MaUoi.

The Saktisamgamatantra places them to the east of Avanti and to the north of Godavari. Thus it indicated eastern Malwa, the ancient name of which was Dasarna or Akara having its capital at Vidisa. The Kamasutra distinguishes between Avanti and Malava, and its commentary identifies Malwa with Purvamalava and gives the name Avanti to Ujjain region. The Kadambari of Bana, too, associates Malava with the river Vetravati surrounding Vidisa and speaks of Ujjayini as a city of Avanti country i.e. the present Malwa.

The Malloi, Malli or Mallai of Alexander's historians occupied the Punjab region. Hiuen Tsang gives the extent of U-She-Yenna (Ujjayini) as 6000 li with its capital of 30 li.

Originally the Malavas lived on the lower Iravati in the Punjab in the 4th cent. B.C. Smith locates them in country below the confluence of Jhelum and Chenab i.e. the Jhang Distt. and a portion of Montgomery Distt. of Pakistan. McCrindle assigns to them a greater extent of territory so as to include the Doab of Ravi and Chenab extending to the confluence of the Indus and the Akesines i.e. Multan Dis-tt. and parts of Montgomery (Pakistan).

Ray Chaudhuri places them in the valley of lower Ravi on both the banks of the river. The Mahabharata associates them with the Trigarttas, Sivis and Ambasthas and places them in Punjabbut before long they seem to have migrated southward and settled somewhere in Rajputana where they seemed to have lived in Samudragupta's time. The fact that the legend on some of the Malava coins found in Rajputana have to be read from right to left as in Kharosthi, the prevalent script in Punjab and the north-west, proves that the Malavas migrated to the Jaipur region of Rajasthan. The Nasik cave Ins. of Usavadata also corroborates it. The Scythian invasion and conquest did not affect them and they appear as a western tribal state in the Allahabad Pillar Ins. of Samudragupta. After Samudragupta, they migrated to the Mandsor region of Madhya Pradesh. It seems that they touched Gujarat a fact approved by the evidence of Hiuen Tsang's work and the copperplate- grant of Valabhi king Dhruvasena dated G.E. 320-321. After about 550 A.D. they migrated still east and spread themselves from Bhilsa to Prayaga. During the rule of Palas, they migrated still further east.

Some have 'tried to identify Malava with Mo-La-Po of Hiuen Tsang but this may not be right for it was then under the Maitrakas of Valabhi. It also' cannot be identified with that Malava country whose capital was UjjayinT for Ujjayirii at that time was ruled by a Brahmin dynasty and the Guptas were certainly not Brahmins. Moreover, according to Vatsyayana Ujjayinidesa was called Aparamalava or western Malava whereas Malava only should be taken to mean eastern Malava.

The name of Malava is also associated with the well-known Krta or Malava (Vikrama) era. Later epigraphic records make mention of Saptamalavas.

Thus we see that the Malava tribe had different settlements in different parts of India. The branch here might had been the one settled in and around that portion of Malava which borders on the Vindhya. They still survive in the name of modern Malwa as also in the name of the Brahmana castes called Malaviyas or Malavikas. These Brahmanas belong to the Malava proper and the adjoining country, and are also found in Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh and Uttara Pradesh.

## Aparita

Aparitas are the same as the Aparanta people.The term is used in two senses, (i) a particular tribe and (ii) all the western tribes of India. The Vayu Purana mentions them as independent tribe of northern India. The Mahabharata locates them in the southern India on the western ghats and uses the term as another name for Surparakaksetra.

The Aparitas are the Aparytoe of Herodotus who classed them with a bordering people in India i.e. Gandaril. The Ariake of Periplus extended southwards from the Gulf of Cambay to the north of Abhira. The Ariake of Ptolemy extended south-ward from Narmada. The Nasik cave Inscription of Gautamiputra Balasri uses it to denote a narrow region of north Konkana, the capital of which was Surparaka (modern Sopara) near Basein.The same is proved by other Inscriptions of Satavahana kings and Rudradaman. The Aparanta in its extended sense included not only Surparaka but Nasik Bharu- Kaccha the Mahl valley, Cutch Surastra, Anarta and Abu etc, and in its restricted sense it denoted the region of the Konkan in the north of Bombay which included Thana -district.

The Raghuvamsa places it on the south of Murata.

Usavadata's Ins. shows Nahapana's political influence as spreading from Poona and Surparaka (north Konkana) to Mandsor and Pushkara. The Nasik record of queen Gautami Bala sri shows that her son ruled over ???? nta as well as other western countries. Later on it was again conquered by Saka Ksatrapa Rudradaman.

According to Bhagavan Lal Indraji, the» Aparanta people occupied the western sea-coast of India. Sircar places them to the west of Malwa,and they are said to have occupied the territory extending from river Mahl to Goa.

## Mountainous Countries Urna: Huna

The Hunas were a northern mountainous tribe which was'associated with the Darva, Khasa, Salva, SauvTra, Saindhava, Sakala and Madra etc.. The Ramayana does not refer to them. The Mahabharata, however, mentions them and associates them with the Parasikas. They were considered sinful and outside the pale of Brahmanism. No work prior to the 5th cent. A.D. except the Raghuvamsa, refers to them. In the Saktisamgamatantra they are placed to the south of Ramagiri and north of Marudesa i.e. desert. Harsacarita, which places them in the Punjab suggests the same area. Ultimately the Hunas were regarded as one of the twenty six clans of the Rajputs.

Dey places them in the country round Sakala or Sialkot (Pakistan) in Punjab and the country round Manasa lake. They first make their appearance about 165 B.C. when they defeated the Yueh-Chi and forced them to leave their lands in north-western China. Gradually they, too, moved westwards in search of fresh fields and new pastures. The branch which proceeded towards the Oxus valley came to be known as the Ye-Tha-I-Li or Ephthalites. The other branch gradually reached Europe. From the Oxus, they turned towards the south about the second decade of the 5th cent. A.D. and crossing Afghanistan and the north-western passes eventually entered India.

They invaded India for the first time in 455 A.D. during Skandagupta's reign but were beaten back. But about 465 A.D. a fresh swarm of nomads crossed the frontier and occupied Gandhara i.e. north-west Punjab. Gradually they advanced to the interior and again attacked Skandagupta who could not resist their repeated attacks and had to succumb.

Huna may be identified with white Huns or Indo-Scythians who were established in Punjab and along the Indus in the beginning of the Christian era as we know from Arrian, Strabo and Ptolemy, their locality being confirmed by the recent discoveries of the coins.

Hall, commenting on the above statement of Wilson writes In the middle ages it is certain that a race called Hunas was understood, by the learned of India, to form a division of the Ksatriyas. Inscriptions testify to the fact that in medieval times Ksatriyas married Hunawives. Venkata Adhvarin, in his Visvagunadarsa pretty evidently means the Portuguese, where he gives the estimate of the Hunas. The Pandits of the present day, as I know from having heard them very often employ Huna as synonymous with Faring! or Fran....... Wilson, himself adverting to the Huna or Turk tribes that figure in Chinese history, suggests, in as much as these tribes did not appear until several centuries after the beginning of the Christian era, in as much the theatre of their recorded exploits is remote om India, that the coincidence of appellation may be merely accidental. The Hunas are often mentioned in connection with the Gurjaras (modern Gujars). The early Gurjaras seem to have been foreign immigrants, closely associated with and possibly allied in blood to the White Huns. The Huns were assimilated in Hinduism and were admitted readily into the frame of Hindu polity as Ksatriyas or Rajputs and thus the Pratiharas and many other famous Rajput clans of the north were developed out of the barbarian hordes which poured into India during the 5th and the 6th cent..

Urnas are said to be the people of northern hilly regions. They are said to have occupied the region of modern Una situated few miles ,west of Indus.Lassen places them in Urnadesa on the Sutlej near Garhwal. They are said to be the people of Unasar in Swat region. Regarding them Rahula Samkritya-yana writes that they never actually reached Hindustan, but the clans of Toramana and Mihirkula were taken to be the Hunas. They were, in fact, a clan of.Sakas and somehow managed to remain in their motherland (Sakadvipa) upto the 5th cent. A.D. when they were forced to flee to central Asia. They destroyed the Kusanas, defeated Skandagupta in 455 A.D. and occupied India. Their king Toramana had an extensive empire from the Caspian sea to the central India. His son Mihirakula attacked Magadha. It was in 534 A.D. that he could be defeated by the joint power of Yasodharman of Malava and Baladitya of Magadha, and was forced to take refuge in Kashmir. During the reign of Toramana and Mihirakula a large portion of the Himalayas seemed to be in their hands. The country to the north of the Manasa lake and the Nilam Pass is still known to the people of Garhwal as the Hunadesa.

## Daradas.

Daradas, belonged to the Ksatriya stock formerly and were later on degraded. They have been placed in the northern mountainous region, where they have been associated with Kashmitras, Abhisaras, Tanganas, Tusaras, Barbaras, Paradas, Cinas, Khasas, Sulikas, Lampakas and Gandharas.The Sanskrit word 'Darada' itself means mountain. The Pandavas passed through Darada country while going to the capital of Subahu. The river Sindhu is said to have watered the territory of Daradas.

The Daradas are placed to the east of Lambatai (Lamghan) and of Sorestone (Basin of Swat) and to the north of the uppermost course of the' Indus, along the north-west frontier of Kasmira. Strabo gives them the name Dardai and connects them with gold producing area probably of Tibet. Pliny places Dardae near the source of the Sindhu. The same location is given by Megasthenes also.

Their territory can be traced to Daradistan, north of Kashmir, on the upper bank of the Indus. Their capital was Daratpuri identified by Stein with Gurez lying in a valley which is nowhere more than about one mile broad.

The seat of the Daradas has not changed since the time of Herodotus, Strabo and Ptolemy, not exactly indeed at the source of the Indus but along its course, above the Himalayas, just before it descends to India. Their territory extended from Chirtral and Yasin, across the Indus regions of Gilgit, Cilas and Burji to the Kishanganga valley. The people living from Gilgit to Lozila are called Daradas even to day. Atkinson places them in Gilgit and Astor. They have been placed in the region to the north and north-east of Indus extending from Chitral and Gilgit to Ladakha and Tibet.

The Daradas are mentioned in many passages of Kashmir Chronicles. Kalhana speaks of Miechhas further to the north. They might have been Mohammadanized Daradas on the Indus and beyond.

## Nasikya

Nasikya, which is the same as Nasiki or Naisika of the Puranas, Janasthana of the Ramayana or Nasika of Ptolemy and Nasikya of the Mahabhasya, and Brhatsamhita was a mountainous country in the west. It was situated on the Narmada within the reach of Pancavati of Godavari for we are told that the 'Nasika' of Surpanakha was chopped off here. The Nasika country is referred to in the two oldest Inscriptions No. 20 and 22 in the caves. It occurs in the Barhut votive label No.38 also. The Buddhiat caves in Nasik, known as Pandulenas and situated about 300 ft. above the road level are very well known. It is modern Nasik, 75 miles north west of Bombay. Nasik, the head quarter of the Nasik distt. lies on the right bank of the Godavari about 4 miles north west of the Nasik road station. The city contains three parts, old Nasikya or the Pancavati on the left bank of the river Godavari, middle Nasik built on nine hills on the right bank of the river Godavari to the south of Pancavati and modern Nasik on the right bank of the river to the west of Pancavati.

## Kiratas

The Kiratas were a non-Aryan aboriginal mountainous tribe. They were considered to be impure, sinful and 'sraddha-Bahya.' They are mentioned in plural as Kiratanam Jatayah and have been placed in the north, north-east, north west, east, south-west, midland and as living on the mountains. They had been associated with Yavanas, Kambojas, Gandharas, Hunas, Pulindas, Abhlras, Khasas, Ketas, Lampakas, Daradas, Sulikas, Barbaras, Cinas, Tusaras, and Miechcas living on the Mandara mountain. In the epics and' the Puranas the name indicates barbarous tribes of eastern frontiers of Aryavarta. Kalidasa places them in the eastern valley of Brahmaputra, and they are especially referred to in one of the Kavyas, the Kiratarjuriiyam. The Saktisamgamatantra assigns them the territory from Taptakunda (hot springs at Rajgir in Patna distt. of Bihar or those near Monghyr) to Ramaksetra (Ramagiri or Ramtek). Those living on the Vindhyas denote a particular tribe.

The Nagarjunikonda inscription refers to them. It is certain that they had several settlements. Their first settlement was near Arsanians (Skt. Urasa). It appears that with the advancement of the Aryans towards the east, the Kiratas who were the aborigines had to flee in different directions. In the midland they lived between Yamuna and Ganga, with the Kurus occupying the region to their south. The river Ganga watered their territory before it reached the land of Kurus. In the north they resorted to the slopes of Himalayas. In the east their territory lay on the bank of HIaldinI, one of the three eastern currents of the Ganga and also on the Nila mountain in the Purusottamaksetra of Utkala (modern Orissa).

Pliny and Megasthenes refer to them as Skyrites. They were flat nosed people of primitive origin dwelling in forests, mountains and living by hunting. Pliny placed them in the territory lying between Tons and Sarda (Kali), Garhwal and Kumaun.

Periplus places them to 'the west from the mouths of the Ganges. They were known as Cirrhadas which was a Bhota tribe. Their descendants are still known as Kirata living in the Morung, west of Sikkim. But they never lived on the sea, as indicated by the text, but in the valleys of the Himalayas.

The Kirrhadia of Ptolemy lived both in the north and the east. They spread not only over the Gangetic India, but also over countries further east.

Lassen places one of their branches on the southern bank of KallSl in Nepal and another in Tipera. They were one of the many Bhota tribes whose name still survives in modern Bhutan. They were allied to Tibetans and inhabited much of Bengal at the time of Aryan migration. Their native capital was at Makwanpur in eastern Nepal. They were a warlike, uncultivated, polygamous race, whose neglect of religious rites caused the Brahma to reduce them to the rank of the Sudras.

Dey places them in Tipera. Their territory included Sylhet and Assam and they occupied the region from Nepal to the extreme east. Wilson used the term Kirata for foresters and mountainous people. They inhabit even today the mountains of eastern Hindustan. They occupied the centre of Kamarupa. Law places them in the Himalayas possibly in Tibet. They lived as criminal tribes with predatory habits like those of hunters and vultures.

The Kedaramandala was known as Kirata-mandala before it was occupied by the Khasas. They lived on the western bank of Kali in Askot where still a tribe called Raji, the descendants of Kiratas, lives.

According to a tradition, still prevalent in Tripura, the first name of the country was Kirata. The Kirata kings had a considerable settlement in Nepal, and held the valley in sway in succession to the Abhiras. The Nepalese still give the name Kirata to the country between Dudhkosi and the Aruna and there is evidence that the Kiratas once occupied a much more extensive area in Nepal.

## Kasmira.

The earliest known references to Kasmira are found in grammatical works, Kautilya's Arthasastra, the epics, the Puranas and the Brhatsamhita. They were a northern mountainous tribe. According to an ancient tradition recorded in .the Nilamata Purana also the country of Kasmira in the hoary past formed a vast lake and was so from the beginning of the Kalpa upto six Manvantaras. It was 6 yojanas long and 3 yojanas broad. A daitya named Jalodbhava, who had obtained a boon from Brahma that he would not be killed in water, lived in it. He could not restrain his diabolical spirits and set about to devastate the neighbouring countries of Darvabhisara, Gandhara, Saka Khasa, Tangana and other settlements. Kasyapa, after roaming in various Tirthas arrived at Kasmira and came to know all about Jalodbhava. He went to Brahma for the destruction of Jalodbhava'who in turn sent him to Visnu. Visnu promised and went to Naubandhana Sikhara.. All the gods accompanied Visnu. There Visnu asked Ananta to split the Himalayas by his tail and make the divine lake empty of water. Thus the demon came to be killed. Since then the lake was turned into a populated valley. The Buddhists also hold that the country was a dragon lake. Kasyapa then settled in the land of Kasmira thus produced. The gods took up their abode in it as well as the Nagas, while the various goddesses adorned the land in the guise of the rivers. Previously men dwelt in it for six months only in the year. This was due to a curse of Kasyapa who angered by the Nagas had cursed them to dwell for the other six months together with the Pisacas. Accordingly men left Kasmira for the six months of winter and returned annually in Caitra when the Pisacas withdrew. After four yugas had passed thus a Brahmin named Candradeva, through the favour of NUanaga, acquired a number of rites which freed the country from the Pisacas and excessive cold. Since then Kashmir became inhabitable throughout the year. This legend *oi* the desiccation of the lake is also referred to by Hiuen Tsang, with a Buddhistic slant. It is also prevalent in popular traditions and is also reproduced in all the Mohammadan abstracts of the chronicles. From Haidar Malika Tarikh it became known to Dr. Bernier and thence to every European account of Kashmir.

According to some geologists like Mr. Drew and Colonel Godwin Austin, a few geological observations such as the locustrine deposits found in the Udras or Karewa plateau support the belief that Kashmir was, in comparatively late geological times, wholly or in great part occupied by a vast lake. But in the view of a later authority even the presence of true locustrine deposits does not prove that the whole of the Kashmir was ever occupied by a lake. Even now true locustrine deposits are still being formed in the hollows of the rock basin, represented by the lakes of the north-west portion of the valley. Probably the conditions have been much the same throughout the geological history of the Kashmir valley, only a minor area of it having at various periods been occupied by lakes.

But it is certain that the locustrine deposits of Kashmir, though of no remote date from the geological point of view are far older than the oldest known monuments of man. The great drainage operation took place at various periods of Kashmir history which extended the cultivable ground and reduced the area covered by lakes and marshes.

Kalhana also, relates the legend told in the Nilamata Purana regarding the liberation of the land from the Pisacas who are substituted by Yaksas in Kalhana's account.

The Nilamata Purana derives the name Kasmira thus: (i.e. who was made Prajapati ? Kasyapa was Prajapati. Since it was founded by Kasyapa, it would be called Kasmira, or Kam is water. Since water was removed from this country, this would be named Kasmira.)

Its old name is said to have been Kasyapapura which is said to have been connected with the Kaspasuros of Hekatois. The Caspapyra of Periplus is the Greek form of Sanskrit Kasyapapura. It is said to be the adjoining district of India proper. Ptolemy names it as Kasperia and its capital Kaspira. Hiuen Tsang gives its extent as 7000 li in circuit. It was enclosed on all sides by high mountains. The soil was fertile and the climate cold and stern. The capital of the country on the west side was bordered by a great river which must be the Vitasta. It was 12 or 13 li from north to south and 4 or 5 ? from east to west.

According to Sadpancasaddesavibhaga of Saktisamgama-tantra it lies between Sharadamatha and Kumkumadri and extends over 50 yojanas. Kumkuma hill may be the saffron growing plateau above Pampur near Shrinagar. Saradamatha is the modern Sardi near the confluence of the Kishanganga and Kanakatori rivers in Kashmira. Buddhist works refer to it as inhabited by the Nagas.

In ancient times Kasmira was included in the Gandhara kingdom. It is mentioned in the Nagarjunikonda Inscription of Virapurushadatta. In Ashoka's time it was included in the Maurya dominion. Rajatarangim clearly mentions Ashoka as a ruling king of Kasmira.

The old Kasmira territory must be identified with the present Kashmir state lying to the north of Punjab. Throughout its history, it was called Kasmira both by its people and by foreigners. Its extent, too, practically remained the same throughout the period of so many years. The name always denoted the valley watered by the headwater of the Vitasta and to the inner slopes of its surrounding mountains. The Padma Purana places it near the river Vitasta. Even the foreigners like Hiuen Tsang, On-Kong and Alberuni recorded the sharply marked natural limits of Kasmira. The Rajatarangim of Kalhana and his successors chronicles prove that the limits of the traditional Kashmir never extended beyond the mountain ranges that surround it.

Kasmira is a fertile plain in the midst of high mountain ranges. The slopes, of the mountains descending towards the central plain are drained by numerous rivers and streams, all of them falling into the Vitasta within the Kashmir plain. The higher zones of the mountain slopes are covered with magnificient forests and above this extend rich alpine pastures close up to the line of perpetual snow.

## Darvabhisara.

Sometimes the Darvabhisaras are mentioned as separate tribes and sometimes as one. They are said to have been a mountaineous people living in the neighbourhood of Kasmlra. According to the Mahabharata they were a Ksatriya tribe, but were later on excluded from the pale of Ksatriyism by Sagara.

It appears that they occupied the whole tract of the lower and the middle hills lying between the Vitasta and Candrabhaga. From Rajatararigim and the topographical points discussed therein it is clear that the hill state of Rajapuri (Rajauri) was included in Darvabhisara. It was subjected to Kasmira in the time of Utpalaplda but Samskarvarman had again to conquer this territory before he proceeded against Gurjaras to the south of Bhimbhar. But during the time of the following weak rulers this attainment was made futile. They occupied the lands immediately to the south and west of the Kasmlra valley and always created troubles for their neighbours. The country of Darvabhisaras roughly corresponded the Punch - Naushehra region between the Jhelum and the Chinab and was probably an offshoot of the old kingdom of Kamboja. It was the Dogra region of Jammu.Lassen places the Darvas between the Indus and Jhelum in the north-west of Kashmir. According to McCrindle Abhisara was the region of mountain girt-valleys now called Hazra which lies between Sindhu and Vitasta.

## Khasa.

The Khasas were a northern hill tribe in the vicinity of Kasmira. They have been associated with Daradas, another famous people bordering on the Khasas on the north and with Sakas also. They came to be known as Miecchas when degraded by Sagara. The Mahabharata places them near the river Sailoda between the Meru and the Mandara mountains. If we may take this Sailoda to be the same as the Sailodaka of the Matsya then they must have originally settled somewhere in Tibet or further north-west. The evidence of the Kalika Puranashows that our ancient historians and geographers knew Khasas even in the centuries just before and after Christ.

The Khasas figure in the Buddhist chronicles of Kasmira and those of Ceylon also where they are mentioned as the people subdued by Ashoka in upper Punjab. The name Khasa of Kasmira chronicles was given to a comparatively limited region, comprising the valleys lying immediately to the south and west of the Pir Pantsal range between the middle course of the Vitasta in the west and Kastavata (Kishtvar) in the east. At various places in the Rajatarangini the rulers of Rajapuri (modern Rajauri) are called the lord of Khasas and their troops as Khasas. They occupied the valleys of Ans river, now called Panjagabhar (Pancagahvara of Srivara IV 213), Banasalo (modern Banihal) and the valley leading from Bahihal to the Candrabhaga i.e. modern Bichtari (Visalata of Chronicles). They later on settled in the valley of Khaisal (ancient Khasalaya) which leads from the Marbal pass in the south-east corner of Kashmir down to Kishtvar.

The Khasas are mentioned by Ptolemy. Pliny knew Cesi as the forest tribe living between the Indus and Jamanes. Babar knew them as living in the high valleys in the neighbourhood of the eastern Hindu-koh. The name Khasa has been used since early times in Sanskrit literature for the people settled in widely different parts of the Himalayan region. As they are mentioned in the Inscriptions-of Palas and Senas of Bengal, Wilson traces them amongst the barbarous tribes on the north-east of Bengal. Stein places them in the Rajapuri (modern Rajauri), Lohara and the valley below Varahmula because VTranaka is regarded as a seat of Khasas.According to the expression Kedare Khasamandale it appears that Kedara-Khanda is synonymous with Khasadesa.

About 2000 B.C. Khasas reached the Himalayas via middle east Asia i.e. Kashtgar and Khotan. We have no evidence in the Vedic literature that the Aryans lived in the Himalayas. They (Khasas) came to India, far before the Aryans and extended themselves from Gilgit to Kali Nadi and then to the eastern border of Nepal. The region from Gligit and Karakoram was occupied by Khasa-Daradas who belonged to the same stock. The Khasas who came earlier and the Aryans who came later drove the Kinnara-Kiratas occupying these territories, to the forests and remote parts and degraded them. The Khasas and the Vedic Aryans lived harmoniously since they belonged to the same family.

The tribes living in the territories from Kangra, Gligit to Nepal are called even today Khasha or Kasha. Thus their territory, which included once the area from Kashtgar to the whole of the Himalaya, had been very extensive.

Kashmir and the Kashmirians are called Kha-Kha (a direct derivative of Khasa). These Kha-Kha chiefs of Vitasta valley retained their semi-independent position until Sikh times and along with their neighbours of Bomba clan have ever proved troublesome neighbours for Kashmir.In the Kashmir census report of 1891 (p. 141) they are classed as a subdivision of the Mohammadan hill Rajputs. The people living beyond Kashmir in Chitral, Kashkar, Yassana and Mastuja are called Khu. The Kulinda, Kuneta and Kanet in Simla and Kulu hills are only subdivisions of the Khasa race.

Atkinson discussing the various evidences writes that according to Pliny, the Khasas at that time lived in the extreme west of their present habitat in Kumaun and Nepal. The tribes east to Kasperaioi living in the Kashwar and Chamba came under the Khasas. The Kunets of Kulu are divided even today between Khasia and Pawa subdivisions. Even today the Tanganas, a subdivision of Khasas, live in the upper part of the Sarada. The Khasas, Koas and Katoras of Kabul, are the same and the other western Himalayan tribes belonged to the same stock. Later on they extended to different parts due to the political reasons and the immigration of other foreign tribes.

## Tusara

The northern Janapada of Tusara lay on way to Dvaitvana from the Gandhamadana mountain. It was crossed by Pandavas when going to the country of Subahu. It was under the mieccha king Mandhata, in the north-west quarter, on the banks of Caksu. They lived in the Vicinity of Kiratas, Tarigana and Kulindas. Ptolemy mentions them under the name Tokharoi. These are probably the Tochari, Tachari or Thogari, a Saka tribe by whom Bactria was taken from the Greeks, and from whom Tocharestan derives the name it still bears. It has been identified by Eitel with Yueh-she, the country of the Indo-Scythians of Greeks and Tartars of the Chinese writers, who destroyed the Bactrian kingdoms in 126 B.C. and finally conquered Punjab and other parts of India. Kanishka was originally a king of Yueh-she. The statement of Hiuen-Tsang about the country of Tu-Ho-Lp makes it clear that they gave their name to the upper Oxus valley including Baikh and Badakshan. They occupied Tukharistan of the Arabs which included the old Bactria and the mountainous country on both the sides of the middle as far as Badakhshan.

Prof. Stein thinks that they were a branch of Yue-che tribe where as Prof. Bagachi holds that Yue-che, Tokharoi and Tukhara were identical.

## Urasa:Uraga

Urasa was a local name denoting in ancient times the hill region lying between the upper course of the Vitasta and the Indus. The country of Uraga lay between Abhisari and Simhapura and Uraga was a mountainous capital of northern India. Hiuen Tsang calles it Wu-la-shi and gives its circuit as 2000 li with its capital of 7 or 8 li in circuit. The land is said to be cultivatable and the air soft and agreeable.

The accounts of Alexander's Punjab compaign name this kingdom as Assakas. Ptolemy knew the Arsa or Ovarsa and its town Itagouros and Taxila, and places it between upper waters of the Bidaspes and Indus i.e. in Hazara Distt. The kingdom of Wu-La-Shi of Hiuen Tsang was situated to the north west of Kasmira and was dependant on it, though this dependence seems soon to have ceased. Pliny and Megasthenes mention a people belonging to these parts called the Arsagalitae.

The Mohammadans generally knew the whole hill tract as Pakhali, which according to Abul Fazi comprised the whole of the hill territory between Kashmir in the east and Indus in the west, lower valley of Kishanganga, the valleys of the stream which flows into Kishanganga from the Kajnag range and the mountains to the north-east of Kashmir. The evidence of the RajataranginT fully confirms this identification and its close connection with Kashmir, which existed in Hiuen Tsang's time and which continued even in later days.

The capital of the Urasa country lay perhaps between the present Mansahra and Abbottaba. Cunningham identifies it with modern district of Rash in Dhantawar to the west of Muzzaffarabad which is to the north west of Kashmir. It included the hilly country between the Indus and Kashmir as far south as the boundary of Attock.

## Kusapravarana

The Kusapravaranas, who are variously called as Kuthapravarana, Kunta-pravarana ' or Karnapravarna occupied the Eastern Himalaya though Mahabharata places them on the shore of the southern ocean.

## Nigaraharas

The Nigaraharas were a mountainous people who cannot be satisfactorily located but they must have been living on the snowy slopes of the Himalayas. Their country has been identified with the town of Nagarahara situated at the confluence of the Surkhar or Surkend and Kabul rivers near Jalalabad (Afghanistan). McCrindle identifies Nagarahara with Nanghenhar or Nanghinar, 4 or 5 miles to the west of Jalalabad. Hiuen Tsang gives the extent of Na-Kie-lo-ho from east to west as 600 li and from north to south as 250 or 260 li. It is said to have been surrounded by over hanging precipices and natural barriers. Its capital was 20 li or 30 li round. Its climate is said to be moist and warm and the country had abundance of cereals, flowers and fruits.

It is Nagara or Dionysolopolis of Ptolemy and Nyas of Alexender's historians. Babar also writes the name as Nangenhar and Nekerhar, which had nine streams. It was to the east of Kabul and was one of the five Tumana Districts of Lamghan. Nunigihar is the name of the Kabul Valley. In 1570 A.D. the town of Jalalabad was built by Akbar. ' According to Lassen it was the capital of the Greek kingdom, perhaps of Agathocles and Pantaleon, who exhibit the symbols of Kionyson on their coins and it was situated on the southern bank of the Kabul river, not far from Jalabad. The name of Dionysopolis existed even at the time of Mahmud of Gazni, for Alberuni mentions the town of Dinus as being situated between Kabul and Peshawar. Jalalabad contains 40 topes dating from the commencement of the Christian era to 700 A.D. on the southern bank of the Kabul river. Nagarahara was the extreme boundary of India. The inscription found at Guserawa 10 miles to the south-east of the town of Bidar, mentioned the name of Nagarahara which is said to be situated in Uttarapatha.

## Trigartta

Panirii associates them with the Yaudheyas which implies their close proximity. Trigarttas were a northern mountainous tribe. Trigartta has been interpreted as watered by three rivers i.e. Ravi, Bias and Sutlej. It is also said to be the country of three strongholds and is identified by some with the modern hill state of Kotoch, which is called Trigarta Ka Mulk.

Numerous references in the Mahabharata go to prove their habitation in Punjab, a fact supported by Abhidhanacintamani, which speaks of Trigartta and Jalandhar as synonymous and Rajatarangimwhich speaks of it as being a region not far from Kashmir. The Visnudharmottara Mahapurana places it beyond the Vipasa. Epigraphic evidences also locate them in the country between Ravi and Satlej with its centre round Jullundur. Thus it comes to denote the modern hill district of Kangra situated btween the mountains of Chamba and the upper course of the Bias. The grant of Soma Varmadeva refers to Kuluta, Kira and Trigartta.

Wilford identifies the place with Tahora, or Tihar situated on the river Sutlej, a few miles from Ludhiana, where interesting ruins were observed by Captain Wade.

Though not much is known about their authentic political history, yet it is certain that'the Trigarttas were a dependency of one or the other king of the Kashmir dynasty during the period 700-1150 A.D.. The other minor tribes that are mentioned in the Puranas as situated in mountainous regions are Apatha, Sarvaga, Mandala and Camara (all referred to in Matsya P.11455-56 only), Ksupana (Vayu Purva. 45135), Kupatha (Matsya P.11447, Vamana P. 1357, Brahmanda P. Purva. Anu. 1667) and Hamsamarga (Vayu Purva. 45135, Vamana P. 1367, Mark. P.6441-56, Brahmanda P. Purva. Anu. 1667 and Brahma P. 2560).

## The Mountains of India

## Himalayas

The Himalayas, also called Himacala Hemadri, Himalaya, Haimavata, Himavat, Himvatprastha, Parvataraja and Nagadhiraja, is the most important of the Indian mountains. It is said to have extended from east to west for 8000 yojanas and is stretched from the eastern sea to the western sea like the string of a bow to the south of the Meru mountain, to the west of Gandhamadana and to the north of Bharata.

The mountain is of the Vedic fame. Both the epics mention it. It is amply described in the Puranic literature as well. The Vayu Purana describes it in details. It places the Himalayas to the south of the Kailasa. Many mythological legends are connected with this mountain. The gods Sankara, Karttikeya and others, and goddess Parvati are closely associated with it. The famous Umavana, Saravana and the hermitages of many sages are situated here. The Matsya Purana gives a detailed description of this mountain. It is covered with perpetual snow and has got many lofty and sky-touching peaks which are covered with a dense forest of Devadaru trees. Its inhabitants are demigods and semihuman beings such as Gandharvas, Kinnaras, etc.. A long list of Himalayan flora, fauna and beasts appear in the Matsya Purana.

The Himalaya of Pali Buddhist literature is a vast region, 500 leagues in height and 3000 leagues in breadth. According to the southern Buddhist conception it extended upto the Gandhamadana range which is a part of the Rudra Himalaya. In the Jain work called Jambudivapannati the Himalaya and Hemakuta are placed in between Bharata and Harivarsa, the latter lying to the south of the former. The Himalaya is mentioned in some of the literary works also.

According to the ancient geographers the name Himavat was applied to the entire mountain range stretching from the Sulaiman along the west of Punjab and the whole of the northern boundary of India upto the Assam and Arakan hill ranges. The Vanaparva of the Mahabharata places the Himavat region just to the west of the Nepalavisaya and it is said to have mainly comprised that portion in which the sources of the Ganga, Yamuna and the Sutlej lay. It has been identified with the Bolor chain which formed the boundary between Turkestan and China.

Ptolemy called it Emodos and Imaos. He applied this name to the central chain from the region of the sources of the Ganges (where the Indus and Sutlej also rise) to beyond the source of the Jaxartes.

Some have identified it with Tibet, others with Nepal and central Himalayas. Law includes in it the parts of the modern Himachal Pradesh, adjoining tracts and some parts of Dehradun. The Himalayas stretch 1550 miles from Afganistan to Burma, in breadth being 200 miles including the foot hills. From west to east they run through the states of Kashmir, Uttara Pradesh, Nepal, Sikkim and Bhutan.

## Himalayan Peaks

Though a number of Himalayan peaks are mentioned in the Puranas, yet it is very difficult to identify them in the absence of proper evidence. Their location is given only with respect of Kailasa, the only peak which can be successfully identified.

## Kailasa

The Kailasa mountain, also known as Hemakuta, Bhutesagiri and Sankaragiri is said to have been situated in the midst of the Himalayas and as extending towards east and west for 80 yojanas and as having entered the ocean on both the sides. The Vayu Purana mentions it as the middle top of the Devakuta, and gives its extent as 100 yojanas in length and 50 yojanas in breadth. The Matsya Purana which describes it beautifully mentions it as the middle portion of the Himalayas.

The river Mandakini issues from the lake Mandodaka which is situated at its base. The Nandana forest is situated on the banks of the Mandakini. The rivers Nanda and Alakananda also flow there. It is the abode of god Siva.

Both the epics mention Kailasa. The Mahabharata includes the Kumaun and the Garhwal mountains in the Kailasa range. The Jains refer to it as the Astapada mountain. It is mentioned in the Sanskrit literature and the Purushottampuri plate of Ramchandra.

The Kailasa range runs parallel to the Ladakh range, 50 miles behind the latter. It contains a number of giant peaks. It is the same as Vaidyuta mountain. The Tibetans call it as Kangrinpoche. It is stated to be about 25 miles to the north of the Manasa lake beyond Gangri, which is also called Darchin, to the east of the Niti Pass. Four rivers are said to have issued from Gangri mountain or lakes, Indus on the north, Satadru on the west, KarnalT Son on the south and the Brahmaputra on the east. The famous sacred place'of Badarikasrama is said to have been situated on it.

## Mainaka

The very statement that it is the son of Mena, the wife of Himalaya goes to prove it to be a Himalayan peak. Mahabharata, too, mentions it as a part of the great Himalayan range near the Kailasa and the lake Bindusarovara.

Some locate it in the south. The Mahabharata mentions a mountain named Mainaka, near the Vaidurya mountain in the vicinity of Narmada and yet another in the Krauncadvipa. Asvaghosa refers to the Mainaka mountain as entering the river to check the course of the ocean. The same story is alluded to in the Ramayana which locates it in the Daksinapatha, but actually it is only a fabulous mountain in the sea between India and Ceylon. It appears to have been situated at the source of the Sona, that river being termed as Mainakaprabhava Paigiter gives the name Mainaka to the group of hills near the eastern source ol the Ganges in the north of the Almora distt.. It may be identified with the Siwalik range extending from the Ganges to the Bias.

## Candraprabha

This mountain is situated to the north-east of the Kailasa mountain. It is said to have extended upto Suvela or Suvarna mountain. A lake named Acchoda is situated at its base, from which issues the river Acchoda, on the bank of which is situated the forest named Caitraratha. The Brahmanda Purana places it near Meru.

## Hemasrnga or Lohita or Suryaprabha

It is said to have extended upto the mountain Suvarna, in the south-eastern direction of the Kailasa mountain. It is situated in the vicinity of the Pisanga mountain. At its base is situated the lake Lohita, from which issues the great river Lauhitya on the bank of which is situated the forest named Visoka.

If we take the river Lohita to be the same as the river Brahmaputra and the lake Lohita to be the Lake Ravanahrada from which the river Candrabhaga and the Brahmaputra rise, then this peak must be in Lahoul or Middle Tibet. Rahula Samkrityayana gives the name Hemsrnga to the Nagasikhara of the Himalaya.

## Kakudman or Trikakuda or Trikakut

It is placed to the north of Meru in the northwestern side of the Kailasa mountain and has been identified with the Yamunottari mountain. There is a hill named Trikuta 8 miles north west of Vaidyanath in the Santhala Pargana in Bihar, which is a group of hills with three curious peaks.

## Vaidyuta

In the front of Trikakut near the Anjana mountain and to the south of the Kailasa is the Vaidyuta mountain abounding in minerals. Lake Manasa is situated at its base, from which rises the river Sarayu on the banks of which is situated the forest named Vaibhraja.

It is a part of the Kailasa range known to Ptolemy as the mount Orondion at the foot of which the Manasarovara lake is situated. Evidently it is the Guria range on the south of the Manasarovara.

## Aruna

The Aruna mountain, which abounds in animals and herbs is situated to the west of the Kailasa range. On Arunacala is situated the temple of Arunacalesvara and Ardhanarisvara Mahadeva. Another mountain of the same name in the south may be identified with Tiruvannamalai or Trinomali in the south Arcot distt. in Andhra Pradesh.

## Sriman

The mountain Sriman is situated in the vicinity of the Aruna mountain. It is very high, inaccessible and is always covered with snow. The lake Sailoda is situated at its base, from which rises the river Sailoda which flows between Caksu and Sita and goes to the salty ocean. A forest named Surabhi is situated on its bank. The goddess Gauri, in the guise of goddess Sri performed penances here. It is called Srngavan also.

## Gaura

The Gaura mountain, abounding in animals and herbs is situated to the north-west of the Kailasa mountain. The lake called Bindu is situated on it. The mountain is called Sarvausadha in the Matsya Purana. It is also known as Gaurisikhara or Gaurisankara. The royal sage Bhagiratha performed penances here and it was here that the Ganga appeared. It has been identified with the mount Everest in Nepal but locally it is not known by that name. Captain Wood's measurement have proved that the Gauri Sankara of the Nepalese cannot be the mount Everest.

## Varuna

The mountain Varuna is situated to the west of Kailasa. It has got high peaks abounding in all sorts of herbs. Other Himalayan peaks are Chagalaand Pisanga.

The Jatakas, Mahavastu, Ashoka Rock Edict XIII, Bana's Harsacarita, Kaisi Rock Edicts, the Ashokan Monoliths at NilgTva, LumbinY, and those in the districts of Champaran and Apadana refer to the Himalayan peaks. The Monghyr grant of Devapala refers to Kedara, a Himalayan peak.

## Attahasa

It is one of the Himalayan mountain peaks, which has been identified with the eastern part of Labhapur in the Birbhum district of Bengal and is seven miles from the Amodpur station of the eastern Railway.

## Anjana

The Anjana mountain which is called Meruputra is said to have been situated to the west of the lake STtoda and in the vicinity of the Brahmagiri. The commentary on the Yogavasistha says that it is famous as NTladari, which, if accepted, would place it in Assam. It is identified with the Sulaiman range in Pakistan.

## Kraunca

The Kraunca mountain is said to be the son of Mena and Himavan. It is the elder or younger brother of Mainaka. At other places it is said to be the son of Mainaka also. It indicates that the Kraunca mountain belonged to the same group of mountains. It is said to have extended from the eastern ocean upto the western ocean. After this mountain is named the country as Krauncadvipa.That portions of the Kailasa on which Manasa is situated included Kraunca-randhra.

## Hemakuta

The mountain Hemakuta, a Varsaparvata of the continent of Jambu, was also known as Bhutesa gin which is surrounded by the river Nanda also known as Ganga. It is said to be shorter by 12000 Yojanas than the mountain Rsabha, the extent of which is given in the Puranas as 1 lakh yojanas. Thus its extent comes to be 88000 yojanas. The Visnu Purana gives its extent as 90000 yojanas and height and breadth as 2000 yojanas. The extent given here is fabulous and far from reality. Jains knew it as Astapada mountain.

It is said to have been situated to the south of Meru and Nisadha and to the north of Himavat.The rainfall here is due to 'Tusara' only hence the breeze that blows on 'Himagiri' is always snowy. Since this mountain is described along with Himavan in the Purarias, it appears that either both were situated side by side or the one (Hemakufa) was the part of the other.

The Mahabharata, which places it in the northern direction calls it Rsabhakuta, while in the Matsya Purana Rsabha is mentioned as a seperate mountain. It is said to be another name for Kailasa mountain. The Varaha Purana gives the source of the rivers Alakananda Ganga and Yamuna in this mountain. Thus it has been identified with the Banderpunchchha range of the Himalayas. The Kailasa and the Bunder-punchchha ranges were called by the general name of Kailasa.

The Kumarasambhava describes the Himalayas as plunged into the eastern and western ocean. The same is said about the Hemakuta mountain. The fact that Dusyanta's chariot descending from the sky stoped at the Hemakuta, and that the Hemakuta includes the Kasyapa's hermitage i.e. Kashmir, points out that the Hemakuta mountain denotes the higher attitudes of mountains in Kashmir. The epithet Kanakarasanisyanda sandhyameghap-arigha iva sanuman as applied to Hemakuta suggests it to be Haramukuta mountain with its dark blue stone, which is known to us as a source of the golden Kanakavahirii river in Kashmir. Thus the Hemakuta in the Vikramorvasiya (Act I) may denote the northern mountains of Kashmir.

## Indrakila

It is one of the Himalayan peaks situated near Jahnavi beyond Gandhamadana. It is said that to practise penance and to please Siva to secure Pasupata weapon, Arjuna came on the Gandhamadana mountain at a place called Indraklla hill. It is described at length in the Kiratarjuriiyam of Bharavi. It is identified with a place called BhTlesvara between Rambag and Shrinagar in Garhwal. The Bhilvakedara was 'the site of the fight of Arjuna and Kirata.

## Gandhamadana

The Gandhamadana mountain, another Himalayan peak has been placed to the south of the Kailasa and Meru. The Vayu Purana and the Linga Purana place it at a distance of 9000 yojanas from Meru in its western direction, and it is said to have extended in the northern and southern direction upto the Nila and Nisadha mountains. At another place, the same Purana places it to the west of Varaha and Malyavan. The Skanda Purana places it to the south of Badari.

Its extent has been variously given as 34000 yojanas 32000 yojanas and'80 yojanas, the last one being the most acceptable. There live the people of the country of Ketumala. A fragment of this mountain is said to have been brought by Hanuman and is said to be situated near Rameshvaram in southern India. Perhaps it is on account of this that the Skanda Purana says that a bridge was constructed by Rama on the Gandhamadana mountain in the southern ocean. The Gandhamadana is mentioned in the Buddhist and classical Sanskrit literature as well. According to the epic writer it formed a part of the Kailasa range, starting from a short distance to the north-east of the Badarikasrama, and is watered by the Mandakirii river. On it were situated the hermitages of the sages Nara-Narayana and Valakhilya. This mountain forms a part of the Rudra Himalaya and the Garhwal mountain through which the Alakananda flows. The Kamet-Gandhamadana range is behind Nanda-Devi and Badrinatha.

## Trikuta
