The Trikuta mountain is the same as Trikakud, Srngavan and Trisrnga, a mountain in the north of Meru. The Matsya Purana places it in the north-west of Kailasa. The Visnudharmottara Purana emphatically says that Srngavan is Trikuta.

The Bhagavata Purana refers to it as a mountain at the base of the Meru. The Vamana calls it the son of Meru showing its relation with the Meru mountain system. The Agni Purana locates it to the south of the Arunoda, Mahabhadra, Sitoda and Manasa lakes. It is associated with the mountain Citrakuta in the Vamana Purana. A mountain called Trikuta is referred to by Kalidasa as a minor hill connected with the Sahya range also. Evidently it is the mountain from which the Traikutakas derived their name. The mountain on which Lanka is situated was also named Trikuta. This Trikuta is situated in the north-east corner of Ceylon and has been identified with Jaunar.

## Bhrgutunga

This peak, which is said to have been named after Bhrgu, is a Himalayan peak. On the evidence of Varaha Purana (ch.146) it has been located as a mountain in Nepal on the eastern bank of the Gandaki. This appears improbable if we reply upon the evidence of Vayu that after ascending the Bhrgutunga one goes to the holy river Sarasvati. It has been placed on the southern bank of the Narmada by the Skanda Purana.

According to Nilakantha, it is the Tunganath mountain which is one of the Pancakedaras. The Tunganath stretches from Badarinatha range upto Rudraprayaga on the banks of Alakananda. This is the range that separates the valley of Mandakim from that of Alakananda. This Bhrgutunga has been identified with the modern Bhairavajhanpa.

## Nilaparvata

The Varsaparvata called Nila. is placed to the north of the Meru beyond Ilavrta. Its extent given in the Visnu Purana is fabulous in its character.The Varsa of the Nila mountain is called Ramyaka.A lake called Payoda is situated on it from which issue the rivers Payoda and Pundarika. The Padma Purana locates Nilaparvata, having the temple of Purusottama on it, near the sacred Gangasagara-Samgama. It is a hilll in Utkala, modern Orissa. The temple of Jagannath is situated on a gentle slope by the sea which is known as Nilaparvata. Another hill about three miles from'Guwahati in Assam, on which the temple of Kamakhya is situated is also known as Nilacala. Yet another hill is located in the region where the sacred places Gangadvara, Kusavarta, Vilvaka and Kankhal are situated.

## Mandara

The mountain Mandara is a Viskambha mountain in the east of Meru and Ilavrta. The Purana s associate it with the mountains Gandha-madana, Mainaka and Meru. It is closely related with Meru with which it is repeatedy mentioned.The evidence of the Padma Purana establishes their close proximity. On it is situated the Caitraratha forest. Covered with snow it has got beautiful caves. It was Mandara which was used as a churning - rod at the time of churning of Ksira-Sagara and it was here that the demon Mahisa was killed. The god Siva, after his marriage with Parvati, is said to have his abode here. Since it is invariably associated with the Himalayas and Kaiasa, Mandara, too, must be an allied peak of the Himalaya. The Kumarasambhava places it in the vicinity of the Kailasa and Gandhamadana. The fact that the sage Kandu performed penances on the banks of Gomati and enjoyed with the nymph called Pramloca in the cave of the Mandara mountain also makes it a peak of the Himalaya system. The Vayu regarded it as the son of Meru through Dharani, which shows that it was in some way related to Meru system of mountains as well.

In some of the Puranas the BadarTkasrama is said to have been situated on the Mandara mountain but the Mahabharata places the Mandara mountain to the north of the Badarikasrama and regards it as a portion of the Himalaya mountain to the east of the Sumeru (in Garhwal). Since the chief territories of the Kiratas lay in the three mountains i.e. Kailasa, Mandara and the Hema mountains, it appears that the aforesaid mountains were in the proximity of each other.

It is the Maiandra of Ptoemy, Mandagara of Periplus and Mallus of Megasthenes and Arrian.

It has been identified with the mount Mandara about 1200 ft. high in the Banka subdivision of the Bhagalpur district on the Bhagalpur Bansi line, 30 miles to the south of Bhagalpur and 3 miles to the north of Bansi, and stands near half way between Rajmahal and Curruckpur hills. It is an insulated conical mountain and is distinctive because it is a mass of granite while all surrounding hills are of limestone. The tanks, named Sitakunda (100 ft. large ̮and 500 ft. wide), Ramakunda, Sankhakunda and Akasaganga, ruins of Buddhists temples and images and a temple containing the foot prints of Visnu are situated on it.

## Vipula

The sacred mountain Vipula is said to be situated on the western side of the Meru mountain. The forest Vaibhraja and the lake Sitodaka is said to be situated on it. The Bopyrrhos of Ptolemy extends from the source of Sarayu to that of Tista and included Sikkim, Himalaya, and the Garo hills.Hiuen Tsang mentions it as Pipulo which is situated to the west of the north gate of the mountain city i.e. Rajgrha. On the north side of the south-west of this mountain there were 500 hot springs of which only a few cold and tepid ones were in existence in his time. Some regard that the mountain Pipulo of Hiuen Tsang is not Vipula but Vaibhara. Ragarding this Law writes, But it might be equally well restored to Vaibhara or Baibhar as Cunningham, in his map of Rajgir (Arch. Sur. Rep. Vol I Pt. XIV), places Baibhar to the west of the north gate of the town. It would be more agreeable to restore it so. On the other hand as Hiuen Tsang places the hot springs on the south-western slopes of Pi-Pu-Lo and we are told that the hot springs of Rajgriha are found at the eastern-foot of the mount Baibhar and western foot of the mountain Vipula, it would seem that he must be speakirg of Vipula.

The Buddhists mentioned it as the best mountain of Rajgriha and locate it to the north of the Gijjhakuta, in the midst of the girdles of the Magadhan hills. It was known in a very remote age as Pacinavamsa. It was later on changed to Vankaka. It then received the name Supassa and ultimately it came to be known as Vepule.

It was one of the five hills encircling Rajgrha. It runs for some length towards the south-east leading to the northern range of hills extending upto the place called Giryek on the Bihar-Sarif-Newadah road.

## Naubandhana-Sikhara

According to the legendary accounts, Visnu in his first incarnation (Avatara,) had bound to this peak the boat into which Durga had changed herself to save the seeds of all beings from destruction. The Nilamata legend holds that the sage Kasyapa and the people, oppressed as they were by Jalodbhava, went to Brahma for his destruction. Brahma assured them his help and said Very soon we would be going to the Naubandhana peak and then Kesava would easily kill him. Accordingly they did. Rudra took his seat on the Naubandhana peak, Hari on the southern peak and Brahma on the northern peak. Ultimately Jalodhavci was killed and people were relieved. Proceeding westwards from Banahal Pass is the group of three snowy peaks reaching above 15000 ft. The Kashmirian tradition locates on them the seats from which Visnu, Siva and Brahma, according to the legend already related, fought Jalodbhava and desiccated the Satisara. The western most and highest of these peaks (15523 ft.) forms the famous Naubandhana nrtha.

## Visnupada

The Visnupada mountain is said to be situated in the Kramasara lake. The god Brahma made his hermitage in the midst of the waters on the Visnupada mountain. At the foot of this peak and to the north-west of it, lies a lake over two miles long, known now as Konsar Nag, the Kramasaras or Kramasara of the NHamata and Mahatmyas. Since it is supposed to mark a foot-step (Krama) of Visnu, it is named Visnupada mountain.

## Haramukuta

The Haramukuta peak lies near the Manasa lake. To the east of the Dudakhut Pass the summits of the range gradually rise higher and higher until the great mountain mass of the Haramukh peaks are reached. These peaks, which are about 17000 ft. in height, are surrounded by the glaciers of considerable size and dominate the view towards the north form a great part of the Kashmir valley. 'There are many legends connected with this mountain and the lakes below their glaciers are regarded as the holiest of the Kashmirian Tirthas. The Haracaritacintamani contains a legend regarding its name, Haramukuta. This Haramukh mountain is 20 miles to the north of Shrinagar.

## Gaun Sikhara

The Gaurisikhara is a Himalayan peak. The goddess Parvati is said to have performed penances here. The Sunworship, which Kalidasa associates with the Kasyapasrama or Kashmir in Sakuntalam is also noticable at this peak, which may thus be easily identified with the site of that name in Kashmir. It is a peak of Kamet range in Malla Penkhanda (79° 42'x30°43').

## Mahadeva

The whole mountain range stretching to the south of Triphar and along the Dal was called Sridvara in ancient times. On the opposite side of the valley rises the old peak of Mahadeva to the height of over 13000 ft.. Numerous references to it are found in the Nilamata Purana, Sarvavatara and other texts which regard it as a sacred place.

## Varaha

It is a hill near Baramula in Kashmir. The mountain on which ancient Pragjyotispura was situated is also called the Varaha mountain.

## Tungaprastha

It is the Tunganatha Sikhara, 12071 ft. above sea level in Kashmir, at a distance of 3 miles from Chopta chatti.

## Krsnagiri

It has been identified with the Karakoram mountain or the Black mountain also called Mus-Tagh. The mountains Jatayu, Narada and VedasTrsa were also the Himalayan peaks.

## Himalayan Rivers

Though the number of the Himalayan rivers is given as much as 500, yet only a few important ones are indicated by names. They are:- Ganga, Sindhu, Sarasvati, Satadru, Candrabhaga, Yamuna, Sarayu, Iravati, Vitasta, Visala, Devika, Kuhu, Gomati, Dhutapapa, Bahuda, Drsadvati, Kausiki, Tritiya, Niscala, Gandaki, Iksu and Lohita.

Ptolemy notes that the Imaos is the source of the Ganges and Indus as well as of Koa and Swat.The river Mrgsammata flows down from the Himalaya and enters the Ganges. The river Uha is stated to have been located in the Himalayas. The Kalika Purana mentions Maha Kausiki and a small river as flowing from the Himalaya mountain.

The Matsya and the Vayu Puranas mention a number of lakes situated on the Himalayan peaks. Thus the lake Mandodaka is situated on Kailasa, the Acchoda on the Candraprabha, the Lohita on the Hemasrnga, the Manasa on the Vaidyuta, the Sailoda on the Snman and the Bindusara on the Gaura mountain.

The Buddhist texts mention seven great Himalayan lakes, Anotatta, Kaunamunda, Patha-para, Chaddanta, Kunala, Mandakini and Sihoppa-pata. Each of them is said to be of 50 leagues in length, breadth and the depth. The Anotatta lake or the Manasa-Sarovara was associated with Kailasa and Citrakuta peaks.

## The Seven boundary mountains.

The Puranas mention seven Kulacalas, i.e. seven mountain ranges, each of which include one major peak, and several minor hills associated with the major one. Ptolemy, too, enumerates seven mountain-ranges probably following some native list. But only two of them namely Ouxenton and Ouindion are mentioned by name.

## Mahendra

The Mahendra mountain, the first in the list of the seven 'Kulacalas', has been placed on the northern coast of India. According to the evidence of the Ramayana, the name Mahendra seems to have been given to the whole mountain range extending from Ganjam as far south as the Pandya country to the whole of the Eastern Ghat range. The Mahabharata regards it as an eastern mountain.Kalidasa places it in the Kalinga country. The Puranas suggest that it stood in juxtaposition with Malaya and Sahya.

It has been mentioned in Raghuvamsa, Uttara Naisadhacarita, Harsacarita, Yoginltantra and Gautami plate of Indravarman. According to Pargiter the name should be limited to the hills between the Mahanadi, Godavari and Wenganga and may comprise the portion of the Eastern Ghats north of Godavari. He thinks that the Mahendra of the Puranas and that of the Ramayana were two different ranges, but Raychaudhuri argues that the same range is meant at both the places.

It was situated between the Gangasagara-samgama and Saptagodavari. The name was mainly given to the hills separating Ganjam from the valley of MahanadT. The eastern Ghats must have been known to ancient Indian geographers as the Mahendragiri as the highest peak of the Eastern Ghat is still known by that name. A small hill in the Tinnevelly Distt. is also known as Mahendra.

It extends from Orissa to Madurai Distt. and runs as detached hills more or less parallel to the eastern coast of India which are known by different names in the different part of the country. The range extending from the northern circars to Gondwana, part of which is near Ganjam is still called Mahendra Malai.

## Malaya

The Malaya mountain is also called Srikhandadri or even Candanadri because of the Sandalwood found there. It is the Candaka of the Jatakas, Podigi or Podigali of Tamil, Bettigo of Ptolemy and the Melizigara of Periplus. The word Malaya seems to have been derived from the Dravadian word Mala or Malai which means hill.

The Ramayana places it in the vicinity of the Rsyamuka mountain. It mentions Lanka and Malaya mountain together which may suggest that a hill bearing this name was situated in that island also.

According to Pargiter, the portion of western Ghats from Nilgiris to Cape Comorin was called the Malaya. The southern extension of the Western Ghats, below the Kaveri, now known as the Travancor hills, including the Chandammum Mountain, extending from Koimbatur gap to Cape Comorin forms the western side of the Malayagiri. Dr. Raychaudhuri has successfully shown that Malaya par excellence is the mountain of the Pandyas. The hill is associated with Colas and Pandyas kings in the Mahabharata also. The Malaya mountain included the Nallamalai, Annamalai and Ailamalai peaks of the southern India.

## Sahya

Kalidasa assodates it with the Aparanta people.The Brahma Purana places it to the south of the Vindhya mountain. Ptolemy calls it Adeisathron.The Sahya mountain is the modern Western Ghats. They form the western boundary of the Deccan and run continuously for a distance of about 1000 miles from the Kundai Bari Pass in the Khandesh distt. of Bombay state (present Maharastra) down to Cape Comorin, the southern most point of India. The Western Ghats are known by different local names and have got a few important passes too. Pargiter, too, identifies it with the northern portion of the Western Ghats. From the rivers which rise in them it may be concluded that it stretched from the river Tapti. down to Nilgiris. The hills are known as Sahyadri even today in Maharashtra and Karnataka.

## Suktimat

There is a difference of opinion among scholars regarding its identification. The great difference in the lists of the rivers that are said to have arisen in this mountain makes, its identification all the more difficult.

Cunningham gives the name Suktimat to the hills south of Sehoa and Kanker separating Chhattisgarh from Bastar. Beglar places it to the north of the Hazaribagh Distt.. Pargiter includes in it the Garo, Khasi and Tipperah hills. C.V. Vaidya has located it in the western India and identified it with the Kathiawar range. R.C. Majumdar and H.K. Dev have identified it with the Sulaiman range. Dr. Raychaudhuri applied the name Suktimat to the chain of hills that extends from Sakti in Raigarh (Madhya Pradesh) to the Dalma hills in Manbhum drained by Kumari in Bihar and' perhaps even to the hills in the Santhal Parganas washed by the affluents of the Babla. The name is still preserved in that of the Sakti hills.

They appear to be the spurs in the eastern direction to the north of Sahya which include the hills of Ajanta Hyderabad and the Golkunda Plateau.

## Rksa

The Rksa mountain has been placed on the river Narmada. It is the Ouxenton of Ptolemy in which is given the source of the rivers Toundis, Dosaran and the Adamas. By Rksa he meant the central region of the modern Vindhya range north of Narmada and he is fairly correct in his identification because Vayu Purana clearly says that the Vindhya itself is the Rksavat. The same Purana relates that Jyamagha roamed in the places lying on the banks of Narmada and went to Rksavan from the peaks of the Mekala mountain, which is also a Vindhya peak.

It is the eastern part of the Vindhya range extending from the Bay of Bengal to the source of the Narmada and Sona and includes the mountains south of the Sona, namely those of Chota Nagpur, Ramgarh etc., mountain of Gondwana in which the Mahanadi has its source, and the mountains in which lay the sources of Narmada, Sona and SuktimatT.

## Vindhya

The Vindhya mountain is said to have been watered by the river Reva. The story in the Vamana Purana relates how it went on increasing its height to obstruct the light of the Sun and how it was made to lay prostrated for ever by the sage Agastya.

It is the Ouindion of Ptolemy. In the Buddhist texts it is said to be in the south of Avanti. This mountain known as Vijha also may be identified with the Satpura range from which rises the Tapti along with other rivers. On a spur of the Satpura range is a colossal rock cut-out Jain image of the Digambar sect called Bawangaj about 73 ft. in height on Narmada in the Burdwan Distt. about 100 miles from Indore.

Pargiter identifies it with the hills and the plateaus of south Mysore. The Vindhyas extend eastward for about 700 miles from Gujarat on the west and Bihar in the east. It has different local names such as Bharner, Kaimur etc.. Its average height is 1500 - 2000 ft., some peaks rising for over 5000 ft.. The Kaimur range, its eastern extension, dominates the Sona Valley. The Vindhya and Satpura ranges unite at Amarakantaka near the source of the Narmada. The Mekala range forms the eastern boundary of the Satpura mountain. This mountain is not of true tectonic type. It represents the southern edge of the Malwa plateau, which got faulted in the remote geological time, resulting in the formation of the Vindhyan mountain. It is believed that the' Vindhya was formed of the sediments derived from the Aravali mountain.

It is very difficult to identify the Rksa and the Vindhya mountains. It is now generally accepted that the three Kulacalas, the Rksa, the Vindhya and the Pariyatra are parts of the whole range now known by the common name of the Vindhyas. The confusion arises from the lists of the rivers said to have arisen in these mountains. The rivers issuing from these two mountains can be divided into two groups (1) Sona-Narmada group and (2) the Sipra-Tapfi group. According to the Kurma, Matsya, Brahmanda, Vayu and Vamana, the Rksa is the source of the Sona - Narmada group while the Sipra-Tapfi group had its source in the Vindhyas. The Markandeya, the Visnu and the Brahma give the source of the Sona - Narmada group in the Vindhyas and that of Sipra - Tapfi group in the Rksavat.

Ptolemy described Ouxenton (Skt. Rksavat) as the source of the Toundis Dosron (Dasarna) and Adamos, and the Ouindon (Vindhya) as that of Namados and Nanagouna (Narmada and Tapfi). Thus Narmada and Tapfi had their sources not in the different mountains but in the one and the same namely the Vindhya. The Puranas give the source of the Dasarna in the Rksa i.e. the Ouxenton of Ptolemy who meant by it the central region of the modern Vindhya range north of the Narmada. From Ouindon he meant only that portion of the Vindhya from which rises the Narmada and the Tapti i.e. the eastern part of the modern Vindhyas south of Narmada.

Dr. Raychaudhuri suggests a number of the passages from the epics, Harivamsa, Puranas, and the Inscriptions to show that Ptolemy is right in identifying them. He concludes, Ancient Hindu writers commonly regarded the Vindhya and the Rksa as interchangeable terms. While the name Vindhya was loosely applied to the whole chain of the hills from Gujerat to the Gaya district, lying on both the sides of the Narmada, the Rksa.... is invariably associated with the middle Narmada region of which MahismatT was the most important city and the Dasarna, a notable river. The Vindhya when distinguished from the Rksavat denotes the chain lying to the south of the Narmada.

From the evidence of the Puranas we may conclude that the Rksavat denoted, that part of the Vindhya range which lies to the north of the Narmada and runs from the Malwa region right upto the sources of that river as well as that of the Mahanadi.

## Panyatra

According to the Brahmanical and Buddhist traditions it was the southern boundry line of the Madhyadesa. The Baudhayana Dharmasutra regards it as the southern limit of the Aryavarta.The mountain seems to have given its name to the country in which it was situated.

It is the Prapiotai of Ptolemy. Pargiter takes it to be the modern Vindhya range situated to the west of Bhopal together with the Aravalli mountain. It extended from the source of the Chambal to the Gulf of Cambay. Dr. Bhandarkar identifies it with that part of the Vindhya in which lay the source of Chambal and Betwa.

It comprised the Aravalli mountains and the hills of Rajputana including the Pathar range which is perhaps a contraction of the word Paripatra. It appears to have included the countries of Aparanta, Saurastra, Sudras, Malapa (Malava) Malaka and others.

## Sriparvata

The Sriparvata or Srisaila is regarded as the Kailasa of the south. Fa-hien and.Hiuen Tsang refer to it as Po-Lo-Mo-Ki-Li; and it is mentioned under the name 'Sintana' in the Nasika Cave Inscription.

Jt has been placed on the river Kaveri. Bana mentions Sriparvata which is the name of a range of mountain in Telingana. In the opinion of Dr. Raychaudhuri it lays 8 miles to the north of Kuddapall.

The hill was dedicated to the goddess Sri by Visnu because she has once performed some austerities here. The Srisaila, also known as Sesacala and Venkatacala is used as a name for Tirupati. This lofty rock which is a part of the eastern Ghats, overhangs the Krishna river in the hilly forest area of the Nandikotkur Taluk of the Kurnool Distt. and is situated 73 miles from the Nandyal Railway Station. It is said that the Eastern Ghats on this side, along with their curves, heights and falls, resemble the serpent Adisesa ani that the seven hills of Triupati are its seven hoods. That is why it is called Sesacala.

Here is situated the famous Mallikarjuna temple, one of the 12 Linga shrines. Law places this ancient shrine on the Rsabhagiri hill on the southern bank of the river Krishna. The distance between the river and the temple is about 2 miles.Other sacred places connected with it are VamT Puskarim lake, Kapila Tirtha also called Alwar Tirtha, and the Papa-VinasanatTrtha. Dr. Burgess found it to be an isolated hill about 1570 ft. high surrounded on three sides by river Krishna and on the fourth partly by the Bhima Nakollam torrent.

## Raivataka

The mountain Raivataka, also known as Puspagiri, Ujjayanta, Vaijayanta and Vastrapatha-ksetra, has been placed to the west of Udayantagiri near Somanatha, in the Pindaraka Ksetra of Surastra. It was near Dvaraka and not far from Prabhasa. The Puranas, Mahabharata and Brhatsamhita refer to it frequently. The whole of the fourth sacga of Magha's Sisupalavadha is given to the description of this mountain. It is also famous in the Inscriptions of Ashoka, Skandagupta and Rudradaman. The Junagarh Inscription of Skandagupta places it opposite to the Urjayat and the Jaunpur stone Inscription of Isvaravarman Maukhari mentions it along with the Vindhya mountain.

There is a difference of opinion among the scholars as to whether Girnar or Ujjayanta is the same as the Raivataka hill or they are seperate hills. The latter is regarded by some to be a seperate hill near the east of Dvaraka. Pt. Durga Shankara Shastri regards Girnar and Raivataka as one and the same hill and says that the Dvaraka was founded by the Yadavas near that. In the Mahabharata days KusasthaE was at the bottom of the Raivataka hill and at that very place the Yadavas established Dvaraka or Dvaravati. It seems that in early times the Raivataka and the Urjayanta might have been two different hills at Girnar but later on they came to be regarded as identical.

Pargiter identifies it with the Barada hills in Halar, the western corner of the peninsula and Fleet with Raivataka, one of the two hills at Girnar and not with Girnar proper.

This sacred hill of 3500 ft. height above sea level, having many Brahmanical and Jain temples, Buddhist caves and numerous ancient sites, is situated in Saurastra about 10 miles east of the modern city of Junagarh in Gujarat. Even though Puranas mention more than 21 peaks associated with this mountain, only five of them, namely Amba Mata, Gorakhnath, Oghad Shikhara, Guru Dattatrey and Kalkas', are considered principal ones at present. The Gorakhnath peak is the highest i.e. 3665 ft. above the sea level.

## Amarakantaka

The Amarakantaka mountain is also known as Somaparvata and Surathadri. Kalidasa calls it Amrakuta because of its numerous mango trees.

The sanctity of the Amarakantaka Kstra which extends upto one Yojana on all the sides has been mentioned in the Puranas. Frorn it issues the river Narmada and the Vayu Purana locates it probably to the southern side of the river Narmada and on the back side (western) of the Kalinga country.

The sacred lake Jvalesvaratirtha, also called Savarta, extends into southern and eastern direction in the western portion of the Amarakantaka. The Puranas, which refer to it frequently, give the names of many tirthas situated from Amarakantaka mountain to the sea near Broach. The Matsya and Padma Puranas give their number as 10 crores; Agni, Kurma and Skanda as 60 crores and 60 thousands, out of which 400 are the chief ones according to the Naradiya.

The Amarakantaka is about seven miles from the Pendra Road station on the Bilaspur Katni Railway line. It is the part of the Mekala or Mikul hills forming the extreme eastern part of the Vindhya range.

## Gomanta

It is a minor hill associated with the Sahya mountain. The Mahabharata gives its location near Dvaraka in which case it would be identical with the Raivataka mountain in Gujarat. The Padma Purana gives its location in the country about Goa i.e. Gomanta. It is an isolated hill in the Western Ghats and forms a part of the Sahyadri to the east of Goa.

Pargiter identifies it with the hill south or south-east of Nasik but Dr. Raychaudhuri points out that to the north of Gomanta was Banavasi and so the hill should be placed in Mysore.

## Kiskindha

Evidently, it must be related to the Kiskindha of Ball of the Ramayana.

Ancient Kiskindha is identical with Anegundi on the northern bank of the river Tungabhadra and is about 3 miles from Vijayanagar. The city of Kiskindha comprises the hills on the opposite side of the valley that separates it from Humpi, which are wild congeries of fantastic naked granitic rocks with narrow valleys between.

## Dardura

The mount Dardura also called Durdhura and Dardulacala has been placed in the extreme south near Malaya. The southern most point of Sahya was the Dardura. It may be identified with the Nilgiris or Paini hills, the highst peak of which is known as Dud-Velli, probably a form of the Dardura. There is a hill called Turturiya which stands a little south of the junction of MahanadT with its tributory, the Seonath.

## Minor Hills

(1) Ujjayanta - It is the mt. Girnar, close to Junagarh in Kathiawar. It is sacred to Nemi-Nath, the 22nd Tirthankara of the Jains. (2) Puspagiri - It is the part of the Malaya range in which the' river Krtamala has its source. (3) Rsabha - The mountain Rsabha which is said to be situated to the east of Kailasa and to the north of Meru is a Varsaparvata. It must be some Himalaya peak. There is another Rsabha mountain in the south. It is the Paini hills in Madura, forming the northern portion of the Malaya mountain. Mahabharata located it in the Pandya territory. It is locally called Varahaparvata. (4) Arunacala - It is the Tiruvannamalai in the south Arcot Distt. in Andhra Pradesh. One whole Khanda of the Skanda P. called Arunacala Mahatmya is devoted to it. It contains the temples of Ardhanaris.vara and Arunacalesvara. (5) Venkat-adri- - It is the Tirumala mountain near Tripati or Tirupati in the north Arcot Distt. about 72 miles to the north- west of Madras and is also known as Sesadri. (7) Rsyamuka It is situated 8 miles from Anagandi on the bank of the Tungabhadra. Here Rama met Hanuman and Sugriva.

## Kalanjara

The Puranas frequently mention it. The name Kalanjara has been etymologically derived as such -

The Kurma Purana places it to the north of the lake Manasa. The Vamana Purana, too, mentions Kalanjara in the vicinity of Himalaya. The Padma Purana associates it with Ksipra while the Brahmanda Purana locates it in the Dasarna region.It is a famous Saivatirtha. Yashovarman conquered the mountain of Kalingir and erected the excellent temples of Ramacandra, Laksmana and Caturbhuja, at Khajuraho, in the Chhatarpur distt. of the Vindhya region. It is also called Rabichitra. It has been identified with the Kalinjar in the Badansa Sub-division of the Banda Distt. in Bundel Khand.

## Arbuda

The mountain Arbuda, too, is regarded as a continuation of the Himalaya mountain. It is the present Mt. Abu which is a part of the Aravalli range of mountains in erstwhile Sirohi state of Rajasthan. It is considered to be very ancient and much older than the Himalayas and the Vindhyas. Ptolemy calls it Apokopo and Pliny called it Mons Capitalia (i.e. the mountain of Capital Punishment).It is famous on account of the hermitage of the sage Vasistha. On and around Abu, the Skanda Purana enumerates various tirthas such as Vasisthasrama, Kofisvara and many others. It is one of the five sacred hills of the Jains which contains the temples of Rsabha and Neminatha.

## Kolahala

This mountain has been identified variously. Dr. R.L. Mitra takes it to be the Brahmayoni hill, including the hill called Mundaprastha in Gaya.According to the Mahabharata it was a range in the country of the Cedis. Beglar identifies it with Kawa-Kol range in Bihar. Dey regards it to be identical with the Bandair range of the south-west of Bundelkhand in which the river Ken (Suktimafi) has its source.

## Govardhana

It is closely associated with Krsna who is said to have lifted it to protect his subject from heavy rains. It is placed in the vicinity of Gokul, not more than two yojanas from Mathura in its western direction. Its actual distance from Vrindavana (Mathura Distt.) is 18 miles.

## Jalandhara

It is both a mountain and a sacred place and it is possible that the two were connected with each other and that the mountain was situated in or near about the country of the same name which was the capital of the great demon Jalandhara from whom it derived its name. It included the state of Chamba on the north, Mandi and Sukhet on the east and Satadru on the south east. It was 1000 li or 167 miles in the length from east to west and 800 li or 133 miles from north to south. It is a town near the western bank of Sutlej in Punjab.

## Chitrakuta

It is a mountain of Ramayana fame. It is said to have been situated at a distance of 10 krosas from the hermitage of the sage Bharadvaja. The river called PayasvinT or Mandakini which was on its northern side flowed near it. Another river named ManinI also flowed there. The forest of Citrakuta was not isolated as the Nila forest joined it.

The Jains called it Cittakuda, and the Buddhists located it not very far off from the Himavanta. *Dey* has identified it with the Kamptanathgiri in Bundelkhand.

It is the modern Citrakuta in the Karwi Tehsil in the Banda Distt. of Uttar Pradesh about 20 miles north-east of Kalinjar, about 60 miles south-west of Prayaga and about 3.5 miles from the Citrakuti station on the Jhansi Manikpur Railway line. The main religious spots here are:- Kotitirtha, Devaganga, Hanumanadhara, Sphatikasila, Anasuya Guptagodavari and the Bharatakupa.

## The Rivers of India

The Puranas mention a large number of rivers which are divided in various groups. This division is based on a very simple principle i.e. according to the mountain in which they have their source. Thus the rivers are divided in eight groups i.e. those issuing from the Himalayas and those having their source in the seven Kulacalas.

## The Himalayan Rivers

The Visnu, the Matsya and the Vayu Puranas give a list of rivers issuing from the Himalaya mountain. The Nflamata gives no such list but records the names of the Himalayan rivers as sacred places. According to the Matsya Purana, the rivers Ganga, Sindhu, Sarasvati, Satadru, Chandrabhaga, Yamuna, Sarayu, Airavati, Vitasta, Visala, Devika, Kuhu, Gomati, Dhautapapa, Bahuda, Drsadvati, KausikI, Trfiya, Niscala, Gandaki, Iksu and Lauhita rise among the Himalayan mountains. The Visnu Purana gives the names only of the Satadru and the Candrabhaga. The Vayu Purana reads Iravati in place of Dhautapapa, Nisdra for Niscala and Lohita for Lauhita. The rest of the rivers are the same. These rivers have got their sacred character also and as such they find frequent mention in the Puranas.

Alberuni too, records a number of Himalayan rivers such as Sindh (or the river of Vaihand, modern Und near Attock). Biyatta or Jailam Candrabhaga- or Candraha, Biyaha to the west of Lahore, Iravati to the east of Lahore, Satarudra or Sataldar Sarsat flowing to the country of Sarsat (i.e. Sarasvata) Jaun (Yamuna), Ganga, Sarayu or Sarwa, Devika Kuhu, Gomati Dhutapapa Lohita and Drsadvati. The other lames of his list have been wrongly taken mostly from that of the rivers issuing from the Pariyatra mountain.

## Ganga

The river Ganga is the most important and the most sacred-river of Bharata. The various names of the Ganga given in the Matsya Purana appear to be more attributive than proper names in their character.

## Its Source

The Puranas give its source in the lake Bindusara which is situated in the Himalayas at the base of Sarvausadha mountain to the north of Kailasa. The Vayu Purana gives a very interesting story about the descent of Ganga. It relates how the royal sage BhagTratha, with a desire to make his forefathers attain heaven, performed penances for a long time on the Himalayas where the Goddess Ganga was already present. After issuing forth from the feet of the moon, Ganga is said to have been divided into seven streams. The Tripathaga, the shining path seen in the midst of firmament, watered the sky and heaven, came to the earth and fell on the head of the god Siva. It thought of going to nether regions carrying Mahadeva with its waters and destroying all. Mahadeva enraged with the pride of Ganga decided to conceal and confront it in his hair. When this happened, the few drops of the Ganga fell on the earth and a lake named Bindusara was formed. Meanwhile, Mahadeva pleased by the penances of BhagTratha granted him a boon. Reminded by Brahma of his previously given boon, Siva calmed down his anger and let the Ganga flow.

Another version of its descent gives its source in the foot of Narayana. Having watered the mount Meru, it bifurcated itself into four streams flowing east, west, north and south. The southern stream was permitted by Siva to flow through India. The great epic, too, locates its source in the Bindusara lake while the Pali works regard southern side of the Anotatta lake to be its source. The Vayu, the Matsya,' the Brahma, the Markandeya and the Ramayana give almost the same account of the descent of the Ganga while the Visnu, the Bhagavata, the Padma and the Mahabharata agree substantially.

After falling from the head of Mahadeva Ganga divided itself into seven streams, three of them (Sita, Caksu and Sindhu) going to the west and three (NalinT, Hradini and PavanI) flowing to the east. The seventh one followed BhagTratha and was called Bhagirathi after him. They have divided the Himavan into two and entered the southern ocean. The Vayu and the Brahmanda give the same seven branches which fall into the salty ocean. Both the Matsya and the Vayu agree that they issue out of Bindusara and avater the country called Himavarsa and the mountainous country of Miecchas. They flow through the countries of Kukura, Randhra, Barbara, Yavana, Khasa, Pulinda, Kulattha and Arigalokya. The list of the Vayu has the countries of Sirindhra, Kuntala, Cina, Barbara, Yavana Dhruha, Rusana, Kuninda and Arigaloka. The Brahmanda has Gandharva, Kinnara, Yaksa, Raksa, Vidyadhara, Uraga, Kalapagrama, Parada, Tarigana, Khasa, Kirata, Pulinda, Kuru, Bharata, Pancala, Kasi, Matsya, Magadha, Suhma, Uttara, Variga and Tamralipta. Then striking the Vindhyas it enters the salty ocean.

The names of seven streams in Padma Puranaare Vatodaka, Nalini, Sarasvati, Jambunadi, Sita, Gariga, and Sindhu. The Mahabharata calls them Vasvokasara, Nalini, Pavani, Jambu, Sita, Gariga and Sindhu at one place and Ganga, Yamuna, Plaksa, Sarayu, Gomati, Gandaki and Rathastha at another. Ramayana, too, speaks of the seven streams.

The Vayu Purana divides Ganga into four currents only, which flow in opposite directions. The Sita falling upon the inferior mountains on the eastern side of Meru, flows over their crests, passes through the country of Bhadrasva and goes to the ocean. The Alakananda flowing south through the country of Bharata and dividing herself into seven streams on its way enters the ocean. The Caksu traversing all the western mountains and watering the country of Ketumala falls into the sea. The Bhadra washing the Uttarakuru country empties herself into the northern ocean.

The Markandeya Purana, too, mentions the four currents of the river Gariga. The eastern one passing through the garden of Citraratha, lake Varunoda Sitanta and other mountains, descends on the earth from Bhadrasva mountain and goes to the sea. The second one Alakananda goes to the south to the Gandhamadana forest at the base of Meru, deluges the lake Manasa, traverses all the southern mountains and goes to Himagiri where it is held by Mahadeva. Released by him, she divided herself into seven currents and fell into the southern ocean. The third Svaraksu goes to Vaibraja mountain, floods the Sitoda lake and then goes to the Trikuta mountain. It gradually falls on many other mountains, passes through the country of Ketumala and then enters the salty-ocean. The fourth current goes to Suparsva mountain at the foot of Meru, and reaches Soma. After passing through the Savitu forest it goes to the lake Mahabhadra. Thus watering the peaks of Sarikhakuta Vrsabha and other mountains, it floods the Uttarakuru country and enters the great ocean.

The four currents of the Ganga are also mentioned in the Visnu, Brahmanda and Siva Puranas. The Visnu relates that the. river issues from the foot of Visnu. After having watered the moon, it falls from the heaven into the city of Brahma and bifurcates itself into four currents namely Sita, Alakananda, Caksu and Bhadra. The Kurma Purana replaces Caksu by Sucaksu and Bhadra by Bhadranamika. According to Wilson these four currents are Hoangho, Ganges, Oxus and Ohy. According to Buddhists they were Ganges, Indus, Oxus and Sita all of which are derived from a great central lake in pleateau of Pamir called Anconta Karakul or Sarik-Kul lake.

According to the Matsya Purana, it passes through the countries of Gandharvas, Kinnaras, Yaksas, Raksasas, Vidyadharas, Uragas, Kimpuru-sas living in the Kalapagrama, Kiratas Pulindas, Aryan countries of Kuru, Bharata, Pancala, Kusika, Matsya, Magadha, Anga, Brahmottara,, Vanga and Tamralipta. After that its flow is obstructed by the Vindhya mountain and then it enters into the southern ocean.

This beautiful river, one Yojana broad and surrounded by rocks, of clear and pure wter, is known as Alakananda, Dyunadi or Dyudhuni and is referred to in the Vedic literature, grammatical works, the Puranas and the Raghuvamsa. It is known as Bhagirathi and Jahnavl to the Raghuvamsa and the Yogiriitantra. The Siva, the Markandeya, the Skanda, the Brhatdharma Purana, the Harivamsa and the Ramayana call it TripathagaminT. The stream which flows towards the Caitraratha forest is called Sita and the other flowing towards the Gandhamadana mountain is called Alakananda. Sita proceeds towards the lake Varunoda and Alakananda towards the Manasa. It is called Varanavati in the Atharvaveda. The Ganga flowing to the south of the Vindhya mountain is called the Gautami-Ganga and the other flowing to the north of it is the Bhagirthi-Ganga.

The Gariga of yellowish colour, the source of all the sacred places, gushes out forcibly, cutting through the mountains at Gangadvara i.e. Haridvara. It is easily accessible at other places but difficult to be reached at three places namely Gangadvara, Prayaga and Gangasagara-Samgama. Its importance in the month of Magha is very great.

Not only the Ganga itself is highly sanctified, but also the people living between the Ganga and Yamuna are considered to be specially honoured.After its confluence with Yamuna, its course turns north-wardly. Its confluence with other rivers such as Sarasvati, Gomati, Sindhu, Aruna, Varuna, Asi, Parusm, GandakI, Sara u and Sona are mentioned in the Puranas.

Kalidasa mentioned Ganga as flowing south-eastwards into th Bay of Bengal.

Ganga of a width of 32 stadia is known to Alexander. The same Width is recorded by Plutarch who gives its depth as 100 fathoms. Strabo mentions it as one of the two greatest rivers of India. It descends from the mountains and turns eastward upon its reaching the plains. Then flowing past Palibothra (Pataliputra) it goes to the sea and discharges into it. Megasthenes records its mean breadth as 100 stadia and least depth 20 fathoms. At many places it spreads out into lakes so that in flat places which are destitute of elevation the opposite shores cannot be seen from each other. Pliny records This river, according to some, rises from uncertain sources and inundates the countries along its course, others say that it rises on the Scythian mountains and has nineteen tributaries of which, besides those already mentioned, the Condochates, Eran Noboas, Cosagas and Sonus-are navigable. Others again assert that it issues forth at once with loud roar from its fountain and after tumbling down steep and rocky channel is received immediately on reaching the level plains, into a lake, whence it flows out with a gentle current, being at the narrowest eight miles and on the average a hundred stadia in breadth, and never of less depth than 20 paces (110 ft.) in the final part of its course which is through the country of the Gangarides. Arrian, too, gives some useful information regarding Ganga. Megasthenes states that of the two, the Ganges is much longer, 30 stadia broad at its source. It receives, besides the river Sones, Sittokalis and Solomates which are also navigable and also the Kondockates, Sambos, Magon, Agoranis and the Omalis. Moreover, these fall into it the Kommenases, Kakonthis and the Anomatis.

Ptolemy writes about the mouths of the Ganges. B.C. Law has tried to give a solution for the identification of the river Tripathaga thus, It is somewhat curious that the Ganges is everywhere in the Mahabharata and the Puranas, qualified invariably as Tripathaga - though .the actual description is that after issuing from the feet of Visnu and washing the lunar orb she divided herself into four mighty rivers and flowed into four directions. Later also in India she is described as a river that flowed in seven streams. It is only in her lower course that she is said to have entered the ocean in three streams on the east. Is it then to be surmised that her description as Tripathaga refers to these three streams. Which are those streams ? Can they be identical with Bhagirathi, Brahmaputra and Meghna, the three courses which mingle together before they enter the sea. He regards the seven streams spoken of as entering the ocean as different tributaries of the great river and the three streams on the east inundating as a great river the south with their overflow as her lower streams of the south-east before she reaches the eastern sea.

The Bhagirathi Ganga or the Ganga itself comes to light in the Gangotri in the Garhwal Distt. and enters the plains at Haridvar. From Haridvar down to Bulandshahar its course is southerly and then south easterly upto Allahabad where it unites with the Yamuna. From Allahabad to Rajmahal its course is easterly. From Haridvar to Allahabad it flows almost parallel to the Yamuna. It enters Bengal below Rajmahal. After flowing past Triveni, Chagda, Guria, Bernipur, Rajganj and Diamaod Harbour through Adiganga or Tolly's Nala, it falls into the sea near Sagar Island. The main stream of the river originally passed southwards, after leaving Jahnu-asrama at Sultanganj through the channel of Bhagirathi with which the Jellinghi forms the river Hugli .from Shibganj above Boalia. The theory attributing the origin of all the seven rivers to the same lake in the Himalayas has to be regarded as a mere flight of fancy. The list of the countries given in the Puranas, through which Ganga is said to have passed, does not appear to be always accurate. However, they include the mountainous countries of the Himalayas and those of northern India.

## Jahnavi

According to the Puranic tradition the sage Jahnu, seeing that his hermitage has been flooded by Ganga, became very angry and drank the whole of it. Appeased by gods, the sage accepted it as his mind-born daughter, who thence forward came to be known as Jahnavi. The Puranic accounts do not differentiate between Jahnavi and Ganga. Jahnavi also is 'Himavatsuta. From Varanasi onwards its course becomes north-wardly. It too, is obstructed by Vindhya and ultimately falls into the ocean.

Dey gives six Jahnus which are nothing but the allegorical representations of the changes in the course of the Ganges. They are:- (1) Bhairavaghati below Gangotri at the junction of BhagirathT and JahnavT (2) at Kanyakubja or Kanauj (3) at Jahngirin Sultanganj on the west of Bhagalpur, (4) at Shibganj -above Rampur Boalia, (5) at Gour near Malda and (6) at Jahnagar 4 miles to' the west of Nadia.

The Ramayana gives its position to the south of the Himavan and says that Jahnavi and Tamasa flow near each other. The Govindapur copper-plate of Lakshamansena state that the Hugh river was called Jahnavi which flowed by the side of Betad in the Howrah district.

It may be identified with Jadaganga which unites with the Gariga in the Bhairavaghati.

## Bhagirathi

According to the Puranas the Gariga was called Bhagirathi after BhagTratha who brought it to the earth. It flows to the south, parses through the country of Suhma in Bengal and enters the southern ocean. It is united with Alakananda and Gandaki.

Bhagirathi is the Ganges itself. This statement is supported by the Sena and Chandra copperpicites. and the Naihati copperplate of Ballalasena. Bhagirathi comes to light in the Gangottari in the territory of Garhwal. The Gariga may be said to have acquired the name of Bhagirathiganga from the point where it is united with the Mandakim (modern Kaliganga or Mandagini) that rises in the Kedara mountains in Garhwal.

## Alakananda

It is said to have flown southward to the country of Bharata. Dividing itself into seven branches on the way it falls into the ocean. The Vayu Purana regards Nanda and Alakananda as two separate rivers and says that Alakananda of sweet and nectorlike water flows on Kailasa mountain. From there it descends on the Gandhamadana mountain in the southern direction. Watering the Nandana forest and encircling the Gandhamadana mountain, it falls into the Uttaramanasa lake. Then passing over the mountains Trikuta, Kalihga, Rucaka, Nisadha, Tamrabh'a, Svetodara, Sumula, Vasudhara, Hemakuta, Devasrnga, Pisacaka, Pancakuta, Kailasa and Himalaya, this great river splitting thousands of rock and watering many places, forests and caves falls into the southern ocean. The Bhagavat Purana gives its source in the Bhutesagiri i.e. Kailasa mountain. In the Agni Purana it is said to have descended on the Bhadrasva hill. Leaping from hill to hill it enters the western sea by seven channels. The Kurma Purana describes its course from Bharata to ocean only.

Alakananda, a river in the Garhwal Himalaya and a headwater of the Ganga, represents its upper course. The Brhatdharma Purana itself identifies it with Ganga. Its upper tributary is formed of the Pinda and another stream, the confluence of which is situated at Shrinagara in Garhwal. Mandakim (KaHganga or Mandagini) is its another tributary. It joins Bhagirathi Ganga on its left side at Devaprayaga.

Dey regards it as a tributary of the Ganges, the united stream of the Visnu-Gariga called Dhavala Ganga or Dauli and Sarasvati-Ganga. It is also called Bishenganga above its confluence.

Only a few mountains from the list of mountains given in the Puranas through which it is said to have passed can be identified with the Himalayan peaks. They are:- Trikuta, Nisadha, Hemakuta, Kailasa and Himalaya. Since the others are mentioned alongwith, they, too, must be Himalayan peaks.

## Sindhu

The Sindhu, one of the seven currents of the Ganga, naturally has its source in the Himalayas.It is frequently mentioned in grammatical works, epics, the Puranas and other literary works. The Puranas record a number of countries through which it is said to have passed. After having watered all these countries it is obstructed in the Vindhya mountain and enters the southern ocean.

The river Sindhu of Vedic fame surpasses all the streams. The term Saindhava in the Taittariyasamhita might have been applied to the people living on the banks of the Sindhu.

The Jambudivapannati mentions this river and gives its source in the twin lotus lakes, one on the side of the lesser and the other on the greater Himalayan ranges.

It is known to the Chinese as Sintu and Sinthus. Arrian calls the Indus as the mightest river of India from which the country derives its name. Akesines, Hydaspes, and Hydraotes are its tributaries, after receiving which., the Sindhu is said to become 300 stadia in breadth. Megasthenes, too, gives its detailed description. According to him the volume of the river is ample even at its source. He records fifteen of its tributaries namely Kainas, Srannoboas, Korosones, Sonos, Sittakotis, Solomatis, Kondochates, Sambos, Magen, Agorins, Omalis, Kommenases, Kakonthis, Andomatis and Amysthis. It flowed past the city Katadupa and Oxymagis from the dominions of a tribe Pazalai and Erramysis from Mathain Indian tribe. Alberuni is aware of its three names as it was known as (1) Sindhu in its upper course, above its junction with Chenab (2) as Pancanada from its junction upto Aror and *\\3)* Mihran from Aror down to the sea. It is the Hindu of the Bahistun Ins. of Darius, Hendu of Vendidad and Hoddu of the Bible.

From the names of the countries through which it is said to have flown, we may conclude that it is being confused with Ganga, because we see that Sindhu is a western river flowing through the undivided Punjab and Sindh. It does not pass through the countries of north-eastern India such as Kasi, Matsya, Magadha etc.. It is possible that Ganga may have been called Sindhu, as Sindhu in Sanskrit is a name applied to a river in general. It is the Ganga which touches the Vindhya spurs in the town of Monghyr. As far as the western countries such as Darada, Kasmira and Kirata etc. are concerned we may say that this portion of the Puranic allusion is correct.

The Sindhu gave its name to the country through which it flowed. It is the greatest river of northern India after which the Indus group is named. It is also known as Sambheda and Samgama. It is a trans-Himalayan river which is fed by a number of glaciers. The upper course of the river along with its important tributaries gave to the country, through which it flowed the name of the land of the five rivers (Pancanada). From this medieval name, the present Punjab derives its name. The country around its lower course came to be known as Sindh after its very name.

The Sindhu is an united flow of two streams one flowing north-west from the north-west side of the Kailasa mountain and the other flowing in a north-westerly and then in a south-westerly direction from a lake situated to the north-east of Kailasa. It flows north-west over a long distance and then turns south below the Karakoram range. After passing Attock, it flows almost south-wards, parallel to the Sulaiman hills. It flows a slightly serpentine and south-westerly course till it enters- into the Arabian sea, forming the two well known deltas at its mouth below Karachi, the larger one called Prasians by Arrian and the smaller one Patale (Sanskrit Prasthala).

It is the greatest tributary of the Vitasta within the Kashmir valley. The true headwaters of the Sindhu are near the Lozi-la and Amburnath peak. The traditional source of this river is given in the sacred Ganga lake on the mountain Haramukha. It has a course of over 60 miles and drains the highest and largest portion of the mountain chain in the north.

## Sita

Sita, the eastern branch originates from the lake Bindusara. Leaping from one mountain to another, it goes to the country of Bhadrasva and thence it falls into the ocean. The Vayu Purana regards it as a western current. The Puranas give a number of countries through which it is said to have passed.Some identify it with the river Yarkand or Zarafshan on which the town of Yarkand is situated. The river is modern Jaxartes also called Sir-Daria, Sir-a corruption of Sita and Daria-a river. It rises in the plateau south of Issykkul lake in the Tutanshan.

## Caksu

This Western tributary of Ganga, which also rises from the lake Bindusara, passes over all the western mountains, waters the country of Ketumala and falls into the western ocean. The Mahabharata records its course through Sakadvipa. A number of countries are mentioned in the Puranas through which it is said to have flowed. The Caksu is the present river Oxus or Amu Daria which rises in the Pamir lake, also called Sari-kul, at a distance of 300 miles to the south of Jaxartes.

## Hiadim

This eastern branch of Ganga, passing through a number of countries falls into the ocean. The Ramayana places it between Kekaya on the west and Satadru on the east. Bharata is said to have crossed this river on his way to Oudh from Kekaya.

It has been identified with the Brahmaputra or rather with its upper course. li ,v-e rely on the evidence of the Ramayana, then it can not be identified with the Brahmaputra. But its description as an eastern river also does not fit in well with the description as given in the Ramayana.

## Nalini

This eastern branch of the Ganga also called Vatodaka, waters a number of countries and enters the great ocean near the Nemimandala kostha. This description of Nalini given in the Vayu is the description of Pavani in the Matsya Purana.

It seems to be the river Padma but from the evidence of the Padma Puraria Nalini and Padma appear to be two distinct rivers. Since it is described as a considerable river flowing to the east from near the source of the Ganga it may be identified with the Brahmaputra.

As it is said to have entered into the sea in the Samimandala which reminds us of Siam, it may be identified with the Salween or the Mekong.

## Pavam

This eastern branch of the river Ganga watering a number of countries falls into a great lake called Saimimandala.

It has been identified with the river Ghaggar in Kuruksetra (Ambala distt.) or rather the united stream of the Sarasvati and the Ghaggar, which is called by the name Sarasvati.

Bharata is said to have crossed the river Sarasvati at its confluence with the Ganga. Though it cannot be said with certainty that Sarasvati ever joined the Ganga, yet it is a fact that to the north of Thanesar there is a celebrated Tirtha on the Sarasvati named Gangatirtha where the Ganga is said to have taken a bath so that she might be rid of her sins. The Ghaggar or Sarasvati flows to the east of the HIadinT, one of the three eastern branches. The Ghaggar was a important river before and the Sarasvati was its tributary instead of being the principal river itself as it is generally believed to be. Sircar identifies it with Irawadi.

## Bhadra

The river Bhadra also known as Bhadrasoma, which is the western current of the Ganga, issues from the lake Bindusara. After passing over different mountains and watering the country of Ketumala, it enters the western ocean. The river is called Bhadrasoma when it comes to the lake Mahabhadra. It is very extensive, rapid and passes over a number of mountains. One of its tributaries waters the country of Uttarakuru and enters the northern ocean. It has been identified with the Yarkand river on which the town of Yarkand is situated. It is also called Zarafshan.

## A few other tributaries of Ganga

The river named Vamsaukasara is a small tributary of Ganga. A forest named Surabhi and a mountain named Hemasrnga are associated with it.

The rivers Sarasvati and Jyotismati issue from the lake named Sarpasarovara, situated on the Hemakuta mountain, and enter the eastern and western ocean respectively. The Vayu has Manasvmi Jyotismati. It is just possible that they were two seperate rivers. The Vayu gives its source in the Sayana Lake.

## Lauhitya

The river Lauhitya, a Himalayan river, has got its source in the lake Lohita which is situated at the base of Suryaprabha mountain in the vicinity of the Pisanga mountain. In the Jambudivapannati its source is traced to the stream which channel out-through the eastern outlet of the Lotus lake. The Kalika Purana gives a legendary account of its origin. According to it, the river Bra'hmaputra i.e. Lauhitya is situated between the four mountains of which the Kailasa and Gandhamadana lie to the north and the south respectively.

The Lauhitya is the principal river of Assam, forming the western boundary of Pragjyotisa. It is our present Brahmaputra which rises from the eastern region of the lake Manasa. There are three important headwaters of the Brahmaputra - the Kupi, the Chema Yungdung and Angsi Chu, all of them rising from the glacial tongues. Since the discharge of the Kupi river is the highest Svenhedinfixed the source of the Brahmaputra in the Kupi glacier, but Swami Pranavananda gives its source in Chema and Yungdung glaciers. The course of the Brahmaputra through the tableland of southern Tibet is known as Sanpa.

The Lauhitya flows in a south-westerly direction from Sadiya down to the place above the Garo hills. Then it flows southward upto its junction with the Ganges at a little above Goaland Ghat.

## Gomati

Since it flows through a region that abounds in cows it is called Gomati.

The Skanda Purana regards it as a tributary of the Ganga near Benaras and Meghaduta mentions it as a branch of the Chambal. The Visnu Purana uses the term Naimise Gomati suggesting its presence in the Naimisa forest. The expression Gomati Dhutapapa ca signifies that the Gomati and the Dhutapapa were two different rivers. This Dhutapapa according to Cunningham was a tributary of Gomati. In the Mahabharata these two words are linked together with the word Dhutapapa used as an adjective.

The Rgvedic Gomati denoted modern Gomal, a western tributary of the Indus in Arachosia of Afganistan and falls into the Indus between Dera Ismail Khan and Pahadpur (Pakistan).

A river of this name flows in the Kangra distt. of Punjab and yet another is in Gujarat on which stands Dvaraka. It is the modern Gomati which unites with the Ganga below Benaras, flows through Ayodhya and abounds in cattle. The capital of Uttar Pradesh i.e. Lucknow stands on it. It rises m Shahjahanpur Distt. and flows into the Ganga about half way between Benaras and Ghazipur.

## Gandaki

The river GandakI is called by various names. The Varaha Purana has the expression Gandasvadodbhava yatra Gandaki saritam vara, suggesting that the river has been formed from the sweat of the cheeks of Visnu who performed penances at its source. It is called Gandaki on this very account. It is also called Salagrami since its source lies not far from Salagrama, the hermitage of Bharata and Pulaha. It is named Narayam after the temple of Muktinatha, an image of Narayana that is placed to the south of Salagrama. It is also known as Cakranadi.

It is the Kondochates of Arrian. and Megasthenes according to whom it abounded in a kind of alligator having a horn like projection on its nose. It skirted the eastern border of Kosala and joined the Ganga opposite Palibothra (Pataliputra).

It is a great upper tributary of the Ganga and has its source called Damodarakunda in the SaptagandakI or the Dhavalagiri range of the Himalayas which is the southern boundary of the central Tibet. It enters the plains at Trivem Gnat. While passing through Nepal, it takes four tributaries on the left side and two on the right side. The upper tributary on its right side joins it to the north-west of Nayakot in Nepal. The Rapti, its lower tributary joins the Ganga between Sonpur (Saran distt.) and Hazipur (Muzzaffarpur Distt.) in Bihar while its lesser stream, bifurcating' at Basarh, flows down into another stream.

## Iravati

It has its source in the lake Ira. It is the same as Aciravati Ajiravati and Airavati. The Matsya locates Pulindas on its bank. The town of Sravasti, too is said to have been situated on its bank. The Vamana Purana refers to its confluence with the river Nadvala and Sindhu.

The Iravati, a tributary of Sarayu is known to Ptolemy as Adris or Rhouadis. It has been variously identified with the Rapti in Oudh on which stands Sahet Mahet (ancient Sravasti) and with the Irawadi. The Iravati is the modern Ravi, which rises in the rock basin of Bangahal and drains the southern slopes of the Pir Panjal and the northern slopes of the Dhaula Dhar. It appears first to view at the south-west corner of Chamba in Kashmir. From Chamba it flows past Lahore (Pakistan) flowing a south-westerly course and unites with the Chenab or rather with the united flow of the Vitasta and the Candrabhaga between Ahmadpur and Sarasindhu in Pakistan.

## Kuhu

The Kubha of the Vedic period appears to have been corrupted into the Kuhu of the Puranic period. The river Sindhu is said to have passed through the country of Kuhus, who are mentioned just after the Gandharas and Urasa in the Matsya.

It is the Kophes of Arrian, Kophen of Pliny, Koa of Ptolemy and Khoaspes of Persians. It is mentioned by Strabo and Curtius also.

Among the western tributaries of the Indus, this is the most important. According to some of the classical writers the Kuhu formed the western boundary of India proper. It is no other than the modern Kabul river. The Kubha enters a valley through the Sulaiman range and joins the Indus a little above Attock (Skt. Hataka) and receives at Prang, a joint flow of its two tributaries called the Swat (Soastes of Arrian and Suvastu of Skt.) and Gauri (Gorria of Arrian). Law identifies it with modern Panjkora, a tributary of Swat.

## Vipasa

The Mahabharata gives an interesting account of its name. It says that the sage Vasistha, broken in heart due to the murder of his sons at the hand of the sage Visvamitra decided to commit suicide. He, therefore, tied and threw himself into the river, but the forceful current of the river unfastened him and saved him. Since it unfastened the bonds, it was called Vipasa.

The Vipasis or Hypasis of Pliny, Curtius and Arrian, the Hypanis of Strabo and the Bibasis of Ptolemy marked the limits of Alexander's advance into India.

In ancient times it seems to be an independant river. It is the modern Bias, a tributary of Sutlej which rises in the Pir Panjal range at the Rohtang pass near the source of Ravi and is fed by a number of glaciers. From Chamba it flows in a southwesterly direction upto its junction with the Satadru. It receives a river named Devahrada at KaravTrapura.

The hot spring and the village of the sage Vasistha are situated opposite to Manali.

## Bahuda

Since the sage Likhita had his severed arm restored to him by bathing in this river, it is named Bahuda. The Vayu mentions it with Gomati and Drsadvati, and the Jatakas associate it with Praya, Dona and Timbaru. The Puranas regard it as a northern river in as much as they give its source in the Himalayas but the epics 'regard it to be a southern river. Pargiter identifies this river with modern Ramganga which meets Ganga on the left near Kanauj. The Vayu's evidence confirms this view. Hemacandra gives its synonyms as ArjunT or SaitvahinI, both implying the white river.Amarakosa also identifies it with the Saitavahini.A main feeder of the Mahanada, which falls into the Ganga below Malda and with which the Bahuda has been identified by Wilford is called Dhavali or Dhauli meaning the white river. Thus it can be identified with Dhavala now called Dhumel or Burha-Rapti, a feeder of Rapti in the region formerly known as Oudh.

## Kausiki

The Kausiki, also called Mahakosi and Mahakausiki has been associated with the Himalaya mountain, in which lies its source. It was Satyavati who was transformed as Kausiki. The epic attributes its origin to the sage Visvamitra. The Buddhist literature mentions it as a branch of Ganga while the Puranas make it a tributary of the Drsadvati. It is said to be a rainy season river flowing in Kuruksetra. On the authority of Megasthenes, Arrian mentions it under the name of Kosoanos, as being one of the navigable tributaries of Ganga.

There is a difference of opinion regarding its identification. Some have identified it with Kusiara of Sylhet flowing through the area known as Pancakhanda. Others identify it with modern Kusi flowing through the districts of Bhagalpur and Purnea (Bihar). It rises in southern Nepal and is a joint flow of four rivers, three of which originate in Tibet. The second tributary called Sun-Kosi (on the western side) is a joint flow of three rivers. Another river Arun is a united current of two rivers. On its eastward course, it is joined with the river Karatoya having the Atrai and Tista for its tributaries. A small river called Saura meets at its mouth. It is famous for its rapidity, the dangerous and uncertain nature of its bed and chiefly for its, constant westerly movement.

There is a tradition that the river Kusi in remote ages passed south-east by the place where Tejpur (Assam) is now situated and thence towards the east until its junction with Brahmaputra, having no communication with the Ganga. When the Kusi joined the Ganga, the united mass of the water opened the passage now called the Padma and the old channel of the Bhaglrathi from Songli (Suti) to Nadia (West Bengal) was then left comparatively dried. This junction must have taken place at some period between the third century A.D., when the Sultanganj Jahnu was established, and the seventh century A.D.. At Jot Narahari, the Kusi joins the Ganga.

There is another Kusi which flows by Almora and Ramanagar in north-western Uttar Pradesh.

## Candrabhaga

The Candrabhaga, the Rgvedic Asikm, the Akesines of Arrian, the Chantabra or Cantaba of Pliny, and the Sandabaga or Sandabal of Ptolemybelongs to the Himalayan group of rivers.

It appears that there were two rivers of this name. One is said to have issued from the Mahendra mountain, and may be identified with Bhima, a branch of the Krsna which is also known as Candrabhaga. Among the numerous references to it in the NTlamata and other Kashmirian texts, only one is such in which a stream different from the Chenab is clearly intended. Here a Candrabhaga is mentioned immediately after the Haramukuta Ganga as one of the Kashmirian rivers flowing into the Vitasta.

The Candrabhaga (modern Chenab) has got its source in Lahoul, south of Ladakh or middle Tibet. It appears to flow just above Kishtwar as a confluence of two, hfll streams. From Kishtwar it flows southwards past Jammu from where it flows in a south-westerly direction forming a doab between it and Jhelum.

## Niscira

The river Niscira has been variously called. It cannot be said that all the words used for it denoted one and the same river. But it is certain that there ..was a river of this name connected with the river Kausiki with which it is often mentioned. Dey has identified it with the river Lilajan which joins the Mohana near Ganga, their united stream forming the river Phalgu.

## Drsadvati

The Himalayan river Drsadvati is of Rgvedic fame and formed the south-eastern boundary of what was known as Brahmavarta. During the epic age it seems to have formed the southern boundary of Kuruksetra. The Matsya also locates Kuruksetra on its bank. The confluence of Drsadvati and Kausiki had particular sanctity. Sometimes Kausiki has been regarded as a tributary of the Drsadvati.It has been variously identified with the (1) Ghaggar flowing through Ambala and Sindh but now lost in the desert sands of Rajputana, (2) with the river Rakshi that flows south-east of Thaneshwar and (3) with the modern Chitrangwhich runs parallel to Sarasvati and which has its source in the Sirmur hills. Since it has been associated with Sarasvati it must be Chitrang.

## Vitasta

The Vitasta, which is the Hydaspes of Alexander's historians, the Bidaspes of Ptolemyand the Vitamsa of Buddhists, is a river of Vedic and epic fame. According to an ancient legend given in the Nilamata and reproduced in the Haracaritacintamani and Vitastamahatmya, the Vitasta is a manifestation of Parvati. After the creation of Kashmir i.e. Safidesa, Siva, on Kasyapa's request entreated Parvati to show herself in Satidesa in the guise of a river in order to purify its inhabitants from the sinful contact with the Pisacas. Accordingly, the goddess assumed the form of a river in the underworld and asked Siva to make an opening by which she might come to the surface. He struck the ground near the habitation of the Nilanaga with the point of his trident. Through the fissure thus made, which measured one Vitasti or span, the river gushed forth, receiving the name Vitasta on this account. The spring basin, which is no other than the famous Nilanaga, near the village of Vernag, in the Shahabad Pargana and the place where the goddess first appeared were known by several names such as Nilakunda, Sulaghata or simply Vitasta.

The legend makes Vitasta subsequently disappear again from fear of defilement by the touch of the sinful people. When brought to light a second time by Kasyapa's prayers it issued from the Pancahasta Naga (modern village of Panzath situated in the Divasar Pargana).

After disappearing again it came forth a third time at Narasimhasrama. Finally the goddess was induced to abide permanently in the land when Kasyapa secured for her the company of other goddesses who also embodied themselves as Kashmirian streams. Kalhana has directly mentioned Nilakunda as the source of the Vitasta.

It is the most westerly tributaries of the Indus, known by different names in Kashmir i.e. Vyath, Virnag, Adpal and Sandran. The present name Jhelum is wholly unknown to the genuine usage of Kashmir. It is formed by the meeting of several streams watering the south-eastern portion of the valley. These four rivers, namely the Sandran, Bring, Arapath and Lidar meet in the plain close to the present town of Islamabad. Sandran is the southern most stream draining the Shahabad Pargana, and receives the waters of the sacred springs. Bring comes from the side valley which forms the Bring Pargana. This river, too, is fed by a number of well known Nagas among which the most famous are the Trisandhya fountain and the springs of Ardhanansvara. The Arapath, coming from the north-east is frequently mentioned in the Nilamata under its ancient name Harsapatha. It drains the valley known as Kother Pargana which takes its name from the sacred tank of Kapatesvara. At the western end of the spur, on the slope of which this Tirtha is situated, issue the magnificent stre&ms by themselves which flow into the Harsapatha. At a short distance below the village of Khanabal where these three rivers unite their waters, they are joined from the north by those of Lidar i.e. ancient Ledari.

The river Sindhu is its greatest tributary within the valley, their confluence being as sanctified as that of the Ganga and the Yamuna at Prayaga. The name . Prayaga is directly applied to the Kashmirian-Tirtha in the Nilamata (vs. 49,146,290,766) and the Vitastamahatmya (XXI 74). Kalhana describes successively the position of the old and the new confluence in relative position to certain temples situated at the village of Trigrami and other points on the river banks. Its new confluence, which Kalhana knew in his own time, is identical with the present one opposite Shadipur. The old one lay about two miles to the south-east of it between the village of Trigrama and Paraspor plateau (the site of the great ruins of Parihasapura). Trigrama marks the position of the ancient Trigrami and a short distance south of it stands the temple of V4snu Vainyasvamin. Kalhana mentions this temple as the point where the Sindhu and the Vitasta formerely united, flowing to the left and right of the Trigrami respectively.

Its confluence with the rivers Visoka, Vitasta, Sakrapatha, Candravati, Daityaram, Krsna, Madhumati, Kularani, Candrabhaga, Dhyanadh-arim, Palasa, Silama, and the Gotranadi is mentioned in the Nilamata Purana.

It rises in the Pir Panjal range in the Kashmir state. Its traditional source is the Nag at Vithavutur - the Vitastatra of Rajatarangim close to Vernag in Shahabad Pargana. Then it turns west a little to the east of the town of Jhelum and to the west of Mirapura and flows southwards. After forming a bulge between Pir Dadan in the north-east and Khosab in the south-west it meets Chenab below Jhang and Jhang Maghiana.

## Iksu

The river Iksu belongs to the Himalayan group of rivers. The Visnu mentions it as a river in the Sakadvipa. A river at Kuruksetra was also named Iksu. At other place it is said to be an affluent of Narmada. Since the Matsya also gives its confluence with both Ganga and Narmada, it seems that there were two different rivers of the same name. The northern one has been identified with the river Oxus. The one uniting with the Narmada has been identified with the river Kalindi (east) which flows through Rohilkhand and the distt. of Kanauj. It has also been identified with the river Kali.

## Sarayu

The river Sarayu has got its source in the lake Manasa which is situated at the base of the Vaidyuta mountain, to the south of Kailasa. A forest named Vaibraja is situated on its banks. The Sarabhu of the Milindapanha had its source in the Himalayas.Mythologically speaking the river Sarayu is said to have issued from the left foot of Visnu.

The river is of Rgvedic fame. It is a well known important river of the Ramayana, with which many important incidents are connected. Ayodhya, the capital of Raghu kings was situated on this river.It flowed at a distance of half a yojana from Ayodhya and was united with Ganga.

It is the Sarabos of Ptolemy and Solomatis of Arrian and Megasthenes.

The river rises in the Kumaun mountain, a little to the north-east of the source of the Ganga. After its junction with the river Kali, it is known as Sarayu, the Ghaghra or the Deva. The river Sarayu is a tributary of the Ghaghra i.e. Gharghara, and joins Ganga at Singhi near the Chapra District (Bihar). At the north west corner of the Bahraich District it receives a tributary called Sarayu from the north-east.

It has been commonly identified with the river Deva. The river Devika is not the same as Sarayu. The Anusasanaparva of the Mahabharata and the Amarakosa distinguish between them. In the Bengal recension of the Ramayana, a Devika river is placed in the south. Although identical through great part of their course, they rise as different streams and enter the Ganga by distinct branches.

## Satadru

The Satadru - the Vedic Sutudri - was the most easterly river of the Punjab. Yaska has mentioned it. The lake Brahmasara is said to have been situated in its stream. It is also called Gaun. It is the Zaradros of Ptolemy and the Hesydens of Pliny.In Arrian's times it flowed independantly into the Gulf of Cutch.

Its source has been traced to the western region of the lake Manasa (which is also called Brahmasara). From there it takes a westerly course until it turns a little towards south-west above mount Komet. In ancient times it took an independant course to the .confines of Sindhu.Pargiter regards Satadru to be a trans-Himalayan river as its basis lies mainly to the north of the Himalayas. The united stream of the Sutlej and Bias from their confluence at Endressa to their confluence with the Chenab is called Ghaggar. It did not join the Beas, as it does now, but persued an independant course to the confines of Sindhu. It flowed south-west from where it issues from the Himalayas, into the channel called the Naiwal and then along the dry bed called the Hakra or Ghaggar at a distance of 30 to 50 miles south of, more or less parallel to, its present course. Its old current dried up owing to its diversion into the Bias valley.

## Sarasvati

The river Sarasvati is said to have issued from the lake Sarpasarovara on the Hemakuta mountain.Another Purana gives its source in the twin mountain peaks called Nara and Narayana, and still others give its source in the grove of Mango or Plaksa trees. Entreated by sage Markandeya it flowed through Kuruksetra and then went to the western direction. The Mahabharata gives its source in the Brahmasara.

The Mahabharata suggests that the name Saras-vati was given to seven rivers i.e. Suprabha, Kancanaksi, Visala, Manorama, Surentha and Vimalodaka. The Vamana Purana gives the names as Suprabha, Kancanaksi, Vimalodaka, Vimala, Mahodara or Manasahrada, Suvenu or Suvarna and Sarasvatoya. It is called Suprabha at Puskara,Kancanaksi at Naimisa Sarasvati at Kuruksetra,Manohara at' Kedara and Vimaloda at Gangadvara. When all of them meet, they are named as Saptasarasvata which is regarded as a very sacred place.

The Sarasvati was known to Rgveda as a mighty river that entered the sea. It is well known in the Brahmana and Sutra literature also. The Ramayana refers to it and the Mahabharata which refers to it frequently regards it as one of the seven currents of the Ganga. It is called Pratiloma Sarasvati, and Praci Sarasvati at Prabhasa and Puskara respectively. It is easily accessible at all places but difficult of access at three places namely Kuruksetra, Prabhasa, and Puskara. The forests namely Ambikavana and Kamyakavana are said to be situated on its bank. It is celebrated for Bhagiratha's penances. The sage Kardama also performed penances on Sarasvati.

Its confluences with Ganga (at Gangodbheda), Aruna, MandakinI and Yamuna are mentioned. It meets the western ocean in Surastra.

During the Vedic age the river appears to have been a mighty one rising from the Himalayas and entering the western sea, but the Brahmanas note its disappearance at Vinasana.
