The epic and the Puranas describe it as flowing into three parts (1) at her source in Plaksa Prasravana in Siwalik range of Himalayas and disappearing at Vinasana near Kuruksetra, (2) at her source in mount Abu to the south western end of the Aravali range in the north of Gujarat on the Arasur hills by the side of the temple of Kotesvara Mahadeva. It flows past Siddhapura and disappears in. the deserts of Kachha, and (3) when she rises in the jungles of Gir in Saurastra and meets the western sea near Prabhasapattana. Its reappearances at three places namely Camasodbheda, Sirodbheda and Nagbd-bheda have been mentioned in the epic and the Purarias.

It is the Harokhaiti of the Zendavesta, and the Haranvatish of the Behistun Inscription.

Rapson has identified it with modern Sarasuti, flowing to the west of Thanesar. It is joined in Patiala by a more westerly stream - the Ghaggar -and after passing Sirsa, is lost in the desert of Bhatnair. A dry river bed can be traced from that point to Indus. Some scholars have identified it with Sindhu. Hillebrandt thinks that it is in a few places the Arghandab. To quote Max Muller, It must have been at one time a mighty river but gradually it lost itself in a desert at a place known as Vinasana, the traditional western extremity of Aryavarta and Madhyadesa. It is a tributary of Indus. In Rgvedic period it was a mighty river that flowed into the sea. When it was known to bury itself in the sands its water may have flowed to Indus. But it is mentioned with Drsadvati probably the Chantang which with it in later times formed the boundaries of Brahmavarta.

The river which still survives flows between Satadru and Yamuna. It issues out from the Himalayas in the hills of Sirmur called the Siwalik and emerges into the plains at Adi-Badari in Ambala. It disappears at Bhavanipur. At Balachhapur it again disappears but reappears at Bara Khera, at Urnai near Pohna. It is joined by Martand and the united stream bearing still the name of Sarasvati ultimately unites with the Ghaggar or Gharghar which was evidently the lower part of Sarasvati

## Devika

The name Devika has been derived as such Devaih Sarvaih samahuta snanartham ka saridvara, Deviketi samakhyata tena sa. It rises in the Mainak hills in the Siwalik range and flows between the Gomati and the Sarayu. It is said to have passed through the country of Madras, Sauviras and Mulasthana (Alberuni identifies it with the country round Multan). Pargiter identifies it with Deeg, a tributary of the river Ravi. It is the modern Deva or Devika in Uttar Pradesh which is another name for the southern course of Sarayu, the northern one being known as Kalindi after its junction with that river in Kumaun. But the Anusasanaparva and the Bhismaparva of the Mahabharata and Amarakosa regard Sarayu and Devika as two seperate rivers.

## Yamuna

The river Yamuna, (Rg vedic fame) also called Kalindakanya and Kalindi, is' a river of Rgvedic fame. The Tritsus and Sudasas are said to have defeated their rivals on this river, The territory of Tritsus lay between the Yamuna and SarasvatI on the east and west respectively. Bharatas, too, are said to have been victorious on its banks.

It is mentioned in the Brahmanas, Sutras, and in the grammatical treatises. It is the Jobares of Arrian and Dismuna of Ptolemy. The holy river Yamuna, the destroyer of Yama's bondage, the daughter of the Sun, one of the Saptaganga, of clear water and fickle waves, is very deep. It has many whirlpools and it smiles as it were through the foam gathered on its banks. Its confluence with Sarsavati is only fanciful. The horrible Kaliyanagakunda is situated on its banks.

It is known to Chinese as Yen-Mok-Na and to Greeks as Erannaboas. The Buddhist texts included it among the five great rivers.

It is our modern Yamuna rising in the Himalayan range below mt. Kamet on the slopes of Bunderpunch a peak situated on the watershed between the Yamuna and the Ganga - at a place called Yamunottari which is eight miles from Kursoli. It is the first and the great western tributary of the Ganga which enters a valley through the Siwalik range and Garhwal and then enters the plains of northern India to flow south-wards parallel to the Ganga. It flows south-easterly from Mathura till it unites with the Ganga and forms the famous confluence at Prayaga. It receives two tributaries on the west side in Dehradun distt. the upper one of which is known as Northern Tons, and four tributaries on the left side between Agra and Allahabad i.e. Carmanvati (Chambal) Kalisindh, Vetravati (Betwa) Ken and Payosni (Paisuni). It served as a boundary between Surasena and Kosala and further down between Kosala and Vamsa. Mathura, the capital of Surasena and Kausambi the capital of Vamsa stood on its right bank.

## Dhutapapa

It is a comparatively less known river. Dey has given the names of two rivers with which it may be identified, the first'is Dhopap on the Gumfi, 18 miles south-east of Sultanpur in Oudh, and the second is a tributary of the Ganga in Benaras region.

## Trtiya and Apaga

The Trfiya of Puranas *is* mentioned in the Mahabharata also. As for the Apaga, the epic places it in Kuruksetra. The Puranas give its source in the Himalayas. 'lt is evidently the Apaya of the Rgveda which is associated with Drsadvati and Saras vati.The Vamana Purana, too, locates it in Kuruksetra region and says that̮it is a river appearing in rainy season only. The Apaga may be identified with Ayak in Kashmir, and the Trfiya with either Tiliya in Gaya or with Tista.

## Gharghara

It is mentioned in the Gangadhar Inscription of Visvavarman. It is the ancient name of the modern river Kalisindh, a tributary of the Chambal. The river Ghagra or Gogra rises in Kumaun and joins the Sarayu.

The Kalika is perhaps the present Kalinadi which flows parallel to the right oT the Ganga until it joins the Ganga at a short distance below Kanauj.The river Campa formed the boundary between Anga and Megadha. The Brahmaril is the Bahmni in Orissa flowing through Cuttack and Dinajpur.

## Kashmirian Rivers. Visoka

According to a Nilamata legend, Sati asked Kasyapa to urge Laksmi to accompany her.

Accordingly he went to Svetadvipa, worshipped Visnu and requested Laksmi to accompany Sati. Laksmi objected to it saying that since Safi is already there, the praises would be accorded to her and she herself would be deprived of the fame. Being glorified by Kasyapa, Laksmi felt satisfied and became devoid of sorrow. Thus she was named Visoka (devoid of sorrow). When she was proceeding to Kashmir under the form of a river Kasyapa urged her to go fast, since Safi was waiting for her and added that she would be praised if she purified the people before Safi did so. Kasyapa told Safi about the arrival of Laksmi i.e. Visoka and Safi came forward to meet her near Dhaumyasrama. The Visoka came from a mouse hole and saw Safi before her. Thus there is a confluence of the Vitasta and the Visoka. Since Vitasta came there first, the joint stream is known as Vitasta.

It is the present Vesau, which is a considerable river. Its traditional source is placed in the Kramasaras or Konsar Nag Lake below the Naubandhana peak. The beautiful spring which is formed by the stream of the Konsar Nag, not far from the village of Sidan is now known as Ahrabal. The Nilamata calls it Akhor Bila from which the present name might have originated. It receives all the tributaries coming from the northern slopes of the Pir Panjal range between the Sidan and Banihal Passes. After an easterly and then northerly course it unites with the Vitasta at Gambhirasamgama below Vijabror.

## Ksiptika

It is present Kutakul (a derivation of Ksiptika-kulya), a branch of the Vitasta flowing past a part of Shrinagar. It leaves the main river on the left bank, a little below the first bridge and close to the Sher Garhi palace and rejoins Vitasta after a course of about one and a half mile near the. last bridge. It forms practically the limit to the west and the south of that part of the city proper which is situated on the left bank of the Vitasta. The Rajatarangim records that it flows in the immediate vicinity of the city. Same is evident from Srivara which mentions the erection of a stone bank between it and the Vitasta.

## Kanakavahim

It is the present Kankanai or Kankanaz flowing past Bhutesvara (Buthser). It flows to the south of Sodaratirtha and meets the river Sindhu. It receives the stream which issues from the sacred Nundkol and Ganga lakes on the Haramukuta and is, therefore, described in the Nandiksetra Mahatmya as having first the name of Kalodaka before it is called Kanakavahim. It receives the water of the Nundkol lake of Nandisa.

## Madhumati

The Madhumati is the present Bandapor Nala (marked Bundkol on the map), which flows into the Volur lake from the north near the village of Kulu. The several confluence of this river are still visited as sacred places. The other stream of this river flows into the Kishanganga from the south at the temple of Sarada at Sardi. Matragom lies just where the narrow defile of the Bandapor stream ends.

## Tausi

The Nilamata associates it with Apaga and Candrabhaga. The name Tausi might have been connected with Sanskrit Tusara (snow) and thus it may mean a' river coming .from the snow. Prof. Buhler has pointed out that it is the ancient name of Tohi which is now borne by several mountain streams joining the Vitasta and Chenab from the southern slopes of the Pir Panjal range. Stein, too, identifies it with Tohi flowing through Parnotsa (Prunts) and joining the Vitasta above the town of Jhelum. Through the valley of the Prunts, Tohi leads the most convenient route towards Lohara (Loha Rin). The nearest other stream generally known as Tohi of Rajauri flows too far to the east.

## Punyoda and Bhadrasoma

The Punyoda rises from the Brahmasara. Since Rama performed penances at its banks it is also known as Ramahrada. The river Bhadrasoma forms the northern course of the Punyoda. It rises among the Suparsva mountain and joins Mahabhadra lake and so it is called Bhadrasoma. Then after crossing a number of mountains it waters the country of Uttarakurus and crossing the great continent joins the northern sea.

## Sandhya

Sandhya is the name of a river as well as that of a Puskarini. It is the famous spring commonly known to Kashmir Brahmins as Trisandhya, situated in a side valley opening to the south of a village of Devalgom in the Bring Pargana. There is another river Sindh in Malwa, a tributary of the Yamuna with which it has been identified.

## Hiranyi

It issues from Haramunda and goes to Mahapadma Lake.

## Kampana

It has been placed by Lassen in Eastern Kabulistan.

## Dugdhaganga

This river, mentioned by Bilhana as the river Dugdhasindhu joins the Vitasta from the south at the western extremity of the city. It is river Dhauli in Garhwal a tributary of the Mandakini or Mandagni.

## Krsna

The Krsna of the Nilamata is the Kishanganga river of Kashmir which has to be crossed above the present Muzzaffarabad in order to reach Urasa, the modern Hazara.

## Gambhira

This is the name of the lowest portion of the Visoka (Vesau). It is known as Gambhira between the point where it receives the Rembyara and its own confluence with the Vitasta. This confluence is known as a tirtha named Gambhirasamgama. It is frequently mentioned in the Vijayesvara-Mahatmya, Vitasta-mahatmya and the Haracaritacintamani.The distance from Gambhira to Vijayesvara is only about 4 miles. The road from Vijabror to Shrinagar on the left river bank passes the Visoka close to its confluence with the Vitasta. Kalidasa mentions a river GambhTra as a tributary of Sipra in Malwa.

## Parosni

The Nilamata twice mentions this river, in the second place in a list of rivers which join the Vitasta outside the Kashmir valley. Evidently, it is some river to the south of Lohara. The Ravi in the Punjab is also called Parusni. The great battle of the kings in the early part of the Aryan migration was fought on the banks of this river and Sudasa, the king of Tritsus, obtained victory over Kutsa, the king of Purus and his allies. A river of this name is a tributary of the Godavari also.

The other rivers mentioned in the Nilamata Purana are Devahrada (Nua. v. 103), Saratta (Nila. v. 93), Durga (Nila. v. 93, Padma P. Svarga 625), Suprabha, Kancanaksi, Visala and Manahrada (Nila. v. 599), Oghanama, Suvenu and Vimalodaka (Nila. v. 600), Bhuvenna (Nila. v. 159), Trikoti (Nila. vs. 231, 485), Harsapatha (Nila. vs. 232, 425, 1389), Candravati (Nila. vs. 233, 485, 1389), Siva (Nila. vs. 233, 1389), Citrapatha (Nila. v. 1254), Mrgananda (Nila. v. 1254), Mrgav (Nila. v. 1254), Kramunari (Nila. v. 1228), Sandili (Nila. v. 1232), Telata (Nila. v. 1238), Bhurjala (Nila. v. 1238), Triloki (Nila. v. 1389), Sugandha (Nua. v. 1389) and Kularani (Nila. v. 1390).

## The Rivers of Mahendra mountain

## Trisama

It is said to have originated in the Mahendra mountain. But the Kurma gives its source in the Suktimat range. Though the river Trisama also called Pitrsoma and the Rsikulya are mentioned as two seperate rivers, yet they are taken to be one and the same river. According to the Markandeya Purana, both the Rsikulya and the Pitrsoma issue from Mahendra range.

## Vamsadhara

The Vamsadhara, another Mahendra-river, can be identified with the modern Bansdhara, an internal river of Ganjam which flows through this district from north to south and receives a tributary on the left. It falls into the Bay of Bengal at Kalingapatam near Chicacole.

## Iksuka

It is difficult to be identified, but it must have been a river like Pitrsoma on the eastern coast.

## Tridiva

The river Tridiva, also called Trivinda and Tridivacala is said to have originated from Mahendra mountain. It is referred to in the Padma Purana and the Mahabharata.

## Rsikulya

The Rsikulya stands among the synonyms of Ganga in Haimakosa. The river which is still called Rsikulya flows past Ganjam and falls into the Bay of Bengal near Berhampur on the South-eastern Railway in the Ganjam Distt. of Orissa. Beglar identifies it with Kiyul, a tributary of the Ganga which rises on the Suktimat mountain in Bihar subdivision not far from Rajgir.

## Languliri

The river, still retaining its old name can be identified with the Languliya on wj-iich stands Chicacole between Vizianagram and Kalingapatam.The Muli, Sakha and Vimala are difficult to be identified.

## Tamraparm

The source of this river has been variously located as lying in the Mahendra, Malaya,Suktimat and Vindhya mountains. It is called Taprobane by Greek writers. Ptolemy notes that the port of Korkai was situated at its mouth and was well known for pearl-fishery. The Raghuvamsa refers to this river called Tambaravari as well known for its pearri fishery. Arthasastra refers to it as Parasamudra.

The hermitage of Agastya and his disciples and the GokarnatTrtha are situated on it. The Mahabharata associates it with the land of the Pandyas where as the Rock Edict XIII of Ashoka places it below the Pandya territory. The Tinnevelly Inscription of Naravarman Sundara II refers to it as Tanporundaaru.

Smith, referring to the Girnar text A Tambapanni, identifies it with the river Tamraparm in Tinnevelly. Law, identifying it with the modern Tannavari observes that it must have flowed below the southern boundary of the Pandya kingdom. The river unites with the Chittor in the Tirunelveli Distt. of Tamil Nadu. Some have identified it with Gundur.

## The Rivers of Suktimat mountain

## Kumari

The river Kumari, also called Sukumari, has got its source in the Suktimat range. It has been variously identified with (1) the'Keorhari rising in the Suktimat range in the Bihar subdivision near Rajgir (2) with the Somesvari by Pargiter, (3) with the Kumar in extreme north-west by Dev and Majumdar and (4) with the Kumar in Manbhum distt. by Raychaudhuri. Tavernier identifies it with the Kuari joining the river Sindh, a tributary of the river Yamuna, 12 miles from Dholpur. It is our modern Kumari which waters the Dalma hills in Manbhum.

## Krpa

It has been identified with the Kubha or the Kabul by Dev and Majumdar, with the river Kapili by Pargiter, and with modern ????, a tributary of the Babia in eastern India by Raychaudhuri and Law.

**Mandaga & Mandavahinl.**

They are regarded as seperate rivers in some of the Puranas. Both of them issue from Suktimat range. The Mandaga has been identified with the Helmand by Dev and Majumdar.

## Palasim.

This river has got its source lying in Urjayat (Luder's List No.965) or Junagadh hills. It has been variously identified with (1) the river Panjshar in the extreme north-west by Dev and Majumdar, (2) with the river Paras, a tributary of Koel in Chota-Nagpur and with (3) the river Paddair which falls into the ocean near Kalingapatam in Ganjam.This is the modern Palasim, a water course with violent torrents, which flows near the Girnar hills. in Kathiawar.

## Kasika.

It is modern Rasikoila. It has also been identified with Kiyul which rises in the Suktimat mountain in Bihar Sub-division not far from Rajgir.

## The Rivers of the Pariyatra mountain

## Kaveri

The Puranas do not agree about its source. It is said to have risen from (1) the mountain Sahya, (2) the Rksa, (3) The Pariyatra, (4) the Vindhya, (5) the Mahendra, (6) the Mahakala lake and (7) the Candra-tlrtha, a spring in the Brahmagiri mountain in Coorg.

The name Kaveri has been derived as Kaves ca tanaya devi Kaveri saritamvara. A legend in Harivamsa holds that Kaveri was changed by her father's curse from one half of the Ganga into the river which bears her name as it is called Ardhaganga.

The Matsya Purana regards it as a northern branch of the Narmada near Mandhata. The Kavyadarsa of Dandin refers to the lands on the bank of Kaveri. Ptolemy mentions it under the name Kaberos, having its source in the Adeisathron, the southern portion of the Sahya mountain. It is called the Beloved of the Pallavas, implying that a Pallava king ruled over the countries on its banks.The South Indian Inscriptions associate it with the Colas. The Calukya king Pulakeshin II crossed this river with his victorious army to enter the Cola country. The Tamil poetry glorifies it. According to Mammekhalai, Kaveri was released by the sage Agastya from his waterpot at the request of the king Kanta and for the exaltation of The children of the Sun. She was the special banner of the race of the Colas and she never failed them in the most protracted draught. The yearly freshes in the Kaveri formed the occasion of a festival in which the whole nation took part.

It is the famous river of the southern India. Its source lies in the Western Chats, flowing south-east from Mysore, through the districts of Coimbatore, Tricinopoly and falling into the Bay of Bengal in the Tanjore Distt. (Tamil Nadu). The Kaveri fall at Sivasamudra is one of the most picturesque sights in southern India. In ancient days it was famous for pearl fishery. The Kaven entered into the sea through the southern portion of the ancient kingdom of Colas. The principal port of the Colas was at Kaveripattam or Pugar on the western bank of the Kaveri while Uragapura, the ancient capital of the Colas was situated on the northern bank of this river.

## Carmanvati.

The Mahabharata gives the legendary account of this river. The river Carmanvati is said to have issued from the Pariyatra mountain. The river Carmanvati, which is frequently mentioned in the Mahabharata, Puranas, and other literary works, is the modern Chambal, a tributary of the Yamuna. It rises in the Arvalli range, north-west of Indore, and flows north-east through eastern Rajputana and falls into Yamuna. Dey gives its source in a very elevated point of the Vindhya among a cluster of hills called Janapara. It has three co-equal sources from the same cluster as the Chambal, Chambela and Gambhira.

## Satira

Some regard it to be Sadamra of the Satapatha Brahmana which formed the boundary between Kosala and Videha. Sayana in his commentary identifies it with Karmanasa. Others identify it with. either Gandaka or Rapti. It is modern Karatoya which issues in the forests of Jalapaiguri.' It flows past Rungpur and then meets the Hala Haliya.

## Candana

It has been identified with the river Sabarmati in Gujarat or with the river Chandan in Santhal Pargana in Bihar which unites with the Ganga.

## Vetravati.

The source of the river Vetravati is said to be either in the Pariyatra mountain or in the Rksa mountain. The river is mentioned in the epics and the Puranas. It is known to Buddhist works also. A city of the same name is said to have been situated on this river. The Kadambari of Bana points out that this river flows through Vidisa. The temple of Bhailaswami was situated on the banks of it at Bhilsa in the Gwalior Distt, 34 miles from Bhopal and eight miles from Sanchi. Dey gives the name of two rivers with which it may be identified. The first is an affluent of Yamuna on which stands Bhilsa i.e. ancient Vidisa and the second is Vatrak, a branch of Sabarmati in Gujarat on which Kaira (Ancient Khetaka) is situated.

## Vedasmrti.

The river Vedasmrti, also known as Vedasmrtaand Devasmrti has been identified with either the river Baita in Oudh between the rivers Tons and Gomati or with the river Besuta in Malwa.

## Vedavati

The Vedavati, also called Devavati, Vahya or Varada, was a river in southern India which formed the boundary between the two kingdoms formed out of Vidarbha. Dey identifies it with the river Hagari, a tributary of the Tunga-bhadra in the districts of Bellary and Mysore. The river, which is called Vedavati even today rises from the Western Ghats, north of Anantapur and meets the Tunga-bhadra, east of Karajgi. It is a southern tributary of the Krishna.

## Vrtraghni

The Vrtraghni, also called Vataghm and Vetraghni has been identified by Dey with Vatrak.It is a tributary of the Sabarmati in Gujarat.

## Mahanadi

At some places it has been replaced by Jyotiratha or Jyotirathya. Its source has been variously located in the Pariyatra, Rksa or Vindhya.It is said to have entered the southern ocean. It seems that there were two rivers of this name (1) one is the river Phalgu. in the Distt. of Gaya and the other is the famous Mahanadi of the Orissa.Perhaps Ptolemy has referred to it under the name Manada. It is properly a river in Orissa, but the name is applicable to any great stream.

The Mahanadi, the largest river in Orissa rises from the hills at the south-east corner of Berar. It flows past Sinhoa, the southern border of the Bilaspur Distt.. Then going in a south-easterly direction it flows past the town of Cuttack and falls into the Bay of Bengal. It receives five tributaries during its course to the ocean. Pargiter opines that it is probably not the same river that bears its old name and flows through Orissa but is a branch thereof that rises near the source of the Son.

## Mahi

The river Mahi which is the same as Mophis of Ptolemy and Mats of Periplus, was one of the five great rivers of the Pali literature. It is a branch of the Chambal in Malwa and a tributary of the Gandaka. The latter one is said to have arisen in the Himalayas and to flow into the great Gandaka about half a mile above its junction with the Ganga but practically into the Ganga near Sonpur.

It is the preset Mahi, that rises in the Pariyatra mountain in the Malwa distt. and falls into the Gulf of Cambay. The Mahisagara Samgama is a celebrated Tirtha. It has a south-westerly course upto Banswara from where it turns south to pass through Gujarat and then falls into the Gulf of Cambay (at its northern extremity at a distance of about 35 miles north from the estuary of Narmada).

## Parnasa

The epic mentions it as a river of the north-western India. It is the river Banas in Rajputana, a tributary of the Chambal, the word Banas being a corruption of Parnasa. Another river of the same name rises near mount Abu in northern Gujarat, and falls into the Gulf of Kachh. It is regarded to be the river Tamasa or Tons a tributary of Yamuna, the Prinas of Arrian, but Matsya mentions both the river Tamasa and Parnasa.

## Para

The Para, which is another name of Kausiki,is said to be a central Indian river. It has been identified with the modern Parvati which rises in Bhopal and falls into the Chambal which is the longest tributary of the Yamuna. This is the western Parvati. The eastern Parvati in Malwa, winding to the north of Narwar falls into Sindhu near Bijayanagar. A feeder of Godavari is also called Para.

## Narmada

It has numerous names and is said to have originated from the perspiration of Lord Siva. The name Narmada has been derived as Namrta tena Narmada. As she caused delight by her frequent appearance and disappearance, it is called Narmada. As it makes a lot of noise while flowing along, it is called Reva. It is called Mekalasuta since its source lies in the Mekala mountain.

The Puranas record its source variously in the Pariyatra, Vindhya, Rksa, Surathadri and the Amarakantaka mountain on the back (Pascardha) side of the Kalinga country. The Mahabharata mentions it as a southern river which formed the southern boundary of the ancient kingdom of Avanti. It is frequently mentioned in the epics and the Puranas. enters the western ocean, into the Gulf of Cambay, at a place called Jamadagnitirtha.

The Matsya gives a detailed description of the Narmada. It is the mind born daughter of the Pitrs.It flows through the country of Daksinapatha. Its water is pure, pleasant and very deep in the rainy season. From the chapter 186 to Chapter 194, the Matsya deals with the importance of the Narmada. The Ganga is most holy at Kanakhala, Sarasvati at Kuruksetra, but Narmada is most holy at every place. Even its water is highly sanctifying. The water of Sarasvati purifies the person in seven days, that of Ganga very quickly but that of Narmada at its very sight.

Its length is said to be 100 yojanas and breadth 2 yojanas. Innumerable Tirthas are situated on the Amarkantaka mountain, its source, the most important being Jalesvara tank on the western part of the Amarakantaka mountain. The river Kapila and Visalyakarim are its two famous tributaries. Its confluence with a number of rivers is mentioned in the various Puranas, but the most sacred confluence is that of Revasagara - samgama also called Narmadasagara - samgama. The chapters 190-193 of the Matsya Purana give a long list of the sacred places situated on its banks.

The Jataka refers to the crabs found in it. It is said to have passed through the forest of Jambu and Laktamala trees. It flowed through Mahismafi, the capital of the Anupa country and the shrine of the goddess Vindhyavasini is said to have been situated on its bank.

It is the Namados of Ptolemy. This is the most important river of the central and western India. It is the great river in Gujarat and one of the greatest of India. It is called Rudrakanya. Lord Siva is Rudra when he is angry and is Sankara when he is in his benevolent mood. Rudrakanya Narmada, too, has two aspects. It is awe-inspiring during its course of the few hundred miles in the moutains, but moves with peaceful tranquility and benevolence in plains.

The river rises in the Maikal range and flows more or less in a south westerly direction. Some hold that it rises in the Amarakanfaka mountain (3500 ft. above sea level) and passes through the mountain ranges of Vindhya, Satpura and enters Gujarat. The portion of .Gujarat lying on its bank was called Revakantha.

Its course in whole comes to be about 700 miles. Its course after descending from Amarakantaka and moving between the two mountain ranges of Vindhya and Satpura is 500 miles during which it is fed by a number of tributaries. About 200 miles from Amarakantaka she passes through the deep cut channel of the marble rocks near Jabalpur. For about 200 miles further upto the town of Nandia, it stretches itself into a great basin. For about 80 miles further up Makrai it moves rapidly and from there upto the Gulf of Cambay where it falls. It flows through Gujarat for about 100 miles. In its upper course there are steep mountains, dense forests on both of its banks, through which it moves on, leaping with awe-inspiring majesty. In its lower course, it passes through the fertile land of Gujarat. Near Broach it is about a mile wide. After that it proceeds towards the sea. The river here widens forming an estuary with its breadth being 16 or 17 miles. Its north bank is very high and steep.

In all there are 35 confluences of the river Narmada the best and the most important being that of Revasagarasamgama. The other important conf i ence is that of Reva and Narmada, a little above Mondia to flow down under either name.

## The rivers of the Rksa mountain

A great confusion about the names and the number of the rivers issuing from the Rksa mountain prevails in the list of rivers given in these Puranas. The rivers Sona, Mandakirii, Dasama, Citrakuta and Tamasa are recorded both in the Matsya and the Vayu. The Pippali, Suni and Suktimanfi of Matsya are the Pippala, Srom and Suktimafi of the Vayu Puraria. The Nandana, Sukrsa, Ksama, Citrotpala, Vimala, Cancala, DhutavahinT, Sum, Lajja, Mukufa and Hiadika appear only in the Matsya while the Narmada, Sumahadruma, Karatoya, Pisacika, Nilotpala Vipasa, Jambula BaluvahinT, Siteraja, Makrunaand Tridiva appear only in the Vayu. The Visnu Purana gives entirely a new list of the rivers as it mentions Tap!, Payosnl and Nirvindhya as rising in the Rksa mountain.

## Karatoya

The Karatoya, also called Karamoda issues from the mountain Rksa. The Amarakosa and the Haimakosa regard Sadariira and Karatoya to be one and the same river.

The Kalika Purana gives its length as 30 yojanas and width as 100 yojanas. It formed the western boundary of Kamarupa between Bengal and Kamarupa and flowed through ancient Pundra country. The Anusasana Pava of the Mahabharata places it near the Gandhamadana forest.

Some scholars have identified it with a branch of the Brahmaputra, which has its origin above Domar in the Distt. of Rangpur and which flows through the districts of Rangpur and Dinajpur (Bangia Desh). It is joined on the left in the Bogra district (Bangia Desh). B.C. Law agrees with Pargiter in identifying it with Karmanasa flowing into the Ganga just above the Son.

## Tamasa

According to the Ramayana, the rivers Tamasa and Jahnavi flowed side by side. Rama halted for the first time on its bank which was not far from Ganga and after crossing it undertook a journey on the road and afterwards reached the river SrimatL Rama praised it for its pure water. Dasaratha is said to have decorated the bank of this river by erecting many sacrificial posts. Its banks were crowded with ascetics.

The Prinas of Arrian and Megasthenes is Tamasa which is otherwise called Parnasa. The Khoh Capperplate Inscription of Maharaja Sarvanath mentions this river which is the modern Tamas or Tons. It rises in the erstwhile Mahiyar State and running through the northern portion of Riva Distt. of Madhya Pradesh flows into the right bank of Ganga about 18 miles south-east of Allahabad. Some hold that the Tamasa or the east Tons has its origin in Fyzabad district and it joins Ganga to the west of Ballia after flowing through Azamgarh. It flows 12 miles to the west of Sarayu.

The bank of this river is associated with the early life of Valmlki.

The name of Tamasa is properly applied to the united stream of the Madhu and Bisni from their confluence at Dhoti. The junction of the Tamasa with the Yamuna near the Sirmur frontier was a sacred place where Ekavira also called Haihaya, the progenitor of the Haihaya race and the grandfather of KartavTryarjuna, was born.

## Dasarna

The river Dasarna is said to have arisen from the Rksa mountain, but the Markandeya gives its source in the Citrakuta mountain, and the Brahma Purana in Pariyatra mountain.

The Dosarena of Periplus and the Dosaron of Ptolemy sounds very much like Dasarna and occurs in the list of rivers issuing from the Ouxenton mountain. Its association with the Kalanjaratlrthasuggests that the holy place of Kalanjara was situated on its bank. The association of this KalanjaratTrtha with mount Citrakuta in the Mahabharata makes the identity of the Dasarna river very difficult.

The country through which it flowed was named after it. It is the modern Dhasan near Saugor flowing between the Betwa (ancient Vetarvati) and the Ken, an important tributary of the Yamuna below the Vetravati and known to Arrian as the river Cainas.

## Mandakini

It is called Mandakini because the water is called 'Manda' which is said to be cool and pure like that of autumnal season. The Vqyu Purana describes it as of deep and pleasing water which can be used both for drinking and bathing purposes. The Vamana regards it only as a rainy season river.

It appears that there were two rivers of this name. (1) The northern Mandakini is said to have issued from the lake Mandodaka situated on the Kailasa mountain and is said to have entered into the ocean. On its bank is situated Nandana forest. The Ramayana mentions it as a northern river near Kailasa. (2) Another river of this name has been associated with the mountain Citrakuta and is said to have its source in the Rksa mountain.

The Himalayan Mandakini is the modern KaE or KaE - Ganga or Mandagni, a tributary, of the Alakananda, which rises in the mountains of Kedara in Garhwal. Its water is pure and speed low. It joins Sonaganga at Sonaprayaga. The southern Mandakini is the modern Mandakin, a small tributary of the Paisundi in Bundelkhand which flows by the side of the Citrakuta mountain.

## Vipasa (2)

A few Puranas mention in its place Visala which is said to have passed through Gaya. There seems to be two rivers of this name, one of which issued from the Himalayas and another had its source in the Rksa mountain. It is the modern Bias that flows past Sagar (Madhya Pradesh) into the Ken.

## Suktimati

The Mahabharata associates it with the Kolahala mountain and says that it flowed by the capital of the Cedis. A city of the same name situated on this river is mentioned in the Cetiya Jataka as Sotthi-Vatinagar. The Kosan Inscription of Maharaja Vaisravana (Yr.107) refers to this locality in the vicinity of Kausambl. The river has been identified variously (1) with the river Suvarnarekha in Orissa, (2) with Mahanadi, (3) with Sakri in Bihar, (4) with Suktel which joins the Mahanadi near Sonepur in Orissa and (5) with Ken a tributary of Yamuna flowing through Bundelkhand, Distt. of Sagar, Damoh and Jubbalpur and falls into Yamuna in the Banda Distt.

## Sona

The river Sona, also called Sonabhadra, is said to have arisen in the Rksa mountain. The Markandeya Purana gives its source in the Surathadri mountain and the Bengal recension of the Ramayana gives its source in the Mekala mountain.

The Skanda Purana associates it with the mountain Aruna and the Matsya and Padma regard it to be a tributary of the Narmada.

It is the Sones of Megasthenes and Arrian, and Soa of Ptolemy. It was also known as Hiranyavaha or Hiranyabahu, the Eranhaboas of the Greek geographers. The word seems to be a contraction of the Sanskrit word Suvarna. The river was named Hiranyavaha either because of its yellow sands or because they contained gold dust. It flowed through the five hills encircling Girivraja and also through Magadha and was called Magadhi. Its course through Rajagrha in Magadha was known as Sumagadha or SumagadhT.' At the time of the Mahabharata, it appears to have passed through the present bed of the Banas, which is immediately west of Arrah.

It is the modern Son the greatest known lower tributary of the Ganga. Its source lies in the Maikala range in the Jabalpur district. After flowing north-east through Baghelkhand, Mirzapur and Shahbad districts it joins the Ganga near Patna, 10 miles above Dinajpur. It receives five tributaries in the Baghelkhand district, four in the Mirzapur district, one in the Palmau district and one in the Shahbad District.

Formerly, even in the time of Akbar, it joined the Ganga at Maner, a little above Bankipur, the western suburb of Patna from which its junction is now 16 miles distant and high up the Ganga. The Son and Sarayu now join Ganga at Singhi or rather between Singhi and Harji Chupra, about two miles to the east of Chirand and eight miles to the east of Chapra. In Ajatastru's time it joined the Ganga well below Patna, not as at present, but some twelve miles above it.

## The Small Rivers of Rksa Mountain

The Baluvahiru has been identified by Dey with the river Bagin in Bundelkhand, a tributary of the Yamuna. The river Siteraja which is known variously cannot be identified. The river Vegavahini is said to have arisen in the Rksa mountain according to some Puranas, but in the Vindhya according to the Vamana. It may be the same as Vegavati, the river Baiga or Bygi in the Madras district. The towns of Madura and Kandpura were situated on its bank. Among the Pisacika, Pippala and Sroni, none can be satisfactorily identified. Pargiter takes Pippala and Sroni as one and identifies it with Paisuni or Parsaroni, a tributary of the Yamuna between the Ken and the Tons.

## Sakuli and Tridiva

Pargiter identifies the Sakuli with the river Sakri, a tributary of MahanadT, which flows into the Ganga between Patna and Monghyr. The river Tridiva is difficult to be identified.

## Citrakuta and Citrotpala

The rivers Citrakuta and the Chitrotpala, too, had their source in the Rksa mountain. The river Citrakuta seems to be connected with the mountain Citrakuta, and may be the river that falls into the Yamuna near Karavi. It was either the same as MandakinT or a part of it. The Citrotpala can not be identified.

## The rivers of the Sahya mountain

## Godavari

The river Godavari is the most important river of the Sahyadri group. Almost all of the Puranas except the Markandeya which gives its source in the Vindhyas, regard it to have arisen in the Sahya mountain. A sacred Tirtha called Traymvaka is situated at its source. In Tryamvaka there is a sacred tank Kusavarta under which the river after issuing from the mountain is said to have flowed. The portion of the river, on which this Tryamvakatirtha is situated is called the Gautaml.A whole section of the Brahma Purana is devoted to the praise of the river Gautaml. It was brought on earth by Gautama, who begged it of from Sambhu. The river is said to have entered the ocean as sevenfold. It has been mentioned to have passed through Janasthana and the Dandaka forest.

It is the Gayavari of the Jains and the Goaris of Ptolemy. The Buddhists record its source as lying in the Jatophataka mountain near which is the Kavitha forest. It has been beautifully described in the Ramayana. Rama is said to have crossed this river at Bhadracalam in the Godavari Distt.. The Pancavati forest is said to have been situated on its banks. The Puranas record many sacred places on its bank. It was commonly accepted boundary of the ancient Kalinga country.

It is the largest and the longest river of the southern India having its s urce in the Western Ghats. The exact place of its source is in Brahmagiri on the side of a village callt d Trayamvaka, 20 miles from Nasik. It flows in a south-easterly direction below the Vindhya range cutting a valley through Western Ghats. It falls into three branches in the Bay of Bengal and forms a large delta at its mouth. Several tributaries join it in the Hyderabad and Madras Districts.

## Krsnaveni

The Krsnaveni is a southern river the source of which is said to be either in Sahya or in the Vindhyas.

It is the Tyana of Ptolemy. Though mentioned in the Puranas at several places, it is one of the very little known rivers of ancient India. It is the united stream of the Krsna and Vena and is the modern Krishna which rises at Mahabaleshvara in the Western Ghats. It falls into the Bay of Bengal at Sippelar, a little south of Masulipatam.

## Bhimarathi

Like the Krsnaveni its source, too, has been given in Sahya as well as in the Vindhya mountain. According to a legend Lord Siva, after destroying the demon Tripurasura rested here. At that time the Magadhan king Bhimaka propitiated Lord Siva and asked him to forgive him of the guilt of hunting the two sages during his expedition. His wish was granted. There were drops of perspiration on Siva's body on account of fatigue produced during his fight with the demon. At Bhimaka's request, these drops of perspiration were turned into a river which .is now called Bhima after Bhimaka.

It is mentioned in the iJaulatabad plates of the western Chalukya king Jayasimha II. It is the modern Bhima, the main tributary of the Krishna,which flows in the north-western portion of the Poona distt. from where it takes a south-easterly course and then falls into Krishna, north of the Raichur, Hyderabad district. A sacred temple of BhTmasankara is situated at its source.

## Tungabhadra

It issues from the Sahya mountain and flows in the south. This is most important among the lower tributaries of the Krishna. The two streams called the Tunga and the Bhadra originate in the Western Ghats on the western border of Mysore at a place called Gangamula. The Tungabhadra meets the Krishna, north of Nandikothur in the Kurnool Distt.. The four sets. of the Ashoka edicts are found within the belt of Krishna and Tungabhadra.

## Vanjula

It is said to have arisen from Sahya according to the Matsya and the Vayu, and from Rksa and Suktimat according to the Vamana. The Brahma Puraria regards it as a Mahendra - river. Dey has identified it with the river Manjira, a southern tributary of the Godavari.

## Vahya

It is difficult to identify the river Vahya. The Agni Purana reads Varada in its place, which can be identified with the Varada or Vedavati, a southern tributary of the Krishna. Dey takes it to be the river Wardha in Madhya Pradesh.

## Apaga

The Rgveda mentions Apaga as Apaya, which is frequently mentioned with SarasvatI and Drsadvati. It is regarded only as a Varsakalavaha river in the Kuruksetra region. It may be identified with the river Ayuk, west of Ravi in Punjab. The Apaga, as a southern river, must be different.

## Sriprayoga

Its source is given both in the Sahya and the Vindhya. Nothing much can be said about it except that it was a western tributary of the-river Krsna.

## The Rivers of the Vindhya mountain Tapti

The Puranas do not agree about its source. It is surprising to note that this river is not mentioned in the Mahabharata. Ptolemy mentions it under the name Nano Counas which is said to have originated in the eastern part of the Vindhya mountain.

The scholars have a difference of opinion about its source J. Ph. Vogel says that it does not rise in the Vindhya while Dey gives its source in the Vindhya mountain at the portion called Gonanagiri and says that it falls into the Arabian sea.

The river is the modern Tapti which rises in the Multai plateau to the west of Mahadeva hills in Gondwana and flows eastward and forms the natural boundary between Madhyapradesh and the north western tip of Berar. It falls into the sea at Surat. It receives a number of important tributaries.

## Payosni

The river Payosni has got its source in the Vindhya mountain. The Visnu and the Brahma Puranas give its source in the Rksa and the Agni Purana in the Sahya mountain.

It is said to have flowed through the countries of Tomara and Hamsamargas and said to have been seperated from Narmada by Vaidurya mountain.The epic regards it as a river in the Vidarbha country.

Pargiter, therefore, identifies it with the modern Puma, the tributary of the Tapti, together with the lower part of Tapti into which the Purna continues.But the Puranas have Payosni and Tapafi distinctly as two seperate rivers in the same verse. The Padma Purana even has Tapfi, Payosni and Pur-ria in the same verse. Some have therefore identified it with the Pain or Painganga, a branch of Wardha in Madhyapradesh. Others identify it with the Purti in Travancore. Cunningham regards it to be the Pahoj, a tributary of the Yamuna between the Sindhu and the Betwa.

Its source appears to be near that of the Krsna. It flows near the beginning of the Dandaka forest, which should place it rather near the source of the Godavari. Hall writes, I do not undertake to prove that, in early times, the name of Payosni may not have been applied to the whole of modern Taptee, but the case was otherwise in the Puranic period as we see from the Visnu Purana, and as appears from Markandeya (5724) and the Bhagavata Puranas (V.1918).

Prof. Lassen concluded that the Payosni is the Tapti in its entirety, partially by the assumption that the Tapi is not mentioned in the Mahabharata. At Chandway a stream now called Purna joins the Tapti. Whether Purna represents the Payosni or whether the Tapti about Chandway does so, is uncertain. Painganga falls into Wardha, so it cannot be Payosni. Simariy Wardha, too, canot be accepted to be Payosni.

## Vena

The river Vena, which is variously called, is regarded as one of the Sapta-Ganga. The Puranas give its source either in the Vindhya or in the Pariyatra mountain.

Pargiter identifies it with the Wainganga and its continuation, the Pranhita. He also suggests its identification with the river Pennar between Krishna and the Kaveri. Dey gives the names of three rivers with which it can be identified. They are (1) the Bena - a branch of the Krishna rising in the Western Ghats, (2) The Krishna itself and (3) the river Wainganga, a tributary of the Godavari rising in the Vindhyas called the Veniganga.

It is the modern Vena, a tributary of the Krishna, the combined stream of the two rivers being often mentioned as Krsnavena or Krsnavem.

## Vaitarani

Its descent to the earth has been ascribed to Parasurama. According to the Mahabharata, it is the name of the BhagirathT Ganga itself, but the same epic takes it to Kalinga and Kuruksetra. The Vamana, too, associates it with Kuruksetra and says that it is only a rainy - season river. The Skanda Purana regards it to be a mythical river only. The Buddhists, in line with the Brahmanical tradition, regard it as a river of Yama. The Ramayana located it in the north. Ptolemy mentions it as Dosaros.

It is the river Vaitarani, which rises in the southern part of the district of Singhbhum. Rising among the hills in the north west of the Keonjhar Distt. it flows in a south-westerly and then in an easterly direciton, forming the boundaries between the Keonjhar and Mayurbhanj Distt. and between Keonjhar and Cuttack. It enters the Cuttack Distt. near Balipur and after flowing a winding easterly course across the delta where it marks the boundary lines between Cuttack and Balasore, it joins the river Brahmani and passing by Candbali finds its way into the city under the name of the Dharma river.

Dey identifies it with the Dantura rising near Nasik and places it on the north of Bassein.Another river with which it can be identified flows between Kedara and Badarinatha. From its reference in the Nilamata it seems to be one of the streams which join Vitasta above Vijayesvara. The Purohitas of Vijayesvara actually take the name Vaitarani to be the appellation of the Rembyara, i.e. Ramanyatavi near Surapura, in its course near and below Supiyan.

## Nirvindhya

This river has been placed between Ujjain and the river Vetravati. It actually lays between Vidisa and Ujjayirii i.e. between the Dhasan a tributary of the Vetravati and the Ksipra.

It is the modern Kalisindh, a tributary of the Carmanvati. As the Kalisindh is probably the Sindhu of Meghaduta, the identification of Nirvindhya with Newaj, another tributary of the Cambal between the Vetravati and Sindh, is more acceptable.

## Ksipra

The Puranas give the source of Ksipra as lying in the Vindhya mountains. The Kalika Purana gives its origin from the lake called Sipra situated to the west of the Himalaya mountain. The Markandeya Purana gives its source in the Pariyatra mountain.

It is known to the Jains. It was a historical river on the bank of which the city of Ujjayini and the forest named Mahakalavana was situated. Its confluence with the river Ksata, known as Ksatasamgama is an important place of pilgrimage.

This is a local river of Gwalior which flows into the Cambal a little below Sitamau. It receives two tributaries.

## Maha Gauri

The Vindhya river Mahagauri, which Megasthenes mentions under the name of Garoia and Arrian under the name Gouraios has been identified by Pargiter with the river Brahmarii flowing through Orissa.

## Nisadha

As the name suggests it must be a river associated with the Nisadha country. It may be one of the small tributaries of the Narmada and the Tapti.

## Durgama

It has been identified by Pargiter with another smaller stream called the Brahmani that flows through the Murshidabad district into the right bank of the Bhagirathi.

## Toya

It is a sacred river which flows through the districts of Rungpur, Dinajpur and Bogra (Bangia Desh). It formed the boundary between the kingdoms of Bengal and Kamarupa during the epic age. It flowed through the ancient Pundra country. The rivers Sitibahu Visvamala, Kumudvati, Sila, and Surasa cannot be identified.

## The River of Malaya mountain

Four rivers, namely the Krtamala, Tamraparni, Puspaja, and Utpalavati are said to have arisen from the Malaya mountain. The source of the Krtamala, also known as Rtumala , Satamala and the Krtamala\[ has been located variouly in the Malaya, Suktimat and the Vindhya mountain.

It may be the modern Vaigai which flows past Madura.

The river Tamraparm is also called Tambaravari or the united stream of the Tambaravan and the Chittar in Tinnevelly which rises' in the Agastikuta mountain. The port of Kolkoi, noted for its pearl fishery, was situated to the north of this river. It is now situated five miles inland. It has been described by Ptolemy.

The rivers Puspaja and Utpalavati may be the rivers Pambai in Tranvancore and the Vypar in Tinnavelly.

## India : Her flora And Fauna

Only the Matsya Purana out of the four Puranas namely the Nilamata, the Matsya, the Visnu and the Vayu gives a long list of herbs, birds and beasts. The other Puranas refer to them only casually. They mention one or the other grain or bird only with reference to the performance of Sraddha or yajna.

## Animals

The author of the Puranas divided them into two categories i.e. domestic animals and forest animals. They are further divided into two classes (1) those who are milk yielding and (2) others whose flesh is eaten. The domestic animals included cow, ox (the height of which from foot to hump has been given as 76 angulas), goat, sheep, horse (height 50 Angulas) pony, ass, camel, buffalo, pigs and black goat. The' cows were of various species such as Kapila (brown cow) Krsna (black cow) Gau (cow) and Camari. The oxen are said to be blue and red also.

The forest animals included boars, deer, species of rhinoceros (vadhrinasa and khadga) and wild ox (Gavaya). The deer (Mrga) were of various species such as Kashirimrga (musk - deer) Krsna mrga (black deer) Ruru (a kind of deer) Prsat'(spotted antelope) Harina (deer), Saranga RohT and Camara (another kind of deer). The other forest animals were godha(the alligator, an iguana), Sarabha (a young elephant, also a fabulous animal who is said to have eight legs and to be stronger than a lion and is mountainous carnivorious animal), simha (lion) varaha (a boar) sasa (rabit) nakula (the mungoose, a Viverra ichneumon), sukara (a hog), mesa (a ram, sheep), avika (sheep, the hair of which were used for making wool), Svana (dog), Srgala (jackal), nyanku (a kind of antelope), Gauramukha, Kapi(monkey), langula (an ape), taraksu (a hyena), vyaghra (tiger), and vyaleya (a beast of prey).

The height of the elephant is given as 108 angulas. Their species are (1) Airavata (having four teeth, white coloured), Madra (a kind of elephant) Anjana, Vamana, Suprafika (greenish), Padma and Nanda (white), Pundanka (grey), Akulikas including Puspadantaka, Brhatsama, Sadadanta, Danta-Puspavana, Tamraparm, Vamadeva (black like collyrium) and Vamana, Nilavrta, Laksana and Pingala (raddish brown, tawny). The elephants Mahapadma and UrmimaE were used in battle field.

The flesh of sasa (hare, rabbit), nakula (mungoose), sukara (a hog), mrga, krsnamrga, gavaya, mesa, khadga, varaha, goat, black goat harina, prsat, ruru and godha was taken. The milk yielding animals were camels, sheep, buffalos and cows.

## Himalayan Beasts:-

Mrga and Mahamrga - deer, Vyaghra - tiger, Kesari-lion, Simha - lion, Dvipa-elephant, Sarabha -a mountaineous carnivorous animal, Vrka-wolf, Rksa - bear, Taraksa - hyena, Vanara - monkey, Golamgula - a kind of monkey with a dark body, red cheek and tail like that of a cow. Sasalekana -Sasa is a rabbit, Marjara - cat, Kadamba - a kind of goose, Vidala - cat, Mpsaka - mouse, Nakula - a mungoose, Simhvana - porcupine, Matariga - an elephant, Mahisa-buffalo, Gavaya - a species of ox, Vrsa - a bull, Camara-deer, Srmara - a kind of deer Gaurakhara ɱ a wild donkey, Urabhra- a ram. Mesa - a ram, sheep, Saranga - a variagated deer, Kukura-a dog, Nila and Mahamla- an ox of a dark blue colour, Karala- a dreadful, formidable animal, one having projecting teeth. Mrgamatrka- a doe, Ramasarabha - the animal called Sarabha having large teeth, Karaka-Sambara - a kind of deer, Krtamala-spotted antelope, Kalapuccha - species of animal living in marshes, Torana (???), Damstra -the animal having large teeth. Khadga - a rhinoceros, Varaha - a boar, Turanga - a horse, Khara - a donkey, Gardabha - an ass.

## Birds:

Suka-parrot. Uluka - owl. Kaka-Crow. Syena -a hawk, falcon. Bhasa - cock. Vardhinasa - a kind of crane. Kukkuta-cock. Mayura-peacock. Kalavimka-sparrow. Kapota - a dove, pigeon. Lava-bird. Tittira- the partridge. Bala - crow. Drsti - crab. Trisamkhanetra - Samkhanakha is a kind of equatic animal. Hamsa - swan, goose, duck. Kalahamsa- a swan. Cakravaka - the ruddy goose. Cakora - a kind of bird, the greek partridge said to feed on moon beams. Bhadra - a species of wag-tail. Kurara - an osprey. Sarasa - the Indian crane or swan. Baka - crane. Kapila - cuckoo. Kapinjala - the Cakora bird. Kraunca - a curlew, heron. Sarika- a kind of bird. Camikara-gold, golden bird. Karandava:- a sort of duck. Jivajivaka- the Cakora bird. Grdhra- vulture. Asvakarni, Trisira, Sumukha and Surasa cannot be idntified.

Kaka, Grdhra and Asvakarni are the sons of Jatayu. Bhasa, Uluka, Kaka, Kukkuta, Mayura, Kalavimka, Kapota, Lava and Tittira are the sons of Bhasi. The mother of Vardhinasa is Kraunchi. Suki had the sons named Trisira. Susukha, Bala, Prstha, Trisamkhanetra and Surasa. The sons of Dhrtarastri are Hamsa, Kalahamsa and Cakravaka. The sons of Syeni are called Kurara, Sarasa and Bakotah. The father of all these is Garuda.

## Crops &amp; herbs

The author of the Puranas does not classifly the vegetation according to the climatic conditions or even region-wise. The basis of the division is either the taste and flavour or its utility. It is an established fact that the Puranas are mostly religious in character and that is why the division is not a scientific one. The Matsya Purana classifies the herbs and vegetation under different groups on the basis of their taste such as sweet, salty bitter, sour, estringe, pungent or acid. Some of the herbs are designated as beneficial and antipoisonous.

The Vayu Purana attributes the growth of vegetation to Ira who had three daughters Lata, VTrudha and ValII, who were the mothers of all kinds of herbs, trees and creepers. From Lata were born all kinds of herbs which have got no flowers, those which have got both flowers and fruits and those growing on the bank of a river. VallT gave birth to clump, cluster of trees, thickets, wood and bushes grass and bamboo etc.. VTrudha was the mother of spreading creepers.

The whole crops were divided into two typesGramyausadhis (cultivated) and Yajnika (which are said to have grown without cultivation and are used in the performance of the sacrifice, worship of deity and Sraddha. They include both rural and wild herbs and plants).

## Gramyausdhis (Cultivated crops)

The cultivated crops (Gramyausadhis) are fourteen in number. They are:-

Vrlhi (a variety of rice), Yava (barley), Masa (horse bean), Godhuma (wheat), Anu (small grains), Tila (oil seed), Priyangu (sesamum), Kulatthaka (a kind of pulse), Udara (a sort of grain with long stalks), Koradusa (kado) Satmaka (a kind of peas, Pisum Arvense), Mudga (a variety of kidney beans), Masura (a kind of pulse), Nispava (a kind of pulse), Adhakya (another variety of pulse), Canaka (chick-pea) and Sana (hemp).

## Yajnikausadhis

The number of Yajnika crops is also fourteen. They are:-

Vrihi (rice or paddy), Yava (barley), Masa (horse bean), Godhuma (wheat), Anu (small rice), Tila (oil seed). Priyangu (sesamum), Kulatthaka (a kind of pulse), Syamaka (black paddy), Nivara (rice growing wild or without cultivation of land), Jartila (wild sesamum), Gavedhu (a kind of grass eaten by cattle), Venuyava (bamboo seed) and Markatak? (a kind of grain).

## Those that are used in Sraddha-

Nagara (an orange), Dirghamulaka (a herb or grain having long roots), Vamsl (bamboo-manna), Karira (the shoot of a bamboo), Surasa (juicy fruits), Sarjjakas (the Sala tree), Bhustrna (the grass growing on the earth) Arista- (soapberry trees), Tumula (that one which makes noise), Vilva, Inguda (medicinal tree Terminalea Catappa), Svadana -vidala (mountain ebony).

## Prohibited in Sraddha

Lasuna (garlic), grinjana (a small red variety of garlic or onion), palandu (onion), pindamulaka (carrot), karambha (flour or meal mixed with curds), Niryasa (exudation of trees or plants, gum, juice, resin), lavana (salt) and Usarana (black pepper, ginger).

## Unclassified

Kulmasa - a kind of grain, half ripe barley. Iksu-sugar cane. Rajamasnau-small and large beans. Alabusa - the bottle gourd. Siddharthaka- white mustard. Krsnatila-black oil seed. Nispava-coriander seed. Ajaji - cumin seed, Nigeda Indica, Ficus oppositifolia. Dhanyaka-grain, rice. Goksira- (milk of a cow). Kusumbha - safflower, safforn. Kumkuma- Safforn. Kustumbaru- coriander seed. JIraka-cumin seed. Gaurasarsapa - white mustard. Raji- black mustard. Maksika-honey. Karpasa-cotton tree.

Kumuda - water lily said to open at moon rise. Usira - the fragrant root of the plant Andropogon Muricatus. Guggula - a particular fragrant gum resin. Raktasaliya- rakta is red and SalTya is one belonging to a house i.e. red rice.

During the time of Prthu, the earth yielded crops on its own accord and the cows yielded sufficient milk. There was no division of the villages and cities. Then perhaps there was a draught. Prthu made the earth even, pulled down the mounds and ridges etc.. It was from the time of Prthu that the cultivation of the land started and such a division came into being.

There was a rule that a person, if found guilty of cutting the trees would be punished. It shows that even at that time the people were fairly concious of the importance of ecological balance, purity of environment and that they fully appreciated the beauty of nature.

Tilaka - A species of tree with beautiful flowers. Kalidasa describes the beauty of-this tree as being akin to that of the saffron-mark on the forehead of a woman. The name suggests a relation to tila the Sesamum plant Sesamum indicum Linn. This plant has flowers that have got a very .pretty appearance. It is a shrub and not a tree. It grows four to five feet in height. Its flower has five petals. The lower one is the longest. In its wild variety there is a prominent spot on the longest petal which is highly suggestive of the saffron mark on the forehead of a woman, (2) a sesamum tree. Haridra - Turmeric. ## The Sacred Places of India

The Puranas, which are basically religious books abound in the description of and references to the religious places regarded as sacred. Their number is so large that it is very difficult to deal with them in so limited a space. This idea of sacredness is so overpowering the minds of the authors of these religious books that not only are the mountains and rivers held in esteem but also the tanks, bathing ghats stoneslabs wells and even the trees are regarded as Tirthas. As the evidence of the Puranas furnishes us with no clues about their nature and place of existence, in most of the cases, it becomes very difficult to distinguish between them and identify them. They are named one after another and that too without any system of arrangement and without assigning them to any particular region. Only the most famous ones are described in details. In the following pages an attempt has been made to classify them. Thus the sacred places lying on the banks of the Naramada are given at one place lakes and hermitage are put aside. The Kashmirian tirthas are mentioned separately. The rest according to their importance are given in alphabetical order. It is not possible to classify them on the regional basis because many of them cannot be identified at all since no other supplementary evidence is available about them that may help us m identifying them.

## Avimukta

The Avimukta also called Smasana and Anandavana is so named because it is never left by the god Sankara. It is situated near Benarasbetween Varana and Asi and extends for two yojanas from east to west and half yojana from south to north. The Skanda Purana gives its extent as five Krosas. A Sivalinga, named Avimuktesvara is situated here. It is regarded very sacred.

## Ayodhya

During the Brahmana period it was only a village, which later on rose to the prominence of a capital city of the kingdom of Kosala during the epic age. It was also known as Vinlta, Saketa, IksavakubhumI, Ramapuri and Kosala. It was twelve yojanas long and nine yojanas broad. It was situated on the bank of the river Sarayund was closely associated with the epic heroes Rama and his forefathers. It is sacred to Jains also, whose first of the twenty four Tirthankaras Adinath was born at Ayodhya. Alberuni places Ayodhya about 150 miles south-east from Kanauj. Some scholars think that Ayodhya and Saketa were identical but Rhys Davids says that both the cities existed in Buddha's time. Perhaps they were adjoining cities. The Skanda Purana mentions various temples and sub-tirthas of Ayodhya such as Gopratara, CakratTrtha and Agnitirtha. Hiuen Tsang mentions it under the name O-yu-T'o having a circuit of 5000 li. Its capital was 20 h in its circuit. It abounded in cereals, flowers and fruits. The climate is said to be temperate and agreeable.

It has been identified with the present Ayodhya, 4 miles from Fyzabad on the banks of the river Sarayu which rises from the Kumaun mountains and after its junction with Kali is known as Ghagra or Gogra.

## Avanti

Avanti is both a river and a city. The river Avanti should naturally be connected with the city of Avanti or Malwa and may be identified with the river Avanti which rises near Mhow and flows into Chambal. Dey takes it to be the river Ksipra on which stands Ujjain. The city of Avanti is one of the seven cities which bestow emancipation. It is said to have been situated on the bank of Sipra in the country of Malava. The Skanda Purana says that it is the city of KusasthaE that came to be called Avanti later on and the same is now called Ujjayiru.

## Agastyasrama

It has been placed on the banks of the river Suvarrimukhari. A Linga called Agastyesa is said to have been situated here. The Ramayana places it on the river Godavari, and the Skanda on Nyankumafi. There is a difference of opinion regarding its location at (1) 24 miles south-east of Nasik now called Agastipur, (2) Akola to the east of Nasik, (3) Kolhapur in the state of Maharastra (4) Sarai-Aghat 40 miles south-west of Itah and about a mile to the north-west of Sankisa in Uttar Pradesh. (5) On Vaidurya mountain or Satpura hills. (6) The sage Agastya is still said to reside at the Agastyakuta mountain in Tinnevilly in which the river Tamraparm has its origin. It may be the same as Kalai-Kael of Marcopolo. (7) About 12 miles from Rudraprayaga in Garhwal is a village called Agastyamuni which is said to have been the hermitage of the sage. It is situated on the river Mandakiiu. There is a temple of Karttikeya at a distance of 6 miles from Agastyamuni.

## Apaga

It is a sacred river flowing through the state of Tamil Nadu. The Rgveda associates the Apaya (Puranic Apaga) with Sarasvati and Drsadvati. The Vamana Purana places it in Kuruksetra.

## Ambara

This river falls from the peak of Meru. After circumambulating it, it waters the Deyabhraja forest and enters the Sitoda lake. Then after passing over a number of mountains such as Supaksa, Sikh! etc. it waters the Ketumaladvipa of the Miecchas and enters the western sea. Though we have no knowledge of the river of this name, yet it is possible that it was a river associated with the country of the same name, which is the present region of Jaipur, so called from its ancient capital of that name which is now called Amer, founded by Ambarisa, son of Mandhata.

## Alaka

The Vayu Purana associates it with Kubera, but at one place we find that Pururava resided there,thus suggesting its location somewhere near Pratisthana in the Himalayas.

## Asvatirtha

It is situated at the confluence of the Ganga and the Kalindi (eastern ???) in the district of Kanauj.

## Arjuna

The hermitage of Arjuna has been located in the north.

## Asita

This tirtha has been attributed to the sage Asita.

## Angaravahika

It is a sacred river.

## Arunoda

It is a sacred lake situated to the east of the Meru mountain. The country named Arunoda has been identified with the Garhwal region, through which flows Alakananda, and which has its capital at Shrinagar.

The Abhaya (Vayu Utt. 1558), Alampura (Matsya P.2250), Angabhuta (Matsya P.2251), Alambusa or Alambhusa (Matsya P.2251) Asvattha (Matsya P. 1351), Aksaya-Prabhava (Vayu Utt. 1528), the mountain Ausira (Vayu Utt. 1529) and the mountain Amara (??. v. 129, Matsya P. 18126 Sk. Kasi 69118) are mentioned in name only.

## Indramarga

It is a northern tirtha. An Indresvara Linga is mentioned in Kasi.

## Istikapatha

It, too, has been placed in the northern region.Utkalavartaka

It is a sacred Ksetra. Uttara and Umatunga,are mentioned in name only.

## Usna

It is the modern Unai where there is a kunda of hot water. The sage Sarabhanga is said to have his hermitage here. He suffered from leprosy. When he visited this place, on his way to forest, he created this hot spring by striking the earth with his bow and the sage was cured of his disease by its water. The Unai-Bansad road station is situated at a distance of 26 miles from Bilimora on the Bombay-Kharagoda line.

## Elapura

It is modern Elur or Ellora. The cave temple of Kailasa was constructed on the hills by Krishnaraj of the Rashtrakuta dynasty of Badami who ruled between 763-775 A.D.. Cunningham has identified it with Verawal in Gujarat butLls not considered as correct.

## Ekambhaka

It may be the same as Ekamrakaksetra which is said to be equal to Varanasi. Since there were Ekamra trees in the previous Kalpa there, it was called Ekamraka. The Skanda Purana mentions it as such M'c 4ldPiR''fl TiTftpi j ??+?? ?? -rrmNt:

## Onkarapatana

Sivalinga named Amaresvara is said to have been situated at Onkara in Kasi.

## Ausanasa

It has been mentioned in name only.

## Kuruksetra

The sacred place of Kuruksetra has been frequently mentioned in the Puranas. Its history can be traced back to the Rgveda where a king named Kurusravana is mentioned. During the Brahmana period it attained a great fame and came to be regarded as a very holy land, for within its boundary flowed the sacred streams Drsadvati, Sarasvati and Apaya. Before the close of the later Vedic' period Aryans had marched into the plains of Ganga and Yamuna and the centre of the Aryan culture was shifted to the Madhyadesa from Sarasvati to the Gangetic delta, the region occupied by the Kurus, Pancalas and other tribes. The land between Sarasvati and Drsadvati, called Kuruksetra, was occupied by the Kurus. Gradually, the Vedic Aryans moved further to the east and south and the centre of Vedic culture was again shifted to Mithila.

According to Taittarlya Aranyaka, Khandava, Turghna and Parinaha formed the borders of Kuruksetra. Khandava forest was situated on the bank of Yamuna. Parinaha has been identified with Porenos of Arrian, a small tributory of Indus.Turghna, according to Prof. Kane is modern Srughna, 40 miles to the north-west of Saharanpur.Cunningham has identified it with modern village Sugh which is surrounded on the three sides by the old bed of Yamuna.

During the epic period it was called Brahma varta. Not only the place, but also the people living here to the north of Drsadvati and south of Sarasvati are said to be living as if in the heaven. Manu calles it as a part of Brahmarsidesa which comes next in merit to Brahmavarta.

Kuruksetra, also called Dharmaksetra and Brahmaksetra was founded by Kuru, the son of Samvarana, on the banks of Drsadvati. For many years, this extensive ksetra was being ploughed by Kuru himself with the intention that all those who died here should be received into. heaven. Indra came to him many times and laughed at such an idea. Gradually Indra was won over but the other gods expressed their doubt saying that the death without a sacrifice did not merit a place in heaven. Kuru and Indra arrived at a compromise and decided that Indra should admit into heaven those who died at this place either while performing a penance or while fighting on this battlefield. So this place became both a battlefield and a land of piety. The same story is recorded in the Vamana -Purana also.

It is called the Uttaravedi of Pitamaha, one of the five Brahmavedis. It is called Ramahrada and Syamantapancaka also. The Puranas relate a legend about the name Ramahrada. It is said that as Ksatriyas murdered Jamadagni, the father of Parasurama he (Parasurama) decided to take revenge and killed so many of them that five big pools were filled with their blood. After performing penances, Parasurama satisfied the Pitrs and asked them to forgive him for the sin of killing such a great number of people. The Pitrs forgave him, blessed him and converted those pools of blood into those of holy water.

The Puranas mention seven forests namely Kamyakavana, Aditivana, Vyasavana, PhalkTvana, Suryavana, Madhuvana and the Punyasirnavana or Sftavana round about Kuruksetra. The rivers flowing through Kuruksetra were Sarasvati, Vaitarani, Apaga, Ganga, Mandakini, Madhusrava, Amiu, Kausiki, Drsadvati, and Hiranvati. All of them except Sarasvati are rainy season rivers. It is called Sannihiti since all tirthas gather together here every month on the Amavasya day. Sannihiti tirtha is also known as Rudrahrada.

More than one hundred sub-tirthas are mentioned in and around Kuruksetra, the most famous ones being:-

(1) Varahatirtha - where Visnu stood in his incarnation of the boar.

(2) Vyasasthana - where Vyasa resolved to die of grief for his son Suka. It is situated a little further to the Varaha-tirtha. It is the modern Basthali 17 .miles to the south-west of Thaneshvar. A Vyasatirtha is mentioned in Yogimtantra and the Narada Purana also.

(3) Saptasarasvatatirtha - It was situated on the banks of the river Sarasvati. The seven rivers namely Suvenu, Mahodara, Oghavati, Visala, Suprabha, Kancanaksi and Vimalodaka are united here; Hence the name Saptasarasvata.

(4) Brahmahrada - where king Kuru was staying when he performed penances as an ascetic.

(5) Cakratirtha - Here Lord Krsna was obliged by Bhisma to take the discus in his hand to attack him.

(6) Astipura where the bodies of the great warriors killed in the Mahabharata war were cremated.

(7) Prthudaka - It is situated in the midst of Kurujangala country on the banks of Sarasvati, at the place where Sarasvati takes an easterly course.If is modern Pehoa in Karnal distt..

(8) Taijasa - 4 shrine of Varuna. It was here that Skanda was coronated as the commander of the armed forces of the gods.

The ancient Kuru country may be said to have comprised the Kuruksetra or Thaneshvar. The region was situated between the Sarasvati on the north and Drsadvati on the south. The territory of Kurus appears to have been divided into three parts Kuruksetra, the Kuru- country proper and the Kurujangala. Kuruksetra, the cultivated land of the Kurus comprised the whole tract on the west of Yamuna and included the sacred region between the Sarasvati and Drsadvati. The field of Kuru or the region of Delhi was the scene of war between the Kurus and the Pandavas in which all the nations of India were ranged on one side or the other.

The region included Sonipat, Amin, Karnal and Panipat. The Kuruksetra proper is 25 miles east of Ambala on Delhi-Ambala line. The city of Thaneshvara, with which it has been identified is one mile from Kuruksetra and Panipat in Karnal Distt. and 55 miles from Delhi.

## Kausambi

The city of Kausambi was founded by Kusamba the son of Kusa and the grandson of Rama.According to the Puranic traditions the royal dynasty of the Vatsa country, to which the king Udayana belonged, traced its descent from Puru and it once ruled in the Kuru kingdom with Hastinapura as its capital. Nirvaktra, the son of Adhisamakrsna of Puru dynasty is said to have moved his capital from Hastinapura to Kausambi when the former was flooded. It was one of the important halting places on the great trade route connecting Saketa and SravastI on the north with Pratisthana on the banks of the Godavan in the south.

The Chinese knew it as Kianshang and Hiuen Tsang gave its extent as 6000 li with its capital of 30 li. It was a fertile country yielding much upland rice and sugarcane. It is famous as a Jain tirtha also as it was the birth place.of their sixth Tirthankara.

Cunningham has identified it with modern Kosam on Yamuna about 30 miles south-west from Allahabad. A stone Pillar Inscription was discovered near Kosam. Another Kosam Inscription of Bhadramagha's reign has been discovered in course of exploration of the ancient site of the Kausambi.An Inscription of the gateway of the fort of Kara (1036 A.D.), the Allahabad Posthumous stone Pillar Inscription of Samudragupta and the Kosam stone image Inscription of Samudragupta and the Kosam stone image Inscripiton of Maharaj Bhimavarman (yr. 139) refer to it.

## Kotitirtha

It appears that there are several tirthas named Kotitirtha in the Puranic tradition; for we see that the tirtha has been located at various places. It has been placed on the southern bank of the Ganges, on Kailasa, in Kasi, in Prayaga, in the courtyard of Mahakala at Ujjayini, on Narmada and yet another in Kuruksetra. It is regarded to be the same as DanuskotitTrtha. The Kalyana Tirthakalpataru gives a number of Kolitirthas:-

(1) on the Mandhata island in Narmada, (2) at the confluence of Narmada and KaverT, (3) at Kantoi on the northern bank of Narmada, two miles from Senore, (4) at Motikoral opposite Karsanpuri 18 miles from Senore on the northern bank of Narmada, (5) on Godavan on way to Nasik, (6) a tank near Mahakala temple at Ujjain, (7) on the seashore in Gokarna, (8) the Kotitirtha on the bank of Sarasvati which appears to be the same KofitTrtha mentioned in the Arbudakhanda of the Skanda Purana or the Kotesvara of Arasur on the source of the Sarasvati.

## Kalapagrama

It is situated on the eastern part of the Himalayas, near Badarikasrama where Manu performed yogic feats. The Brahmanda Purana places it in the Uttarakuru country on the base of the Gandhamadana mountain which is a peak of the Meru mountain. It is a village where Maru and Devapi, the last kings of the solar and lunar races respectively, performed penances to reappear again as the kings of Ayodhya and Hastinapura after the subversions of the Mieccha kingdom by Kalki. It is situated near the source of the Sarasvati, a tributary of the Alakananda in Badarinatha in Garhwal.

## Karavirapura

It has been located at the confluence of the Vipasa and the Devahrada in the Nilamata, and in Kubjamraka as its sub-tirtha in the Varaha Purana.Its mahatmya is mentioned in the Devibhagavata, Matsya, Padma and Brahmanda Puranas. It was the capital of the Brahmavarta, and was situated on Drsadvati. The Padma Purana locates it on the mountain Sahya and the Brhatdharma on the river Kaveri.

It is the modern Kolhapura, which was the capital of the former Kolhapura state and is situated near the southern bank of the river Pancaganga. It was one of the most ancient religious and trade centre of western India. It was a very important centre of Buddhism also. A number of Buddhistic coins and relics of first century B.C. are found here. It has about 250 temples of which that of Mahalaksmi is the most ancient and also the most important.

There has been a long standing belief that this place in older days was surrounded by a dreary jungle and was full of numerous holy temples and sacred pools. Major Graham observes that convulsion which altered the ancient aspect of this place was probably affected by the earth-quake and slight shocks had again occured during the year 1853. Many geological facts support this opinion. In support of this it has been noted that the bed of the river Pancaganga was found at an elevation of about 70 ft. above its present level.

## Kalinadi

The western Kali, a tributary of the Hindon flows through Saharanpur and Muzzaffarnagar districts of Uttar Pradesh. The eastern Kali rises in Kumaun and joins the Ganga. It is called Kalini and Kalinadi also. The town of Samkasya stood on its eastern bank, and Kanauj on the western bank, 3 or 4 miles from its junction with the Ganga. From its source to its junction with Dhavalaganga, Gauri and Candrabhaga it is called Kaliganga and after its junction it takes the name Kalinadi.

## Kalanjana

Perhaps it is the same as Kalanjara which is in Badansa sub division of the Banda Distt. of Bundelkhanda. It was the capital of Jajabhukti Bundelkhand) at the time of Chandelas after it was conquered by Yashovarman. It contains the temple of the Nilakantha Mahadeva. There is also a colossal figure of Kalabhairava, and a Sivalinga named Kalanjara. It is the name of a hill also, which is called Rabichitra as well. A city of this name was situated on the mountain Citrakufa.

## Kanchi

The Kanchi or Conjeevaram, one of the most ancient towns of the southern India is situated in the Chingleput district about 45 miles south-west of Madras. The city is sacred to both Siva and Visnu and is divided into two parts the western portion is called Siva Kanchi (big Kaftchi) and the eastern portion is called Visnu Kanchi (the little Kanchi).

Hiuen Tsang gives its extent as 6 miles in circuit. It was visited by Buddha and Ashoka built several stupas here. The Kailasanath temple, the Muktesvara temple, and Vekunthaperumatha temple were built by Narasimhavarman II (695-722 A.D.) and Nandivarman II (730-800 A.D.) respectively. Here is the famous Sivalingam Ekamranatha in the famous Tirukkachchi Ekannbann or the Ekamranath temple.

The Pallavas had their capital here from the earliest times of the 5th cent. A.D. upto about the middle of the 9th cent. A.D. (except for a part of the 6th century). Then it came under the Cholas of Tanjore and remained under them upto the 13th century except for the Rastrakuta occupation from 945-970 A.D..
