For sometime it remained in the hands of Kakatiyas of Warrangal and the Pandya kings of Madurai. Subsequently it passed into the hands of Mohammadans, Marathas and English people.

## Kanakhala

It appears that there were more than one Kanakhala tirtha for the Puranas locate it on Narmada, on mount Abu and near Gangadvara on a Himalayan peak. Abu road is a famous station on Ahmadabad Delhi line. Delwada is one mile from Abu civil station, and the Kanakhala tirtha is situated in Oriya village situated' at a distance of four miles from Delwada.

The one near Gangadvara is situated two miles to the east of Haridvara at the junction of the Ganga and the Niladhara and about a mile south of Mayapura. The name has been explained thus who (kam) is that villain (Khala) who does not obtain liberation at this spot? i.e. everyone is liberated here.

The legends say that Daksa Prajapati performed a sacrifice here. The place is still pointed out as the Daksasthana. There is also a famous temple of Daksa named Daksesvara here, around which a number of small deserted temples can be seen. The place where Safi was burnt is known as Safikunda.

It marks the bottom of the Himalayas. It is bigger than Haridvara and is more clean and picturesque in appearance.

## Kubjamra

Since the sage Raibhya saw Visnu as Kubja (a hunch backed) on the mango-tree here, the place is called Kubjamra. It has been identified with Haridvara. The Varaha identifies it with Hrsikesa at one place and Gangadvara at another. Here it is to be noted that the Mahatmyas of Kubjamraka and Hrsikesa have been treated separately in the Purana. The Kurma identifies it with Kanakhal.The Skanda places it in Kasi.

It is a celebrated place of pilgrimage at some distance to the north of Rishikesh.

## Kusasthali

It was founded on the Vindhya mountain by Kusa. It is perhaps the same as Kusavati of the Ramayana. The Skanda Purana places it in Avantivisaya and identifies it with Ujjayirii. It was the capital of the Anartas. The Visnu Purana records its destruction at the hands of the Punyajana Raksasas. It was later on called Dvaraka. The Vayu Purana corroborates the fact of its being related to Reva, the son of whom was Raivata. It was upto the time of Raivata that it was called KusasthaE and it was after him that it was destroyed by the Yaksas and Raksasas. It was during the time of the Yadavas that it came to be known as Dvaraka.

## Kusavarta

Since the sage Gautama obstructed the current of Godavan or Gautanu with a darbha blade here, it was named Kusavarta. It is a sacred tank near the source of Godavan at a short distance from Trayamvakesvara temple which is 21 miles from Nasik. The water of Godavan comes from under the earth into it. There is another Kusavarta tirtha which is often grouped with Gangadvara, Vilvaka and Kanakhala. It is situated below Gangadvara.

## Karapatha

It was also called Angadiya after Angada who is said to have founded it on the border of the Himalaya mountain. It is mentioned by Kalidasa and has been placed by some in the country of the Mallas, who had their two settlements at Pava and Kusinara. Kusinara has been identified with Kasia in the Gorakhpur district. Pava has been identified with Padraona, a Tahsil in the Deoria Distt. in Uttar Pradesh.

It is modern Karabagh or Kalabagh or Baghan as now called, on the rfght bank of the .Indus at the foot of the Salt range (Pakistan) locally called Nili hill in the Bannu district. It is Carabat of Travernier but the distance he gives from Kandahar does not agree with its actual distance from that place. It should be marked that there is a place called Karabagh on the route from Kandahar to Ghazni, 35 miles south-west from the latter which is called Karupatha in the Ramayana.

## Kedara

It is a sanctury of the Himavan. The Siva Purana gives a legend about Kedara which relates that the two sages Nara and Narayana, being the incarnations of Hari, were once practising penances at Badarikasrama. They used to prepare an earthenlingam of Siva and worship it. Siva was pleased with their devotion and granted them a boon. They prayed that Lord Sankara should stay at Kedara permanently in the form 'of a Jyotirlinga for the benefit of the humanity.

The temple of Kedarnatha is situated in the Himalayas at an elevation of 11735 ft. above sea level in Patti Mala Kaliphat in the Nagpur Pargana. The temple stands on the ridge that juts out at right angles from below the snowy peak called Maha-panth (22850 ft. high).

The Kedaranathadhama is situated on the southern side of the junction of the Mandakim and the Dudhganga. This Mandakim or Kaliganga has its journey from Kedaranatha, from a lake which is said to produce blue lotuses, and joins the Alakananda at Rudraprayaga. It requires eight days to go from Kedara to Badarinatha. The peak of Kedaranatha is said to be in Badarikasrama.

The worship of Kedaranatha is said to have been established by the Pandavas. There are the idols of Onkaresvara, Pancakedara, Usa, Aniruddha and Gangotri. The temple of Mandhata is also there. Close to the Kedarnath temple is a precipice called Bhairavajhanp where devotees ended their lives by flinging themselves from the summit.

## Karavana

It is situated 15 miles south of Baroda and eight miles north-east of Miyagam Railway station. The special holiness attached to the Narmada and its pebbles as lingas is probably due to the antiquity of this shrine of Kara van.

## Kancanadvara

A Kancanadvara temple is situated near Kancanatirtha in Trayamvakesvara 17 miles from Nasik.

## Kayavarohana

It is a sub-tirtha in Varanasi.

## Ksiranadi

It may be the same as Ksirasara, Ksiranadi or Ksirabhavani, which is situated12 miles from Shrinagar in Kashmir. The goddess is within a reservoir of water which assumes different colours in the different part of the day. The KsTraganga represents the upper course of the MandakinT.

**Krsnatirtha**

It is situated in the Agneyakona of Sivarajogopala temple in the Campakaranyaksetra of Daksinadvaraka i.e. Mannargudi. The Skanda Purana relates that it was founded by the sage Krsna on Venkatacala at the place where he performed penances.

## Kalika

It is a river which represents the confluence of KausikT and Aruna. It may be the Kali or Kailaganga.

## Kasi

Kasi, the abode of Visvesvara, is regarded as very sacred. It is located on the confluence of Ganga and Yamuna. Its extent is given as five Krosas.The most important holy spots in Kasi are the temples of Visvanatha, Manikarnika, Pancanada and Kapalamocana.

Besides, there are the Tirthas called Kamalalaya (Matsya P. 1332), Kamalaksa (Matsya P. 1334), Krtasauca (Matsya P. 1345, 2216,17987. V.D.P. Ill 12517. Garuda P. 8120. Vamana P. 905), Kampana (Nlla.v. 93), Kalamahl (Matsya P. 16360,89), Kalasarpi (Garuda P. 8110, Vayu Utt. 1587. Brahmanda P. Madhya. Upo. 1398), Kadambesa (Nila.v. 118, Linga P.I92161), Kirakatma (Vayu Utt: 1582), Krpucandra (Matsya P.2252), Kumarakosala (Vayu Utt. 1537) and Kalikasrama on the river Vipasa (Nila.v. 5,87,105). These tirthas are difficult to be identified since the Puranas give no other information except their names. The tirtha named Kartikeya is also called Karttika-pura. The Agni Purana mentions it under Gayamahatmya.

## Gaya

The city of Gaya was founded by Gaya, son of ?? after whom it was so named. He performed severe penances on the Kolahala mountain in Gaya.The gods, satisfied with him, granted him a'boon that the city would be known after him. It was situated in Kikatadesa near Ganga and extended for five Krosas. It was a holy place in the country of Kkata which in its western boundary included the river Punpun and the forests of Rajagrha. In the north it extended as far as the Pretasila and in the south as far as the sacred Bodhi tree at Bodhagaya. The greatness of Gaya is described in the Mahabharata, Narada, Padma, Kurma Varaha, Garuda and above all in the Vayu, which in its concluding chapters deals exhaustively with the subject. It contains the description of 45 sub-tirthas of Gaya, situated in about 35 square miles. Usually only seven or even three of them are visited.

There has been a great controversy about the antiquity of the Gayatirtha. A paper entitled 'Bihar in the Agni Purana' has been published in the Journal of the Bihar Research Society (Vol XL Part I 1954 p. 1-7) where the author on the authority of the well known stanza Ayodhya Mathura Maya Kasi Kanchi Avantika, Puri Dvaravati caiva Saptaita Moksadayikah goes to prove that from the absence of any mention of Gaya in this verse, it appears that the importance which Gaya enjoyed was only a local one and that it did not attain the nation-wide importance before this time (i.e. the time of the Agni Purana). Besides, we see that while in the Gupta period there is only one inscription found at Gaya, in the Pala period (750-1200 A.D.) no less than six inscriptions are available there., All this goes to prove that Gaya attained importance only in the period subsequent to 750 A.D..

But this view does not appear to be acceptable because firstly the text of the Agni Purana, as a whole, cannot be assigned to a definite period. The matter had been interpolated by different redactors at different times, and secondly the date of the composition of the above stanza is not certain. If we rely on the evidence of this stanza, than we would have to accept that even the tirthas like Prabhasa, Puskara and Prayaga attained all India importance only after 7th cent A.D. but such is not the case. A ruler of the northern Maratha country visited Prabhasa and Puskara and this fact is recorded in an Inscription of the 2nd cent A.D. and we see that Prayaga is mentioned even in a Khila verse of the Rgveda. It is an established fact that these three Tirthas, though not mentioned in the above verse, had attained imporance much prior to the 8th century A.D. Undoubtedly the traditional stanza regarding the sanctity of Gaya which is available in the epics, Puranas and Dharmasastras is older than the above stanza.

The Mahabharata is believed to have assumed its present shape between the 4th cent. B.C. to 5th Cent. A.D. while Ramayana is supposed to have its present form and contents as early as 2nd cent.

A.D.. We find two subsections of Gaya in the tirthayatra section of the Vanaparva of the Mahabharata. Moreover, the Mahabharatadescribes how at Brahmasara within Gaya, the royal sage Gaya performed many sacrifices. Consequently Brahmasara and the Aksayavafa in Gaya are stated to have become famous throughout the three worlds.

The same Gaya is known from the Ramayana, and the Puranas. Asvaghosa also refers to him.The Yajnavalkya Samhita which does not specially deal with the importance of the tirthas recognises Gaya's greatness and sanctity. Gaya as a personal name is well-known from the Vedas. The unanimous testimony of the Puranic writers and the medieval lexicographers suggest that the Gaya region was known to Rgvedic seers as Kikata.Yaska, whose date has been fixed between 700-500 B.C., certainly points to the antiquity of Gaya as a recognised holy place of antiquity. Commenting on a passage of Rgveda, he writes, According to one author Sakapuni the three steps of Visnu are the earth, firmament and heaven; while according to another author Aurnabhava the three steps of Visnu are the three places called Samarohana, Visnupada and Gaya Siras. It is evident that all these three places are situated in Gaya. Therefore it is maintained that long before the time of Yaska and his predecessor Aurnabhava, Gaya was the location of the three steps of Visnu and was famous as a VisnutTrtha. This also proves the pre-Buddhistic importance of the place.

The earliest literary works of the Buddhists very often mention Gaya. Evidence of the Buddhist works of the Gupta age also support its antiquity. Buddhaghosa (5th cent. A.D.) and Dharmapala (6th cent. A.D.) also refer to it. In Buddhist literature Gaya is sometimes called Brahmagaya to avoid its confusion with the Buddhagaya. The Buddhists regarded it very sacred. The Buddhagaya is seven miles from Gaya. Siddhartha practised penance at Uruvila. Having bathed in the Nairanjan (LTlajana) he set under a pipal tree at Bodhagaya and received full enlightenment. It was one of the first places which embraced Buddhism during the life-time of Buddha and became the headquarter of Buddhism. It appears that it embraced Hinduism between the second and the fourth centuries of the Christian era. The story of Gayasura of the Vayu Purana is an allegorical representation of the expulsion of the Buddhism from Gaya.

Fa-hein visited the city in the 5th cent. A.D. and found it empty and desolate and when Hiuen Tsang visited it in 637 A.D. he found it to be a thriving Hindu town.

It practically lies in a valley, for it is bounded on the north by Murti and Ramsila Hills, on the south by the Brahmayoni hill, on the east by the river Phalgu and on the west by the Katari hill. It is 192 miles from Calcutta and 127 miles from Benaras, and comprises the modern town of Sahebganj on the north side and the ancient town of Gaya on the southern side. In the southern portion of the town called Cakrabheda in the Caitanyabhagavata, is situated the celebrated temple of Visnupada, erected by Ahalyabai, on the site of a more ancient temple. The Visnupada had been set up prior to Fa-hien's visit. The temple of Mandagauri, one of the 52 Pithas is situated on a spur of the Brahmayoni range called the Bhasanatha.

## The sub-tirthas of Gaya.

## Gayasiras

Once Gaya performed penances here. Brahma put a stone slab on his head and performed his sacrifice. It is also called Phalgutirtha. The Garuda Purana gives its extent from Janardana mountain to the well called Uttaramanasa. Hiuen Tsang places it at 5 or 6 li to the south-west of the city and it is said to have dark gorges and inaccessible cliffs. Its extent has been given as one Krosa. It is also called Visnupada. It is the modern Brahmayoni hill or rather a low spur of the Brahmayoni hill, about a mile in area forming the site of the old town of Gaya.

## Mundaprstha or Krauncapada.

It is a hill in Gaya. Since that stone slab was placed by Siva on the head of that demon it was called Mundaprstha. As the sage performed penances here under the guise of Kraunca, the slab bearing his foot-prints was called Krauncapada. It stands on the exact spot where the head of the demon Gaya fell at the time of his death. It covers an area of a Krosa and a half measured from north to south and from east to west. A legend related that Dharmavrta, the wife of Marici, was cursed by him to be a stone. She asked the gods if she could be the most sacred stone in the universe. She was promised that this would happen when she would be placed on the head of Gaya and all gods would stand on that stone. The sila was accordingly placed on the back of Gaya's head and was called Mundaprstha. It is the Brahmayoni hill in Gaya especially that portion of it which contains the Visnupada temple. The Caranapranta of that sila is called Prabhasa as it shines with the rays of the sun.

## Pretasila

It has been placed at three places, at Gayasira, Prabhasa and Pretakunda. it is a 540 ft. high hill situated five miles north-west of Gaya, sacred to Yama. There are three tanks at its foot named Sati, Nigra, Sukha and the fourth one called Ramakunda.

Viraja and Mahendra are also hills in Gaya.The Vaitaram is a pond.

Ramatlrtha ... It is a sacred pool called Ramahrada and Ramasara also.

Bharatasrama ... It is situated in the Leiihana tirtha in the Visalaksetra.

Kundaparvata...!! is said to have been situated on the right hand of the Sila and is also called Bhasmakutagiri.

Udyantakagiri....!! is situated on the left hand and left foot of the Sila. The Vaditrakagiri and the Sitadri mountains are also situated in Gay a.

The Rukmanikunda is situated in the front of the Sitadri mountain. The river Kapila is to the west of the Sitadri mountain. The hollowed fountain of Agnidhara rises from the lake sacred to Brahma and is usually known as the Kapila. It is situated to the north of Kapalamocana. Another Kunda at Gaya is known as the Saras vatakunda.n

The Adipada mountain is situated on the belly of the Sila. The tirtha called Madhusrava is situated at the junction of the Ghrtakulya, Madhukulya, Devika, Mahanadi and the Sila. Ganga in Kasi is also known as Madhusrava. The Madhusrava is a small rivulet which flows down into the Phalgu river throughout the year, from the east side of the Gayasira hill (modern Brahmayoni). The Ghrtakulya, Madhukulya and Dadhikulya are three little parallel cataracts that flow down the southern slope of the Bhasmakufa hill during the rains and completely dry up during winter and summer. A little higher up towards the peak of the Brahmayoni hill there are three such other cataracts called Suprabha, Agnidhara and Kapila, the last one feeds the waters of the Rukmanikunda. Brahmatirtha is a well one mile south-west of Visnupada. The Kardamalatlrtha (a puskarim) is situated in the navel portion of the Gaya, near Mundaprstha.Grdhrakuta is situated on the left hand of the Sila. Since the sage here attained perfection in the form of vulture, it is called Grdhrakuta. The tirtha Sulaka-ksetra is situated on it. According to Cunningham it is a part of the Sailagiri, the Vulture peak of Fahien and Indra-Silaguha of Hiuen Tsang. It lies two miles and a half to the south-east of new Rajgir. Sailagiri is evidently a spur of the Ratnakuta or Ratnagiri but the name Sailagiri is not known to the inhabitants of this place. Buddha performed austerites here.

The Devadaruvana is situated on the hip (Nitamba) portion of the Mundaprstha. The Pretaparvata is on the left foot'of the sila on which is situated the Pretakunda. The Brahmayoni is a hill crevice, and Niksara is a Puskarira. From Nagakuta to Grdhrakuta, from Grdhrakiya to Yupa and from Yupa to Uttaramanasa is called the Sirobhaga of the demon Gaya and this part is called the Phalgutirtha. These are holy pools. The united stream of the Nilajana and the Mohana is called by the name of Phalgu. The Nilajana is united with the Mohana near the Nora hill, about a mile below Buddhagaya, and eventually flows into a branch of Punpun. The Phalgu flows through Gaya and the whole channel of the river from Brahmasardvara to Uttaramanasa is regarded as holy. The holy pond (Vapi) of Matanga is situated in Kosala in the holy forest of Dharmaranya, which is at a distance of 6 miles from the Visnupada. It is now called Maltangi and contains the remains of a large tank marked by an ancient embankment called Matanga-VapT and a modern temple with a lirigam of Matarigesvara.

The Visnupada is the chief place of worship. It derives its name from the god Visnu enshrined within it. The hill is supposed to be placed on the head of the demon Gaya. It is situated in old Gaya. The foot print of Visnu 16 X 6 is found in an octagonal basin 4 ft. in diameter and is plated with silver. The Mahanadi is situated to the east of the Gaya enterance. The Gayatri tirtha and Savitri tirthas are situated on it. The Mahanadi is the river Phalgu in the district of Gaya. The Uttaramanasa is a sacred pool at Gaya, one mile north of the Visnupada. Five tirthas are supposed to be situated in it. The Daksinamanasa, also called the Udici tirtha is situated near Devaghata. It is also a sacred pool in which three tirthas are supposed to have been situated. Kanakhala lies to the north of the shrine of Mundaprstha at Gaya. Pandusila is situated in the Champakavana lying to the south of Gayasira and to the west of the river MahanadI. The Krauncapada, having the impression of the Kraunca's feet lies close to the lake of Trfiya which is situated to the north of the river Vaitaram. The lake Niscira lies to the north of the Krauncapada hill. The Kausiki and the Kotitirtha are sacred pools at Gaya. Another tirtha here is named Gajakarna. The place where Visnu washed his face after killing the demon Heti is known as Gadalola.It is one mile south of the Visnupada near Maranpur. KanakanandT is situated to the north of the Mundaprstha, while Dharmaprstha is a sacred tank in Gaya. A little to the north of Visnupada is the sacred Surajkund. About half a mile is the famous Aksayavata and close to it is the temple of Prapitamahesvara, and to the westward is a large tank called the Rukmanikunda. Ramagaya is another hill which is a place of pilgrimage.

## Gangadvara

The ancient town of Haridvara, also called Gangadvara and Mayapuri, and regarded as one of the seven sacred cities, is situated on the right bank of the Ganga at the foot of the Sivalikas. Here it should be noted that the Kurma Purana, too, has located it on a Himalayan peak. At a short distance from the town, near the Hara-ki-Pairi is situated a great temple called Gangadvara.

The Mohammadan historians use the word Gangadvara. Hiuen Tsang describes it as Moyula or

Moyura, situated on the north-west frontier of Hadawar on the eastern bank of the Ganges.

The Ganga moves for about 200 miles in the mountains and gushes out at this place into the open plains. The Ganga here is divided into many shallow channels. The famous legend of the royal sage Bhagiratha associates him with the descent of the Ganga. Perhaps he laid down the way for the river with his engineering skill and brought her to the place where it flows now.

Haridvar, Kanakhal and Jvalapur, all these three towns are situated near each other. It was here that Daksa, father of Safi, performed sacrifice which ultimately ended in his subjugation by Siva.The sage Kapila lived here and his place is still known as Kapilasthana. The width of the Ganga is a mile here. A number of ghats and temples are situated on its banks. The water of the Ganga here is very clean and pure. From Haridvara starts the route for going upto the Himalayan region, particularly to Kedarnatha and Badarinatha.

The five sacred places, Gangadvara, Kusavarta, Vilvaka, Nilaparvata and Kanakhala are situated in the vicinity of each other. It is called Gangadvara, since the Ganga enters the plains here. Kanakhal is situated 2 miles away from this. Niladevi temple is situated on the Nilaparvata on the opposite bank of Ganga. At Kusavarta people perform sraddhas. In the Vilva-tirtha is the temple of Vilvesvara Mahadeva.

About 4 miles from Haridvar is Jvalapur and 24 miles from Haridvar is the famous pilgrimage spot of Rishikesh. Haridvar and Kanakhal mark the bottom of the Himalayas.

## Gokarna

The Gokarnatirtha is said to have been situated on the Sarasvatisamgama on the shore of the western sea. A linga was installed here by Ravana, which was called the Gokarna linga. It is 30 miles south of Sadasheogad which is 3 miles south of Goa. According to the Svayambhu Purana, Svayambhu produced holy men one of whom was Gokarnesvara in the Gokarna, which is identified with the one situated on the river Bagmati. Kalidasa mentions it as a place of pilgrimage in southern India. This tirtha of Gokarna is in the north Kanara district of Karnataka and is still a celebrated place of pilgrimage. Another holy place of the same name is in Nepal which is probably the one mentioned in the Monghyr copper-plate inscripiton of the king Devapala of the Pala dynasty of Bengal. The Vayu Puraria locates it in the southern region but the fact that the river Tamraparm issued from the mountaineous region near Gokarna proves that the Vayu's Gokarna was not the same as in the North-Kanara Distt.. King Bhagiratha of Ayodhya performed penances at Gokarna to bring down the Ganga. The Padma Purana locates it on the Himalaya, This is evidently the modern Gomukhi 2 miles beyond Gangotri. The Saura Purana mentions southern Gokarna situated on the river Sindhu (Indus). The Brahmanda Purana mentions it on the shore of the western sea and as extending for half a yojana.

It is situated at Gendia, a town in the province of the north Kanara, Karwar Distt. 30 miles from Goa between Karwar and Kumta, a celebrated place of pilgrimage. The Kap-Copperplate of Keladi Sadashivanayaka refers to Gokarna. The Gokarna copperplate of Kadamba Kamdeva (Saka. 1177) was discovered here.

## Gauritirtha

It is a holy place at a very short distance below Gangotri where Kedar Ganga debouches into the Bhagirathi. Below it is a small temple dedicated to the goddess Ganga. It is a lake also on the Kailasa mountain which is the source of the river Sindhu and Sarayu. There is another sacred pool of this name which is at a distance of 12 miles from Kedarnath and which contains a spring of hot water. It is situated eight miles south of Kedarnath temple onthe right bank of the MandakinI in Malla Kaliphat Pargana Nagpur. This is regarded as the birth-place of Karttikeya. There is another hot spring of this name on the bank of the KaIi-Gariga on the boundary of Nepal and the Almora District.

## Ganatirtha

It is perhaps the Ganesvara Siva temple situated near the mountain in the Gavadi village at a distance of-6 miles east to the village Nima Ka-Thana in Jaipur state. A Ganesvara tirtha is situated on the bank of Narmada.

## Gangasagara

It is the same as Sagarasamgama. It is the confluence of the Ganga and the sea and a tirtha called Samgamesa is said to have been situated here. There is another GangasagaratTrtha below Brahmagiri where Godavan first appears and then goes to Kusavarta. The sage Kapila had his hermitage here. The temple dedicated to the sage Kapila was erected in 430 A.D. but washed away by the sea in 1842. This ancient city of Gangasagara at the eastern extremity of the Cakravarfiksetra and of Bharatavarsa, Kumaridvipa and Aryavarta became one of the famous all India tTrthas at least before the rise of the Guptas in the 4th Cent. A.D. if not in earlier times. References to it are found in the records of Ashoka (269-232 B.C.), Rsabhadatta (119-23 A.D.) and the TTrthayatra section of the Mahabharata prove its antiquity. The fact that it was a well known tirtha even in north-western India is proved by its mention in Alberuni's work who mentions it as where the Ganga flows into the sea.

Due to several factors, the chief amongst them being the change of the main course of the Ganga through the Padma as well as the importance of the descendants of the valiant Vangas of old as guardians of the sacred places, the all-India importance of the GangasagaratTrtha gradually waned and its enviable position as the greatest tirtha in the eastern India was ultimately usurped by the medieval Puri tirtha in Orissa. As a local Tirtha, it preserved its importance in eastern India throughout the medieval period inspite of the depredations of Magh and Portugese pirates in lower Bengal.

The Chandidasa Shrikrsnakirtana (14th and 15th cent.) and Jayananda (a poet of the Burdwan region, 16th cent.), refer to it. A number of medieval Sanskrit works e.g. Gangavakyavali of Vidyapati (1375-1450 A.D.) also refer to it. As late as the first half of the 18th cent. considerable portions of the old city existed and people used to gather in the GangasagaratTrtha.

The Gangasagara city seems to have been often encroached upon by the sea and affected by the occasional changes in the surface of the mouths of the Ganges and by the creation of new mouths. But some of the old temples and images at Gangasagara survived as late as 1842 A.D. when the remaining part of the ancient city was finally and completely washed away by the waves of the sea.

The tirtha Gangavindhya and Godasramacannot be identified in the absence of any detailed information. The Gangodbheda tirtha is said to have been situated on the bank of the Gomati. The Guhyesvara and Ghantesvara can also not be identified.

The Candrika tirtha has been identified with the river Candrabhaga i.e. Chenab. The Chagalandaalso called Chagalaka and Chagalesvara is placed in Prayaga or KasL The Chakravaka, Carmakotaare mentioned in name only. The JayantatTrtha and Jaya was the name of the'city founded by the king Nimi, on all sides of the hermitage of the sage Gautama. Jambumarga was a great forest on the Kalanjara mountain The Agni Purana places Jambumarga between Puskara and mt. Abu and mentions Kalinjara separately as a place of pilgrimage. Jambu is placed on the mt, Abu and Wilson has identified it with the mt. Kalanjara in Bundelkhand.

**Dvaraka**

The city of Dvaraka, also known as Dvaravati was founded by Krsna. He killed Karhsa at Mathura. Thereafter Jarasandha of Magadha and Kalayavana simultaneously attacked Mathura. Finding that Yadavas were practically surrounded on all sides, Krsna decided to come down and construct a strong fort near the sea. He asked the ocean for a piece of land measuring twelve yojanas and founded the city of Dvaraka there. Krsna brought the citizens of Mathura to Dvaraka. This place was not far from the Raivataka mountain. The description in the Mahabharata and Puranas suggests tha-t Dvaraka in Krsna's time was near the sea and the Raivataka mountain where flows Gomati. The Matsya Purana attributes the foundation of the city to Balarama. The extent of the city at one place is said to be 5 yojanas and width 10 yojanas, and at another place it is given as 100 yojanas. The city was under the protection of Andhakas, Bhojas and Vrsnis.

The etymological derivation of the name Dvaravati has been given as :

-?\|?\|???? cfiifun -q3f ??1 trchr: i wft a.KicircMr..;'

The evidence of the Mahabharata suggests that Kusasthali and Dvaraka are identical. The Skanda Purana, placing it on the bank of the southern ocean says that formerly it was called ,Kusasthah since Kusa had founded it in the territory of the Anartas.The Matsya clearly says that Dvaraka at present is called Kusasthali. The Mahabharata, which refers to it frequently contains a legend that after the death of Krsna the city was washed off by the encroachment of the ocean upon the mainland where the city was situated. Harivamsa, too, describes the city in details.

The Pali literature connects Dvaraka with the Kambojas but does not clearly says that Dvaraka was their capital. The Jatakas place Dvaravati with the sea on one side and the mountains on the other.In the Jain literature Baravai or Dvaravati is said to be the home of Krsna and Andhaka-Vrsnis and as built by Revata.

Its location and the identity are still a point of dispute. The evidence of Vayu does not throw much light on the problem. But the fact that Prasenajit, when he went for hunting, lost his life in the region where the Rksaraja, probably a chief of the people living in the Rksa mountain, ruled suggests that the Dvaravati of Vayu is not the same as the present one.

Dr. Takakusu opines that Dvaravati represented Ayuthya or Ayudhya, the ancient capital of Syam. Another place suggested for its ancient site is said to be the Dvarasamudra or the modern Halabid in Hassan District of Mysore (Karnataka).

The place which is at present a port in the western part of Saurashtra in the Okhamandal region and on the bank of the river Gomati is popularly identified with the original Dvaraka and is considered as one of the seven holy cities. Dey, Altekar, Pargiter and Pusalker consider this modern town of Dvaraka as the original site of the Dvaraka. Some identify it with an island near Somnath. Durgashankara Shastri and Bhallasali identify it with the modern Junagadh which was called Girinagara. According to Stein, Dvaravati corresponds to the present Dvarbidi, the designation of that position of the Vitasta valley which extends from above Muzzaffarabad to a short distance beyond the village of Buliasa. It seems that originally it was situated near mount Girnar but in later times it has been recognised as Dvaraka on the sea-shore on the extreme western coast of the Kathiawad. It is the Baraka of Ptolemy.

Mula Dvaraka is 3 miles south of Kadinar. The legend says that it was between Porbunder and Miyam near Madhavapur or Shrinagar. It is also suggested that it may be in Sindh (Pakistan). In the south, Mannargudi is also known as Dvar'aka. In Marwad there is a place called Kheda Dvaraka. The Beta Dvaraka is an island 20 miles from Dvaraka in the Bay of Cutch. It contains two main temples, one that of Ranachoda ji and the other Sankhoddhara. Sites and Buildings are named and identified here as Krsna's palaces and places connected with Rukmam and other wives of Krsna. On the way to Bet Dvaraka is the Gopitalao, the white earth of which is called Gopicandana. Near Dvaraka is another ancient site known as Pindaraka.

There are five 'Gokaras' at Dvaraka i.e. Gomati, Gomaya, Godana, Gopicandana and the sight of Gopinath. The famous tirtha named Cakratirtha is situated in Dvaraka at the confluence of Gomati and the ocean.

**Dasapura**

The famous city of Dasapura in Malava is the present Mandsor in Malwa. It is called Dasor by the people of the neighbouring village.

The tirthas namely Dindi-punyakara and the Durgadvara cannot be identified.

The hermitage of Arjuna was situated at Devasunda. The Devikatirtha extended for four Krosas. There is a well named Vrsa here. Pt. Rahul

Samkrityayana has identified it with the river Dabaka. Matsya mentions a Dharani tirtha. The Narasimha tirtha has been variously placed (1) on the bank of the Indradyumna sarovara, (2) on the river Devika in the Madra country, (3) in Kashmir, situated 3 or 4 miles from Mahadevasrama and (4) near the Trayamvakesvara which is 17 miles from Nasik. The Agni Purana mentions it under Gayamahatmya and the Brahma Purana places it on the Ganges. The Narakatirtha is said to have been situated to the 'west of Prayaga on the southern bank of Yamuna and the tirtha of Dharmaraja and the Niranjanatirtha of .Aditya is placed on the northern bank of Yamuna. The tirthas named Lalita, Nanda and Nilakunda are mentioned in name only. The Nakulesatirtha may be the same as NakuKsvara. The chief shrine of Siva called NakulTsa or Nakulisvara was at Karvan 15 miles south of Baroda and 8 miles north east of Miyagam Riy. Station. The temple belongs to the Pasupata sect of Saivism. The'Skanda places it in Kasi, and there is one Nakulesvara in Nepal also.

Trayamvaka is situated on the banks of GautamI i.e. Godavan. The Nasik Trayamva-kaksetra is one of the famous tirtha of India. The source of the Godavan lies near by. The town Trayamvaka is 17 miles from Nasik and is situated at the base of the mountain. The Trisandhya is the temple near Trayamvakesvara on the Godavan. It has been placed in KasI and Puskara also. The Matsya mentions a TapatI tirtha also.

**Naimisa**

It was the famous resort of the sages on the bank of the Gomati. It was situated in the country of Kurujangala and the rivers Drsadvati and Sarasvati flowed through it. It was called-Naimisa since the Nemi of the disc of the god Visnu was shattered here. The Varaha Purana derives its name thus Since the entire army of demons was destroyed at this place in one Nimisa, it was called Naimisa. It was the abode of Aryan sages who wrote the Puranas here. The Mahabharata, too, was recited by Sauti at this very place. It is a place of great antiquity and has numerous sacred tanks and temples.

It is known as Misrika also since Vyasa mixed all the tirthas here. The Hatyaharana tirtha is very close to Misrika. It is stated that after killing Ravana, a Brahmin as he was, Rama bathed at this place and was purified of the sin of Brahmahatya. The famous Cakravapl is also situated here.

It is the Namakhai of Ptolemy and is identified with the modern Nimsar on the left bank of Gomati in Sitapura district at the junction of the roads from Sitapur and Khairabad. It is 20 miles from Sitapur, 24 miles from Sandila station and 45 miles north west of Lucknow.

Ain-i-Akbari describes Nimkhar as Nimkhar is a famous large fort and a place of great religious resort. The river Gowdy runs near it. In the neighbourhood is a reservoir of water called Brahma varta-kunda. This reservoir is the CakratTrtha where the wheel of Brahma fell and where Lord Visnu flung his discus at the demons. This tirtha is hexagonal in shape. Other sacred places here are Pancaprayaga, GodavarT, Kasi, Garigottari and Gomati.

**Pancanada**

The sacred tTrtha of Pancanada has been placed in Kasi. Since there are the five rivers namely Bhagiratip, Yamuna, Sarasvati, Kirana and Dhutapapa, the place is called Pancanada. At another place the names of these five rivers appear as Gomati, Laksmana, Candrabhaga, Kusavati and Jambavati. The Brahmaputras (sons of Brahma) bathed in them and named them as Pancanadyah. It is a tank in Kasi according to the Skanda Purana.The country of Pancanada' is Punjab, (undivided Punjab), the country of five rivers i.e. Satadru, Vipasa, Iravati, Candrabhaga and Vitasta. The name is especially applied to the region watered by the collected stream of the'Ghara (the joint system of Sutlej and Bias) and the Trinab (united stream of the Ravi, Chinab and Jhelum), from their confluence to Methunkote near which the united water joins the Indus.

It is a place of pilgrimage in Kuruksetra also.The five rivers of Japyesvara called Jatodaka, Trisrota, Vrsadhvani, Svarnodaka, and JambunadI are also called Pancanada. The confluence of the five southern rivers namely Krsna, Vena, Turiga, Bhadra and Kona is also known as Pancanada.

The Parvatika may be the same as Parvati, the river Parab in the Kohistan of 'the Jalandhar Doab which falls into the river Bias a couple of miles above Bajoura. The Pasupata tirtha was situated in Karvan. The Skanda locates it in Kasi. The Patalatirtha is the same as PatalavatI, a branch of the Chambal mentioned by Bhavabhuti in his Malatimadhava. It is the Polaitah of Tod. The Pindarakatirtha has been placed near Golagar in Gujarat 16 miles to the east of Dvaraka. It was here that the sages cursed Samba, Krsna's son that he would give birth to a Musala which would destroy the Yadu race. Pt. Rahul Samkrityayana speaks of a PindaranadT in Himalayas. The Puspa is the same as Puspavati, the river Pambai in Travancore. The Prthudaka tirtha is situated on the bank of Sarasvati. It is the modern Pehoa in the Karnal Distt. (Panjab), on the river Sarasvati where the celebrated Brahmayonitirtha is situated. It is 14 miles to the west of Thaneshvara.

**Pratisthanapura**

Pururava, son of Ila was coronated in the city of. Pratisthana. It seems that there were two Pratisthanapura, one in the vicinity of Prayaga on the northern bank of the Yamuna and the other on the banks of the Godavari. The first may be identified with Jhusi, opposite Allahabad across the Ganga which is still called Pratisthanapura. The second Pratisthanapura was the capital of Asvaka or Mahara'stra in the Aurangabad District. It is the Paithan of the Periplus, Baithana of Ptolemy and the Potali of the Buddhists. It was a great emporium of commerce in the Andhra country of which it was the capital.

The Pancavana seems to be the same as Pancavati. The tirtha called Pandukula was situated near the ocean.

The tirthas Patapanjara, Purvamitrapada,Puspanyasa, Prangukha, Pancasvamedha- tirtha,Priyamelaka, Panduvisala are mentioned in name only.

**Pampatirtha**

The Pampatirtha, the modern Hampi is situated on the southern bank of the Tungabhadra and at the north-western extremity of the ruins of Vijayanagar. The river Pampa, a tributary of Tungabhadra rises in the Rsyamuka mountain, 8 miles from the Anangandi hills. On the banks of this river Rama met Hanumana. Ramayana describes it beautifully. A lake named Pampa is also mentioned in the Ramayana.

**Padmavati**

Padmavati, also known as Padmapura is another name of UjjayinT. It was the capital of the Ganapatinaga. It is said to have been situated at the confluence of the two rivers Sindhu and Para (Parvati) in the Vidarbha. It was the birth place of Bhavabhuti. It is the modern Padam Pavaya, 25 miles north-east of the city of Narwar in the Gwalior district of the Madhya-Pradesh.

**Purusottamaksetra**

It is said to have been situated in the country of Odra or Utkala on the southern'ocean or Lavana Sagara upto Viraja mandala and the river Chitrotpala'. The Purusottamaksetra, also known as SrTksetra, is a sandy place, 10 yojanas in extent and including two distinct portions, one of them being Balukakhanda which lies exactly on the shore of the Bay of Bengal between the sacred places called Svargadvara and Cakratirtha and is famous for )agannatha temple having the idol of Balarama, Subhadra and Krsna.

It extends from the Lokanath temple on the west to the Balesvara temple on the east and from Svargadvara on the south to the Matia stream on the north-east. The Purusottamapuri plate of Ramachandra mentions it as lying on the southern bank of the Godavari in Bihar Besides the main temple of Jagannath, there are many other minor temples such as Markandesvara, Lokanath, NTlakanthesvara and some tanks.

**Pundankapura**

It is situated in Kubjamraka. It may be identified with the modern Cidambaram which is also called as Tillai, Porumpattapuliyar, Pundarikapuliyar, Pundarikapuram, Vyaghra-puram, Srirambalam and Citrakuta. Cidambaram is the headquarter of the Taluka of the same name in the south Arcot distt. (Tamilnadu) and is famous for its great Siva temple. According to the tradition there are five Sivalingas, corresponding to the five elements of earth, water, fire, wind and ether in the south. The ether linga is situated at Cidambaram.

For some time it was the capital of the Colas. It is situated on the Colaroon river, 21 miles from Cuddalore and 151 miles from Madras.

**Plaksaprasravana**

It is said to have been situated on the Sarasvatiin the Himalayas. It is mentioned with r „ference to Sraddha, while describing the glory of Kuruksetra for the performance of Sraddha. Pururava is said to have roamed all over the earth in search of UrvasT. *He* came to Kuruksetra and saw her bathing in the pond at Plaksatirtha. The river Sarasvati rises in the hills of Sirmur in the Himalayan range called the Siwalik and emerges into the plains at Adi-Badari in Ambala and is regarded as one of the most sacred rivers by the Hindus. The fountain from which the river originated is said to have situated at the foot of a Plaksa tree and hence it was called Plaksavatarana or Plaksaprasravana and is frequented as the place of pilgrimage.

**Puskara.**

It is said to have been situated in the Visalapun in the western direction and in the vicinity of the Trisarhdhya tfrtha. It is modern Pokher, 7 miles north of Ajmer situated in the narrow valley surrounded by hills. It contains a sacred lake named Puskara. There are five principal temples here i.e. those dedicated to Brahma, Savitri, Badarinarayana, and Siva. There are several ghats e.g. Brahmaghat, Gangaghat, Kapalamocanaghat, Svarupaghat and Badarighat. The town is picturesquely situated on the lake with hills on three sides.

The Padma Purana locates it on the river Sarasvati. It is said that Sarasvati starts from this Puskara lake after it disappears at Vinasana in Kuruksetra and takes a westerly course from here.There are five currents including Praci Sarasvati which are collectively called Panca-Srota Sarasvati. The lake Puskara is said to have been surrounded by a beautiful forest. The Skanda Purana gives its extent as five yojanas and places it to the north of Candrabhaga and to the south of Karatoya. According to the Puranas there were more than one lake which were known by the name Puskara, because the Puranas speak of Puskara in plural (cf. Puskarani, Tripuskara). These ponds were and are still known as Jyestha Puskara, Madhyama Puskara and Kanistha Puskara. The place is sacred to god Brahma who performed a sacrifice here. Near the Yajnakunda at Puskara is the hermitage of Agastya. Rama, Laksmana and STta are said to have their ceremonial bath at Puskara. The sage Visvamitra settled down here to perform penances. It was here that Sunahsepa came to Visvamitra, asking for his refuge. Besides, here were the hermitages of Vasistha, Cyavana, Bharadvaja, the seven sages (saptarsi), Markandeya and Bhargava. The Mahabharata groups it together with Prabhasa and Naimisa. The legend says that it was here where Janamejaya, the son of Panksita, performed his serpent sacrifice.

**Prabhasa**

It is also known by the name of Soma-tirtha as Soma or moon was cured of the consumption brought upon him by the inprecation of Daksa, his father-in-law. It has been located on the sea-shore.The well-known Prabhasapatan or Somanathpatan on the southern coast of Kathiawad is regarded as the essence of the Anarta country, a name used for Gujarat as a whole. It is called Somanath because a divine image of god Somanath has been installed here. It is called Prabhasa since moon regained, his lost brilliance here. The seventh Khanda of the Skanda Purana known as Prabhasakhanda gives a detailed description of Prabhasa. The first 365 chapters describe Prabhasatirtha, next 19 chapters describe Vastrapatha or mount Girnar and then 63 chapters mention mount Arbuda and the last 44 chapters describe Dvaraka. The seventh chapter of the Prabhasakhanda gives the legendary origin of this tirtha. The 187th chapter gives the importance of Prabhasa-pancaka. The western portion of Prabhasa is called Adiprabhasa. Not very far from it in the southern direction is the Vrddhaprabhasa. The Jamadagnlsvara is situated near the Vrddhaprabhasa, to the south of which is Jalaprabhasa. Krtasmaraprabhasa is situated on the bank of Sarasvati. The confluence of the Sarasvatiand the ocean is regarded very holy.

The Purana then relates the story how Adiprabhasa was named Vrddhaprabhasa. The sages came here with a resolve that they would not leave the place unless they have the sight of the liriga of Sankara. Since they became old in this persuit, the place was named Vrddhaprabhasa. Pracisarasvati at Prabhasa is said to yield more merit. The area of this tirtha is said to be 12 yojanas, the ksetrapTtha to be of 5 yojanas and the C.arbhagrha of a Gavyuti or a Krosa. To the east is the' temple of Tapodakasvami in the west of Madhava. To the south is the ocean and to the north is the river Bhadra. Here are the 5 Sakaras i.e. Sarasvati, Samudra, Soma, Somagrha and the sight of the Lord Somanatha.

The Mahabharata described it as a southern tirtha to which a visit was paid by Arjuna. Sage Dhaumya mentions Prabhasa with other sacred places such as Camsodbheda and Pindaraka.

The river Sarasvati is said to have disappeared near Vinasana and reappeared near Camsodbheda in Prabhasa. The Skanda also describes Sarasvati in Prabhasa as Pancasrota. It was near Prabhasa on the sea-shore that Lopamudra met and married Agastya. It was here that the Yadavas got drunk fought amongst themselves and were annihilated. The place where Krsna gave up his body is known as Dehotsarga and is situated near Prabhasa.

## Prayaga

The etymological derivation of the name Prayaga has been given as such. Prayaga, also known as Madhyavedi of Brahma and the Prajapatiksetra, extended for five yojanas in the vicinity of the river Gariga, on the northern bank of Yamuna at its confluence with the Gariga. It was named Prayaga (from the root Yaj) since the god Brahma performed a great sacrifice here.

Rama and Laksmana, coining to the confluence of the Gariga and the Yamuna, saw smoke coming out of the city. They distinctly saw two different colours of the water. This epic refers to Prayaga as a forest area cut off from the resources of civilization and having within it a small clearing about a Krosa or two miles in extent. The Pauranika story of lla also describes the prohibited area of Kumaravana in Prayaga, but the Mahabharata refers to it as a fertile place.

The place from the city of Pratisthanapura (Jhusi) in Prayaga upto the front part of the pool of Vasuki is said to be the Ksetra of Brahma where live the Kambala, Asvatara, and Bahumulaka nagas. Here are the five kundas in the midst of which flows the Ganga. The region between Gariga and Yamuna and their confluence is very important and surpasses even the greatest sacred places such as Naimfca and Puskara etc..

The Buddhists called it Payaga or Prayaga, a tirtha or Ghat on the Ganges. The Chinese knew it as Po-LoAe-Kia, the circuit of which was 5000 li and the capital of which extended for 20 li. It is praised by Hiuen Tsang for its climate, crops and the people.

The area from the east of the Ganga from Pratisthana (also known as Samudrakupa) to the pool of Vasuki in the north, from Kambala and Asvataranagas in the west to Bakhumulaka in the 'south, in the form of a square is the sacrificial alter of Prajapati. The Prayagamandala is a larger area then Prayaga and is described as a circle of one and a half yojanas round the Brahmayupa. The Inscription discovered at Bhita (of the Maharaja Gautamiputra, Sri Sivamegha, Rajan Vasisthiputra Vrsdhvaja of the 3rd or 4th cent A.D.) and Apshad stone inscription of Adityasena and Kamauli grantrefer to Prayaga. The importance of Prayaga has been described at length in the Matsya and the Kurma Puranas.

It is the modern Allahabad, which is regarded very holy. Its reference in Rgveda (Khila portion) shows the sanctity and traditional importance of this place from the time immemorial.

The confluence of Ganga and Yamuna is regarded very holy. Pratisthanapura and Samudrakupa are said to be situated on the eastern bank of the Jahnavi (Ganga).

The Hamsaprapatana is situated to the north of Pratisthanapura on the eastern bank of Ganga. The Hamsapandura is famous as Urvasiramana. The Aksayavata is situated in a dark chamber in the fort of Allahabad. The Kotitirtha, too, is very holy. At BhogavatI there. is another tirtha named Dasasvamedha which is situated to the north of the residence of the serpent Vasuki and to the west of the confluence. The Manasa tirtha is situated on the northern bank of Ganga. The Rnamocana tirtha is situated on the northern bank of Yamuna and to the south of Prayaga. The Rnamocanatirtha is so named because the sage Lomasa became free of his debt here. It is placed on the river Sarayu also.The Agnitirtha is situated on the southern bank of the Yamuna. The Kambala and Asvataranagas are situated on the southern bank of Yamuna. The Manikarnika is a celebrated ghat in Benaras. The Trivem denotes the confluence of three rivers, Sarasvati being in concealed form. It is also called Veni because Ganga takes a turn here and bends like the Vem of a woman. It is about a mile and a half from the Allahabad city.

The Bhadrasarovara may be the same as Bhadrakarna, a sacred reservoir in Trinetresvara or modern Than in Kathiawad. The tirthas named Bhairava, Bhadrakalesvara and Bhadratirtha can not be identified.

## Brahmasara

The Brahmasara which is the same as Ramahrada has been placed both in the Gnvii region, and in the Kuruksetra. It is called Brahmasara because Brahma performed penances on the bank of this tank.

The Brahmatirtha is a well (Brahmatirtha' thakupake) which is mentioned under Gayamaha-tmya. It has been placed in the Varanasi as well as on the bank of the Narmada and on the bank of Satadru. A Brahma tirtha is also mentioned as situated in Prabhasa, on the bank of Gautami and to the north of the Bindusara.

## Brahmavarta

The region lying between SarasvatI and Drsadvati where Aryas first settled themselves was given the name of Brahmavarta. It was afterwards called Kuruksetra, identified generally with Sirhind. From this place they occupied the region known as Brahmarsidesa. Its capital was Karavirapura on Drsadvati and Barhisamati.

It is also a landing ghat on Ganga at Bithur in Kanpur district. Another Tirtha of this name is situated on the bank of Narmada near the confluence of Burarha and Narmada. It is said that Brahma performed penances here and installed the Brahmesvara Siva.

## Mithila

Mithila, the capital of Videha, was famous after the name of the king Mithi, its founder. It is called Miyulu in the Buddhist annals. It was also called Icinakapura which was the capital of the kingdom of Videha. It was situated about 35 miles north-west from Vaisah. It was 7 leagues in area with the kingdom of Videha being 300 leagues in extent. Its distance from Campa was 60 yojanas. It has been identified with the Tirhut division, excluding the district of Saran (Bihar).

## Mahismati

It was named after Mahisman, son of Satrajita.It was the country of the famous king Sahasrarjuna,on the banks of Narmada. It is the Mahissati of the Buddhists which is said to be the capital of Avanti.It was also the capital of the Cedis at the time of the Kalachuris. Mahesvara or Mahesh, situated on the'right bank of the Narmada at a distance of 40 miles to the south of Indore was the capital of the Haihayas of Anupadesa. Pargiter is right in identifying it with the Mandhata on the river Narmada.

## Manasa Tirtha

It is the famous lake on the Kailasa mountain.There is another Manasatirtha to the west of Mathura and yet another in the Kubjamrakaksetra.

## Mahavodhi

It is a pipal tree in Buddhagaya, 6 miles to the south of Gaya where Buddha attained enlightenment in 522 B.C..

## Mahalaya

The Mahalaya, same as the Rudramahalaya extended for four yojanas. It has been placed on the Himalayas as well as in Varanasi. It is also known as Onkaranatha or Amaresvara.

## Mrttikavarapura.

It was situated near the city of Dvaraka on the bank of the ocean.

The Mrttikavati has been identified with the country of the Bhoias by the side of the river Parnasa (Banas) in Malwa. Its capital was Saubhanagara or Salvapura, identified by Cunningham with Alwar.It was situated near Kuruksetra and comprised the portions of the territories of Jodhpur, Jaipur and Alwar.

## Manipura

It was the capital of the Kalinga country.Lassen takes it to be the Manphur Burder and places it to the south of Chikakole, but Oppert disagrees and identifies it with Manaluru near Madura. The situation of the capital of the Kalinga as described in the Mahabharata and the Raghuvamsa and also the name itself support its identification with the Manikapattana a seaport at the mouth of the Ckilka Lake. Mr. Rice has identified it with Ratanpur in Madhya Pradesh. It is situated at the base of Girnar and a fortress on it were regarded to be important from the military point of view. The area around it is very fertile and it appears that since the,time of Nandas and the Mauryas at least, Junagarh or Girnar has been a flourishing town. This ancient Manipur was thereafter called Candraketupura, afterwards Raivatanagara and then Puratanapura.

## Mahakala

The Mahakala, one of the 12 Jyotirlingas of the god Sankara, is the presiding deity of the city of Ujjain. The Skanda Purana places it on the southern ocean and on Narmada as well. Its extent is given as one yojana. The temple stands near a lake at Ujjain and has 5 storeys, one of which is underground.

## Mayapuri

The limits of Mayaksetra are given as from Gangadvara to Ratnastambha and from Nandagiri to Kastithogiri. It included Haridvara, Mayapuri and Kanakhala. Present Mayapura is situated between Haridvara and Kanakhal,' about a mile from Haridvara. There are three old temples, one of Mayadevi, other of Bhairava and third of Narayana. It is one of the seven sacred cities. Cunningham says that it is a very ancient site identical with that of the populous city, 3-12 miles in circuit, described by Hiuen Tsang. Beyond the old Narayana Ball temple is a large mound 750 ft. square which is the relic of the fort of the mythical king Vena.

## Mathura

Mathura, also called Madhupuri was founded by Satrughna after killing the demon Lavana, the son of Madhu. He ruled over here with his two sons Sub, hu and Surasena. The country thence forward came to be known as Surasena. It extended for 20 yojanas in the midst of Govardhana and Yamuna, having a shape of the half moon.

Though Mathura is unknown to the Vedic literature but it is mentioned in the works of the grammarians. It was a centre of Buddhism for several centuries. The famous disciples of Buddha such as Mahakaccyana, Upagupta and others lived here. The Jains knew it as Sauripura or Suryapura. They appear to have been firmly established from the middle of the second century onward in Mathura. Fhe evidence of the numerousmscrsptions from Mathura, which date mostly from he time of the later Kushanas (after 78 A.D.), proves that the Jains were not only firmly established by that time but had become subdivided into smaller groups at an earlier date.

The Greeks knew it as Methora and Madoura, situated on the right bank of Yamuna higher up than Agra from which it was 35 miles distant and to the south of Indraprastha, midway between Indraprastha and Kausambi. Fa-hien called it Mat'-Aov-Lo and Hiuen Tsang gave the extent of Mo(Mei)Tu-Lo as 5000 li, with its capital being 20 li in circuit. The soil is said to be fertile, climate hot and the chief industry being agriculture. Arrain notes this city, on the authority of Megasthenes, as the capital of Surasena. Pliny called river Yamuna as Jomanas which flowed into the Ganges through the Palibothra between the towns of Methora and Chrysobara. Ptolemy, too, mentions it. Lassen transcribes Chrysobara as Krsnapura and locates it at Agra. Cunningham identifies it with Kesuvapura mohalla of Mathura. S.N. Majumdar suggests that the Gokul on the left bank of the Jui-nna (Yamuna) and five miles south-east of Malhura may be identified with it.

Ugrasena and Kamsa were the kings of Mathura, -the home of Vrsni and Andhakas. When it was attacked by the demon Kalayavana and others, the Vrsni-Andhakas left Mathura and established their capital at Dvaravati. It was beseized by Jarasamdha, the king of Magadha also. When going to Mahaprayana, Yudhisthira installed Vajranabha on the throne of Mathura. BhTma Satvata expelled him from Mathura and he and his descendants ruled over here. When Guptas rose to power, Mathura was under the Nagas. These Nagas and Yaudheyas- wer& overpowered by Samudragupta. Later on Menander, the king of Kabul and Punjab occupied it. The Hindu king of Mathura were finally displaced by Saka Ksatrapas (1st cent. A.D.). In 2nd century A.D. it came under Huviska, the Kushana king.

It was a rich, flourishing and a populous city, and was also a great centre of Vaisnava cult. The Bhagavata religion also arose here. The Varaha Purana mentions various places in Mathura which are connected with the incidents in Krsna's life. Kamsa-Ka-Tila, near the southern gate of Mathura has been identified with the place where Karhsa was killed. The Visranti ghat on the bank of Yamuna is the place where Krsna rested after killing Karhsa.It was at Yogaghat that Kamsa dashed Nanda's daughter Yogamaya to the ground.

The present temple of Dvarakadhisa is the biggest temple of Mathura. In the north is the temple of Mahavidya. There are said to be 12 forests in the vicinity of Mathura namely the Madhuvana, Talavana, Kumudavana, Bakulavana, Kamavana, Khadiravana, Vrndavana, Bhadravana, BhandTravana, Belavana, Lohavana and Mahavana. Another famous ghat on Yamuna is the Dhruvaghat where Dhruva performed penances.

The city is on the right bank of Yamuna, in the Agra division of Uttar Pradesh. The city known as Madhupuri (present Moholi) was situated 5 miles to the south-west of the modern Mathura. Strictly speaking it is the Uttara-Mathura which is identified with Maholi. Modern Mathura is not on. the ancient site but has moved to the south due to the encroachment of Yamuna.

The Mahananda or Mahanada may be the same as Mahanandi, a place of pilgrimage in the Karnal district. The Mulatapi is the river Tapti so called from its source at Multai. The Mitrapada is the same as Sarvamitrapada. The Matagrhatirtha is also called Matatirtha. The tirthas named Mandodan, Makarandaka, Mahalinga, Maharudra and Mahajanmesvara are mentioned in name only.

## Ramatirtha

It has been mentioned under Gaya .Mahatmya in the Agni Purana and Setu Mahatmya in the Skanda Purana. The Narada Purana locates it in Kuruksetra. It appears that there were several Ramatirthas. It is a village in the Vizagapatam Distt. where an Inscription of Visnuvardhana has been found on the wall of a cave in a hill.

Another Ramatirtha is a holy reservoir in Sopara near Bassein, about 40 miles north of Bombay. Yet another Ramatirtha is situated near Trayamvaka 3 miles north of Hangal in Dharwar Distt. Bombay.Another Ramatirtha was situated to the south of the Visrantitirtha in Mathura.

## Ramesvaram

It is an island in the gulf of Mannar at the southern most point of India in the Ramnad Distt. of Tamil Nadu, where the Bay of Bengal meets 'the Arabian sea. It is 31 miles long, seven miles wide and is described in the Puranas as the Gandhamadana hill. It is named after Sri Rama who installed one of the 12 Jyotirlingas of Lord Sarikara here and named it after himself. The temple of the Ramanathswami is the famous temple here. It is a fine specimen of Dravadian architecture with big towers, carved walls and extensive corridors. There are tanks inside the temple. A Sivalinga, and the image of Annapurna Parvati and Hanumana are present in the temple. The buildings are large and are extended in an area of about 865 ft. from east to west and 657 ft. from north to south. The two oceans on the east and west side are called Ratnakana and Mahodadhi. There are several sub-tlrthas in the vicinity such as Madhava tirtha, Cakratlrtha, Amrtatirtha, Sivatlrtha, and Sarvatlrtha. The DhanuskotitTrtha is 12 miles away from Ramesvaram. Here Rama got rid of the sin of killing Ravana. Kalabhairava, who severed the fifth head of Brahma had to do penance by bathing here at Sivatirtha. Krsna performed the repentence for killing his maternal uncle here at Kotitirtha. A Ramesvara tTrtha is also situated on Narmada which may be identified with the temple of Ramesvara on the southern bank of Narmada at Jagore opposite Baravari, 6 miles from Ore and Narmada confluence. Another Ramesvara temple is situated at Maloda on the southern bank of Narmada; and one Ramesvaralinga is situated in Kasi also.

The Rudrakotitrtha is situated in Avimukta near the confluence of Ganga and Yamuna. The Rudrasara is situated on the southern bank of the Narmada. From Bhrgutunga mountain one reached Rudrakunda going through Devaganga. An.other Rudrahrada is situated in .the Sannihititirtha in Kuruksetra. The Raghavesvara is a Siva-linga and Rukmakunda is a holy pond.

## Varanavata

It has been Identified with Bulandshahar, near Delhi, which is said to have been founded by Janamejaya. At Qhal, 21 miles north-east of Bulandshahar, he performed the serpent-sacrifice.It is the modern Barnawa, 19 miles to the north-west of Meerut where Duryodhana tried to burn the -Pandavas alive. It was one of the five villages demanded by Krsna from Duryodhana on behalf of Yudhisthira.

According to Puranas it is a Saivatirtha. The Skanda Purana locates it in Kashmir and the Padma Purana places it on Varahagiri.

## Vijaya

Perhaps it is the same as Vijayapura which was situated on the river Ganga and was the capital of Laksmanasena. Hence it has been identified with Lakhnauti or Gauda which was also situated on Ganga. It was perhaps called Vijayapura after Ballal's father Vijayasena who conquered Bengal. But Vijayapura has been identified with Vijaynagar on the Ganges (Ganga) in Virendra or Barind, in the district of Malda in West Bengal. The Senas, after subverting the Pala kingdom, are said to have made Vijayanagar their capital and subsequently removed to Laksamanavati which was after-wards called Gauda.

## Vilvala and Vilvaka

A Vilvatirtha is situated on the river Pravara between the villages Srivana and Ukkala in the Sriramapura Tehsil of Ahmednagar district. The Vilvesvaralinga was installed here by Indra. A Vilvalatirtha is situated near Trayamvakesvara, 17 miles from Nasik. A Vilvesvara Siva temple is situated in Garhwal Distt. on the bank of Alakananda. Vilvaka, which is usually grouped together with Gangadvara, Kusavarta, Kanakhala and Nilaparvata is situated in Haridvara.

## Vinayaka

Perhaps it is the same as Vinayakahrada. The Ksetra of Vinayaka is situated 3 or 4 miles from Dhanmandal above the Bhuvaneshvara Riy. station on the top of a mountain in Orissa. The confluence of Triyugi and Mandakini is also called the Vinayakadvara. There are 8 places sacred to Vinayaka namely (1) Moreshvar-6 miles from Jajuri, (2) Ballala-46 miles by boat from Bombay, (3) Lenadri-50 miles from Teligaon station of Central Riy., (4) Sidhatek on the river Bhima, 10 miles from the Diksal Station of Central Riy., (5) 'Ojhar-containing the temple of Vinayaka Vighneshvara, (6) Sthevara-also called Thevra, (7) Ranjavagrama and (8) Mahada (the last three are on the Central Riy.).

The Lalita, (Lalitikam) is a stone slab of the shape of an tortoise under an Asvattha tree at a distance of 100 Iksus from Vindhyesvari temple on the Vindhya mountain. The goddess Lalita is in Varanasi, near the pool called Ghantakarna which is situated to the west of Vyasesa. The sacred places called Vasuprada, Vyasasara are mentioned in name only.

## Vinasana

The spot in the great sandy desert in the Sirhind District (Patiala) where the river Sarasvati loses itself after taking a westerly course from Thanehsvara is called the Vinasanatirtha.

## Visala

The city of Visala was founded by Visala, son of Trnabindu. According to the Puranas it was situated in the midst of Nandanavana on the Himalayas and was identical with Badari. It has been identified with Basad in the Muzzaffarpur Distt. in Bihar. It is the Boisali of the Buddhists. The town is said to have been situated on the northern bank of Ganga and not on the Gandaka.In the time of Ksemendra it was oh the river Balgumati. A river of this name was an affluent of the Gandaka in VaisalT. The Agni Purana mentions it under Gayamahatmya.

The Viranagara was situated on the bank of the river Devika. It was very beautiful, prosperous and inhabited by Pulastya. The Vyasatirtha was situated on the bank of Sarasvati or Drsadvati. The Vaikunthatirtha is a place of pilgrimage about 22 miles to the east of Tinnevelly on the river Tamraparni. The Vaivattilamukha is situated on the river Candrabhaga.

The Visvapadatirtha Vedavadana Veda- sira,Vaikrtagiri, Vyamocca and Vaisravan- alaya are mentioned in name only.

## Vastrapatha

It has been placed both in the north and in the south in the country of Surastra. The mount Girnar has been known by different names one of which is Raivataka. The Raivatakaksetra is called Vastrapatha. The Vastrapatha sub-Khanda of the Prabhasa Khanda of the Skanda' Purana describes in 19 chapters the sacred places round about Girnar. It included not only the hill of Girnar and Raivataka but also the area surrounding the hill side. It is called Vastrapatha because it is supposed to cover the area occupied by the fallen cloth of Siva. The extent of this ksetra is given as such, To the north is the river Bhadra, on the eastren side it extends over 2 yojanas, on the southern side is the Balisthana and the Ujjayanta hill and to the west it extends upto the confluence of river near Vamanapura (Vanthali)

## Varahaksetra and Varahatirtha

The Varahatlrtha has been located in Varanasi by the Kurma Purana and in Mathura by the Varaha Purana. The Varahaksetra is the present Baramula in Kashmir on the right bank of the Jhelum where Visnu is said to have been incarnated as Varaha. There is a temple of Adivaraha. Another tirtha of this name is situated at Nathpura on the Kusi in the district of Purnea below TrivenT. The Agni Purana mentions it under Gayamahatmya. There is one Varahaksetra near Ayodhya and another on Kosi in Nepal.

## Vayupura

The Vayu ,Purana relates a story about it. In Brahmaksetra, Brahma asked the sages as to where the god wind is seen. The sages replied that they had not seen him. Meanwhile they saw an atom in the form of a city which was later on named after Vayu. Another story relates that the sages, aggrieved by the accusation of the murder of a brahmin, went to Brahma who asked them to go to the Pavanapura where their sins were to be removed. Accordingly the sages were purged of their sins. Since then this city which was formerly founded by Vayu became the destroyer of sins.

## Vraja

It is the Purana Gokul or Mahavana, a village near Mathura across Yamuna where Krsna was brought up by Nanda in his childhood. The name of Vraja was extended to Vrndavana and the neighbouring village, where Krsna's early life and love affairs took place. The room, the scene of the birth of Mahamaya and the change of Krsna for her, and the Nanda's house are situated on two high mounds of earth. At a short distance are the mortors overturned by the infant Krsna and the place where he broke the twin Arjuna trees. Nanda's palace at Gokul (new Gokul) was turned into a mosque at Aurangzeb's time. The place where Krsna killed Putana, known as Putanam-Khar, is outside the town. Astigrama was the birth-place of Radha.

Vrajamandala is said to have extended over an area of 84 Krosas comprising many villages and sacred places associated with the adventure of Krsna and Radha. Krsna killed the demon Aghasura at Kamavana, Vakasura at Nakavana and Putana at Mahavana. Balarama killed Pralambasura at BhandTravana. Madhuvana was situated at the village of Maholi, Talavana at Tarsi where Balarama defeated Dhenuka. Krsna got rid of the sin of killing the bull Arista at Syamakunda and Radhakunda. In Govardhana town, with the mountain of the same name on the bank of the tank called Manasaganga, was the ancient temple of Harideva. It was at Paitho that the people took shelter from the storm of Indra under the hill uplifted by Krsna. The marriage of Krsna and Radha is said to have taken place at Gantholi.

Growse identifies Mahavana with the 'Kisoboras' of the Greeks and supposes that the modern Vraja was the ancient Anupa country.

## Vaidyanathadhama

It is a famous Jyotirlinga in the temple of Vaidhyanatha in.Devgarh in the Santhal Pargana in Bihar. This Devgarh is a railway station surrounded by Data jungle to the north-east, by Nandaha Pahar in the north west, by Trikutaparvata in the east and south and a small rivulet called Yanunajar and the river Dharna in the west. Originally this Jyotirlinga was called Ravanesvara, but afterwards it was worshipped by the hunter Baiju and came to be known as Vaidyanatha. This Vaidyanatha or Deogarh is called Pampapuri or Palugaon and Dey thinks that it is a corruption of Paralipura or Paraligrama. In the stotra of the 12th Jyotirlinga, the Jyotirlinga of Vaidynatha is said to be at Parali. The Haritakivana and Karmanasa river are near by. The Trikuta mountain is about 6 or 7 miles east of Vaidyanatha. The well near the main entrance of the country yard is known as Candrakupa The Sivagahga lake (600 ft. x 900 ft.), the western portion of which is called Manasasarovara, is also there. A big temple of the Vaidyanatha is also situated near a hill at the village called Parani, a few miles away from Paithan and Gangakhed in Hyderabad. There is also a temple of Vaidyanath at Kiragrama on the east of Kangra district, 30 miles of Kot Kangra on the Binnau river in Punjab. Another Vaidyanatha temple is at Dabhoi in Gujarat.

## Varanasi

It was known to Buddhists under different names such as Surandhana, Sudarsana, Brahma Vardhana Puspavati and Ramya. In the Puranas it is known as Varanasi, Kasi, Rudravasa, Anandakanana, Mahasmasana, Avimukta and GaurTkanana. Formerly it extended from the northern bank of Ganga to the southern bank of Gomati and now it stands between Varana and Asi from which it derives its name. Its extent is recorded to be five Krosas. The Brahma Vaivarta Purana and the Bhagavata Purana record its burning at the hands of Krsna. The Vayu Purana gives the story of its destruction during the rule of the king Divodasa of the lunar race by a demon named Ksemaka who was ordered by Siva to find out a habitat for himself and ParvatL The king, enraged by the refusal of granting the desired boon to his queen, ordered to destroy the image of Ksemaka, which was installed at the outer gate of the city. Consequently he was cursed by Nikumbha (Ksemaka) that his city i.e. Varanasi would be deserted without any cause. After many years the king Alarka in the lineage of Divodasa killed Ksemaka and again inhabited the city. The story is nothing but a mythological allusion to the fact that during the reign of Divodasa Buddhism superseded Siva-worship at Benaras. Later on Buddhism was superseded by Saivism after a short period. In 1027 A.D. it became a part of Gauda and again Buddhism was introduced. It were Pal kings of Bengal who introduced Buddhism in Benaras and Siva-worship was not restored till it was annexed in the llth century by the king of Kanauj.

During the epic period it was the capital of Kasi. During the Buddhistic age this kingdom of Kasi was a part of Kosala. According to the Atthakatha of Buddhaghosa he was born at Benaras. In 1027 A.D. it came under the kingdom of Gauda and Chandra Deva took i't in 1072-1096 A.D. from the Pal kings and annexed it- to the kingdom of Kanauj. By the close of the 12th century A.D. it was conquered by Muhammad Gauri. Hiuen Tsang calls it Po-Lo-Ni-Sse and gives the extent of the country as 4000 li. Its capital bordered the Ganges and was 18 or 19 li in length and 5 or 6 li in breadth. The climate was soft and crops abundant.

## The sub-tirthas of Varanasi are :-

(1) Smasana - This is situated to the south of Varanasi. The Sivalinga called Krttivasesvara was situated to the east of Antakesvara, to the south of Sarvesvara and to the north ofMatalisvara. Probably it was demolished by Aurangzeb and was not re-erected. (2) Madhyamesvara and (3) Visvesvarasituated at Manikarnika in Kasi are other famous Sivalirigas. There is at present a temple of Adi Visvesvara to the north-west of the JnanavapT mosque of Aurangzeb on the other side of the Lala Lajpat Rai Road. (4) Onkara near the confluence of Matsyodari and Gariga and (5) Kapardisvara are also important. (6) The Pisacamocana is a kunda said to be situated at a distance of 11 bows from Kapilesvara. (7) The Manikarnika, which is one of the Sakti-pTthas, is the most sacred of all the cremation ghats. The name Manikarinka is derived as Tadamdolatah karnat papatamanikarnika Manibhih khacita ramya toto'stu Manikarnika. (8) JnanavapT is a well situated behind the temple of Adikesava, an oldest temple which is mentioned in Prabodhacandrodaya. (9) Kapalamocana - The Puranas relate a story about this tirtha. Siva relates to Parvati that in ancient days Brahma had a fifth head also. That fifth head told him that he knows his (Siva's) birth. This enraged Siva who cut it off. Brahma cursed Siva that since he had cut his head without any reason, it would follow him wherever he will go and he would roam all over the world disturbed by this Brahmahatya. Siva went to Visnu who asked him the whole thing and advised him to please Brahma because then only the head would desist from following him. Siva came to this place called Avimukta. Since the head desisted from following him here, this very Avimukta came to be called as Kapalamocanatirtha. In the 12th century the Kapalamocanaghat was situated on Ganga. Now a ghat and a tank near the Rajghat Station is known by that name. There is a temple near this tank dedicated to Kapalamocanesvara.

It seems that there were more than one Kapalamocana tirthas. The Padma Purana locates it in Mayapun. There is one Kapalamocanatirtha in Tamralipta or Tamluk and another on river Sarasvati in Kuruksetra. Cunningham places the holy tank of Kapalamocana on the eastern bank of the river Sarasvati, 10 miles to the south-east of Sadhora.The Sivalingas called AvimuktesVara, Tilabhan-desvara and Dasasvamedhesvara are also famous. The tirtha oPancayatana is also situated here.

## Vrndavana

It was previously known as Kesipura and later on came to be known as Vrndavana after Vrnda, a form of Laksmi. It was situated on the river Yamuna near Govardhana and extended for five yojanas.It was a charming forest abounding in grass, fruits and Kadamba trees, and was very dear to Radha and Madhava. The construction of the city of this name is described at length in the Brahma Vaivarta Purana. It had the holy mountain Govardhana and the sacred river near it. At Vrndavana there is a curve or dragging of the river Yamuna attributed to Balarama, the elder brother of Krsna. It appears to have attained importance at the time of Kalhana. It was visited by Bilhana also.

The modern Vrndavana is situated six miles north of Mathura. The most ancient temple here belongs to the 16th cent. A.D.. The chief temples are those of Govindadevaji, Radha-Vallabha, Gopinath, Jugalakishore and Madanmohana. It is famous for peacocks and monkeys. The Puranas record 12 forests near Vrndavana in the Mathuramandala namely (1) Madhuvana (2) Talavana (3) Kumudavana (4) Bakulavana (5) Khadiravana (6) Vilvakavana (7) Lauhavana (8) BhandTravana (9) Madrakavana (10) Kamikavana (11) Chatravana and (12) Vrndavana. There are a few difficulties in identifying the ancient Vrndavana with the modern one. Dey enumerates them as follows :-

(1) modern Vrndavana .is 16 miles from Mathura whereas it took Akrura the whole day from sunrise to sunset to drive from Vrndavana to Mathura in a car drawn by swift horses.

(2) Nanda moved from Gokul, which is 6 miles from Mathura, across the Yamuna to Vrndavana to escape molestation from the myrmidons of Kamsa.

It is, therefore, not likely that he should select for his sojourn modern Vrndavana which is only 6 miles from Mathura and on the same side of Yamuna leaving the natural barrier of a river.

(3) Vrndavana has no mountains where as the ancient one is said to be mountaineous.

(4) Ancient Vrndavana and Mathura seem to have been situated on the opposite sides of Yamuna.

## Salagrama

The term is usually applied to a stone an ammonite, which is supposed to be a type of Visnu and which is asked to be worshipped in the Uttarakhanda of the Padma Purana and the Brahma Vaivarta Purana. As these stones are found chiefly in the Gandaka river, the Salagrama tirtha was probably situated at the source of this river where Bharata 'and Pulaha performed penances. Jada Bharata's hermitage was on the Kokaveni river on the north of Redigrama and that of Pulaha in the Redigrama village itself.

## Syamantapancaka

It has been located on the bank of the river Sarasvati in Kuruksetra. Diti is said to have performed penances at Syamantapancakaksetra on the bank of the river Sarasvati. Balarama is said to have built five pools here to propitiate his sins.

## Saptagodavari

A Saptagodavari tirtha is mentioned in the Puranas which denotes the country, the sacred place as well as a tank (Hrada). The seven currents of the Godavari are Vasistha, JKausikT, Vrddha-GautamT, Gautami, Bharadvaji AtreyT and Tulya. It is a sacred place at Solangipur, 16 miles from Pithapura, not far from Rajmahendri in the Godavari District (Andhra Pradesh).

## Sthanesvara

It is one of the oldest places in ancient India, which is said to have derived its name either from the Sthana (abode of Isvara or Mahadeva) or from the junction of the names of Sthanu and Isvara. Hiuen Tsang knew it as Sa-Ta-Ni-Shi-Fa-Lo, the circuit of which was 1100 miles. It was the capital of Srikanta janapada and contained a fort the ruins of which lie in about 1200 sq. ft. at the top. S.N. Majumdar identified it with Thuna (Sthuna) mentioned in the Buddhist literature, as a brahmin village forming the western boundary of Madhyadesa. The famous tirtha of Kuruksetra is situated on the southern side of Thaneshvar about 30 miles to the south of Ambala and 40 miles north of Panipat. Thaneshvar is situated 25 miles south of Ambala on the river Sarasvati.

## Siddhaksetra
