The Puranas locate it in different places i.e. in Kalinga, on the Himalaya and on Ganga. Visnu is said to have been incarnated here as Vamana. He attained perfection in penances here. The hermitage stood long before Vamana incarnation and was visited by»Rama and Visvamitra. There is a difference of opinion regarding its location. Ramayana (Kiski Ch. 4333) locates it in the Himalayas between Kancanajanga and Dhavalagiri on the bank of the river Mandakim. According to others it is at Buxar in the Shahbad district. Siddhesvarapura on the river Poon Poon, now i a lied Siddharampur, is the place where the sage Visvamitra had his hermitage called Siddhasrama and where he took Rama and Laksamana to fight with Raksasas and secure the safety of the hermitage.

## Sarasvata

It is a pond mentioned under Varanasi Mahatmya. A firth of this name is situated on the river Godavarl which may be same as Sapta-Sarasvata tirtha. There is a legend about this tTrtha which runs thus, Once the sage Manakanaka lived here. A point of Kusagrass pricked his hand and instead of blood, it emitted the vegetable juice. Seeing this peculiar sight, he went mad with joy and begain to dance. Gradually everything round about, objects both movable and immovable, began to dance with him. Brahma and other gods were surprised, afraid and asked Siva about that strange dance. Siva came down and enquired about the whole matter. On coming to know all, Siva said that there was nothing strange about it. He, too, showed him the snow white ashes coming out of his finger when pierced by his nail. The sage was ashmed, gave up dancing, and Siva's mission was successful. The Pushkar lake in Ajmer is also called Sarasvata.According to Hemakosa it was situated to the north-west of Hastinapura. The Skanda Purana locates it on the confluence of. the Sarasvati and the Ganga.

## Sindhu-Sagara Samgama

The confluence of the river Sindhu and the ocean is regarded very holy.

## Sravasti

It was founded by Sravasta, son of Yuvanasva, after whom it was known as Sravasfi. According to Buddhaghosa, the city was so called because originally it was the residence of Savattha. The name was accorded to the city also because everything necessary for human being (Sarvam Asti) was available here. It is also called Saravati and Savasti. It is mentioned by the authors of Astadhyayi and the Puranas. The Markandeya places them in the west and the Matsya Purana in the country of Gauda on the bank of Aciravati i.e. eastern Bengal. But it is more usually placed in Kosala by which a part of Oudh region is commonly understood. It figures throughout the Buddhist literature as the capital of the Kosalan kingdom. It was a great centre of trade.

It was also an important seat of Brahmanism and the Vedic learning. The Nighantus, the Accalakas, the Eksatakas, and the Paribhajakas were very familiar to this city. The Jains called it Candrapun and Candrikapuri.

When Fa-hien visited it there were only a few inhabitants and the city was in ruins. During the time of Hiuen Tsang also it was mostly in ruins but had good crops and soft and agreeable climate. It was known as Shi-Sah-Lo-Fusih-Lai or Shi-Sah-Lo-Fu-Shi-Ti and its extent was 6000 li. Cunningham has identified it with a great ruined city on the southern bank of Rapti called Sahet-Mahet, about 58 miles north of Ayodhya. The entire site lies on the borders of Gonda and Baharaich districts of Uttar Pradesh.

The tirtha of Sugandha is a holy place on the Gandhamandana mountain. It is one of the 52 Sakti Pithas in Nasik on the Godavari also. The Svarnabindu, according to the Nilamata is a northern tirtha but the Skanda places it on Narmada. There are more than one Somatirtha,which are placed variously in (1) Varanasi (2) on the bank of Yamuna, (3) on the bank of Sarasvati, (4) in Kuruksetra and (5) in Saurastra.

The tirtha of Sankara is situated in Nepal, immediately below the town of Patan at the confluence of the Bagmati and Manimati (Manirohini). The Skanda places a Sankara tirtha in Dvaraka also. The Sriranga is the Seringpatam in Mysore. The Svamitirtha has been identified with Tirupati in Madras. The Samalanatha is the present Samoiji in Mahi Kantha, Bombay. The temple of Samaiji is said to have been built in the 15th century in the old city.

The Sivakunda (Matsya P. 1333), Sivadhara (Matsya P.2249, Padma P. Srsti 1142), SriparnI (Matsya P. 2249, Padma Srsti 1142) Srivrksa (Vayu Utt. 1538) SripatitTrtha (Matsya P.2274, Brahma P. 2310, Sk.P. Reva 1921, 19513), Satakumbha (Nua.v. 87), Sailakirti (Vayu Utt. 1586), Satamukha (Naa.v. 118), Sakambhari (Nila.v. 88. Padma P. Svarga 2814. Brahma P. 2339) in Tehri, Samsaramocana (Matsya P.2267, Padma P. Srsti 1160), Sahastraksa (Matsya P. 1334, 2252, Padma P. Srsti 1145, Sk. P. Nagara 10830, 10910), Santana (Matsya P. 1334), Somapana (Matsya P.2262), Saptahrada (Vayu Utt. 1556), Saumitri-Samgama (Matsya P. 2253), Suvarnakhyama (Nila.v. 88) or Suvarnaksa (Matsya P. 18125) and Sagarodakama (Nila. v. 83-95) are mentioned in name only.

## Hastinapura

It was named so after Hasti, its founder and was situated on Bhagirathi. It was also called Gajahvya and its ruler Gajasahvyas. The Kuru kings were closely associated with it. During the reign of Nicaksu, son of Adhisamakrsna, this city is said to have been carried away by Ganga and the king is said to have transferred his capital to KausambI, the present Kosam in Allahabad. It was finally ruined by the enchroachments of the Ganges but vestiges of it were, at least until lately, to be traced along the river nearly in a line with Delhi, about 60 miles to the east.

Rsabha, the first Tirthankara, was an inhabitant of Hastinapura. The city was often visited by MahavTra, the propagator of Jainism. Kalidasa mentions it as the capital of Dusyanta.

It has been traditionally identified with an old town in Mawana Tehsil in Meerut which is 22 miles north-east of Meerut and south-west of Bijnor on the right bank of Ganga. Garhmukteshvara, containing the temple of Muktesvara Mahadeva was a quarter of ancient Hastinapura.

## The Hermitages.

## Pulahasrama

Here the king Rsabha and afterwards his son are said to have performed penances. The sages Pulaha and Bharata performed penances near the source of the Gandaka. Jada Bharata's hermitage was situated on the Kokaveni river to the north of the Redigrama and that of Pulaha was situated in the Redigrama itself.

## Haritasrama

The sage Harita, author of the one of the Sarhhitas had his hertmitage at Ekiinga, situated in a defile at a distance of about 6 miles from Udayapur in Rajasthan.

## Kapilasrama

It has been located by the Visnu Purana on the banks of the Iksumati river which is one of the Himalayan rivers. The Saundarananda Kavya and the Divyavadana locate it not far from the river BhagTrathT by the side of the Himalayas. The Skanda Purana has a KapilesvaratTrtha in the midst of the Bhrguksetra on the northern bank of the river Narmada, which was established by the sage Kapila to atone for his burning the sixty thousand sons of Sagara. He is said to have performed penances on the bank of Narmada. This, place may be identified with a fall of Narmada named Kapiladhari situated 2-12 miles north-west of Kabira-Chautara, which is at a distance of three miles from the Amarakantaka mountain. This sage Kapila is said to have his hermitage at this Kapiladhara.

If we rely upon the evidence of the Brhatdharma Purana, we would have to locate it in the island of Sagara near the mouth of the Ganga. The ruins of a temple dedicated to him are situated on the south-east corner of the minor island into which the island of Sagara is divided by creeks and rivers.

**Badari**

The Badari, Badarivana or Badariksetra or Badarikasrama has been regarded as an abode of Narayana on the Gandhamadana mountain in the Himalayan region. It is also known as Visala and Kokamukha. The temple of Badannatha is situated in the Malla Peukhanda pargana, about 184 miles from Haridvara, 105 miles from Shrinagar (Garhwal) and about 25 miles south of the Mana Pass. It is on the right bank of the river Alakananda. The valley in which it stands is 3 miles long and one mile broad. To the east and the west of the valley stand the two famous and lofty mountains, Nara and NarayanaThis Himalayan temple is situated 30° 44' north lat. and 77° *32'* east long. at an elevation of 10284 ft. above sea-level.

Many tirthas are situated around Badari. Indraloka, Pancasikha, Sarasvatatlrtha and Naradakunda are situated near by. The Taptakunda situated near the temple is 16.5 by 14.5 ft. and contains a thermal spring of hot sulphurous waters.

The region around Badari is called the Vaisnavaksetra and in it are situated the Pancabadaris i.e. (1) The Badarinarayana or the Visalabadari, (2) Yogabadari at Pandukesvara (3) Bhavisyabadari near Tapovana (4) Vrddhabadari at Animath and (5) Dhyanabadari near Silang. There are five Tirthas in the Badarinarayana namely the Rsiganga, Kurmadhara, Prahlada-dhara, Taptakunda and Naradakunda. There are also five sacred silas in Badarikasrama namely Naradasila, Varahasila, Markandeyasila, Nrsimhasila and the Garudasila A little north of Badannatha, on the right banK of the river Alakananda is the famous Brahmakapala which the pilgrims invariably visit. About 8 iiles from BadarT is the Vasudhara-tirtha where the eight Vasus practised penances. It is a difficult place to reach as the water falls from the rocks. The Badari is also famous as the place of the hermitage of Vyasa who is also called Badarayana as he was born in the forest of Badari. Parasara, the father of Vyasa, also lived here. Mitra and Varuna practised penances here. A visit to it by Pandavas has been recorded by the Mahabharata which also says that pandu, the father of the Pandavas cursed by the sage, also performed penances here, At Visnuprayaga i.e. the confluence of Alakananda (called the Visnuganga here) and Dhavalaganga, the sage Narada performed penances. The Alakananda joins Virahi at Virahisamgama, 4 miles from Chamoli. At Garudaganga people pick up stones whose application is supposed to have the power to destroy the poisonous effect of serpent bites.

The Badarikaksetra is measured from Kanva's hermitage to Nandagiri, about 3 yojanas by 12 yojanas. It is the 0-Ch-Li of Hiuen Tsang. Cunningham has identified it with Edar in Gujarat province, the SauvTra of the Puranic period. It is situated off a river called HiranyanadT mentioned in the Dhavala Ins. at Vasantagada near mount Abu.

## Vasisthasrama

The beautiful hermitage of Vasistha, full of sages and adorned with various kinds of flowers, creepers and trees has been located by Kalidasa in the Himalayas. Dilipa and his queen, desirous of having a son started for this hermitage. It is described at length in the Ramayana.

It has been said to be situated variously on the mount Abu in the Aravalli range, (1) at a place one mile north of Ayodhya and (2) on the Samdhyacala mountain near Kamarupa in Assam. It is the modern Vangath, situated about two miles below the Bhutesh.

The Valakhilyasrama, too, was situated on the peak of Himalayas named Gandhamadana. Besides, the hermitage of Saubhari, Tandulika are also mentioned.

## The lakes and the Nagas.

## Acchoda

It is the mind born daughter of the Pitrs and is situated on the Candraprabha mountain. A river named Acchoda issues out of it. It is mentioned by Bana in his Kadamban and has been located on this evidence in Kashmir. Bilhana, too, refers to it. It is the Achetavat 6 miles from Martanda and on its bank is situated the Siddhasrama.

## Bindusara

Previously it was called Bahusara. Bindusara is its later name. A legendary account of its origin has been given in the Puranas. The Tripathaga, the shining path seen in the midst of the sky, after watering the sky fell on Siva's head who with his yogic powers confronted it there. The few drops of the enraged Ganga fell on the earth and out of them was formed the lake Bindusara. The seven currents of the Ganga issue out of the Bindusara which is situated at the base of the Gaura mountain in the north of the Kailasa. It was in the vicinity of this lake, that the king Bhagiratha performed penances for many years for bringing down the Gariga from heaven. The BhTsma Parva of Mahabharata gives its situation near the Mainaka mountain.

It is situated on the Rudra Himalayas, 2 miles south of Gangottari. A KardamesatTrtha is said to be situated in the lake Bindusara.

## Kramasara

It is situated on the Visnupada mountain. The etymology of the name has been given thus in the Nilamata. Kramata Visnuna Lokan krtam paden tat sarah Kramasaram idam proktam.... Proceeding westward from Banahal pass are situated three snowy peaks. The western most and the highest of these peaks (15523. ft) is the famous Naubandhanasikhara. At the foot of this peak and to the north-west of it lies a lake over two miles long known as Konasar Nag, the Kramasaras or Kramasara of the Nilamata and the Mahatmyas. It is supposed to mark the foot steps of Visnu.

## Visnupada

It is said to have been situated on the Nisadha mountain but the Nilamata places Kramasara lake on this mount. The Visnupada lake, sacred to Sati, is known as Kramasara lake, which is derived in the Nilamata as such. Kramata Visnu Lokan Krtapadena Tatsarah, Kramasaramidam Proktam Yatha Visnupadamca Tat or Istavani Kratubhistatra yada deva Pitamaha. Kramasarastada Proktah.....'.

## Uttaramanasa

The Uttaramanasa, in the northern direction, is the residence of the Sodaranaga. It is the sacred Gariga lake situated below the eastern glaciers of mount Haramukh in Kashmir and popularly known as Gangabal. It is mentioned in Haramukhamah-atmya and Haracarita also.

## Kalodaka

It is the previous name of the NandTsara where NandT performed penances. The name is derived from a legend that it was the joint residence of both Kala (Siva) and his servant Nandl. The whole connection of the sacred sites takes the name of Nandiksetra by which Kalhana usually designates it. It is the present Kundkol lake fed by the glaciers and situated near the Gungabal lake. The situation of the lake in the glaciers of the Harmukh peaks is circa. 75° long 34 20' lat. at an altitude of about 13000 ft. above sea level, and forms one of the chief stations on the pilgrimage to the sacred Gariga lake which lies at a short distance above it.

## Manasa-Sarovara

It is said to have been situated at the base of the Vaidyuta mountain from which issues the river Sarayu. It is situated on the Kailasa mountain in the Hunadesa in the western Tibet. Its Hunnic name is Cho-Mapan Moorcroft has described it graphically, according to whom it is 15 miles in length (east to west) by 11 miles in breadth (north to south). The circumambulation of the lake is performed in 4, 5 or 6 days according to the stay of pilgrims in the 8 Gumbas on the bank of the lake. On the south of the lake is the Guria range. Sven Hedin says, Even the first view from the hills caused us to burst into tears of joy at the wonderful magnificent landscape and its surpassing beauty. The oval lake lies like an enormous turquoise embedded between two of the finest and the most famous mountain giants of the world, the Kailasa in the north and Guria Mandatta in the south, and between huge ranges, above which the mountains uplift their crowns of bright white eternal snow.

The Manasasara of the Nilamata and Jonaraja is about 2 miles long and occupies a rock basin deeper than the other lakes of Kashmir. Its ancient name is derived from the sacred one on the Kailasa. The Manasa of Nilamata is the present small lake of Manasabal to the north of an isolated hill. known as Ahatyung rising about a thousand feet abov». the plain. This hill is situated close to the village of Sambal near the present Sindhu. The saras named Kaundinyasara Tailasara, Udvartansara, AtasTsara, Siddhartakasara, Madhuparkasara, Usnodaka Devasara. and Pancagavyasara are mentioned in name only.

## The Nagas

The Nilamata Purana mentions a number of Nagas in Sati-desa i.e. Kashmir. They are the tutelary deties supposed to live in the spring and the lakes of the valley. They live under the form of the snakes living in the water of the springs and the lakes protected by them. Thus springs are generally called Nagas in Kashmir. They are supposed to take the form of hailstorm and to obtain food by destroying the crops. Though a good many of them are mentioned in the Nilamata (verses 881-915), only the most important ones are being dealt herewith.

## Karkotakanaga

It is mentioned alongwith Nila, Vasuki and the Taksaka nagas in the beginning of the list of the Kashmirian Nagas. It seems to have been worshipped in the different localities. The Karkotakanaga, after whom the Karkotaka drainage basin was named, was probably supposed to live in one of the small mountain lakes on the Tosamaidah Pass, leading to Loharin. It has left its name to this day on the mountain ridge called Kakodar.

Another Karkotakanaga is mentioned in the village of Utarna in the Kutahar Pargana 75°22' longitude and 33°43' latitude. This spring might have been alluded to in the Haracarita.

## Maksikasvamin Naga

It was the name of the part of Shrinagar or a suburb immediately adjoining the capital. It can be identified with the island of Mayasum which lies at the south-east corner of Shrinaear between the Vitasta and the two branches of the Tsonth Kul or Mahasarit. The name Mayasum can be traced back to Maksikasvamin through immediate apabhramsa form.

## Susravanaga

It is situated in the vicinity of the Vijayesvaratirtha. The mountain lake in which the legend locates it is situated near the source of the Lidar River at 75° 34° longitude 34° 5' latitude. It is popularly known as Sesanaga and forms one of the station of the great Amaresvara pilgrimage. The legend identifies the lake with the serpent Sesanaga, but in the same chapter the lake is also named as Susramanaga, which is the Kashmirian form of the Sanskrit word. The older families of the purohitas still know the lake as Susrama Naga.

## Aksipalanaga

It is the modern Achabal in the Kutahar Pargana 75° *17'* longitude 33° 41° latitude famous for its 'beautiful springs and described by Bernier and Vigne. It is situated at a distance of 5 or 6 miles from Anantanaga.

## Sodaranaga

The Uttaramanasa lake i.e. modern Gangabal is the place of Sodaranaga who is mentioned in connection with the shrine of Bhutesvara and KanakavahinT river.

The Bindusaranaga is said to have been situated in the eastern direction and Srimathakanaga is said to have been situated in the southern direction.

## Lohita

It is situated at the foot of the Suryaprabha mountain also called Hemasrnga and Lohita. From it issues the river Lauhitya. It is also called Ravanahrada identified with the, Rakastal. It is mennoned as a small lake now called Candrabhaga from which issues the river Candrabhaga in Lahoul or Middle Tibet.

The lake Sailoda is situated at the base of the Sriman mountain and from it issues the river Sailoda.

## Hastikarnanaga

It is placed at Vyaghrasrama i.e., Vigahama the modern village Vaghom situated in the Dachunpor Pargana 75°10' longitude 33°50' latitude not far from the right bank of the Vitasta. A spring issuing in this village is still known as Hastikarnanaga and is mentioned by this name in the Vijayesvara Mahatmya, Amaresvara Mahatmya, Tirthas and the Haracarita. The Hastikarna mentioned by Srivara 'in connection with the stream Marl or Mahasarit must be a different one in the vicinity of Srinagar.

**Anantanaga**

It is situated at the southern end of the town of Islamabad or Anantanag situated at the foot of the western extremity of the Martand plateau.

Islamabad, the ancient capital of Kashmir on the right bank of the Jhelum is just the gate to the Lidar valley.

## Vasukinaga (Nilanaga)

A legend about its origin is given in the Nilamata which relates that the snakes at the order of their mother Kadru (during the famous dispute between Kadru and Vinata) tied themselves to the body of the horse. Seeing their deceitful deed, Garuda, the son of Vinata started eating them. Terrified as they were, Vasuki, one of the elder Nagas went to Visnu, worshipped him and got a boon that if they live in Satidesa (i.e. Satisara) they would have no fear from Garuda. Visnu asked him to coronate the Nilanaga in the kingdom of these snakes living in the Satidea.

The ancient traditions, alive even today, place Nilanaga at the head of all the nagas or spring deities of the land. His residence, the famous fountain near the village of Vernag, is situated in the Sehshabad Pargana at the foot of the Banhal Pass 75°I7' longitude and 33°32' latitude. Near the Nilanaga, Visnu is said to have first placed the ploughshare with which the Safisara was drained and Parvati was brought to light there in the form of the river Vitasta by a stroke of Siva's trident. Hence the tirtha is also called Vitasta and Sulaghata. The Nilanaga is now commonly known by the name of Vernag, which is derived from the old designation 'V'er' of the present Shahabad Pargana.

The pond, having an octagonal shape, must also in ancient times have had a circular form as the name Nilakunda denotes. The name Shahabad is comparatively modern as Abul Fazi still knows the place as Ver, which still survives in the name'Vernag popularly given to the spring which is said to be the abode of Nilanaga and the traditional source of the Vitasta. Abul Fazi noted the temples to the east of it which are no longer there, their material being used perhaps in building the fine stone enclosure which Jahangir built round the spring. The spring is called Nila perhaps due to the deep blue colour of its water'. Kalhana refers to the circular pond which shows that the spring even in his time had an artificial enclosure similar to the present one.

## Mahapadmanaga

There is a legend as to how the lake became the residence of the Mahapadmanaga. Originally, it was occupied by the wicked snake Sadarigula, who used to carry off the women of the country. Nila banished him to the land of the Darvas. The site left dry on his departure was occupied by a town called Candrapura. The sage Durvasa, when not received with hospitalities in. this town, cursed it and foretold its destruciton by water. Later on when the Mahapadmanaga sought a refuge from Garuda in Kashmir, Nila gave him the city of Candrapura and told him to ask the king for the residence. Accordingly Mahapadma went to the king in the guise of a Brahmin and asked him for a habitat. The king granted him his wish. Then he told the king about the curse and asked him to leave the city. Accordingly when the king left the city, it turned into a lake which was named Mahapadma after the Naga who is located in the lake as its titular deity. This name, most common in the chronicles, Nilamata and other old texts is found in the Tang Annals. The name Ullala from which the present Wular is derived is found only in one passage of Jonaraja's chronicle.

The Mahapadmanaga is considered to be the titulary deity of the Wular lake (Mahapadmanaga). Srivara speaks of a flood in Kramarajya as uniting itself with the waters of the Padmanagasara by which only the Wuler can be meant.

From Rajatarangim and the passages discussed in the notes there on, it appears that the Mahapadmanaga of the lake was identified with the Kaliyanaga subdued by Krsna. The lake itself is referred to as Mahapadmasara in Rajatarangim and it is mentioned as Mo-Ho-Po-Lo-No-Lung in the Annals of the Tang dynasty. A legend recorded by Jonar (939) directly identifies the Mahapadmanaga inhabiting the Wular with the Kaliya Naga. The same is mentioned in Dhyanesvara Mahatmya which speaks of Kaliya as worshipped in the Ullolasara or Wular.

The Wular lake acts as a huge flood reservoir for the greatest part of the drainage of Kashmir. Its dimension varies at different periods owing to the low shores to the south being liable to inundation. In normal years the length may be reckoned at about 12 miles'and its width at ? miles, with an area of about 78 square miles. During the flood times, it extends to about 13 miles in length and 8 miles in the width. Its depth is nowhere more than 15 ft. which js continually lessening in those parts where the stream debouches into it.

## The Sacred places of Kasmira

The Nilamata Purana has given a great number of sacred places. The verses 990-1070 enumerate many of them, and the verses 1151 to 1162 describe the places sacred to Visnu out of them only the important ones are being discussed herewith.

## Bhedagiri and the Tirtha of Gangodbheda

The Gangodbheda Mahatmya relates how the sage Pulastya performed long penances in the Satidesa and made the Ganga gush forth near him from Himvat mountain for the purpose of his sacrifice. When Pulastya decided to discharge the river after finishing his worship, Sarasvati stopped him from doing so and announced that a tirtha named Gangodbheda would arise at the place from where the river issued. On the top of a hill where th? level ground only extends for ten Dhanus, a great pond would be formed and at its eastern foot a stream called Abhaya would issue. A boon to the sage was granted for which he asked that the river may rest for ever by his side. The boon was granted and the Gangodbhedatirtha was created. With a desire to see the goddess SarasvatI the sage performed severe penances. Having been worshipped by him, Sarasvati explains her sixfold nature to him. With reference to this the sage gave her the name of Bheda and worshipped her as Hamsavagisvan Bheda. Since than the goddess received worships at Gangodbheda tirtha.

The Nuamata gives a brief reference to the goddess Bheda at Gangodbheda but the meagre information of the Nilamata does no.t help us in identification of the tirtha as it merely mentions that the shrine of Bheda was made by Pulastya.

Stein tried to identify it in September 1895. He relates that a Bheda Devi was known to be worshipped at the village Hal-Mogulpur in Shrikru close to the Kooshopoora. The site at which Bheda Devi is worshipped is a small enclosure round a magnificient old Chinar tree. A small stream coming from some nearby spring is conducted through the enclosure. But this could not be the site where the Sarasvati was worshipped. There is a lake on the summit of the Bheda hill and a place named Budabrar in Kashmir! and Bijabrari in Pahari which has got a relation with the Bhedabhradu or Bedabradu of the glosses of the Rajatrangini. At this point (marked on the map by the entry Bhedagiri) the stream bends round a small hillock on the flat top of which is a square tank of limpid water. It occupies the northern portion of the pleateau and forms a square of about 55 ft. and is fed by a beautiful spring. Close by is a large boulder on which two lingas are carved in high relievo. On the north-east and north-west sides, the small pleateau falls off steeply towards the stony bed of the hill stream which flows about 70 ft. below the level of the tank. To the south-west of the little pleateau the place has got the traces of old walls and fragments of large red bricks.

This Budabrar agrees in all details with the account of the site sacred to Bheda. Here is a natural pond formed on the top of a small hillock. Even the measurement of the ten bows is remarkably accurate as referrig to the size of the tank itself.... At the eastern foot of the tank, just as the Mahatmya has it, issues a spring which neither falls to flow nor leaps down over the steep slope. In the ancient tank filled by the spring of Budabrar, the sacred basin of Garigodbheda is easily recognised. The water of the spring which fills the tank is said to be warm in the winter. The Mahatmya also relates that the snow never falls here. Even Abul Fazi knew of this particular feature.

A tirtha named Gangodbheda is mentioned in the Vanaparva of the Mahabharata and the Padma Purana but it cannot be said that they are one and the same.

## Amaresvara

Amburnath, together with the sacred Ganga lake on the mount Haramukuta is the most popular of the Kashmirian pilgrim places. A high peak, situated about 10 miles east, south-east of the Zozi-la marks the point where the range forming the eastern boundary of the Kashmir branches off from the main chain. The chain running almost to south reaches the southern most headwaters of the Vitasta and then turning to the north-west, it joins the Pir Panjal Range at the Banahal pass.

The tirtha of Amaresvara is situated at its northern end dose to the great snowy peak already mentioned. It is a celebrated shrine of Siva in a grooto in the Bhairavaghati range of the Himalaya, about 60 miles from Islamabad, the ancient capital of Kashmir. The cave is situated on the west side of a snowy peak, 17037 ft. in height, locally known as Kailasa. A little stream called Amarganga, a tributary of the Indus, flows by the left side of the cave over a white soil with which the pilgrims besmear their body to cleanse away their sins. The path to the cave lies along the side of. the Amarganga stream. The cave is a natural one 50 ft. in breadth at the base and 25 ft. in height. The Linga, at the inner extremity of the cave is about 20 or 25 ft. from the entrance. Regarding it Stein writes, The Linga which is an embodiment of Siva Amareshvara is a large block of transparent ice formed by the freezing of the water which oozes from the rock, which is evidently a dolomite rock. The pedestal of the linga is 7 or 8 ft. in diameter and 2 ft in height. It is 3 ft. in height and rises from the centre of the pedestal with the figure of a serpant entwining it. There is a peculiarity regarding the formation of this liriga that it has got some connection with the moon. It keeps on growing with the passing of everyday after the day of the new moon till it attains its full height on the day of the full moon. The process of forming and dissolving goes on every day, and on the day of the new moon no sign of the image exists at all.

## Asramasvami

The sage Parasurama went to several tirthas and performed penances. Pleased by him, Siva granted him a boon. He asked the permission of Siva to bring the 'Archa' establr red by the sage Grdhrakuta to the place of his Asrama. That place was called Asramasvami.

## Kapatesvara

The Nilamata Puraria places it between Nandi and Bhutesvara. It gives a story about its origin. Once one crore sages performed penances on the bank of the Drsadvati in Kuruksetra so that they may have a sight of Siva. Seeing this, Siva told them in dream to go to Kashmir where there is a pure home of Naga. Accordingly the sages went there. When they did not have a sight of Siva they started the churning of the woods that surrounded the 'Sarovara'. Then Siva appeared before them and they attained 'Rudratva Now there was a Brahmin named Gauraparasara who neither bathed in that water nor touched the wood and went on performing penances. Then Siva told him in the dream to bathe and touch the wood and thus attain oneness with Rudra. But the Brahmin did not agree since he would not find peace without having his sight. Samkara told him that he had appeared before him in the guise of the wood. Pleased as he was with him, he granted him a boon that Nandi, his servant, in the guise of wood would be always visible to the people and would be known as Kapatesvara.

This is situated in between the tirthas of Nandi and Bhutesvara, close to the village of Kother in the Kuthar Pargana. It consists of a large tank fed by springs and enclosed by a massive stone wall which the tradition, referred to by Kalhana and still locally remembered ascribe to the king Bhoja of Malwa. At the spring called Papa-sudana Siva is worshipped under the name of KapatTsvara having shown himself there according to the legend in the guise of the pieces of woods floating on the river.

Alberuni knew Kapatesvara and the legend connected with it. There is a story that pieces of wood sent by Mahadeva appear annually in a pond called Kudaishahr to the left of the source of the Vitasta, in the middle of the month of Vaisakha.Abul Fazi has recorded that in the village of Kotihar there is a deep spring surrounded by stone temples. When its water decreases, an image of Mahadeva in sandal wood appears.

## Cakresvara

The position of this linga cannot be given with precision. The evidence of the Nilamata, Mahatmyas and Tirthas show that Siva under this name was worshipped at more than one places. The Nilamata places. it on the river Madhumafi (probably the stream of Bandapur). Another Cakresvara was near Vijayesvaratirtha ' and two more Cakresvara Sivalingas are found at the spring called Cakratirtha close to Tsesvara (Isabar) near Shrinagar and in a cave near the Harsesvara tirtha above Khunamoh.

## Jyesthesa

It seems that Siva, under this name, was worshipped at several places in Kashmir i.e. (1) below mount Haramukuta in the sacred territory called the Nandlksetra, (2) near Tripuresvara (modern Triphar) (3) in the neighbourhood of Shrinagar. (4) It is the emblem worshipped from early times close to Bhutesvara (modern Buthiser) above the village of Vangath. In the Nilamata the consecration and the first worship of the Jyestharudralinga is distinctly attributed to the sage Vasistha. (5) An old tradition dating back at least 16th century connected the Takhat hill with the worship of Siva Jyestharudra of Jyesthesvara. A linga of this name is worshipped even now at the tirtha of Jyether, hardly more than one mile from the east foot of the hill. This tirtha, which must have derived its name from Jyesthesvara lies in a glen of the hill side, at a short distance from the east shore of the Gogri Bal portion of the Dal. Its sacred spring known as Jyesthanaga is a favourite place of pilgrimage. The presence of the fragments of several colossal lingas found in the vicinity of Jyether prove that the place was regarded sacred from an early period.

## Tripuresvara

It is the modern Triphar situated at a distance of 3 miles from the Dal. It is mentioned several times in the Nilamata, some older Mahatmyas and the RajataranginI but it is no longer regarded as a seperate sacred place. Only the little stream called Tripuraganga, near Triphar is now visited as one of the Mahadeva pilgrim station.

## The confluence of the Vitasta and Sindhu.

This confluence has been regarded exceptionally sacred since very early times. The Nilamata identifies it with the famous confluence of Prayaga. Kalhana has described the position of both the old and the new one in relation to certain temples situated at the village of Trigrami and other points on the river banks. The new one in his time was identical with the present confluence opposite Shadipur, the old one being about 2 miles to the south-east of it between the village of Trigrama and the Paraspor plateau.

## Tungesa

It is mentioned in the Nilamata in connection with the tirtha of Visnuvaraha at Varahamula. Though the position of this temple is not certain, yet it must have been some where in Shrinagar or in its immediate neighbourhood.

## Nandiksetra

The story related in the Nilamata says that Siva went to the Kalodaka lake where he saw Nandi performing penances. He asked him to choose a boon and accordingly granted him the long life saying, You would live near me at a distance of one yojana in the eastern direction. Your place would be created by Vasistha and mine by Aura. It is situated 23 miles south of Srinagar in Kashmir in a high alpine valley at the foot of the eastern glaciers of the Haramukh peaks including the Gangabal lake and the Nandisara or Nandkol or Kalodaka which is regarded as the residence of Siva and Nandi. Kalhana included in it, the neighbouring site of Bhutesvara or Buthisar in the Kankai valley below Nundkol. The temple of Jyesthesvara is situated in this valley.

## Nandisila.

It is probably a site, connected with the legend of Nandin, on the Haramukuta mountain. According to the Nilamata, Nandin was produced by Silada from pulverized rocks and he performed austerities in the lake named after him while holding a large rock on his head. Nandisila is the present village named Nadihel in the Hamal pargana.

When Siva reached the bottom of the mountain, he asked Parvati, who accompanied him on his mission of granting Nandin a boon, to stay there as she was very delicate. The path by which Siva went was called Pathlsvara. When Siva started ascending the mountain it began to elevate itself. Then Siva struck it on the head and since then the mountain was called Mundaprstha. Then the mountain asked Siva to forgive him. Pleased by him Siva said that the water which originated by striking his foot would be called Krparutirtha on the earth.

## Nandana Naga

The Nandanasara lies near the Darbal Pass (height above 13000 ft.) beyond the lower Ratau Pir Range in Kashmir.

## Bhutesvara

It is the modern Buthisar in the valley of the Kankai stream (Sanskrit Kanaka vahirii). The legend relates how Siva came to take up his residence in that neighbourhood in the form of Bhutesa. The tradition recorded in the Nandiksetra Mahatmya and current among Kashmirians has located Siva Bhutesa's residence on the mountain spur which streches south-east from the Haramukh peaks. This spur bears to the present day the name of Buthiser. In the narrow gorge of the Kankanai river, which flows past the southern foot of this spur and some two miles above the hamlet of Vangath are found the ruins of some 17 temples of various ages and dimensions.

It is closely connected with the legends of mount Haramukuta and is often mentioned in the Rajatarangim. The shrine of Jyesthesvara immediately adjoins it.

## Pancahasta

It is the modern village of Panzath in the Divasar Pargana. A large spring in its vicinity is held sacred as one of the place where Vitasta originally came to light.

## Bhima Devi

It is described in conjunction with the Suresvari tirtha. The Haracarita-cintamani names it with the seven springs of Theda. A cluster of the small villages (Krur, Dampor, Mauzagam, Pohul) lies along the lake shore about one and a half miles further north from Thid. These villages are jointly named as Bran.

This can be identified with Bhimadevi which Kalhana notices along with Theda. This identification of Bran with Bhimadevi is supported by the Nilamata which mentions this tirtha in connection with that of Suresvari, which lies at a distance of about two miles to the north on the ridge of the hill-range above Bran. The tirtha of Bhimadevi is no longer known but it may probably be located at the five springs issuing from the hill side near the hamlet of Dampor, at a spot now marked by a Mohammadan shrine.

## Martanda.

The ruins of the temple are situated near the northern edge of the alluvial plateau (Udar) of Matan. The temple is dedicated to Visnusurya who is worshipped under the form of Martanda. About one mile to the north-west of the temple and at the foot of Udar, there is a magnificiant spring which an ancient legend connects with the birth of the sub-god Martanda. It is visited by numerous pilgrims and is also known throughout the country. The ancient remains at the sacred spring itself are very scanty. All the more imposing are the ruins of the great temple erected by Lalitaditya in the honour of the presiding deity of the tirtha.

It is the modern Bavan where is situated a pond which is filled by the water of a spring. The temple was situated on a hillock 2 or 3 miles from Bavan on way to Shrinagar.

## Pathisvara

Pathisvara is situated in the direction of the sacred mountain Haramukuta. Its other name is Istikapatha, by which is perhaps meant the locality referred to in the Nilamata as Pathesvara Istah. It is the modern Hamlet of Ramaradan in the Lar Pargana from which the ascent on the Harmukuta pilgrimage begins.

## Yogesa

It is a name of Visnu. The shrine may be identical with that of Visnu Yogasayin mentioned near the confluence of Vitasta and the Sindhu.

## Vasisthasrama

It is the modern Vungath, close to Buthser, which has received its ancient name from the sage Vasistha who is supposed to have resided here.

## Varahatirtha

It is the same as Varahaksetra. It is located on the river Jhelum.

This may be identified with the ancient Varahamula (present town of Baramula) at the western entrance of the valley. This place has been held sacred since ancient times as the residence of Visnu in his incarnation as Adivaraha. The legends connected with the sacred site and the various localities in its neighbourhood are described in the Varahaksetra Mahatmya, other Mahatmyas and the Nilamata as well. Kalhana uses the term Varahaksetra for the whole of the neighbourhood including the site of Huskapura on the left river bank. The later chronicles and the Mahatmyas use both the words i.e. Varahaksetra and Varahatirtha, used indifferently in the same sense. The ancient temple of Varaha was situated on a narrow strip of ground between the foot of the hills and the right bank of the river Jhelum.

(1) It is Baramula in Kashmir on the right .bank of the Jhelum, where Visnu is said to have incarnated as Varaha.

(2) Another place of the same name exists at Nathpur on the Kusi ? t'-e district of Purnea below the Triveni.

It is the same as Kokamukha of the Varaha Purana sacred to Varaha (Chs.122, 141-142).

## Somatirtha

This is a well-known sacred place within the city of Shrinagar on the right bank of the Jhelum just below the second bridge. The ghat is popularly called Samayar. The Vitastamahatmya describes at some length the healing powers of a bath taken here in the Jhelum. Another Somatirtha is situated near Vijayesvara.

## Sodara

The sacred Sodara spring, an incarnation of the Sodaranaga, worshipped originally near the sacred site of Bhutesvara below Haramukuta mountain is situated at the village of Sudarabal situated in a deep inlet of the Dal known as Sudarakhun. This Sudarabal is situated about a mile east of the hamlet of Antabavan, which is only a quarter of a mile to the east of Vicarnag and on the other side of the old canal called Lachamkul (Laksmikulya).

## Suresvari

The goddess Durga is worshipped as Suresvari on a high crag rising above the village Isabar from the range which encloses the Dal lake on the east. The Isabar lies about two miles further north on the Dal shore and a little beyond the Nishat Bagh. The name Isabar is derived from the shrine of Tsesvara, which was erected by the king Sanidhimat in the honour of his teacher Tsvara. The goddess is seated on a rugged rock, some 3000 ft. above the village.

A natural rock on the top of the crag is regarded as her husband's residence. The Suresvari Mahatmya gives a legend regarding the residence of the goddess along with her husband at this place. Due to the difficulty in construction of any shrine on the rugged rock many shrines in her honour are constructed on the gentle slopes on the shore of the lake below. Kalhana and the authors of Kashmirian texts attribute special sanctity to it. There are many holy springs in its neighbourhood one of which Satadhara is mentioned by Ksemendra. The Suresvari Mahatmya describes in detail the route of the pilgrimage beginning from it. The Satadhara spring and the Suresvaritlrtha are mentioned by Jonar Srivara and Sarvavatara.

## Sarada

This is the ancient shrine of Sarada traced by Stein in September 1892 at Sardi in the upper Kishanganga valley, on a small hill above the junction of the Kishanganga with a small stream called Madhumati which flows from the mountain range to the south-east.

About this TTrtha Alberuni writes, In inner Kashmir about two or three days of journey from the capital in the direction towards the Bolar Mountain, there is a wooden idol called Sarada which is much respected and frequented by pilgrims. Abul Fazi also mentions the shrine of Sarada. A naga called Saradakunda, some 4 miles to the north of the Dal Lake is visited by many pilgrims.

Kalhana mentions Saradasthana in connection with the Sirahsila castle. This evidence proves the existence of Saradasthana in close neighbourhood of this fort. The general description of the locality points towards the upper Kishanganga valley. The numerous references in the Saradamahatmya and the work of Abul Fazi also support this. Similarly a note in Pt. Sahib Ram's Tirthasamgraha connects one of the Saradasthana with the Lotav Pargana adjoining the Kishanganga valley from the south.

The legend holds that the sage Sandilya performed severe penances to obtain the sight of the goddess Sarada. In accordance with a divine advice he went to Syamala Maharastra. There at Ghosa (Gus) Mahadevl appeared to him and promised to appear in her true form in the Sarada forest. The sage next went to Krsnaganga (present Krishnanag situated above the village of Drang and called Suna-Drang by local people). From thence he ascended the mountain range to the north and passing through the forests called Rangavati and Sostambhara arrived at Tejvana, the residence of Gautam on the Kishanganga. The Mahatmya then relates how the sage reached the Saradavana (present Sardi). Then it is related how the goddess revealed herself to him and invited him to her residence of Srisaila.

The Pitrs approached the sage and asked him to perform sraddhas. On his taking water from the Mahasindhu, half of its water turned into honey and became the river named Madhumati.

The mention of this confluence leaves no doubt about the location of the shrine. Sindhu here is the Kishanganga locally called as 'Sindh' and Madhumati is the stream that joins Kishanganga at Sardi from the south, Sardasthana stands at the junction of Madhumati with Kishanganga about 2-12 miles above the identified site of Sirahsilakotta.

A story recorded in a Jain work of the great grammarian Hemacandra proves that its fame has spread even to far off Gujarat. Abul Fazi noted the sanctity of the shrine and correctly indicates its position on the bank of the Madhumati.

Later on, the Tirtha was neglected perhaps because of the difficulties to the pilgrims due to the troubled political conditions of the upper Kishanganga valley.

## The Tirthas lying on the banks of Narmada.

Ankukesvara - It is mentioned in the Matsya Purana only.

Agastyesvara - A Siva temple on the northern, bank of Narmada at Akatesvara opposite Raaver. It is said that here the sage Agastya checked Vindhyacala from elevating.

Agnitirtha - It is situated on the northern bank of Narmada.

Angaresvara - Perhaps it is the same as Angaratirtha. It is said to have been situated on the northern bank of the river. The sage Angaraka performed penances here and installed a linga. Hence the name.

Apsaroyuga - It is the same as Apasaresa. The Brahma Purana locates it on the river Gautami. It is situated at Jigora on the southern bank of Narmada opposite Barawada 6 miles from Ora-Narmada confluence. Another Apsaresa Siva temple is situated at Aksol, 18 miles from Bharuch on the northern bank of Narmada.

Anaraka - There is no going to hell after seeing this, hence the name. The Kurma mentions it in Prayaga. ̮

Ayodhya - It is situated at Dilwada on the northern bank of Narmada.

Ayonisambhava - Same as Ayonija, situated on the northern bank of Narmada in Malsar city.

Ahilya - It was here that.Ahilya, the wife of the sage Gautama performed penances and installed a linga. An Ahalyesvara temple is situated at Bhalod opposite Dilwada on the southern bank of Narmada.

Asvatirtha - It seems that there were more than one Asvatirthas. One of them is located on Narmada. Another is situated at the confluence of Ganga and Kalindi in the district of Kanauj. There is an Asva-kranta mountain in Kamakhya near Gauhati in Assam.

Abhratakesvara - It is situated on the northern bank of Narmada.

Adityayatana ̮- It may be the same as Adityesvara and Adityesa. It is placed on the northern bank of Narmada. An Adityatlrtha has been placed on the banks of Sarasvati also. The tirtha Adityesvara is sacred to Sun. It is a temple near Bagadiyagram on the northern bank of Narmada at a short distance from Uluka tirtha which is 8 miles from Sulapani. Another Adityesvara tirtha is situated near Motikorala on the northern bank of Narmada opposite Karsanpuri, 18 miles from Malsar near̮Smor.

Asadhitirtha - It is another tirtha on the bank of Narmada, in Kasava.

Rnatirtha or Rnamocana - There is a Rnamuktesvara temple in the city of Chanod, The sages established this image for repaying of the debt. Another Rnatirtha is situated at Barua village at a short distance of Bharabhuta which is at a distance of 8 miles from Bharucha, on the northern bank of Narmada. Since no debt of Pitrs remains after seeing thisit is named Rnamocana.

Rsitirtha - It is a beautiful tirtha attributed to Narada, which is situated at the confluence of Jharjhari, Sussari and Ammapaveti rivers in the village of Parasoda on the bank of Sabarmati in the Bijapur District of Mehasana. The sage Shrnga had his hermitage here. Another Rsitirtha is situated on the Brahmakamandalu Ganga (Karha river).

Another Isinrtha is said to have been situated on the sea-shore and yet another is said to have been situated to the south of Dhruvaflrtha mentioned under Mathura-tirthaprasamsa.

Karanja - There is a Karanjesvara Siva femple at Nilagarh, I mtfe from Onkaresvara, on the Mandhata island in Narmada river. Another temple of that name is situated at Kanjetha, 2 miles from Sinor on the northern bank of Narmada.

Kapila, Kapilodaka and Kapilesvara- It is situated on the norhtern bank of Narmada. Bathing in it gives the fruit of the donation of a Kapila cow and the gift of a Kapila cow here results in the achievement of the fruit of the gift of the whole world. A Kapilatirtha is situated near the confluence of Sataka river. Mahu station is at a disance of 13 miles from Indore. At a distance of 34 miles from Mahu is Mandavagarh and 25 miles from Mandavagarh is Khalaghat. It is here that Brahma performed penances and a Kapila cow emerged from his sacrificial altar.

A Kapilodakanrtha is i situated in the front of Sulapani on the northern bank of Narmada where a Siva temple named Kapilesvara is situated. Another Kapilesvara temple is situated at Chanoda at the Malhar Rao bathing ghat of Narmada. It is said that the sage Kapila performed his penances here.

. A Kapila tirtha is said to'have been situated at Kuruksetra also. It is a river in Mysore. The portion of Narmada near its source, which issues from the western portion of the sacred Kunda and which after running for about two miles falls over the descent of 70 ft. into what is known as Kapiladhara is also called Kapila.

Kanthesvara - It is situated on the northern bank of the river.

Kanya - It is situated on the right bank of Narmada at the confluence. A temple of the goddess Kanyakumari is situated on the bank of Indian ocean at Cape Comprin . Dey places it in Kuruksetra.

Kusumesvara - It is situated on the southern bank of Narmada which has been established by Kama, hence it is called Kusumesvara. Here the god Cupid, burnt by Siva attained his body. It extends for half a yojana.

Kuberabhavana - There is a Kuberesvara temple on the bank of Narmada, one furlong from Somesvara temple at Karnall, situated at a distance of one mile from Ora and Narmada confluence. A tirtha named Kubertunga is mentioned in the Vayu Purana also. It is difficult to say whether both are one and the same.

Kundalesvara - It is situated on the northern bank of the river.

Kotisvara - The Kie-Isi-Shi-Fa-Lo of Hiuen Tsang, is situated on the southern bank of Narmada. It is placed near the mouth of the Kosi river on the western shore of Cutch.

Gangesvara - it has been installed by Ganga and is placed to the west of Samgamesvara.

Gargesvara - It is situated on the bank of Narmada.

Gautamesvara - It was founded by Gautama on the northern bank of Narmada. It seems that there are more than one Gautamesvara tirtha, one of them is at Gautamalaya in Trayamvakesvara, 17 miles from Nasik. There is another temple of Gautamesvara Mahadeva at Bodhan village, 13 miles from Kima Station which is 10 miles from Surat on Surat - Bharoch line. Yet another temple of Gautamesvara is situated at Bhaloda on the southern bank of Narmada opposite Dilwada. It is placed also at the distance of 15 bows to the north of Markandeyesvara.

Candratirtha - It is a sacred place mentioned in the Vayu Purana in continusation of the description of the Amarakantaka mountain. The Kurma Purana places it in Varanasi. There is a Candresvara Siva temple at Kothiya, 6 miles from Malsar near Smor on the northern bank of Narmada. The Mahabharata, too, records its importance and says that there are three mountains and three springs here. It was the residence of the sages Valakhilyas.

Cakratirtha - It is said to have been situated on the shore of the southern ocean near Phullagrama upto Devipatan. It is called Senapura also. It seems that there are a number of Tirthas that are named Cakratirtha (1) at Kuruksetra - which is the same as »Ramahrada, (2) to the north of Mathura, (3) on Gomati in Prabhasa, (4) at 6 miles from village Trayamvaka near the source of the Godavari, (5) in Benaras, a kurida dosed by an iron railing in the Manikarnikaghata.

Jvalesvara - It is situated on the Amarakantaka mountain at the source of Narmada. One of the two cities of Tripura fell on the earth while burning so it was called Jvalesvara. Perhaps it is the Jalesvara of the Kurma. A river named Jwala is said to have flown here. There appear to be more than one Jvalesvara temples on the bank of the Narmada, for one is said to have been situated on the Mandhata island in the Narmada, other at the confluence of Mahesvan and Narmada, at a short distance from Mahesvara city (ancient Mahismatipuri) and a third at the source of the river Jvala at a distance of four miles from the Amarakantaka mountainThe Skanda Purana gives its importance. It locates the Jalesvara Rudra linga in Bhrgukaccha. The Kurma Purana also describes a hrada named Jalesvara on the western side of the mountain. Since Siva threw his Pasupatastra ablaze here it is called Jvalesvara.

Jamadagnyatirtha - It is situated at the confluence of the Narmada and the ocean.

The Tapsesvara linga was installed by a hunter, who due to repentence performed penances on Narmada. 'It is situated at Tarasali on the Southern bank of Narmada at a distance of two miles from Siddhesvara.

Turasamga - It is situated on the bank of Narmada.

Tripura - It is said to be 200 yojanas long and 100 yojanas broad as situated in a beautiful region, the ground of which is even, devoid of trees and mountains. The city had four gates in four directions. It is said to have been built by the three sons of Tarakasura. According to the Matsya , it was the capital of the king Bana, whose daughter Usha was abducted by Aniruddha, the grandson of Krsna. According to the Puranas, the Tripura was the ancient Sonitapura.

The story of the destruction of Tripura is an allegorical representation of the expulsion of the Buddhists by the Saivas. It is the present Teor on Narmada, 7 miles to the west of Jabalpur and 3 miles north of Gopalpur Ghat on the northern bank of the Narmada.

Dasasvamedhajanana - It is situated to the west of Gangavadana.

Dipesvara - It was the hermitage of Vyasa. In ancient days Mahanadi returned back as she was afraid of Vyasa and started flowing to the right.

Dhara - It is both a river and a tirtha on the northern bank of the Narmada.

Dhautapapa - It was established by NandT and is situated in the vicinity of Cakratirtha and the Bhrgutirtha. A Siva temple named Dhutapapesvara is situated in a city named STnor on the northern bank of the Narmada.

Nanditirtha - A Nandisa, a Nandlkunda and a NandT - asrama have been mentioned in the epics and the Puranas.

Nandikesvara - It is the penance grove of Nandl situated on the northern bank of the Narmada at Baragada, ? miles from Ora and Narmada confluence. A temple of NandTkesvara is situated here.

Narmadesvara - It is a temple situated at Fatehpur, 12 miles from Malsar 'near Sinor on the northern bank of Narmada. The Narakatirtha is also situated on the river Narmada.

Nagatirtha - Perhaps it is the same as Nagesvara. It is situated on the northern bank of river Narmada. The snakes who did not obey their mother Kadru to help her in her gaining immortal victory over her co-wife, were cursed by her to fall into the mouth of Agni. They worshipped, Mahesvara Siva and obtained a boon that if they bathe in the water of Narmada they would have no fear of death. They installed a Linga here whi,ch was thus named the Nagatirtha. Another story tells us that the serpent Vasuki was purged of his sin of taking the drops of perspiration of dancing Siva, here on Reva bank. There are two Nagesvara temples on Narmada, one on the northern bank at Kanjetha, 2 miles from Sir-tor and the other on the southern bank at Karsanpuri, which is 18 miles from Malsar near Sinor. A Nagatirtha is also placed in the Kuruksetra, Gangadvara, Kanakhala and the mount Arbuda.

Naradesvara - It is situated on the northern bank of Narmada at Chudesvara about 7 miles from Ora-Narmada confluence. It is said to have been built by Narada.

Pancayatana - A linga named Onkara is known as Pancayatana. It has been placed on Narmada as well as in Varanasi. The Pandavesa or Pandutirtha is on Narmada near Ayoni-Sambhava.

Puskariru - It is situated on the northern bank of Narmada. There were two Puskarini tirthas, one at Kapilatirtha on the northern bank of Narmada opposite Sulapani, 50 miles from the confluence of Hatani and Narmada rivers. Another is near Kambalesvara temple situated at Bagadiyagrama which is at a short distance of Ulukatirtha, 8 miles from Sulapani.

Pingalesvara - The Pingalesvara temple is situated at Pingalavarttam. There is a Pingalesvara temple on the northern bank of Narmada at Motikorala opposite Karsanpuri which is 18 miles from Malsar near Smor. Another Pingalesvara temple is situated near the Nandahradatirtha at some distance from the confluence of Ora and Narmada.

Pippalesvara - It is placed on the northern bank of Narmada. It is situated at ? miles from Mardana on the northern bank in PitamaK village, 26 miles from Barawaha station near Omkaresvara road station on Ajmer Khandwa line on the Western Railways.

Pitamaha - It is placed on the northern bank of Reva, and is named after Pitamaha Brahma who bathed in it.

Balakesvara - It is situated near Agastyesvara. A Balakatirtha is situated near the Gandhamadana mountain also.

Bahunetra - It is also a Narmada tirtha.

Brahmesvara - The Brahmesvara tTrtha also called Amohaka is situated on the northern bank of Narmada on the Bhrgu mountain. North of this temple is the Kamalesvara temple, to the south of which are seven temples of the seven sages. It is said that god Sankara struck the mountain here and the river Sarasvati - Ganga emerged. At the place where the Trisula struck, a Kunda was formed which remains in Narmada. It is here that Brahma installed a linga which was thereafter called Brahmesvara. It appears that there are more than one Brahmesvara tirtha, one of them is situated near a village called Kamdarola which is about 4 miles from Sinor. Brahma performed penances here and the altar of Brahma is called Brahmesvara. Another one is situated at Jigora on the southern bank of Narmada opposite Barwada, 6 miles from Ora and Narmada confluence. Still another temple of Brahmesvara is situated near the shrine of Ananta Vasudeva on the eastern bank of Bindusagar, a lake at Bhuvaneshvara and yet another has been placed on the banks of the Sarasvati.

Bharabhuti and Bhrngatirtha are also situated on Narmada. Bharabhuta is situated at a distance of eight miles from Bhroach.

The Bhrgutirtha is situated to the west of Dasasvamedhajanana on the southern bank of Narmada. Dey identifies it with Bheraghat, containing the temple of Chaunsath Yoginis, 12 miles west of Jabalpur on Narmada between marble rocks. The sage Bhrgu had his hermitage on the Narmada at this place.

Bhrgutunga - It is the BhrgupTtha regarded holy for Sraddhas. Yayati performed penances in the forest here. The Skanda Purana places it on the northern bank of Reva.

Bhadresvara - It is an ancient temple situated at a place which is 1 mile near Vai on the river Krsna.

Bhimesvara is situated on the southern bank of Narmada near the Chudesvara temple. and Bhargavesa and Karkotesvara are also on Narmada.

Mandavya - The sage Mandavya had his hermitage here. It is an ancient temple on the southern bank of Narmada at Ankaleshvara which is 5 miles from Bharucha.

Mahesvara - A Mahesvandhara,Mahesvarapura and Mahesvarapada (A subsidiary tirtha of the Somatirtha)-have also been mentioned. It is Mahes or Chuli Maheshvara on Narmada.

Mukutatirtha - A Sivalinga named Mukutesvara is situated at Sisodara opposite Sinor.

The Meghakara is also a Narmada-tirtha.

Meghanada - There is a temple called Meghesvara at Jigora opposite Barawada, 5 miles from KaranaE, and one mile from the Ora-Narmada confluence on the southern bank of Narmada. Dey regards it to be the river Meghna, which is the Brahmaputra in the southerly course towards the ocean after leaving Assam.

Yantresvara extends for one Yojana on the northern bank of the Narmada. The Yama'sposition on Narmada is not specified.

The Ravanesvara on Narmada is a Siva linga in the Banesvara temple at an island in Narmada near the city of Mahesvari A Siva temple of this name is situated near Lohargalaa famous tirtha of Rajasthan, 20 miles from Sikar or Navalgarh situated on the Savai-Madhopur and Luhas Railway line.

Varunesvara - It is a Siva temple at Barawada on the northern bank of Narmada, at a distance of 6 miles from Ora-Narmada confluence. Another Varunesvara temple is situated at Motikoral on the northern bank of Narmada opposite Karsanpuri, which is 18 miles from Malsar near Sinor.

Vatesvara - There is a Vatesvara temple at Chudeshvara, 7 miles from Ora-Narmada confluence. Another Vetesvara Siva temple is at Sasavada Road station, 7 miles from Poona.

Varahatirtha - Janardana is worshipped here as Varaha. It is situated on the northern bank of the river Narmada. God emerged here with the earth under the guise of Varaha. Two sacred places named Trishulaghat and Trishulatirtha are situated on the road from Jabalpur to Nagpur on both the banks of Narmada at a distance of nine miles from Jabalpur. The Narmada flows here cutting the mountain just like a Trisula. The Brahma Purana places it on Godavari and the Kurma mentions it under Varanasi Mahatmya.

Vimalesvara - It is situated at Bargaon, 4 miles from KapilatTrtha on the northern bank of the Narmada. Another Vimalesvara tirtha is situated at Visoda on the southern bank of Narmada.

Visnu - It was famous as Yodhinipura. The one on Narmada is situated at the confluence of Kadiya and Narmada rivers. Another Visnutirtha, a drykunda is situated at Kapila and Godavari.

Sakratirtha - Indra cursed by Gautam lost all his lusture and the kingdom. Seeing him as such the sages etc. persuaded Gautama to give him his lusture back. After that Indra came to Narmada at this place which was afterwards came to be known as SakratTrtha. It is situated on the southern bank of the river. A Sakratirtha in Kubjamrakaksetra is recorded in the Varaha Purana near Amaresvara.It is situated at Indrabanogram, on the southern bank of Narmada, which is situated opposite Garudeshvara which in its turn is 16 miles from Sulapani. Another Sakratirtha is at Barawada village near Gaughat which is 15 miles from Malsar near STnor. There is a Sakresvara temple built by Indra.

Salagrama - It is a sacred village on the bank of Narmada.

Suklatirtha - It is situated on the northern bank of the river to the north of the Sthanuvata. It is situated at Shuklaghat on the northern bank of Narmada at a distance of 16 miles from Betthari. The sage Kasyapa is said to have his hermitage here. A fair is held here at the time of eclipse. It is 10 miles to the north-east of Bhroach in Gujarat, a sacred place near which HumkaresvaratTrtha and the Ravitirtha are also situated.

Sukresvara and Sukratirtha is a Siva linga installed here by Sukra, the son of Bh.rgu.

Sankhoddhara- There is a Sankhoddhara tirtha near Maharudra which is situated at a distance of about two miles from Bharuch. The island of Bati (Beyt) belonging to the Gujarat state is situated at the south-western extremity of the Gulf of Cutch. Visnu is said to have destroyed a demon named Sankhasura at this place and to have delivered the Vedas.

Sikhitirtha - The Matsya (19382), the Skanda (Reva Ch.202, 22996) and the Padma Purana (Svarga 2079) mention it.

Sulabheda - It is situated on the southern bank of Reva and has been installed by Sulapani. It is the Sulpan Mahadeva or Makri Fall, a place of pilgrimage near the junction of the Narmada and a mountain stream called the Sarasvati.

Svatantresvara - Matsya places it on Narmada.

Skandatirtha - It is situated on the southern bank of Narmada. It is a temple at Kandrol, 4 miles from SInor on the southern bank of the river.

Samgamesa - It is situated on the southern bank of Narmada. The Linga Purana locates it on a confluence. It is situated on the Nilaganga-samgama on way to Trayamvakesvara.Another Samgamesa Siva temple is situated at Gamona, 14 miles from Sulapani, still another Samgamesvara temple is situated at the confluence of Hatani river on the northern bank of the river.It is placed in Kasi and at Gangasagarasamgama also.

Savitri - It is situated is Bhrguksetra (modern Bharoch).

Siddhesvara - It is situated on the southern bank of the river. A Siddhesvara temple is situated near Vai which is a famous tirtha on the Krishna river. Another Siddhesvara temple is in a forest at a distance of two miles from the southern bank of Narmada in a forest near Varunesvara. Another

Siddhesvara temple is in Varangala city on the VadT-Bejwada line at a distance of ? miles from Kajipet. A Siddhesvara temple is situated in Siddhapur also. It seems that the Siddhesvaratirtha mentioned in the Siva Purana is a different one situated in Kasi.

Somesvara or Somatirtha - It is situated on Narmada. The Skanda Purana locates it on the western ocean, on the Reva-Uri samgama. A Somesvara temple is situated on the northern bank of Narmada at Dilavada, one mile from Motikoral opposite Karsanpuri, 18 miles from Malsar near Sinor. Another SomesvartTrtha is at KarnaH, I mile from the confluence of Ora and Narmada. A Somesvaralinga was installed by Rama at Tulajapur, 24 miles from Sholapur station. It seems that the Somatirtha referred to as a northern tirtha in the Nilamata is one situated in Kasi.

Hiranyadvipa is mentioned in the Matsya only (19368).

Hamsatirtha - It is the place where Hamsa performed penances and attained salvation. It is situated at a distance of two miles from Matrtirtha,on the northern bank of Narmada at a distance of 22 miles from the confluence of Hatani and Narmada at Hapeshvara. Another Hamsatirtha is situated at Mahabaleshvara. The Varaha Purana locates it in the Salagramaksetra.

Airanditirtha is situated on the northern bank of Narmada at the confluence of Airandi and Narmada at Kolyad.

Indratirtha is situated on the southern bank of the river Narmada at Indore ghat, 8 miles from Malsar. Another Indratirtha is situated near Kusavarta in the eastern portion of Govardhanaksetra which is at a distance of 2 yojanas from Mathura.

## Miscellaneous

This chapter deals with certain geographical facts which though recorded in the Puranas, have not been treated in detail.

## Climate

Though the Puranas abound in detailed descriptions of the continents, mountains, and the territorial divisions of India they are strikingly silent about its climate. The Puranas give practically no information about the climate. The only available remarks about it in the Puranas are casual. They mention the six seasons, i namely the Vasanta (spring), Grisma (summer), Varsa (rains), Sarada (Autumn), Hemanta (winter) and Sisira (cold). The spring season falls in the month of Caitra and Vaisakha. The summar months are Jyestha and Asadha. During Sravana and Bhadrapada, it is the rainy season. The months of Asvina and Kartika are the Sarada period. The Hemanta and Sisira fall during the months of Margasirsa and Pausa, Magha and Phalguna respectively. The same six seasons are mentioned in the Matsya Purana though no specific duration has been given there. The only other reference about the natural phenomena is to the snowfall in winter.

## Occupation

The entire population of the India was mainly divided into four castes. This broad based division was on the basis of the respective duties of the individual. Those who performed sacrifices, accepted gifts and were devoted to the studies were called Brahmanas. The Ksatriyas were devoted to the science of war. The Vaisyas were engaged in business agriculture and the cattle breeding. The Sudras were devoted to' the services of the three castes Brahmanas, Ksatriyas and Vaisyas. They also performed transactions and were artisans as well.Besides, there were RangaJIvinah (actors), Mallas(athletes, boxer, wrestler), Bhallas (fighters), Varmukhya (the chief of a number of harlots), Nata (dancer), Nartaka (dancing preceptors) and Silpi(mechanics, artisans, architects, goldsmiths etc.). An advanced art of architecture can be inferred from the minute details given about the construction of the various types of forts and buildings etc.. The idol making, too, was in an advanced stage.

Moreover, there was a class of people called Sutas who previously earned their living by devoting themselves to the art of war just as the Ksatriyas did, but later on they started driving the chariots etc..

Another important occupation was agriculture.The god Brahma is said to have milked the earth for the first time. He made Vayu the calf and milked all the seeds of the earth. The minute details given for construction of the ponds, deep pools, tanks, very large oblong or circular reserviors of water and canals etc. show that many artificial means of irrigation were used. A proper method of the plantation is also given.

There are references to the Ksaumasutra (Silken thread), Karpasika (cotton), Urna (wool) and Kauseya (silken cloth in general) which shows that the textile industry was developed to a considerable extent. Clothes were made of cotton and the hair of sheep. References to blankets, golden cloth,very- fine cloths and sarees are also found in the Puranas.

There is also a reference to the oil of black Tilaswhich shows that people made oils from oilseeds. The salt was made from the water of the ocean and the lake Manasa. We have references to pig-breeding, cattle breeding, sugar industry and tanning as well.

The Sanskrit Karpasa, Greek Karpasos was the seed fibre of the Gossypium herebaceum and Gossypium Arboreum, native in India and woven in cloth by the native of that country before the dawn of history. Cotton thread and cloth are repeatedly mentioned in the laws of Manu (80Q B.C.)-Prof. A. H. Sayee in his Hibbert lectures shows that it was exported by- sea to the head of the Persian gulf in the 4th millenuium B.C. and it found its way very early to Egypt. Herodotus describes it as a wool better than that of a sheep, the fruit of the trees growing wild in India. It seems that muslin and silk, a product of Punjab and Sind was also known to him. Roman traders found silk at the mouths of the Indus and the Ganges and at the Gulf of Cambay and in Travancore wither it had been brought by various routes from north-west China. Periplus mentions two kinds of cloth, Monache cloth and Molochine or Mallow cloth. It also refers to Argaritic Muslins. McCrindle observes that India has two distinct species of cotton Gossypium merbaceum and Goossypiunn Arboreum or tree cotton. The former only is made into cloth while letter yields a soft and sflky texture used for padding cushions, pillows etc.. Molochine cloth was a course cloth dyed with a preparation of a variety of hibisars native in India.

The textile industry of both Trichinopoly and Tanjore has been well known from very early times. Some of the finest fabrics that were sent to Rome came from this very place. In middle ages, the gold threaded embroideries which were so much in demand in Sorocen markets, came from here. Periplus notes the great quantity of cotton cloth made at Masalia (Modern Masuli Patam). The muslin of the finest sort called the Gangetic muslin of the Dacca district was the most delicate of all the Indian fabrics.

The trade in silk yarn and silk cloth existed in northern India soon after the invasion of the Aryans. In the Ramayana and Mahabharata silk is mentioned several times as gift from foreign countries. Some trade might at least went further west. Generally Romans and Greeks confused between silk and cotton but Pliny is fairly correct in describing cotton.

The means of conveyance were Gajayana, Asvayana, Goyana and Narayana.

## Gems

The ocean has been regarded as the birth place of Laksim from which we may infer that costly stones were divied from the ocean. A number of varieties of costly stones is given in the Puranas. They were :- Muktaphala - a pearl, Vajra - a diamond, Gomeda - a gem brought from the Himalaya and Indus and described as of four different colours i.e. white, pale yellow, red and dark blue, Pusparaja -Topez, Garutmata - an emerald, Nila - ratna - blue gem, Vaidurya - cat's eye gem, Pravala - coral, Svarna - gold, Rajata - silver, Vidruma -coral, Panduraga - ruby Indramla - emerald, MahanTia -a kind of sapphire or emerald. Rajavarta - a diamond of an inferior quality, Suryakanta - sunstone or sun crystal, Candrakanta - moonstone suppose to ooze away under the influence of moon, Syamantaka - a kind of valuable gem said to yield daily eight loads of gold and to protect from all kinds of danger and portents, Sarpa-mani - a snake gem, Valaksamani -white gem, Sphatikamani - a crystal quartz, Pittaka-an inferior variety of gems, Dhuli-markata - the dust of emerald, Saugandhika - a ruby, Rajamani - a royal gem, Marakata - emerald, Mukta - a pearl, Hirakah - a diamond, Sutuhi, Brahmamard, Suramani, Ramya Jyotirasa, Mukhya, Ruciraksa, Karkokata, Sesamani and Sarojaraga.

Periplus makes mention- of the Topaz, the best sort of the Topaz coming from India, the red sea-gem being the true topaz. Lapis Lazuli and their export to Europe etc. was also current during those days.

It also mentions the great quantities of five pearls which came from the fisheries of Gulf of Manar and brought to be sold in the Chera ports.

India and Ceylon were prominently the source of the production of precious stones of all kinds which were exported to every part of the civilized world. These tranparent stones were classified in various groups.

(1) Beryl group - Beryllium of Pliny. Beryle, says Pliny are prodced in India. The most esteemed Beryle are those which in colour resemble the pure green of the sea....

(2) Diamonds - Adamos of Pliny. India was for long the only source of diamonds known to European nations. Garcia-de-orta (1563 A.D.) mentions various eastern mines, such as those of Pisnagar (Vijayanagara) and the 'Decam' (Deccan). Ball in his translation of Tavernier's Travels gives full particulars of all Indian sources of diamonds. The principal districts were:-

(1) Southern group:- districts of Kadapa Bellary, Karnal, Kistna and Godavari (Golconda etc).

(2) Middle group:- Mahanad valley, districts of Sambalpur, Chanda.

(3) Northern group-Vindhyan conglomerates near Panna. Sapphire was the Hyacinthus of Pliny

and the principal source of Sapphires here was the watered gravels of sourthern Ceylon. They were derived from the watered crystaline rocks and at the time of the Periplus the natural market would have been on the Malabar coast.

(3) Pearl.

(4) Ruby - Carbunculus of Pliny.

(5) Sapphire - of many colours blue, violet, yellow, green, white, produced mainly on southern Malabar hills now scarcely found in India but more frequently in Ceylon. It is the Hyacinthus of Pliny.

(6) Spinel - included among the twelve varieties of Pliny's Carbunculus.

(7) Topaz - Chrysolithos of Pliny. Periplus shows that it was imported from the Red sea.

(8) Turquoise - Callaina was (of Pliny) as a product of Persia and not found in India but reaching its north western part.

(9) Garnet - One of the twelve varieties, perhaps the Alabandic of Pliny's Carbunculus. They were common in many parts of India, those of Rajputana being the best.

(10) Jade & Jadeite were found mainly in Turkistan and upper Burma. Though they were not produced in India, yet all of them found their way to Indian markets.

(11) Lapis lazuli - or Ultramarine, also from Persia largely used for decoration of all kinds and in demand in India, Egypt and Meditterranean world from the earliest times. It is the Sapphros of Pliny.

(12) Quartzose including (1) Rock Crystals white coloured (Crystel of Pliny) (2) Agate-Carnelian, bloodstone, Chrysoprase Jasper, Chalcedony, and Cat's eye.

(13) Tourmalines - Varying from black through red, dark blue, olive green and white. The red variety was the commenest in India. It is the Lychnis of Pliny.

## Forests

Though the Puranas mention a number of forests in India, yet they neither give their situation nor their extent. It is only in some cases that the one is said to have situated on the bank of this river or that river. Thus Surabhivar'ia has been located on the bank of Sailoda or Vasvokasara on the mountain Harisrnga and the Vaibraja forest is said to have been situated on the banks of the Sarayu river in the Ketumalavarsa.

The forest named Madhuvana or Madhavavana on the banks of the Yamuna was named after the demon Madhu who lived there.The one situated on the bank of the Lauhitya river was called the Visoka forest. The forest named Anjana was situated near Saketa. The Kamyakavana has been located in the Mathura district as well as on the banks of the Sarasvati. The forest named Caitraratha was in Alaka on the mount Kailasa on the banks of Mandakini and Sarasvati. The Devadaruvana was situated on the Himalayas. It has been placed in Saurastra also. It has been placed close to the western ocean in the Siva Purana. It has been identified with Aundha in the Hyderabad state. The one situated on the Himalayas has been identified with Jagesvara.Another forest situated on the Himalayas was called Saravana which was the birth place of the god Kartikeya. Sikhandavana was situated on the mountain SikhandT and mountain Himavan. The forest on the Gandhamadana mountain was called Kumaravana. This has been identified with Kumaun region. The Denukaranya was situated in Gaya and the Mahakalavana, sacred to the deity Mahakala was situated in Avanti. Here flowed the river Sipra. The Talavana was on the banks of Yamuna. The Rukmaparijatavana or Parijatavana was situated on the mountain Hemakuta, where flows the river Mandakini. The Pankajavana or Campakavana was situated on the banks of Campaka river in the Gaya region.

Besides, the forests named Pandarakavana,Savitravana, Siddhavana Haimakavana, and Rudravana are also mentioned.

The forest of the elephant named Anjana extended from Kausiki to ocean and thence to the river Ganga. The forest of the Suprafika elephant extended from the north of the Vindhya upto the south of the Ganga and from the origin of the Ganga upto the country of Karusa. The elephant named Vamana had his forest as extending from the western portion of the country of Utkala upto the country called Vedi. The forest of Yama extended from the banks of the river Lauhitya upto the mountain on the western bank of the ocean.
